LESSON 1:  HOMEOSTASIS  
 
Human physiology is the study of how the 
human body functions as opposed to human  
anatomy, the study of the structure of the human 
body.  One might consider that the basic function of 
the human organism, and all other living things, is 
to maintain homeostasis. Homeo stasis is the 
condition of being in the optimal steady state.  
Disease follows directly from a loss of homeostasis. 
For example, consider the cell, the basic 
unit of life. While there are many kinds of cells, 
they all have certain functions in common. All 
living cells need to produce energy to stay alive.  
They all consume oxygen and carbohydrates and 
other nutrients for this purpose. All living cells are 
surrounded with a liquid extracellular fluid. For 
blood cells, this fluid is called plasma. For all other 
cells, it’s called interstitial fluid. The fluid within 
the cell (intracellular fluid) is called cytoplasm or 
cytosol. While extracellular and intracellular fluids 
are both mostly water, they do have important 
differences. Extracellular fluid has high 
concentrations of sodium ions, chloride ions and 
bicarbonate ions. Cytoplasm has more potassium 
ions, magnesium ions and phosphate ions. This 
"steady state" gradient of ions is maintained by 
every cell through the constant expenditure of 
cellular energy. If the ions were allowed to go to 
equilibrium, the cell would die.   
This is one of many examples of 
homeostasis. Maintaining normal blood pressure, 
normal blood oxygen concentration, and normal 
body temperature are others. The normal body 
temperature is about 98.6o F, and is maintained over 
a wide range of environmental temperatures . The 
body expends energy to maintain this constant 
internal temperature. After death, this "steady state" 
temperature will equilibrate with that of the 
environment.     
ORGAN SYSTEMS 
 The basic unit of life is the cell. Groupings 
of like cells  are called tissues. Different kinds of 
tissues combine to form organs. For example, the 
stomach has muscle tissue and nerve tissue and 
connective tissue and epithelial tissue. Groupings of 
organs that together are able to perform some 
homeostatic function are called organ systems .  
For example, the stomach combined with the 
esophagus, intestines, and other digestive organs, 
provides nutrients for the body. Put all the organ 
systems together and you have an organism. The 
human organ systems and their basic functions are 
listed below. 
 The Circulatory System  continuously 
replenishes the raw materials and removes waste 
products of cells. There are 25 trillion red blood 
cells carrying oxygen to me et the metabolic needs 
of the other 50 trillion to 75 trillion cells outside the 
circulatory system. Every living cell is within 25 to 
50 micrometers of a blood capillary. 
 The Respiratory System  brings oxygen 
into the body where it rapidly enters the bloo d.  
Without oxygen, cells cannot produce enough 
energy to sustain life functions. The respiratory 
system also transports carbon dioxide, a metabolic 
waste product, out of the body. 
 The Digestive System , as mentioned 
above, provides the body with basic nutrients.  
 The Musculoskeletal System  aids in the 
quest for survival by allowing communication, 
locomotion and internal and external movement.  
 The Urinary System , with its pair of 
kidneys, removes certain metabolic wastes from the 
body and keeps water and electrolytes in balance to 
control blood pressure. 
 The Nervous System  allows the body to 
perceive its environment and then to respond to 
conditions as necessary. Control involves a three -
step process. Sensory receptors (1) communicate 
information regarding conditions in the internal or 
external environment to the central nervous 
system, which (2) assesses the conditions and then 
(3) acts to modifiy them if necessary by stimulating 
motor effectors to respond with muscle contraction 
or gland secretion. 
 The Endocrine System, the other control 
system along with the nervous system,  exerts

===== PAGE 6 =====
2 
control by using blood-borne hormones to produce 
effects in target cells. 
 The Reproductive System  doesn't just 
provide homeostatic mechanisms for the organism, 
but it contributes to the survival of organisms in 
general because every organism will ultimately fail 
and new ones must take their place. 
CONTROL SYSTEMS OF THE BODY 
 The body relies on thousands of different 
control mechanisms. Control in the body is carried 
out by the nervous system  that is basically an 
electrochemical control system, and the endocrine 
system that is a chemical system. The degree of 
effectiveness of a control mechanism is measured in 
terms of gain. The higher the gain, the better the 
control.  Gain is directly related to the ability to 
correct deviation and inversely related to the 
amount of "error" that remains. Suppose, for 
example, blood pressure drops to 50 mmHg below 
normal. Homeostatic mechanisms are able to 
increase the pressure, subtracting 2 5 mmHg from 
the error (correction), but pressure is still 25 mmHg 
below normal (error). The gain is ( -25/25) or -1. 
The negative sign is characteristic of negative 
feedback control systems. Suppose blood pressure 
increases 30 mmHg above normal and the body 's 
homeostatic control mechanisms lower it 20 mmHg 
toward normal, the gain is:  
indicating better control than the first example..   
Most of the body’s control mechanisms 
involve negative feedback. "Negative" means that 
when something deviates from normal, the control 
mechanism causes it to reverse direction and move 
back toward normal. Consider a sudden loss of a 
liter of blood. That represents a loss of about 20% 
of the total blood volume. Initially, the output of the 
heart would fall below normal due to the reduced 
volume. The nervous system, sensing the reduced 
output, would stimulate the heart to contract harder 
and more frequently, bringing its output back up 
toward normal. 
Negative feedback is used to maintain 
normal body temperature with a gain of a bout -30, 
indicating it is much more efficient than the 
mechanism for blood pressure control described 
above. Compared to short -term control of blood 
pressure by the heart, long -term control of blood 
pressure by the kidneys is able to correct errors 
completely. That is to say, the control mechanisms 
of the kidneys have infinite gain.   
Only a few normal control mechanisms 
involve positive feedback; the correction and error 
moving in the same direction . Usually, positive 
feedback loops result in vicious cycles that severely 
disrupt homeostasis and often result in death. For 
example, consider a sudden loss of two liters of 
blood. Now the output of the heart falls by more 
than half. The nervous system responds, but 
because of the reduced blood flow to the heart  
muscle itself, the heart is not getting enough of the 
oxygen it needs to function. The heart weakens and 
pumps even less blood. Further reduction in blood 
flow results in less oxygen delivery and the heart 
weakens more. The output falls again making it 
even weaker, and so on until the vicious cycle 
causes it to fail completely.   
There are a few examples of positive 
feedback systems that lead to desirable outcomes.  
In childbirth, the pressure of the infant’s head 
against the cervix stimulates stretch rece ptors 
causing uterine contractions. The contractions 
increase the force exerted on cervix causing more 
intense stimulation. In response, contractions 
become stronger, and so on until the baby is born. 
 Another kind of control mechanism is feed 
forward. Thi s is an important mechanism for 
“programming” the brain.  An example is a medical 
student in surgical rotation making his/her first 
incision through skin.  How much pressure must be 
applied?  After the first attempt, the brain assesses 
the result. Excessiv e bleeding means too much 
pressure and less pressure will be applied on the 
next attempt. The brain goes forward and makes a 
"blind" attempt to correct it self on the next time 
around.
Gain = Correction
Error = –20
10 = –2

LESSON 2:  TRANSPORT ACROSS CELL MEMBRANES 
 
The membrane that surroun ds living body 
cells is in the form of a fluid mosaic ; proteins 
floating in a phospholipid matrix. The membrane is 
a double layer of phospholipid with proteins on the 
surface, embedded in, or completely bridging the 
lipid portion.  Further, the proteins are  not 
stationary, but moving within the matrix (fluid). 
The structure of the cell membrane is also 
characteristic of the many organelles found within 
cells. The lipid nature of the membrane prevents it 
from dissolving in body fluids that are water-based. 
The membrane not only serves as a water-insoluble 
boundary, but also carries out considerable 
transport and metabolic activity.  Lipid soluble 
materials can pass through the lipid portion of the 
membrane, while water -soluble materials move 
through pores in t he proteins that penetrate the 
membrane. 
The concentrations of electrolytes and 
nutrients are usually different in the interstitial fluid 
than in the cytoplasm.  More sodium and chloride 
ions are found outside than inside the cell and more 
potassium and magnesium ions are found inside 
the cell. The cell membrane is responsible for 
maintaining these and other chemical gradients that 
are vital to the life of the cell. Molecules that are 
transported across the cell membrane use one or 
more of the transport mechanisms described below. 
PHYSICAL TRANSPORT  
 The three physical transport processes for 
consideration are diffusion, osmosis and filtration. 
These transport systems occur in both living and 
non-living systems and require no cellular energy 
(adenosine triphosphate, ATP). 
 Diffusion is the movement of particles 
from an area of higher concentration to an area of 
lower concentration as a result of the random 
motion of the dissolved particles. Particles move 
continuously across a freely permeable membrane 
in both directions, but net diffusion occurs from the 
side of high concentration to the side of low 
concentration. When the concentrations have 
reached equilibrium, movement across the 
membrane continues equally in both directions.    
 Lipid soluble molecules and very small 
neutral molecules can diffuse through the lipid 
portion of the membrane.  Charged molecules and 
large lipid insoluble molecules pass through the 
protein pores.  Passage of certain molecules through 
the pores is controlled by a part of the pr otein that 
acts as a gate, opening and closing the pore 
channel.  Gates open in response to voltage changes 
or ligand molecules binding to them.  This enables 
cells to control transport of certain molecules 
electrically or chemically. 
 As summarized in the  following equation, 
the rate of diffusion (D) changes directly with 
temperature (T), solubility (S), membrane surface 
area (A), and the magnitude of the concentration 
gradient (C 1-C2).  The diffusion rate is inversely 
related to the molecular weight (MW) of the 
particles and the thickness (d) of the membrane. 
(Molecular weight is a way of including the 
velocity of molecules. The velocity of a molecule’s 
random motion is inversely proportional to its 
molecular weight.)  
The gradient for diffusion need not b e a 
concentration gradient.  Diffusion can occur down 
pressure or electrical (charge) gradients as well.  
The distribution of many ions across the cell 
membrane is determined by a combination of these 
gradients. 
 In the body, blood carried to the lungs is 
high in CO 2 (carbon dioxide) but low in O 2 
(oxygen). Air inhaled into the lungs is rich in O 2 
with almost no CO2. When blood in the capillaries 
comes into close contact with the air spaces in the 
lungs, net diffusion of O 2 occurs into the blood 
while net diffusion of CO2 occurs into the air filling 
the lungs.   
EXAMPLE:  A U -tube is divided by a membrane 
permeable to both salt and water. Initially pure 
water is added to one side of the tube while 20% 
salt solution is added to the other. In time, we will 
find that both sides of the membrane have equal 
(10%) concentrations of salt. Net diffusion has 
occurred down the concentration gradient.  
Referring to the diagram above, more salt will 
move into the salt-free side than will move into the 
20% salt side.  More water will move into the 20%

===== PAGE 8 =====
4 
salt (80% water) side than will move into the salt -
free (100% water) side. When concentrations of salt 
on both sides are equal, the system is in the state of 
equilibrium and equal diffusion is occurring in 
both directions.   
 Facilitated diffusion is a special kind of 
diffusion found only in living cells.  Here, a protein 
carrier located within the cell membrane binds to 
the molecule and moves it across the membrane, 
releasing it on the other side. Glucose is carried 
across c ell membranes in this way. The carrier 
protein transports glucose from the side of high 
concentration to the side of low concentration. The 
lipid-insoluble glucose molecule is easily 
transported in this way. In facilitated diffusion, the 
carrier molecule is not able to transport molecules 
against a concentration gradient.   
 In simple diffusion, the rate of diffusion is 
directly proportional to the concentration gradient.  
In facilitated diffusion, the rate is proportional to 
the gradient only until the car rier mechanism is 
saturated.  Any further increase in the gradient does 
not increase the diffusion rate.  
 Osmosis is movement of water across a 
differentially permeable membrane, permeable to 
the water but not to the molecule dissolved in the 
water. Water moves from the side of greater water 
concentration to the side of lower water 
concentration. Because protein pores are selective 
and gated pores can open and close, the cell 
membrane is differentially permeable . In this 
situation, a concentration gradient  may be 
maintained across the membrane . All particles 
cannot move to a state of equilibrium.  Water 
diffusion under such conditions is called osmosis.   
EXAMPLE: If we divide a U -tube in half with a 
differentially permeable membrane such that it is 
permeable to water but not to salt, and then if we 
add a solution of 20% salt to one side and a salt -
free solution to the other side, we will discover that 
osmosis will cause a net movement of water toward 
the side with the greater concentration of 
impermeable particles (side containing 20% salt). 
In the example diagrammed below, the 
volume on the left side (the side that started at 20% 
salt, 80% water) will increase and the volume on 
the right side (that started at 0% salt, 100% water) 
will decrease.  The 20% salt  side has a greater 
osmotic pressure (water pulling power) than the 
salt-free side. The higher osmotic pressure of the 
high salt side is reflected by the fact that the side 
with salt is able to support the added weight of the 
rising column of fluid. The di fference between the 
levels of water on the two sides is a measure of 
osmotic pressure.  
If there is no difference in the osmotic 
pressure there will be no net movement of water to 
either side. T he system is isosmotic. If there is a 
difference in osmotic p ressure, the side with the 
greater osmotic pressure is called hyperosmotic 
relative to the side with lower osmotic pressure. 
The side with the lower osmotic pressure is called 
the hypoosmotic side. The net movement of water 
will be toward the hyperosmotic side, that being the 
side with the greater concentration of impermeable 
particles and the lower concentration of water. 
EXAMPLE: A solution of 0.9% sodium chloride 
(NaCl) is isosmotic to red blood cells. If we add red 
cells to a beaker of 3% NaCl (97% wate r), they 
crenate (shrink). The solution in the beaker is 
hyperosmotic to the blood cells. The blood cells are 
hypoosmotic to the solution in the beaker. Water 
moves down its concentration gradient. It moves 
out of the cells and the cells shrink.    
 What w ould happen to red blood cells 
placed into a beaker of pure water?  They will swell 
and perhaps even burst. This is called hemolysis.  
The water in the beaker is hypoosmotic to the red

===== PAGE 9 =====
5 
cells and the red cells are hyperosmotic to the water 
in the beaker. T he higher osmotic pressure inside 
the cells is reflected in the net movement of water 
into the cells. Water moving down its concentration 
gradient, moves into cell and causes it to hemolyze.  
  Clinically, solutions to be added to blood 
are made in isotonic concentrations. That is, they 
are made to become isosmotic when added to 
blood.  Isotonic saline is 0.9% NaCl. That is, 0.9% 
of the total volume of the salt -water solution is 
taken up by sodium chloride molecules. Osmotic 
pressure is determined by the number of 
impermeable particles. Since glucose molecules are 
much larger than sodium  and chloride molecules, 
the same number of glucose molecules added to the 
water would take up 5% of the total volume. A 5% 
glucose solution is, therefore, isotonic. 
 Filtration occurs when water and 
dissolved particles are pushed across a membrane 
by hydrostatic pressure. Movement is from the 
side with greater pressure to the side with lower 
pressure. Water and many kinds of dissolved 
particles are able to move across capillary walls by 
filtration.   
EXAMPLE:  If we pour a mixture of water, salt and 
charcoal into a funnel lined with filter paper, the 
water passes through and it tastes salty indicating 
the salt also passes through. The filter paper 
becomes black indicating tha t charcoal does not 
pass through. Observing the fluid dripping from the 
funnel, we see that the greater the quantity of fluid 
still in the funnel, the faster the rate of filtration.  
The force of gravity acting on a greater quantity of 
fluid above the filt er causes a greater hydrostatic 
pressure against the filter and increases the 
filtration rate. 
 In the kidney, some of the blood plasma 
and small molecules in the plasma are filtered into 
the urine. Blood cells and large protein molecules 
remain in the blood.  
PHYSIOLOGICAL TRANSPORT 
 In the body, certain concentration gradients 
must be maintained across the cell membrane.  
Diffusion alone would result in equal 
concentrations on both sides. This would disrupt 
homeostasis. Physiological processes can move 
molecules against a concentration gradient from 
the side of low concentration to the side high in 
concentration. In this way, concentration gradients 
across cell membranes can be maintained.  
However, this requires a constant expenditure of 
cellular energy (ATP). 
 The three physiological transport processes 
we will consider are active transport, endocytosis 
and exocytosis. These transport systems are unique 
to living systems and require cellular energy in the 
form of ATP. Most of the ATP is produced by the 
body is used to maintain the active transport pumps 
of its cells. 
 Active Transport is similar to facilitated 
diffusion in that the transported molecule binds to a 
carrier protein to be moved across the membrane 
and the transporters can saturate.  Unlike facilitated 
diffusion, movement can occur from the side of low 
concentration toward the side with high 
concentration and cellular energy is required. The 
energy is supplied by ATP. When ATP molecules 
are broken down to ADP, energy is released. The 
energy release occurs on the inner side of the cell 
membrane. The ADP returns to the mitochondria to 
be recharged into ATP again.   
 An example of active transport is the 
sodium-potassium exchange pump. Electrical 
impulses in nerves cells are the result of a sudden 
influx of sodium ions followed by an efflux of 
potassium ions. As generation of nerve impulses 
continues, the concentration of sodium ion inside

===== PAGE 10 =====
6 
cells and potassium ions outside cells would 
become too high and nerve activity would stop. 
Here, active transport moves sodium ions out of the 
cell to their already high extracellular concentration 
while moving potassium ions  into the cell. The 
normal gradients of sodium and potassium ions is 
restored.   
 Many protein carriers have receptors for 
two different molecules. The transport of one drives 
the transport of the other. If the receptors for the 
two molecules are on the same side of the protein, 
the two molecules are transported in the same 
direction. This is called co-transport. If the 
receptors are on opposite  sides, the two molecules 
will be transported in opposite directions. This is 
counter-transport. The exchange of sodium ions 
for potassium ions described above is an example 
of counter-transport. Here, using ATP for energy, 
the same protein carrier transpo rts three molecules 
of sodium ion out of the cell and two molecules of 
potassium ion into the cell. Only one molecule of 
ATP is required.  
 An example of co-transport is found in the 
absorption of nutrients from the gut. As we have 
seen, the concentration of sodium ions inside cells 
is very low. So sodium ions can be transported from 
the gut into the cells lining the gut by facilitated 
diffusion. The protein carrier for sodium ion also 
has a binding site for glucose molecules, so glucose 
is pulled into the  cell with sodium ions. As the 
concentration of glucose builds within the cell, a 
concentration gradient develops allowing glucose to 
move out the back of the cell and into the blood 
using facilitated diffusion. The only energy needed 
is that for the sodiu m-potassium exchange pump 
needed to maintain the low internal concentration 
of sodium ions.   
 Endocytosis is a mechanism for bringing 
material into the cell. It includes pinocytosis (that 
literally means “cell drinking”) for molecules and 
macromolecules a s large as proteins and 
phagocytosis (“cell eating”) where whole cells can 
be engulfed by certain other cells. 
 In endocytosis, cell membranes form 
themselves around a n extracellular molecules or 
cells, completely surrounding and then engulfing 
them. The m aterial inside the cell is now 
completely encased by a spherical vesicle made 
from cell membrane. ATP supplies the energy 
required for this process. 
 Endocytosis can be receptor -mediated. In 
one form of pinocytosis, for example, proteins may 
bind to receptor sites on the outer surface of the cell 
membrane. With the help of protein fibers on the 
inner surface of the membrane, pits form and the 
cell membrane surround s the bound proteins by 
moving up and over them, the edges of the 
membrane fusing when they meet.  
 Pinocytosis is a process occurring in all 
cells. Phagocytosis is not. Not every cell is a 
phagocyte. Certain white blood cells are able to 
remove bacteria and dead tissue from wounds using 
phagocytosis. A cellular organelle called a 
lysosome filled w ith hydrolytic enzymes moves 
toward the encapsulated mass; the membranes of 
the lysosome and vesicle fuse so that the enzymes 
now can digest the foreign material. 
 Besides certain white blood cells, 
phagocytes also include scavenger cells called 
macrophages. Macrophages wander through body 
tissues engulfing foreign cells and other unwanted 
materials as described above. 
 Exocytosis is a mechanism for moving 
molecules out of the cell. For example, some newly 
synthesized proteins are packaged into vesicles  of 
membrane by an organelle called the Golgi 
apparatus. At the periphery of the cell, the 
membrane of the vesicle can fuse with the cell 
membrane and the protein can leave the cell 
without disrupting the cell membrane’s barrier 
function. Similarly, the debris  left in the 
phagocytotic vesicle following digestion by 
lysosomes, is ejected from the cell by exocytosis.
