LESSON 3:  MEMBRANE ELECTRICAL POTENTIALS  
THE RESTING MEMBRANE POTENTIAL 
 The gradient for diffusion need not be a 
concentration gradient. Diffusion can occur down 
an electrical gradient as well. For cells, the inside 
is negatively charged relative to the outside, due in 
part of high concentrations of negatively charged 
proteins inside cells. At equilibrium, neither the 
concentration nor the charge is equal on both sides. 
If we assume that the outside of the membrane is 
positively charged and the inside negatively 
charged, that the concentration of potassium ions is 
greater on the inside than on the outside and that 
potassium ions are freely permea ble to the 
membrane, then the concentration difference would 
favor movement of potassium ions from inside to 
outside, but the positively charged potassium ion 
would be repelled by the positive charges outside 
the membrane favoring movement to the inside.  At 
equilibrium, the concentration of potassium ions 
would remain higher on the inside, and the inside 
would remain negatively charged. If one knew the 
concentration of potassium inside and outside at 
equilibrium, one could calculate the electromotive 
force (EMF) in millivolts across the membrane at 
37oC (body temperature) using the Nernst 
equation for a positively charged ion: 
The EMF across a cell membrane is called a 
membrane potential. For a negative ion, the Nernst 
equation at 37oC becomes  
Below is a  table of concentrations and 
calculated Nernst p otentials for several ions 
associated with the cell membrane.  
Ion Inner Conc. Outer Conc. EMF 
 (mEq/L) (mEq/L) millivolts 
Potassium (K+) 140 4 -94 
Sodium (Na+) 14 142 +64 
Chloride (Cl-) 3.5 103 -90 
Using glass micropipettes filled with strong 
electrolyte solution and having tips less than a 
micrometer in diameter, it is possible to measure 
membrane potentials in single nerve fibers. The 
membrane potential for certain resting nerve fibers 
is -90 millivolt s.  That is, if the outside of the 
membrane is assumed to be 0 millivolts, the inside 
of the cell membrane is -90 millivolts.  Knowing 
the EMF, how does one interpret the EMF values in 
the table?  Two other techniques can be used to 
track the movement of i ons across a nerve cell 
membrane and help answer the question. 
 The Nernst equation assumes the ions are 
freely permeable to the membrane; that they are 
free to equilibrate. In fact, sodium ions are 100 
times less permeable than potassium ions and 
chloride ions half as permeable as potassium ions.  
 It makes sense, then, that the membrane potential is 
closer to that of potassium ion and chloride ion than 
to sodium ion. A model that takes into account the 
concentration gradient and permeability is 
described by Goldman-Hodgkin-Katz  equation: 
      The EMF calculated using the above 
equation is -86 millivolts.  This is less negative than 
the measured value of -90 millivolts.  This is due to 
the sodium-potassium electrogenic active transport 
pump. Using ATP fo r energy, this pump actively 
transports three positive sodium ions out of the cell 
for every two positive potassium ions it pumps in.  
The net loss of positive charges from the inside 
makes the membrane potential fall below the 
predicted -86 millivolts obt ained from the GHK 
equation. The measured value is -90 millivolts. That 
the chloride ion Nernst potential is exactly equal to 
the measured membrane potential is consistent with 
the fact that the very permeable chloride ion is 
distributed completely passive ly across the 
membrane.  
A stimulating electrode can be used along 
with the measuring electrode to "clamp" the EMF at 
a predetermined value.  This is the voltage clamp 
technique.  According to the Nernst equation, if the 
EMF is changed using the stimulating electrode, the 
ions will have to redistribute across the membrane 
to change the ratio of the concentration inside to the 
concentration outside appropriately. This becomes 
a tool that can be used to study ion fluxes. 
 It’s easy to change the electrolyte 
concentrations surrounding an isolated cell, but

===== PAGE 12 =====
8 
how could one make changes inside the cell? This 
can be done using the patch clamp technique. Here, 
a small "patch" of membrane can be sucked against 
the pipette tip and the voltage or ion concentration 
inside the pipette tip can be "clamped" at a 
predetermined value to study ion or voltage fluxes 
across a single protein channel.  When the cell 
membrane surrounding the patch is disrupted, the 
inner surface of the membrane patch is now 
exposed to the bathing solution.    
THE ACTION POTENTIAL 
 While the membrane potential is common 
to all living cells, certain types of cells can change 
the membrane potential as a means of 
communication. The ability to change membrane 
potentials and transmit those changes through  the 
cell is crucial to two cell types: nerve cells and 
muscle cells. The ions responsible for this change 
in potential are sodium and potassium ions.   
 The resting potential is due to the 
distribution of sodium and potassium ions across 
the membrane through leak channels according to 
the GHK equation and through the carrier proteins 
of the sodium-potassium pump, as described above. 
Another type of channel, the voltage gated  
channel, is responsible for the change in membrane 
potential associated with nerve and muscle.   
 Under the appropriate stimulus 
(mechanical, chemical or electrical stimulation of a 
nerve, for example) the membrane potential begins 
to become less negative. At a certain point (usually 
between -70 and -50 millivolts), the threshold of 
the sodium voltage gated channel is reached. The 
activation gate opens and suddenly the membrane 
permeability for sodium ions is 5 to 50 times 
greater than it is for potassium ions. As expected, 
according to the GHK equation, the increase in PNa 
causes the calculated EMF to approach the Nernst 
potential of sodium ions (+61 millivolts). In fact, 
the influx of positively charged sodium ions causes 
the measured membrane potential to reach about 
+35 millivolts before the inactivation gate of the 
sodium voltage gated channel closes and stops the 
influx of sodium ions. That is, the inside of the 
membrane is now 35 millivolts positive relative to 
the outside. This rapid reversal in membrane from 
negative to positive is called membrane 
depolarization. If the membrane p otential 
increases enough to open the sodium gates, it is said 
to have reached threshold. The action potential is 
then triggered in all-or-none fashion. Below 
threshold, there is no action potential.   
 The same increase in voltage that caused 
the opening of sodium voltage gated channels also 
causes the opening of potassium voltage gated 
channels.  However, these channels are slow, that 
is, slow to open.  In fact, their opening is delayed 
until after the sodium channels have already closed. 
 When the potass ium channels open, positively 
charged potassium ions flood out of the cell and the 
inside becomes negative once more. This is called 
membrane repolarization.   
 After repolarization, the membrane 
potential has returned to its resting state; the inside 
is negatively charged again.  
 As illustrated below, the cell membrane 
acts as an electrical capacitor.  Only the inside and 
outside edges are charged.  The rest of the 
cytoplasm and interstitial fluids are neutral.  
Therefore, only minute quantities of sodiu m and 
potassium ion that 
are adjacent to the 
cell membrane 
actually exchange 
during an action 
potential. The 
quantities are, in 
fact, so small that 
millions of action 
potentials can occur before it becomes necessary to 
reestablish the gradients.  This is a ccomplished by 
the sodium -potassium exchange pump mentioned 
above.  It is an active transport pump that requires 
ATP to drive it.

===== PAGE 13 =====
9 
 The depolarization process elevates the 
membrane potential of the cell membrane 
surrounding the point of stimulation enough  to 
elicit depolarization of the surrounding membrane.  
That, in turn, causes membrane adjacent to it to 
depolarize, and so on. The action potential 
propagates in all directions from the original point 
of stimulation until all the membrane has been 
depolarized. The wave of depolarization is followed 
in a fraction of a millisecond by a wave of 
repolarization. The propagated action potential is 
the nerve impulse.   
FACTORS AFFECTING ACTION POTENTIALS 
 Many nerve cells have processes that are 
many feet in leng th. For example, the nerve cells 
that innervate muscles in you toes, actually extend 
all the way down your leg from the spinal cord.    
Some of these long nerve cell processes are coated 
with a fatty substance called sphingomyelin 
(myelin). The coating is called the myelin sheath. 
 The substance acts like an electrical insulator.  
Along the length of the nerve process, the coating 
is interrupted with gaps called the nodes of 
Ranvier. When the action potential propagates 
down the long nerve process, only tho se areas at 
the nodes need to depolarize.  This greatly speeds 
the velocity of the action potential s. They literally 
"jump" down the membrane, a process called 
saltatory conduction.   Another factor that affects 
the velocity of the action potential is the diameter 
of the nerve process. Large diameter nerve 
processes conduct faster than small ones.  A small 
unmyelinated axon may conduct at less than a 
meter per second. A large myelinated fiber 
velocity may exceed 100 meters per second.  
 The action potentials of heart muscle cells 
are affected by a third voltage gated channel; the 
calcium channel . The calcium channel carries 
positively charged calcium ion (and a little extra 
sodium ion) into the cell just after depolarization.  
This acts to hold the inside po sitive for a longer 
time. The action potential has a positive plateau.  
Prolonging the depolarization enables the heart 
muscle cells to remain contracted for a longer 
period of time. The timeframe for single 
contractions of skeletal muscle cells is in 
milliseconds, whereas cardiac muscle cell 
contractions approach half a second in duration.  
Some action potentials repetitively 
discharge rhythmically.  Again, the heart is a good 
example.  In non -rhythmic nerves, the resting 
membrane potential remains at a co nstant value 
below threshold until a stimulus occurs.  In the 
heart, certain clusters of cells called pacemaker 
cells have cell membranes that slowly become more 
and more permeable to sodium ions, gradually 
bringing the membrane potential to threshold. That 
is, an increase in sodium permeability brings the 
membrane potential closer to the Nernst potential of 
sodium ion. Once threshold is reached, the 
membrane fires an all -or-none action potential.  
After repolarization, the increasing sodium ion 
permeability again brings the membrane potential 
toward threshold, and another impulse occurs.  This 
is the rhythmic nerve activity that triggers the 
repetitive rhythmic beating of the heart.  The 
electrical activity originates within the heart muscle 
itself.  Even i f all the nerves to the heart were cut 
(as during a heart transplant), the heart continues to 
beat rhythmically. 
 Electrolyte imbalances can interfere with 
the normal generation of action potentials. Calcium 
ions decrease the permeability of membranes for  
sodium ions. Therefore, higher than normal calcium 
ion concentration in the blood inhibits action 
potentials, depressing nerve reflexes while low 
calcium ion concentration excites the nervous 
system causing uncontrolled contractions.  Low 
extracellular pot assium ion concentration 
suppresses generation of action potentials 
potentially leading to paralysis. High extracellular 
potassium excites nerves by bringing the resting 
membrane potential toward threshold. But if 
threshold is exceeded, the nerve becomes 
permanently depolarized and nerve activity stops  
completely. As the heart stopped beating, death 
would follow rapidly.

===== PAGE 14 =====
10 
LESSON 4:  SKELETAL MUSCLE 
  
Nearly half the body weight is muscle.  
Muscle has the ability to contract and relax 
producing movement.  It moves the bones of the 
skeleton and moves materials through internal 
organs.  The three types of muscle are skeletal, 
smooth (also called visceral) and cardiac. 
SKELETAL MUSCLE 
 Skeletal muscle is made up of long, 
unbranched muscle fibers that generally run the full 
length of the muscle. Skeletal muscle fibers are 
striated. That is, alternating light and dark bands 
are visible in the cell when viewed under a 
microscope. Each cell has multiple nuclei located 
close to the outer cell membrane. In common w ith 
other types of muscle, skeletal muscle has a rich 
blood supply and the cells have abundant 
mitochondria for ATP production. (The terms 
muscle "cell" and muscle "fiber" are used 
synonymously.) 
 The muscle cell has several unique 
features. The cell membr ane of a muscle cell is 
called the sarcolemma. At regular intervals along 
the cell, this membrane penetrates into the interior 
of the cell forming a tubular channel passing all the 
way through. These penetrations are called 
transverse tubules. The cytoplas m of the muscle 
cell is called sarcoplasm. The endoplasmic 
reticulum, specialized in muscle cells as a reservoir 
for calcium ions, is called the sarcoplasmic 
reticulum. On either end of the sarcoplasmic 
reticulum segments there are enlarged cavities 
called terminal cisternae.   
 The predominant organelle found in all 
muscle cells is the myofibril. Individual muscle 
cells have hundreds, even thousands of them. It is 
the myofibril that is responsible for the striations of 
skeletal muscle. In muscle cells, l ong parallel 
strands of myofibrils are surrounded by 
sarcoplasmic reticulum. Each myofibril is 
composed of a chain of individual units called 
sarcomeres. The sarcomere is the basic unit of 
muscle contraction. It is made up of alternating 
interlocking strands  of protein fibers actin and 
myosin. The figure shows diagrammatically the 
arrangement of the various components of the 
muscle cell. 
 Contraction is the result of the myosin 
molecules sliding over the actin molecules. In 
muscle contraction, the lengths of the actin and 
myosin fibers do not decrease. Rather, actin and 
myosin fibers bind to each other and then slide over 
each other, shortening the sarcomere. Individual 
contractions of skeletal muscle are very rapid. A 
contraction lasts less than a tenth of a second. 
SLIDING FILAMENT MECHANISM 
 In the muscle, an electrical impulse (action 
potential) alters membrane permeability and causes 
calcium (Ca++) ions to be released from the 
sarcoplasmic reticulum and terminal cisternae. The 
calcium ions bind to a protein on the actin molecule 
called troponin. Attached to the troponin is short 
protein fiber called tropomyosin. Without calcium 
ions bound to troponin, the tropomyosin is 
positioned such that it blocks the binding of actin 
and myosin and contraction cannot oc cur. When 
calcium ions bind to troponin, troponin changes its 
molecular conformation and the attached 
tropomyosin is pulled out from between actin and 
myosin. The actin and myosin molecules then are 
able to bind forming cross-bridges.   
 On the head of the  myosin molecule, the 
point where it binds to actin, is a molecule of 
ATPase, the enzyme that releases the energy stored 
in ATP. ATP binds to myosin as the previous cross-
bridge breaks and is then broken down into ADP 
and P i (inorganic phosphate) even befo re myosin 
and actin bind.  The energy released is stored in the 
myosin head like a cocked spring.  When the next 
cross-bridge forms, the energy is immediately 
released causing the myosin head to flex and pull 
itself over the surface of the actin fibers, shortening 
the muscle. Another ATP binds, breaking the cross-
bridge, and the process repeats.  Reabsorption of

===== PAGE 15 =====
11 
calcium ions into the sarcoplasmic reticulum and 
binding of another ATP to myosin breaks the cross-
bridges and the muscle relaxes. 
 The figure abo ve illustrates the relative 
positions of the four proteins of the myofibril both 
before (left) and after (right) the introduction of 
calcium ions. 
 For the muscle to relax, calcium ions must 
be returned to the sarcoplasmic reticulum.   This is 
accomplished by an active transport calcium pump 
that also requires energy.  So ATP is required both 
for contraction and relaxation.  A contracted muscle 
deprived of ATP cannot relax. After death, for 
example, contracted muscles can remain contracted 
for several days until the structural integrity of the 
cell deteriorates.  This is called rigor mortis. 
ENERGY FOR CONTRACTION 
 Energy is required for the sliding filament 
mechanism.  That energy comes from ATP. The 
ATP is supplied from three main sources: 
1. Creatine phosphate, found inside muscle cells , 
reacts with ADP to yield ATP plus free creatine. 
Creatine phosphate is rapidly used up, providing 
energy for about ten seconds of strenuous exercise.  
2. Glycogen, a carbohydrate stored in muscle cells, 
can be metabolized to pyruvic acid or lactic acid in 
the sarcoplasm.  The reaction doesn't require 
oxygen, but produces less than 5 % as much ATP 
as is produced in the mitochondria. The glycogen 
stores can provide sufficient ATP to last for about a 
minute or so.  
 These fir st two sources of ATP require 
only the resources found within the cell. The third 
mechanism requires that both glucose and oxygen 
be continuously delivered to the cell through the 
bloodstream.  
3. Pyruvic acid is catabolized (broken down) in the 
presence o f oxygen (aerobic respiration) in the 
mitochondria. Pyruvic acid is provided by 
anaerobic metabolism of glycogen or glucose 
coming from outside the cell. Glucose can be 
provided from carbohydrates or synthesized from 
proteins or fats. Fats are a particular ly rich source 
of energy. ATP provided by the mitochondria can 
sustain muscle activity indefinitely, provided 
sufficient oxygen and glucose are available.   
 During severe exercise, increased blood 
flow may not be able to supply enough oxygen to 
muscle cells.  The aerobic production of ATP in 
mitochondria cannot continue.  The concentration 
of lactic acid in the sarcoplasm increases rapidly.  
The lactic acid accumulation inside muscle cells 
causes the pain associated with muscle fatigue.  
Lactic acid can be  converted back to a fuel for 
aerobic ATP production if enough oxygen is 
supplied.  This usually occurs after the exercise, but 
while we're still breathing heavily. Some of the 
ATP produced is used to recharge the creatine 
phosphate stores that are also de pleted by fatigue.  
The amount of oxygen required to recharge the 
muscle is called the oxygen debt .  Increased 
ventilation even after exercise has ended provides 
the oxygen necessary to pay back the oxygen debt. 
MUSCLE TONE 
 Tone refers to the internal tension within a 
resting muscle caused by the overlap of actin and 
myosin molecules. There is a relationship between 
the maximum tension that can develop in a 
contracting muscle and the length of that muscle 
before contraction. For example, if a resting muscle 
were stretched to a resting length such that the actin 
and myosin filaments did not overlap, contraction 
could not occur. Without overlap, crossbridges 
cannot form. It has been shown that when resting 
sarcomere length is about 2 micrometers, the 
strength of contraction will be maximal. Muscles 
cells develop to a length that results in optimal 
sarcomere length.

===== PAGE 16 =====
12 
 When a muscle contracts against a load that 
is too great for it to move, it cannot shorten. It 
nevertheless develops increased internal tension in 
an effort to shorten. This is known as an isometric 
("same length") contraction. The length of the 
muscle remains the same while internal tension 
increases. When a muscle actually moves a load, it 
shortens in length, allowing the internal tension to 
remain the same. This is an isotonic ("same 
tension") contraction.   
SINGLE MUSCLE TWITCH RESPONSE 
 If a muscle is to contract, it must be 
stimulated.  The stimulation is the electrical 
event (depolarization). It takes only a few 
milliseconds for the stimulat ion to travel to 
and through the muscle, for the calcium ions 
to be released, and for the actin and myosin 
to bind before the mechanical event 
(contraction) occurs. This brief time is called 
the latent period. The muscle cell polarity 
returns to resting co ndition (repolarization) 
and shortly thereafter, the muscle tension 
returns to the resting state (relaxation). The 
electrical events occur before the mechanical 
events. 
 For a short time after application of a 
stimulus, the muscle is in an absolute refractory 
period.  During this period, the contraction of the 
muscle cannot be altered by additional stimuli no 
matter how strong.  After the absolute refractory 
period, a sufficiently strong stimulation can cause 
the muscle to contract again even before it is 
completely relaxed.   
SUMMATION, TETANY AND FATIGUE 
 Repeated stimulation following the 
refractory period but before the muscle is fully 
relaxed produces contractions that are stronger than 
those a simple twitch would produce. The 
contraction resulting fro m the second stimulation 
adds to the contraction from the first stimulation.  
This is called summation. The tension builds 
because the sarcomeres have not moved completely 
apart to the resting position before the second 
contraction began. 
 If stimulations are given with extreme 
rapidity (very high frequency) the muscle cannot 
relax at all between successive stimuli. The 
individual contractions become indistinguishable 
and fuse into a single sustained contraction, a 
phenomenon known as tetany. The idea is to  send 
stimuli so frequently that just before the muscle 
begins to relax, it gets stimulated again. With time, 
the maximum tension generated during tetany may 
decline as ATP stores are exhausted. This is 
fatigue. 
 The smooth, sustained movements of our 
skeletal muscles are the result of tetanic 
contractions. Twitch contractions are rare. An 
example of a twitch response is the blink of an 
eyelid. 
 Sometimes the initial contractions of a cold 
muscle are of a diminished intensity. After several 
contractions, the muscle is at peak intensity. The 
muscle had to "warm up". This phenomenon is 
called treppe.  Limbering up before exercise helps 
to bring all muscles to maximum efficiency.

===== PAGE 17 =====
13 
THE MOTOR UNIT 
 A single nerve fiber and all the muscle 
fibers (cells) it innervates are called a motor unit.  
For very delicate movements, a nerve fiber may 
send branches to only a few muscle cells.  For 
example, to produce the delicate movements of the 
eyes, the motor units need only a few fibers each.  
For coarse movements produced by the leg muscles, 
for example, fifty or a hundred muscle fibers may 
be innervated by a single nerve cell.  When that 
nerve depolarizes, it causes all muscle cells of the 
motor unit to contract. 
GRADING MUSCLE CONTRACTIONS 
 A stimulus of a certain strength will 
contract a whole muscle to a certain tension. As the 
stimulus strength is increased, the tension generated 
in the muscle also increases until the maximum 
contraction is achieved. Each motor unit in the 
muscle has its own threshold for firing. Some motor 
units need only a weak stimulus, some need a 
stronger one. Increasing the strength of the stimulus 
causes more motor units to fire and results in a 
stronger contraction. Weak stimuli will not elicit 
any response if they are below the threshold of all 
the motor units. As the stimulus strength increases, 
the thresholds of more and more motor units are 
reached. More motor units fire causing greater and 
greater contraction strength. At some stimulus 
intensity, all motor units of the muscle are activated 
and the muscle contracts with its maximum 
strength. Increasing the intensity of stimulation  
above that required to fire all the motor units cannot 
increase the strength of contraction because all 
muscle cells are now contracting; there are no more 
inactivated motor units available. To lift a light 
load, the nervous system stimulates only a few 
motor units in a given muscle. To lift a heavy load, 
the nervous system activates more and more motor 
units until the load is lifted. If all motor units are 
active and the load still can't be moved, the muscle 
just isn't strong enough. 
RIGOR MORTIS 
After death, the muscles can remain in a 
contracted state for up to a day or more depending 
on environmental conditions (temperature). This is 
because it not only takes ATP to  allow muscles to 
contract; it also takes ATP to allow them to relax. 
 Without ATP to break cross -bridges between 
actin and myosin and to supply energy for active 
transport to pump calcium ions out of the 
sarcoplasm and back into the sarcoplasmic 
reticulum, the cross -bridges remain intact. The 
binding of actin and myosin, in the presence of 
calcium ion, form s cross-bridges that “lock” the 
filaments together . This is just a spontaneous 
chemical reaction. Without ATP, calcium ion 
cannot be removed to allow th e filaments to 
unlock, and the myosin head cannot flex to pull 
itself along the actin (contraction). Only after the 
muscle proteins begin to decompose under the 
action of enzymes released from the rupturing 
lysosomes, will the muscle tissue relax.  This st ate 
of tonic contracture after death is called rigor 
mortis.

===== PAGE 18 =====
14 
LESSON 5:  NEUROMUSCULAR TRANSMISSION & SMOOTH MUSCLE 
 
SYNAPSES 
 Nerve cells have long processes extending 
off the main portion of the cell. The main part of 
the cell, containing the nucleus, is called the nerve 
cell body or soma.  Processes that conduct impulses 
toward the cell body are called dendrites.  
Processes that transmit impulses away from  the 
cell body are called axons. The processes transmit 
action potentials from one end of the cel l to the 
other.  If the combined stimuli coming in from other 
nerve cells along the dendrites and cell bodies of a 
given neuron are strong enough, the cell membranes 
of that neuron will depolarize.  The resulting action 
potential is transmitted down the ax on toward 
another nerve fiber or a muscle fiber.   
An impulse reaching the tip of the axon 
cannot be electrically transmitted to another nerve 
cell or to a muscle cell. The cells communicate with 
each other chemically. A transmitter substance is 
synthesized in vesicles found in the terminal part of 
the axon. At the junction of a nerve cell with the 
muscle cell, or with a dendrite or the body of 
another nerve cell, there is a gap called a synapse.  
As mentioned, action potentials in the presynaptic 
fiber ca nnot cross to the postsynaptic fiber 
electrically. Rather, action potentials in the 
presynaptic fiber stimulate the presynaptic fiber to 
release the transmitter substance. This substance is 
carried across the synapse and binds to receptors on 
the postsynap tic cell. If sufficient transmitter is 
released and comes in contact with the receptors, 
the chemically stimulated postsynaptic cell will 
depolarize and an action potential is generated. The 
action potential then propagates across the entire 
postsynaptic cell. 
 At the neuromuscular junctions of skeletal 
muscle, the action potential in the presynaptic nerve 
stimulates hundreds of vesicles on the nerve 
endings to release the transmitter substance 
acetylcholine that diffuses across the synapse. On 
the muscle side, acetylcholine binds to receptors on 
the surface of the sarcolemma. These receptors are 
associated with sodium channels. The channels are 
referred to as ligand gated channels. As ligands 
(acetylcholine molecules in this case) bind, more 
and more sodium ion is gated into the muscle cell, 
raising the membrane potential toward threshold.  
When threshold is reached, the membrane 
depolarizes. The enzyme acetylcholinesterase that 
is produced by the muscle cell membrane then 
breaks down the acetylcholine so that if the muscle 
is to stay contracted, the nerve cell must continue to 
fire action potentials at high frequency to continue 
supplying new chemical transmitter. When the 
nerve stops firing and the last of the acetylcholine is 
broken down, the muscle cell will relax.   
The disease myasthenia gravis  is 
characterized by muscle weakness due to damaged 
acetylcholine receptors on the muscle cells. 
 
Membrane Sodium Ion Channels 
Channel type Characteristic 
Leak  Channels for diffusion of ions with 
distribution de termined by the 
GHK equation.  
Ligand-gated Channels open when activated by 
a chemical neurotransmitter such 
as acetylcholine. 
Voltage-gated Channel opens as voltage is 
raised by the electrical activity of 
an approaching nerve impulse. 
Accounts for the a bility of action 
potentials to be conducted. 
Na+/K+ Counter-transport that removes 
Na+ from the interior of the cell 
following depolarizations. Three 
Na+ leave for every two K + that 
enter the cell.

===== PAGE 19 =====
15 
The action potential stimulated in the 
muscle cell qu ickly passes across the entire 
sarcolemma, then down into the transverse tubules 
deep into the cell. It is passed directly to the 
membranes of the terminal cisternae that are 
adjacent to the transverse tubules, and then to the 
rest of the sarcoplasmic reticulum. Calcium ions are 
then released from these membranous structures 
and diffuse into the sarcoplasm that bathes the 
myofibrils. This process is called excitation-
contraction coupling . The calcium binds to 
troponin, pulling the tropomyosin away so actin and 
myosin cross -bridges can form. The actin and 
myosin slide over each other and the sarcomeres 
shorten.  ATP supplies the energy for contraction. 
 When the stream of nerve impulses stop, 
the muscle will relax. Calcium ions are pumped (by 
active transport  requiring ATP) back into the 
sarcoplasmic reticulum and terminal cisternae.  
Without calcium  ion, the troponin moves 
tropomyosin back into its blocking position, 
breaking the cross -bridges and the sarcomeres 
relax. 
 Synapses are not unique to neuromuscula r 
junctions. They are found between adjacent nerve 
fibers as well. Action potentials cannot cross nerve-
nerve junctions either. Presynaptic nerves release 
neurotransmitter substances to stimulate the 
postsynaptic nerves. Some use acetylcholine. Some 
use on e of the other two dozen or so known 
neurotransmitters.  
NERVE SUPPLY TO MUSCLE TISSUE 
 The central nervous system monitors the 
state of contraction of each muscle through sensory 
nerve impulses originating from stretch receptors in 
the muscles themselves. The ability to perceive the 
state of contraction of a muscle is called 
proprioception. The impulses necessary for a 
muscle to contract are sent from the central nervous 
system by action potentials through motor nerves. 
When the brain decides how much a mu scle needs 
to be contracted, it checks the proprioceptors to 
determine how much it's already contracted, then 
sends motor impulses to change the degree of 
contraction appropriately.  The communication with 
proprioceptors is so rapid that the nervous system is 
able to make adjustments to contractions as they are 
happening. Proprioceptors also help us to perceive 
our orientation in the surrounding environment.  
SMOOTH MUSCLE 
 There are two types of smooth muscle : 
multiunit and single-unit. We will consider only the 
single-unit muscle, also called visceral muscle 
because it composes the large muscles associated 
with the visceral organs (except the heart).  Most 
smooth muscle is of this type.  It is called “single 
unit” or “unitary” because stimulation causes 
groups of muscle cells to contract as a single unit.  
Multiunit muscles are very small and found 
only is a few places in the body. Like skeletal 
muscle, each cell acts independently and requires 
its own stimulus. The small muscles in the interior 
of the eye and muscles associated with hair follicles 
of the skin (goose bumps) are multiunit muscles. 
Single unit smooth muscle is composed of 
tapered cells, much shorter and smaller than 
skeletal muscle cells.  
Each cell has a single 
central nucleus.  The 
myofibrils are arranged 
randomly along the 
length of the fibers, so 
the cells do not appear 
striated.  In visceral 
muscle, the sarcoplasmic 
reticulum is less well 
developed than in 
skeletal muscle.  Some 
cells almost totally lack 
sarcoplasmic reticulum.  
The calci um ion 
transport function is therefore taken over by the 
sarcolemma. That is, to relax the muscle, calcium 
ions are pumped completely out of the muscle cells.  
SMOOTH VS. SKELETAL MUSCLE  
 Smooth muscle cells are elongated - fat in 
the middle and tapering t o points on either end. 
Cells are coupled to each other by gap junctions. 
These are membrane openings between cells that 
allow ion fluxes (electrical activity) to be conducted 
from cell to cell. Actions potentials are conducted 
from cell to cell electrical ly across electrical, and 
not chemical, synapses. Because they are coupled 
electrically, hundreds or even millions of cells can 
contract together (as a single unit = “unitary”).  
Smooth muscle cells are connected to each 
other by mechanical coupling. Cells are physically 
connected to each other such that they pull together.

===== PAGE 20 =====
16 
While contractile elements are composed of 
interlocking actin and myosin strands, the filaments 
are not arranged in parallel strands, as in skeletal 
muscle. Rather, the filaments are arranged in a grid 
pattern, like a three -dimensional hair net. Actin 
filaments are supported on one end by dense bodies 
dispersed throughout the cell or attached to the cell 
membrane. The free end slides across myosin 
filaments, pulling the dense bodies toget her as the 
cell shortens.  
Calcium ion is required for contraction of 
both skeletal and visceral muscle, but the initiation 
of cross-bridge formation is different.  In visceral 
muscle, a calcium ion binds to a molecule called 
calmodulin that then activates the enzyme myosin 
kinase to initiate cross-bridge formation.  Once the 
cross-bridge between actin and myosin is 
established, it remains fixed until enzymatically 
broken. This is referred to as the “ latch” 
phenomenon. To relax the muscle, another enzyme, 
myosin phosphatase, is required to break the bond. 
Thus, the actin and myosin cross -bridges are 
sustained for a much longer period of time in 
visceral muscle compared to skeletal muscle. In 
skeletal muscle, crossbridges are broken and 
reformed during the en tire time the muscle cell is 
contracted. The muscle contractions of smooth 
muscle last for two or three seconds. That’s about 
20 times longer than in skeletal muscle. To work 
over a given length of time, therefore, visceral 
muscle requires significantly less ATP than skeletal 
muscle, since ATP is required only to initiate cross-
bridges, not to sustain them.   
 Visceral muscle can maintain constant 
force over different lengths by stress-relaxation. As 
a hollow organ (such as the stomach or urinary 
bladder) fills, the pressure inside would be expected 
to increase. The muscles in the wall, now under 
stress, relax just enough to allow the organ to 
enlarge and maintain constant pressure. 
 Unlike skeletal and cardiac muscle, visceral 
muscle cells can divide to increase muscle mass. An 
example is uterine smooth muscle mass that 
increases greatly during pregnancy as the muscle 
cells divide. 
CONTROL OF VISCERAL MUSCLE 
 While skeletal muscle is under voluntary 
control, v isceral muscle is under involuntary 
control. Contractions are initiated below our level 
of consciousness. The branch of the nervous system 
that controls involuntary muscle is the autonomic 
branch. Voluntary skeletal muscle is controlled by 
the somatic nervous system. (The details of the 
somatic and auton omic nervous systems will be 
discussed later.) Autonomic control usually 
involves dual innervation. One nerve synapses with 
the muscle and releases a transmitter that causes 
contraction; another nerve synapses and releases a 
different transmitter that inhibits contraction . In 
skeletal muscle, every muscle synapses with an 
axon that stimulates it to contract. In visceral 
muscle, not every muscle cell requires a nerve 
stimulation to contract. Nerve fibers spread over a 
sheet muscle cells, not actually forming  a synapse 
with every cell. With gap junctions, they don’t need 
to. The stimulation of one cell can cause those 
around it to contract as well. In fact, visceral 
muscles can contract without any nerve stimulation. 
 Many visceral muscles respond to humoral 
(chemical) stimulation. For example, in the lungs, 
smooth muscle in the walls of blood vessels 
contract in response to lower than normal tissue 
oxygen levels. This acts to reduce flow to those 
parts of the lung obviously not receiving as much 
oxygen from th e air for some reason. In skeletal 
muscles, low oxygen levels cause smooth muscle in 
the walls of blood vessels to relax- just the opposite 
of the response in the lung. In this case, low oxygen 
signals the need for more oxygen delivery, so 
vessels dilate providing added blood flow.  
Hormones can also control visceral 
muscles.  An example of this occurs in the uterus 
during pregnancy. The hormone progesterone 
inhibits contraction of uterine smooth muscle 
during pregnancy, preventing spontaneous abortion 
of the developing fetus. But at the time of labor and 
delivery, progesterone levels fall and another 
hormone called oxytocin is released from the 
pituitary gland. Oxytocin stimulates contraction of 
uterine smooth muscle as part of the birth process. 
 Just stretching a visceral muscle can cause 
it to contract.  When the wall of the small intestine 
is stretched near its maximum by the food entering, 
the muscles will reflex ively contract, forcing the 
contents to the next segment.  In this way, food is 
passed from one segment to the next.

===== PAGE 21 =====
17 
LESSON 6:  THE HEART
 
The circulatory system consists of a pump 
and system of vessels that carry blood to and from 
the cells of the body.  The heart is the pump, 
forcing blood into arteries. Arteries are the 
distribution vessels.  From arteries, blood flows into 
the capillaries, the exchange vessels.  Veins 
receive blood from the capillaries.  They are 
collection vessels that carry blood back to the heart. 
 The heart is a double pump, pumping blood 
simultaneously to the lungs with the right chambers 
and the rest of the body with the left chambers.  
Blood, low in oxygen, is pumped from the right 
heart into the pulmonary artery that branches to the 
lungs.  There the blood is oxygenated.  Blood rich 
in oxygen returns to the left heart  through 
pulmonary veins and is pumped into the aorta.  
After circulating through the systemic capillaries, it 
returns to the right heart through the vena cavae to 
be pumped to the lungs again.  Cardiac muscle is 
responsible for the pumping action of the heart. 
CARDIAC MUSCLE 
 Cardiac muscle is striated like skeletal 
muscle but under control of the involuntary nervous 
system, as is visceral muscle.  Cardiac muscle cells 
are branching cells arranged end to end or end to 
side.  This arrangement of muscle cells results in a 
squeezing action forcing blood from the heart.  
Cardiac muscle has intercalated discs at the cell 
boundaries that serve to decrease electrical 
resistance between cells.  Because of gap junctions 
in the discs, action potentials are transferre d from 
cell to cell electrically with no chemical transmitter 
substance release across synapses required.  As a 
result, many individual muscle cells contract 
together as one integrated unit.  Cardiac muscle 
myofibrils are arranged to give the striated 
appearance.   
 Cardiac muscle contracts more rapidly than 
visceral muscle but more slowly than skeletal 
muscle, contractions lasting a little less than 0.5 
seconds.   Cardiac muscle is under involuntary 
neural control.  But nerve impulses do not initiate 
contractions.  Contractions originate from within 
the muscles themselves; the contractions are 
myogenic (born in the muscle).  Specialized cells 
within the heart (called pacemaker cells) discharge 
spontaneously and rhythmically.  The autonomic 
nerves to the hea rt only control the heart rate and 
strength of the contractions.  Even if all nerves to 
the heart were cut (as occurs in heart transplants), 
the heart still continues to beat.  
 Blood flow through the four chambers of 
the heart is illustrated in the figure.  [Structures are 
numbered sequentially with the flow direction.]  
 The right chambers carry deoxygenated 
blood.  A series of heart valves keep the blood flow 
unidirectional.  They open and close passively in 
response to the pressure differentials caused by 
contraction and relaxation of the myocardium.  The 
tricuspid valve, also called the right AV 
(atrioventricular) valve, has three flaps.  The 
bicuspid, also called the left AV or mitral valve, 
has two flaps.  The AV valves are supported by 
chordae tendineae.  These strong, fibrous strands 
are in turn attached to papillary muscles of the 
ventricular walls.  Blood flowing out of the atria 
causes the AV valves to open into the ventricles.  
Blood trying to reflux into the atria pushes the 
valves closed.  The chordae tendineae prevent the 
valves from opening backwards into the atria.  The 
pulmonary and aortic semilunar valves do not 
require this extra support. Their cup-like attachment 
to the vessel walls prevents them from opening in 
the wrong direction.  Valv es of this type are also 
found at regular intervals throughout the venous 
system helping to maintain unidirectional venous 
return toward the heart.   
 The valve action of the heart is totally 
passive. Valves open and close properly even 
during external or internal cardiac massage.

===== PAGE 22 =====
18 
Valvular insufficiency results when any valve fails 
to close completely.  Some backflow can then 
occur.  In many instances, it is necessary to replace 
the defective valve with a prosthesis. Mitral 
stenosis, for example is the det erioration and 
scarring of the mitral valve. The valve can't close 
tightly. As a result, the ventricular contraction 
forces blood back into the left atrium, and increases 
blood pressure in the pulmonary veins and 
capillaries.  The high pressure results in pulmonary 
edema (a fluid accumulation) in the lungs.  The 
right ventricle that must now pump blood to the 
lungs against this elevated pressure begins to 
enlarge.  Unchecked, mitral stenosis can lead to 
right heart failure and death.  
PACING CARDIAC ELECTRICAL ACTIVITY 
 A third voltage-gated channel, the calcium 
channel, affects action potentials in the cardiac 
muscle cells of the heart.  The calcium channel 
carries positively charged calcium (and some 
sodium ions) into the cell just after depolarization.  
This acts to hold the inside positive for a longer 
time.  The action potential has a positive plateau.  
Prolonging the depolarization enables heart muscles 
to remain contracted longer. 
 Action potentials in the pacemaker cells of 
the heart repetitively di scharge rhythmically.  In 
non-rhythmic nerve and muscle cells, the resting 
membrane potential remains at a constant value 
somewhere below threshold until a stimulus occurs. 
In the pacemaker cells and electrical conducting 
cells of the heart (discussed belo w), the cell 
membrane is more permeable to sodium ion . As 
sodium ion leaks into the cells, the resting 
membrane potential gradually becomes less 
negative, creeping toward threshold .  Once 
threshold is reached, the membrane fires an all -or-
none action poten tial.  After repolarization, the 
process repeats, bringing the membrane potential 
slowly toward threshold again, and another impulse 
occurs.  This is the rhythmic nerve activity that 
triggers the rhythmic beating of the heart.      
 The electrical pacemaker of the heart is the 
sinoatrial (SA) node found in the right atrium near 
the opening of the superior vena cava.  The SA 
node depolarizes spontaneously, establishing the 
rate of the heart (about 70 beats per minute at rest). 
The depolarization spreads through the heart along 
the pathway indicated by arrows in the figure 
below. Near the interventricular septum (ventricular 
wall separating the two ventricular chambers) is the 
atrioventricular (AV) node.  If the SA node is 
damaged, the AV node can pace the hea rt.  Its rate 
is about 50 beats per minute.  Impulses from the 
AV node are carried down the interventricular 
septum by special conducting fibers called the 
bundle of His  that soon split s into right and left 
bundle branches traveling down to the apex and 
then up through the outer walls of the heart.  
Numerous small 
fibers called 
Purkinje fibers  
branch off the 
bundle branches 
carrying the 
nerve impulses 
deep into the 
myocardial 
tissue to the 
muscle cells.  
 While the actual stimulus for contraction 
originates within the nodal tissue itself (the heart is 
myogenic), the rate and strength of contraction can 
be controlled by the autonomic nervous system.  As 
was the case in visceral muscle, cardiac muscle is 
dually innervated. There are nerves to stimulate the 
heart and nerves to inhibit it. The sympathetic 
branch of the autonomic nervous system increases 
heart rate and strength of contraction while the 
parasympathetic branch decreases heart rate and 
contraction strength.  Cardiac activity can also be 
controlled wi th hormones. The hormone 
epinephrine from the adrenal gland, for example, 
has an effect similar to that of the sympathetic 
nerves. It stimulates the heart.

===== PAGE 23 =====
19 
THE ELECTROCARDIOGRAM (ECG, EKG) 
 Because 
the electrical 
currents within the 
heart move 
according t o the 
very regular 
pattern described 
above, and 
because body 
tissue is a good 
conductor of 
electrical activity, 
we can observe the 
pattern of 
electrical activity in the heart by placing sensitive 
recording electrodes on the skin surface. Placing 
electrodes in the same positions on different 
patients produces ECGs having very similar 
patterns. Changing positions of the electrodes 
changes the shapes of the ECG waveform, but the 
same waves still appear. In the ECG work -up, an 
array of ten electrodes is placed over and around 
the heart as shown in the figure.  The 
electrocardiograph unit alternates between 
electrodes to produce 12 different ECG patterns.  
The typical Lead II pattern, obtained by activating 
the negative electrode of the right arm and 
positive electrode of the left leg, is shown below. 
 In the ECG, the P wave  occurs during 
depolarization of the atria.  The QRS complex 
corresponds to ventricular depolarization.  (The 
downward Q deflection occurs only if the activity 
crosses the ventricular septum from left to right and 
is often missing.)  The upward deflection to R 
occurs as the negative depolarization wave moves 
down the septum toward the positive electrode.  As 
the depolarization wave reaches the apex and 
reverses direction, the wave also reverses,  moving 
down toward S as the depolarization wave moves 
up toward the negative electrode through the outer 
walls of the ventricle.  The T wave corresponds to 
ventricular repolarization.  Atrial repolarization is  
iso-electric, meaning that it produces no waveform. 
 The ECG corresponds only to the electrical 
events within the heart, the depolarizations and 
repolarizations.  The patterns do not correspond to 
mechanical activity- contractions and relaxations.  
The electrical events occur before the mechanical 
events.  A time interval is associated with the waves 
and between the waves in the normal ECG.  The P 
wave normally is about 0.08 seconds; the QRS 
complex about 0.06 seconds, and the interval 
between P and R is about 0.08 seconds. 
 In a patient with suspect ed damage to the 
myocardium (ischemia, necrosis, infection) the flow 
of current in the area of injury is altered.  Using the 
combined changes that appear on the 12 different 
leads, each looking at the injury from a different 
axis, the actual location of th e injury can often be 
determined.   
 ECG time intervals can be predictive of 
conduction abnormalities.  For example, the 
interval between P and R produces a delay between 
contraction of the atria and contraction of the 
ventricles, giving the ventricles ade quate time to 
fill.  If the interval is too short, the ventricles 
contract prematurely.  If too long, a cardiac 
arrhythmia could occur as the AV node begins to 
pace the ventricles and the SA node paces the atria. 
 Recall that the two nodes pace at differen t rates, 
the AV being slower than the SA.  If each P wave is 
not associated with a QRS complex, this has 
occurred.  It's called a heart block. 
 Instantaneous heart rate can be determined 
from the ECG.  An abnormally high rate is called 
tachycardia. Causes include stress, anxiety, drugs 
and fever. An abnormally low rate is bradycardia. 
It can occur in hypothermia and diving under water, 
as examples. Flutter refers to a very rapid rate that 
still has a regular or nearly regular ECG pattern.  In 
ventricular fibrillation, pattern regularity is lost. 
Coordination between contracting muscle cells is 
lost and the contractions no longer generate enough 
pressure to pump blood. The regular ECG pattern is 
replaced with electrical noise. Ventricular 
fibrillation is life  threatening and must be quickly 
treated with cardiac massage and converted with 
electrical defibrillation. Here, 440 volts AC or 
about 2000 volts DC are briefly passed through the 
heart to stop all electrical activity.  The hope is that 
when spontaneous electrical activity resumes, the 
SA node will again establish the rhythm.

===== PAGE 24 =====
20 
LESSON 7:  HEMODYNAMICS  
 
 The cardiac cycle refers to cardiac activity 
for one complete heart beat.  There are two periods 
in each cycle for both the atria and ventricles.  
Systole is the period of contraction of cardiac 
muscle and diastole is the period of relaxation.  
Other events of the cycle include changes in atrial, 
ventricular and arterial pressures and volumes; 
opening and closing of heart valves; electrical 
events (ECG); and the heart sounds.  The timing of 
these events in one cardiac cycle is illustrated 
below. The pressures shown in the figure 
correspond to those of the left heart. Pressure 
curves for the right heart are similar in form but 
lower in magnitude, the maximum pressure is about 
25 mmHg.  Lower pressure is required to pump 
blood to the lungs.   
THE CARDIAC CYCLE 
 Heart sounds are caused by closure of 
heart valves. There are two main heart sounds.  The 
first sound (lubb) is caused by closure of the 
atrioventricular valves. The second heart sound 
(dubb) is associated with closure of the aortic and 
pulmonary (semilunar) valves. The sounds are 
actually produced by blood turbulence that occurs 
near the closing valves.  Minor leaks in valves can 
cause additional soft sounds called heart murmurs. 
 Note on the figure that whenever the 
pressure relationship across a valve changes (that is, 
whenever two pressure curves cross), the valve 
changes position from open to closed or from 
closed to open. Pressure opens and closes the 
valves.  So when ventricular pressure suddenly 
exceeds atrial pressure at the beginning of 
ventricular systole, the AV valves are pushed 
closed and when ventricular pressure exceeds aortic 
pressure (also at the beginning of ventricular 
systole), the semilunar valve is pushed open. 
 There is one exception.  Because of the 
momentum of blood forcefully ejected from the 
ventricle, the semilunar valves doesn't close the 
instant arterial pressure exceeds ventricular 
pressure.  Note that when the aortic valve does 
close, there is a temporary drop in aortic pressure as 
the blood flows back against the valve and into the 
coronary arteries.   
 The force of blood pumped from the 
ventricles into the arteries distends the elastic tissue 
in their walls, storing potent ial energy.  Recoil of 
the walls when the flow from the ventricle stops is 
converted back into kinetic energy of flow.  
Therefore, while flow in the ventricles is 
periodically interrupted by closing of valves, flow 
through the blood vessels is continuous.  
 Note that 75% of ventricular filling occurs 
even before the atria contract.  Atrial fibrillation is 
not a life-threatening event. 
 CARDIAC WORK 
 By plotting the cardiac cycle as a pressure 
volume relationship, the work of the heart can be 
determined. Du ring diastolic filling, pressure 
increases only slightly (Phase I). When the ventricle 
contracts, pressure increases significantly, but 
during the time when both sets of valves are still

===== PAGE 25 =====
21 
closed, volume cannot change (Phase II).  When the 
semilunar valves d o open, volume decreases as 
blood is ejected under pressure.  Pressure continues 
to increase until sufficient volume is lost, at which 
time the pressure declines (Phase III).  Then, as the 
ventricle relaxes, pressure drops suddenly.  Again 
volume cannot ch ange during the time when both 
sets of valves are closed (Phase IV).  The area 
within the curve is the work required to raise the 
blood pressure to the required level.   It represents 
about 99% of the total work of the heart.  An 
additional small amount of  work is required to 
create the kinetic energy for blood flow. This 
normally represents only about 1% of the total work 
of the heart, but can increase significantly under the 
right circumstances- valve failure, for example.  In 
the figure, note that stroke volume is about 70 ml at 
rest and that while aortic pressure cycles between 
80 and 120 mm Hg, ventricular pressure cycles 
between 0 and 120 mm Hg. Ventricular pressure 
has to drop below atrial pressure or the ventricles 
can’t fill with blood from the atria. 
HEMODYNAMICS OF BLOOD FLOW 
 When we consider control of circulation, it 
will become apparent that pressure, flow and 
resistance are all important.  These three variables 
are related by Ohm's law:  
where Q dot is flow, P is pressure and R is  
resistance. 
 Given an average adult blood flow of about 
5 L/min or 85 ml/sec, and a pressure drop from 
aorta to vena cavae of about 100 mmHg, total 
peripheral resistance (TPR) is about 1.2 
mmHg/ml/sec.  Given an average pressure drop in 
the pulmonary circulation of about 14 mmHg, the 
TPR of the lungs is only about 0.16 mmHg/ml/sec. 
 Assuming laminar flow, we can use 
Poiseuille's law to identify factors that contribute to 
resistance.  Poiseuille's law states that flow (Q dot) 
through cylindrical tubes is related to the difference 
in pressure  (P), the length of the tube (l), the 
viscosity of the fluid flowing in the tube ( ), and 
the radius of the tube (r).  The relationship is 
summarized as follows:  
Note that increases in  or l cause decreases in Q 
dot. You can jus t as easily remember these 
relationships if you think about how each of these 
factors would be expected to affect flow.  You 
expect flow to be increased if pressure increases or 
if the tube expands. But if the fluid becomes thicker 
(more viscous) it won't flow as well.  As the fluid 
drags against the walls of the tube (frictional drag), 
flow also decreases; so increasing the length of the 
tube also decreases flow. 
 Note that radius has more impact on flow 
than any of the other variables.  It is raised to th e 
fourth power.  Doubling radius would increase flow 
sixteen-fold.  While length of vessels can change 
and viscosity of blood can change, minute -to-
minute regulation of blood flow is achieved most 
efficiently by changing vessel radius using 
vasoconstriction and vasodilation.   
 Another hemodynamic relationship relates 
blood flow (Q dot) and vessel cross -sectional area 
(A) to velocity (V) of the flowing blood.  Note that 
flow is in units of volume per unit time (ml/min, for 
example) while velocity is in uni ts of distance per 
unit time (cm/sec).  The relationship is summarized:  
As blood flow increases, velocity increases.  
However, blood flow from one vessel bed to the 
next is fairly constant, about 85 ml/sec at rest.   But 
as total cross -sectional area incr eases, velocity 
decreases.  Arteries and veins, with a relatively 
low cross -sectional area, have a high blood 
velocity.  This is appropriate for distribution and 
collection of blood.  Taken together, the total cross-
sectional area of capillaries is very la rge, giving 
them a low blood velocity.  This allows adequate 
time for exchange between capillaries and the 
adjacent tissue cells.  Even so, because capillaries 
are so short, blood only spends a few seconds in 
them.

LESSON 8:  ARTERIES, CAPILLARIES AND VEINS 
 
Blood vessels are designed to deliver blood 
to the tissues and exchange materials as required by 
the cells. No living cell is more than 20 or 30 
microns from a blood source.  All vessels are lined 
with a smooth single layer of endothelial cells.  The 
smooth surface is necessary to prevent clotting.  For 
capillaries, this is the only layer.  Arteries and veins 
have muscle and connective tissue layers beneath 
the endothelium.  Visceral muscle in vessel walls 
controls the pressure and distribution of blo od.  
Connective tissue is elastic enough to allow vessels 
to distend, yet strong enough to contain the blood 
beyond the elastic limit. 
ARTERIES 
 Arteries are the distribution vessels of the 
circulatory system, carrying blood from the heart to 
the capillaries.  While all arteries are muscular, it is 
in the small arteries, called arterioles, where 
discontinuous rings of muscle produce most of the 
resistance in the circulatory system.  As much as 
two thirds the total peripheral resistance is found in 
arterioles. In addition, small precapillary 
sphincter muscles at the ends of the arterioles 
control blood flow into individual capillary beds, 
allowing blood to enter tissues with high need and 
diverting it away from tissues with low need.  
Elastic tissue allows ar teries to distend under the 
pressure of systole and then to recoil during 
diastole, providing a continuous blood flow even 
when there is no ventricular flow.   
 Blood in arteries is under high pressure.  
The contractions of the ventricles produce pressure 
pulsations in arteries ranging, on average, from 120 
mm Hg during left ventricular systole to 80 mmHg 
during diastole.  The pulsations are damped out 
when the blood passes through the high resistance 
of the arterioles.  The difference between systolic 
and diastolic pressure is pulse pressure, and is a 
measure of arterial compliance. Abnormally high 
pulse pressure is a sign of stiffening arteries, as 
occurs in older adults.   
The pressure can be measured by providing 
sufficient pressure to occlude flow throu gh an 
artery (usually the brachial artery in the arm) and 
measuring the pressures at which flow returns.  
When cuff pressure is above systolic pressure, there 
is no flow and no sounds are heard in the artery.  
When cuff pressure is below systolic pressure but 
above diastolic pressure, blood flows only when 
the ventricle contracts, producing thumping 
sounds in the artery (Korotkoff sounds). The 
pressure at which the sounds first begin is systolic 
pressure.  When cuff pressure is below diastolic 
pressure, the sounds disappear as the blood flows 
continuously again. The pressure at which the 
sounds first disappear is diastolic pressure. 
CAPILLARIES 
 Capillaries are the exchange vessels.  The 
endothelial lining is the only cell layer of the 
capillary. As exchange vessels, they need to be thin. 
 Not all capillaries are equally permeable, however. 
 Capillaries of the liver and spleen are highly 
permeable; while capillaries are the brain have low 
permeability, forming what is called the blood-brain 
barrier. There are  about 10 billion capillaries 
providing an exchange area about 500 to 700 square 
meters.  Capillaries are very narrow; red blood cells 
must pass through single file.  Most exchange 
occurs by diffusion and most diffusion occurs 
through clefts between endothelial cells.  On transit 
through an average capillary bed, the water of 
plasma exchanges with that of interstitial fluid 
about 80 times, mostly across intercellular clefts.  
All of the principles of diffusion discussed earlier 
apply to exchange of nutrient s and wastes across 
the capillaries.   
 In addition to diffusion, hydrostatic 
pressure (the pressure of filtration) and osmotic 
pressure gradients create additional fluid exchange. 
Plasma is hyperosmotic to interstitial fluid.  The 
osmotic gradient reflect s the fact that the plasma 
contains 7 to 8% protein but interstitial fluid 
contains only 2 to 3% protein.  The net effect of 
osmosis is to pull fluid into the capillaries.   On the 
other hand, capillary blood is under pressure.  At 
the inflow to the capill aries, pressure is about 30 
mmHg and drops to about 10 mmHg at the outflow. 
 As blood enters capillaries, hydrostatic pressure is 
greater than osmotic pressure and fluid filters into 
the interstitial fluid.  By the time blood reaches the 
end of the capilla ries, the hydrostatic pressure has 
fallen enough so that the osmotic pressure gradient 
is greater and water pulled back into the capillaries.

===== PAGE 27 =====
23 
  The volume exchanged by filtration and 
osmosis (20 ml/min for the whole body) is small 
compared to that exchan ged by diffusion (4000 
ml/min). While it isn't large by comparison, 
diffusion occurs equally in both directions while the 
fluid lost from the capillaries by filtration is greater 
than the fluid gained by osmosis.   Of the 20 ml/min 
that passes out of the c apillaries by filtration and 
osmosis, all but 2 ml is returned.  That’s about 3 
liters/day.  Also, there is a slight net loss of protein 
from the blood as well.  It is vital that this excess 
fluid and protein be returned to the circulatory 
system.  This is accomplished by a parallel venous 
system, the lymphatic system, discussed below. 
 Capillaries are composed of a single layer 
of squamous (flat) endothelial cells and yet are able 
to withstand pressures of 30 mmHg or more.  This 
is explained by their very small radius .  Wall 
tension (T), pressure (P) and radius (r) are related 
according to the law of Laplace: 
T = P × r 
Wall tension in capillaries is extremely small 
because the very small radius of capillaries more 
than compensates for the relatively high pressure. 
THE LYMPHATIC SYSTEM 
 The excess 
protein and fluid 
enters the lymphatic 
capillaries that are 
located in close 
proximity to the 
blood capillaries. 
Lymphatic capillaries 
are much more 
permeable than blood 
capillaries. This 
enables them to also 
remove proteins and 
foreign material 
including bacteria and viruses from interstitial fluid 
as well as excess fluid.   The fluid collected, called 
lymph, passes into lymphatic veins.  Lymph is 
clear and colorless; very similar to interstitial fluid. 
 Lymph vessels ultimately empty into two major 
branches; the right lymphatic duct  and thoracic 
duct.  The right lymphatic duct receives lymph 
collected from the upper right quadrant of the body. 
The thoracic duct receives the rest.  The ducts 
return lymph to the bloo d through the respective 
subclavian vein ("below the clavicle").  The excess 
fluid lost in the blood capillaries is thus returned to 
the blood.  Lymphatic veins, like those carrying 
blood described below, have smooth muscle in their 
walls and valves to ins ure the uni -directional flow 
of lymph.   
 As lymph moves toward its entry points 
into the general circulation, it passes through 
clusters of small kidney bean sized filters called 
lymph nodes.  As shown by the dots in the figure 
above, nodes are particularly numerous in cervical 
(neck), axillary (armpit), antecubital (elbow) and 
inguinal (groin) areas.  Numerous nodes are also 
found in the abdominal cavity and mediastinum 
(area around the heart).  Afferent lymphatic veins 
carry lymph into the nodes.  There the lymph is 
cleaned and filtered. The nodes contain an 
abundance of phagocytic cells in sinus channels 
that destroy much of the cellular debris picked up 
by lymphatic capillaries.  Efferent lymphatic veins 
carry the filtered lymph back toward the circulatory 
system.  Unfortunately, the permeable lymphatic 
capillaries als o serve as an entry point in the 
circulatory system for stray cancer cells , allowing 
them to spread throughout the body. 
 The lymph nodes are also a site of 
lymphocyte production. Lymphocyt es are 
associated with our immune system that participates 
in the defense of the body.  Unlike phagocytes that 
scavenge many types of invading cells, 
lymphocytes can only attack the specific cell type 
they are programmed to recognize.   
 Since lymphatic ca pillaries are more 
permeable than systemic capillaries, the lymphatic 
system accomplishes another task.   While the 
digested products of carbohydrates and proteins are 
easily absorbed from the gut into systemic 
capillaries, digested fat molecules are too l arge.   
The more permeable lymphatic capillaries absorb 
digested fats from the intestines and transport them 
into the blood through the lymphatic veins. 
 The spleen is an organ associated with the 
lymphatic system.  It serves as a blood filter.  As 
blood passes through the "pulp" of the spleen, old 
and damaged red blood cells are removed and 
excess circulating red cells are stored.  If oxygen 
demand increases, these stored red cells can be

===== PAGE 28 =====
24 
released, increasing the concentration of circulating 
blood cells.   
VEINS 
 Veins and the smaller venules leading out 
of the capillaries, provide the collection function for 
the circulatory system. Returning blood to the heart 
is not always an easy task.  For example, in an erect 
person, standing perfectly still, venous pr essure at 
the level of the heart is near zero, but venous 
pressure in the feet is about 90 mm Hg. This is due 
to the gravitational effect on hydrostatic pressure. 
The pressure of blood in the feet is increased by the 
weight of the blood above the feet.  
 To reduce the effect of gravity, veins have 
a series of one-way valves (similar to the semilunar 
valves of the heart) along their entire length.  Blood 
can only move toward the heart.  Contraction of 
smooth muscle in the veins displaces blood and, 
because o f the one -way valves, the blood is 
displaced only toward the heart.  Contraction of 
skeletal muscles squeezes veins running through 
them, also displacing blood toward the heart.  This 
is called "milking" the veins.  During inspiration, 
the same process that sucks air into the lungs, sucks 
blood into the thorax, also increasing venous return. 
Expiration compresses the thoracic veins but, as 
with venous blood flow caused by contraction of 
skeletal muscle, blood can still only move toward 
the heart due to the one-way valves. 
 As a result of the action of the skeletal 
muscle “pumps”, venous pressure in the feet of a 
walking person is only about 20 mm Hg. If one then 
stands perfectly still, venous pressure in the feet 
will increase to 90 mm Hg within 30 seconds or so. 
The high venous pressure is transmitted back into 
the capillaries, causing increased fluid filtration into 
interstitial spaces (edema). Within 15 to 30 minutes 
of standing still, up to 20 % of the blood volume 
can be lost, with an accompanying drop i n blood 
pressure. This contributes to the tendency to lose 
consciousness, as sometimes occurs in soldiers 
standing in ranks for long periods of time.  
 If arteries are resistance vessels, veins are 
volume vessels.  They are about 24 times as 
compliant as arteries.  About 64% or 3 to 3.5 liters 
of circulating blood is found in the venous system 
at rest.  Contraction of vascular smooth muscle, 
especially in the veins, can reduce the overall size 
of the vascular system to maintain adequate blood 
pressure in the face of a loss of as much as 20% of 
the blood volume. 
 [Note: Arterial blood pressure is also 
affected by gravity. Blood pressure measured in 
the arm is at the level of the heart and mean blood 
pressure is about 100 mm Hg. If measured in the 
ankle of a standing person, normal mean arterial 
blood pressure would be about 190 mm Hg.] 
 Varicose veins  is a problem that often 
occurs in persons engaged in activities that require 
standing motionless for prolonged periods or in 
pregnant women with increased venous pressure in 
the lower extremities due to the increased pelvic 
pressure reducing venous return. Here, the veins 
become so distended that the valves are unable to 
plug the lumen even in the closed position. As a 
result, the distended peripheral veins protr ude and 
fluid begins to leak into the tissue from the veins 
(edema). Untreated, the increase in tissue fluid can 
compromise nutrient diffusion to tissue cells, 
causing muscle weakness and skin deterioration.  
CORONARY ARTERY DISEASE 
 In coronary artery disease, the large arteries 
that provide nutrients to the heart muscle itself 
begin to narrow as fatty materials accumulate 
within the vessel walls.  The lipid deposits of 
neutral fat and cholesterol produce an irregular 
mass of yellow, mushy (athero) debris that plugs 
the vessel lumen and forms a rough surface ideal 
for thrombus (blood clot) formation.  Myocardial 
cells are ravenous for nutrients, especially oxygen.  
As blood flow decreases, ischemia (oxygen 
starvation) rapidly develops.  Ischemia predisposes 
the heart cells to death (necrosis) and leaves a scar 
of dead tissue ( myocardial infarction ). It also 
predisposes the heart to fibrillation and failure to 
pump blood ( cardiac arrest ). Risk factors for 
coronary artery disease include high blood pressure, 
high cholesterol and triglycerides, smoking, obesity 
and stress.

===== PAGE 29 =====
25 
LESSON 9:  CONTROL OF CIRCULATION  
 
Blood pressure  (BP) is the product of 
cardiac output  (CO) and total peripheral 
resistance (TPR). 
BP = CO × TPR 
To understand how blood pressure is controlled we 
need to see how cardiac output and total peripheral 
resistance are controlled. 
 Cardiac output is the quantity of blood 
that is ejected by one ventricle per minute.  It is 
calculated as the product of stroke volume (SV), 
the volume of blood ejected  from a ventricle per 
beat, and heart rate (HR), the number of beats per 
minute. 
SV × HR = CO 
Inserting normal adult resting values: 
70 ml/beat × 70 beats/min = 4900 ml/min 
 One of the benefits of regular endurance exercise is 
to increase the size of the c ardiac muscle cells 
(hypertrophy).  The added strength allows stroke 
volume to increase so that cardiac output can be 
maintained with a lower heart rate. In a marathon 
runner, resting cardiac output might be 
100 ml/beat × 50 beats/min = 5000 ml/min 
At maximum rate of exercise, the runner’s cardiac 
output can be 50% higher than a non -athlete but 
with a lower heart rate.  
CARDIAC OUTPUT CONTROL 
 Extrinsic control relies on the nervous and 
endocrine systems.  Neural control is exerted by 
the sympathetic and pa rasympathetic branches of 
the autonomic (involuntary) nervous system.  
Sympathetic efferent fibers to the heart increase 
myocardial contractility and thus increase stroke 
volume.  Sympathetic nerves also increase heart 
rate.  Therefore, the sympathetic bra nch increases 
cardiac output.  The receptors on the heart that 
respond to sympathetic stimulation are called beta 
receptors.  Beta blockers are often used to treat 
people suffering from high blood pressure. 
Parasympathetic innervation has the opposite 
effects.   
  The center of the reflex control mechanism 
for cardiac output is in the medulla oblongata, the 
lowest portion of the brain s tem just above the 
spinal cord.   Within the medulla oblongata is a 
cardiac accelerator center  (CAC) that acts 
through sympathetic motor nerves and a separate 
cardiac inhibitory center  (CIC) acting through 
parasympathetic motor nerves.   
  Endocrine control is exerted by several 
hormones. The most important are norepinephrine 
and epinephrine, two hormones of the adrenal 
glands.  Both have the effect of increasing heart rate 
and myocardial contractility, thereby serving as a 
backup system for the sympathetic nerves. Of the 
two, epinephrine is the most potent. 
   Intrinsic control mechanisms require no 
participation by either the endocrine or nervous 
systems.  It is the organ, in a sense, controlling 
itself. Stroke volume has an intrinsic control 
mechanism referred to as Starling's Law of the 
Heart.  The law states that as the ventricular fibers 
are stretched or elongated by increa sed ventricular 
filling, the fibers contract with greater force and 
more blood is delivered into the arteries with each 
beat.  The more the fibers are stretched (without 
damaging them), the greater the stroke volume.  
During exercise, for example, blood flow increases. 
As a result, more blood is returned to the heart.  As 
the increased venous return is forced into the 
ventricles, the stretched fibers contract with more 
force, increasing stroke volume. 
VASCULAR RESISTANCE CONTROL 
 Total peripheral resistance  (TPR) is 
normally regulated by changing the radius of blood 
vessels.  Vasoconstriction decreases vessel radius, 
increasing TPR and (if flow remains constant) 
increasing blood pressure. (Refer to the 
hemodynamics equations presented earlier.) 
Vasodilation would have the opposite effect. 
  Extrinsic control is by means of the 
nervous and endocrine systems. Neural control of 
TPR is also centered in the medulla oblongata.  
Besides the cardiac centers, there is also a 
vasoconstrictor center (VCC) that sends eff erent 
impulses to certain vasoconstrictor fibers 
innervating the smooth muscle in the walls of the 
blood vessels.  Receptors in vascular smooth 
muscle that initiate constriction are called alpha

===== PAGE 30 =====
26 
receptors.  Alpha receptor blockers are also used to 
treat hi gh blood pressure.  As vessels narrow, 
vascular resistance and blood pressure increase. A 
vasodilator center has not been identified in the 
brain. Vasodilation is achieved by inhibition of the 
VCC. The centers within the medulla oblongata 
that control card iovascular reflexes are called the 
vital centers for obvious reasons.  Centers 
controlling reflexes essential for survival are 
generally located in the brain stem; the first part of 
the brain to have evolved. 
 Endocrine control of TPR is exerted by 
several hormones, but again, the best examples are 
two hormones of the adrenal glands.  
Norepinephrine is a potent 
vasoconstrictor of blood vessels.  Its 
effect is to increase resistance and blood 
pressure.  Epinephrine, on the other 
hand, constricts vessels in some beds 
and dilates vessels in other beds.  For 
example, during exercise, epinephrine 
vasodilates vessels to skeletal muscle, a 
tissue that requires more blood.  But 
epinephrine acts to vasoconstrict vessels 
in the digestive and urinary systems.  It 
functions to redistribute blood more 
than to increase total peripheral resistance during 
exercise. 
REFLEX PATHWAYS  
 We have until now been considering the 
efferent nerves controlling blood pressure.  Let's 
now consider the entire reflex arc, using as an 
example one of the many types of sensory receptors 
involved in control of circulation. The 
baroreceptors, are located in the arch of the aorta 
and in the carotid arteries of the neck.  They 
respond to changes in blood pressure.  The rate of 
baroreceptor firing (depolarization) is sent to the 
vital centers through sensory nerves.  An increase 
in blood pressure causes an increase in receptor 
firing rate.  The CIC responds and decreases heart 
rate and stroke volume.  The VCC is inhibited and 
the vessels vasodilate, decreasing resistance.  Blood 
pressure falls back to normal.  A drop in pressure 
below normal decreases firing rate of the receptors. 
The afferent fibers stimulate the CAC increasing 
cardiac output.  They also stimulate the VCC 
causing vasoconstriction th at increases resistance.  
This causes blood pressure to increase back to 
normal.   
 A common example illustrating the 
efficiency of these reflexes is the normal response 
to standing from a lying position.  Initially, blood 
pools toward the lower extremitie s decreasing 
venous return to the heart.  By Starling’s Law, 
decreased filling reduces tension in the ventricular 
wall and contractions are weaker causing cardiac 
output to fall.  Decreasing cardiac output decreases 
blood pressure.  The baroreceptor firing  rate is 
decreased and sensory input to the brain triggers a 
reflex increase in heart rate and arteriolar 
constriction bringing blood pressure back to 
normal.   
 
AUTOREGULATION (OF BLOOD FLOW) 
 It is important to keep in mind that what is 
controlled in the  larger arteries is blood pressure 
but what the body tissues need is blood flow.  
According to Poiseuille's Law, given in the figure, 
if pressure falls, all other variables remaining 
unchanged, flow will fall.  This is shown by the 
dashed line in the figure.   
 
 Some organs require a very constant and 
adequate blood flow.  The heart and brain are 
examples.  Organs like the skin are more tolerant of 
variations in flow.  Organs that do require a steady 
flow have developed a mechanism to maintain flow

===== PAGE 31 =====
27 
constant even when blood pressure changes.  This 
ability is called autoregulation.  Again referring to 
the equation in the figure, if pressure decreased, 
then flow would decrease. Knowing that the most 
effective way to change flow is to change radius 
(r4), what c ould one do to radius, to cancel the 
effect of a drop in pressure? According to the 
equation, an increase in radius would compensate 
for a decrease in pressure. The changes would be in 
the direction of canceling each other and flow could 
remain constant.  That possibility is shown by the 
solid line in the figure and is, in fact, what happens 
in autoregulation. 
 One explanation is as follows:  Suppose 
blood pressure falls.  Initially, flow would also 
decrease. Certain metabolites normally carried 
away in the  flowing blood begin to accumulate. 
Some of the metabolites are vasodilators and vessel 
radius increases returning flow to normal. As the 
blood pressure mechanisms discussed earlier bring 
blood pressure back to normal, flow will increase 
above normal.  Exc essive amounts of vasodilator 
substance are cleared from the tissue and the vessel 
would constrict to its normal diameter, bringing 
blood flow back to normal.  This is an intrinsic 
control mechanism that does not involve the 
nervous or endocrine systems. 
 Other mechanisms used to explain 
autoregulation include tissue oxygen tension and a 
myogenic response.  Hypoxia that might occur if 
flow falls, acts as a potent vasodilator in many 
systemic blood vessels.  The drop in pressure that 
reduces flow is countered by an increase in radius, 
bringing flow back to normal even though blood 
pressure is low.  In the myogenic theory, visceral 
muscle in blood vessel walls can reflex ively 
contract when wall tension is increased, causing
