vasoconstriction.  Recall in the dis cussion of 
smooth muscle that contractions often occur with 
no neural stimulation and that one stimulus is 
increased tension. Tissues experiencing reduced 
flow for extended periods (weeks, months) begin to 
develop new vessels to provide a permanent 
increase in flow. This is called angiogenesis.  It is 
hypothesized that nutrient deficiencies in the tissues 
may release angiogenic factors that promote 
development of new vessels.  Angiogenesis occurs 
rapidly in new tissues including tissues of newborns 
or canc er tissue, but occurs very slowly in 
established tissues.  New vessels can also develop 
around blocked arteries or veins, providing 
collateral circulation. 
LONG-TERM BLOOD PRESSURE CONTROL 
 Pressure control mechanisms discussed so 
far gradually lose their ability to regulate over a 
period of hours or days.  Long -term week-to-week 
or month-to-month control requires the renal-body 
fluid control mechanism of the kidneys.  As blood 
pressure rises, more sodium ion and water are 
passed from the blood into the urine to be excreted. 
Reducing the fluid volume in the blood reduces 
blood pressure.  The loss of additional fluid in the 
urine as a result of increased blood pressure is 
called pressure diuresis.  Increased blood pressure 
also causes increased excretion of s odium ions. 
This is called pressure natruresis (from the Latin 
for sodium). It promotes water loss because sodium 
ion increases the osmotic pressure of the urine, 
pulling water along with it.  When pressure falls 
too low, salt and water are retained by the  kidney, 
bringing volume and pressure back to normal.   
 The renal-body fluid mechanism is able to 
exactly correct any imbalance completely.  
Therefore, it is a system with infinite gain.   The 
baroreceptors, for example, can only partially 
correct an imba lance.  For every increase of 8 
mmHg in pressure, the baroreceptors can only 
bring pressure back down by about 7 mmHg.  The 
gain is therefore about -7 ( correction divided by 
the error).

===== PAGE 32 =====
28 
LESSON 10: URINE FORMATION   
BODY FLUID COMPARTMENTS   
About 55 to 60% of the weight of an 
average individual is water.  A person weighing 150 
pounds, for example, has about 85 pounds (or 40 
liters) of water in his/her system.  Therefore, it is 
important to understand how water is distributed in 
the body, how distribution is controlled, and how 
water intake is balanced by water output.  The 
urinary system plays a dominant role in fluid 
balance. 
INTAKE VS OUTPUT 
 On an average day in an average adult, 90-
95% of the fluid added to the system is ingested, 
two-thirds as liquid and one -third as the liquid 
component of solid food.  About 5-10% is obtained 
through metabolism.  For example, the 
mitochondria produce water (and CO 2) from the 
oxidation of glucose to produce ATP.  The total 
amount of intake per day averages about 2500 ml. 
 
Source Input (ml) Output (ml) 
Metabolism        250  
Moist food        750  
Beverages      1500  
Kidneys    1500 
Lungs      350 
Skin      350 
Feces      150 
Sweat      150 
Total      2500   2500 
  
Normally, intake is balanced by output.  
Most water lost per day is excreted in the urine.  
The rest is lost from sweat, expired water vapor, the 
feces, and water evaporation through the skin.  (See 
table.)  
During exercise, fluid loss increases 
significantly due ma inly to increased water loss 
from breathing and sweating.  The kidneys are the 
organs of water balance.  That is, if you become 
dehydrated, you can’t exhale dry gas to conserve 
water or secrete a concentrated sweat low in 
water.  But if you become dehydrat ed, you can 
conserve water by excreting a concentrated urine. 
If you become over -hydrated, you excrete a dilute 
urine. 
 
DISTRIBUTION OF BODY FLUIDS 
 More than two -thirds of body fluid (about 
28 liters) is found inside cells as cytoplasm.  This 
fluid is the intracellular fluid.       
 The volume of all fluids outside cells, 
extracellular fluid, is about 14 liters, or about one-
third of total body water.  About 3 liters of this is 
intravascular; the fluid component of blood called 
plasma.  Small amounts are f ound in endolymph, 
perilymph, aqueous humor, cerebral spinal fluid 
and a few others. The rest is interstitial fluid.  
 Body fluid volumes can be measured using 
an indicator dilution principle. For example, to 
measure blood plasma volume, 10 milligrams of 
Evan's blue dye is injected into the bloodstream.  
Evan's blue is impermeable to blood cells and is 
unable to leave the bloodstream.  Therefore, in 
time, it distributes itself uniformly throughout the 
plasma. After allowing sufficient time, a blood 
sample is drawn and the plasma concentration of 
dye is determined.  If it were found to be 0.0033 mg 
per ml of plasma, then the plasma volume is 10 mg 
divided by 0.0033 mg/ml or about 3030 ml.  There 
are appropriate markers for measuring various body 
fluid compart ment volumes. Those that can’t be 
measured can be calculated from known volumes. 
 Extracellular fluids contain high 
concentrations of sodium, chloride and bicarbonate 
ion.  Plasma contains about 7% protein and 
interstitial fluid about 2% protein.  Intracel lular 
fluid (cytoplasm) contains large amounts of 
potassium and phosph ate ions and about 25% 
protein. 
THE URINARY SYSTEM  
 The urinary system functions to regulate 
body water and control plasma electrolyte levels. In 
doing so, it regulates blood volume and  therefore 
helps maintain blood pressure. It also helps to keep 
blood pH within normal limits. The kidney is 
responsible for excreting many metabolic wastes 
including urea, uric acid and creatinine. 
THE KIDNEY 
 The two bean shaped kidneys are located in 
the back of the abdominal cavity at the level of the 
waist.  Blood entering each kidney from a renal 
artery is separated into waste products that leave

===== PAGE 33 =====
29 
through a tube 
called the ureter, 
and the purified 
blood returned to 
the body through 
the renal vein. A 
tough fibrous 
capsule forms the 
outer layer of each 
kidney.  Each 
kidney is 
composed of inner 
and outer regions 
with a central cavity.  The outer region is called the 
cortex.  Below the cortex is a region called the 
medulla, formed by microscopic tubules, including 
those that carry the newly formed urine to the 
central cavity.  The tubules extend from a broad 
base at the cortex to a narrow tip.  For this reason 
the clusters of medullary tubules are called the 
renal pyramids.  The tip of the pyramid is a thi n 
membrane called the renal papilla.  A large tube 
called the renal calyx collects urine as it crosses 
the papilla and carries it to the central cavity called 
the renal pelvis. 
 The urine is transported from the pelvis of 
each kidney to the urinary bladder through a tube 
called the ureter (one extends from each kidney).  
Visceral muscle in the walls of the ureters moves 
the urine to the bladder where it is stored.  Urine 
passes from the bladder to be voided through a 
single tube called the urethra.  In male s, the 
urethra also transports semen from the reproductive 
organs. In females, the urinary tract and 
reproductive tract are completely separate.  
 The functional units of the kidney are the 
one or two million nephrons. Each nephron 
consists of five parts: Bowman's capsule, the 
proximal convoluted tubule, the loop of Henle, the 
distal convoluted tubule, and the collecting duct.  
Most of the parts of the nephron are found in the 
cortex.  The collecting ducts and some of the loops 
of Henle extend down into the renal pyramids form 
the medulla of the kidney.   
 Each nephron has a network of blood 
vessels associated with it.  An afferent arteriole 
carries blood to a tuft of capillaries called the 
glomerulus located within Bowman's capsule.  An 
efferent arteriole  then carries blood from the 
glomerulus to peritubular capillaries  that 
surround the rest of the nephron tubules.  Exchange 
of materials between blood and urine occurs across 
both the glomerulus and the peritubular capillaries. 
The relationship between these structures can best 
be described by considering the four basic 
processes in urine formation: filtration, 
reabsorption, secretion and concentration. 
FILTRATION 
 About 20% of the total systemic blood flow 
passes through the kidneys (~1200 ml/min).  About 
20% of the blood plasma flowing into the 
glomerulus through the afferent arteriole  is 
filtered into Bowman's capsule.  That amounts to a 
combined glomerular filtration rate (GFR) that 
averages 125 ml/min.  That's approximately 50 
gallons/day.  About 99% of t he filtered water 
volume will be reabsorbed (most by osmosis) back 
into the plasma downstream from Bowman's 
capsule.  Only about 1500 ml/day will be excreted.  
The pressure in the glomerulus is 10 to 20 mmHg 
higher than that in Bowman's capsule, providing the 
hydrostatic pressure gradient for filtration. 
 The glomerulus is essentially a tuft of very 
permeable capillaries.  Any molecules with a 
molecular weight of less than 5000 are freely 
permeable to the capillary membranes.  Above that 
size, molecules bec ome less and less permeable.  
Molecules above 65,000 to 70,000 molecular 
weight are virtually impermeable.  That means that 
waste products like urea and creatinine as well as 
nutrients like amino acids, glucose and minerals 
like sodium ions and potassium i ons are all small 
enough to filter from the plasma into Bowman's 
capsule. Large molecules including plasma proteins

===== PAGE 34 =====
30 
are too large and remain in plasma, keeping the 
osmotic pressure of the plasma high (important later 
on for the reabsorption of water).  Lik ewise, blood 
cells are too large to filter and remain in the blood 
vessels.   
AUTOREGULATION OF FILTRATION 
 If blood pressure were to increase above 
normal, glomerular hydrostatic pressure would 
increase, GFR would increase and the volume of 
urine excreted would increase. This is referred to as 
pressure diuresis. The extra fluid lost as a result 
would reduce the blood volume and pressure would 
go back down.  
 But an autoregulatory (self -regulating) 
mechanism is necessary to control the amount of 
fluid loss. If blood pressure increased by 25%, and 
there was no autoregulation of filtration, urine 
output would increase by about thirty -fold.  This 
would cause a fatal drop in blood volume and blood 
pressure. A 50% drop in blood pressure would stop 
filtration comp letely and the nephrons would be 
damaged irreversibly.  
 GFR is controlled intrinsically by the 
kidney itself (autoregulation). Suppose blood 
pressure decreases below normal. GFR would 
initially decrease. Without control, the filtration 
rate might fall to dangerously low levels that could 
result in kidney failure. But the initial decrease in 
GFR and urine flow rate causes a decrease in the 
concentration of sodium and chloride ions in the 
urine of the distal convoluted tubules. This is 
because the reduced flow allows the urine to spend 
more time in the nephron and all during that time, 
sodium and chloride ions are being reabsorbed into 
the peritubular capillaries. Macula densa cells, in 
the walls of the distal convoluted tubules, sense the 
reduced ion levels and signal adjacent 
juxtaglomerular (juxta = next to)  cells located in 
the walls of the afferent arterioles.  The afferent 
arterioles respond to the direct stimulation by the 
juxtaglomerular cells and dilate. The efferent 
arterioles respond by constricting. The constriction 
of the efferent arterioles involves an  endocrine 
mechanism (the renin-angiotensin System) to be 
discussed later. Dilation of afferent vessels and 
constriction of efferent vessels increases the 
hydrostatic pressure in the glomerulus and t he rate 
of filtration increases.  Fluctuations of mean arterial 
blood pressure between 75 and 160 mmHg produce 
only slight changes in GFR. 
 The nervous system is able to exert limited 
control over filtration.  The sympathetic nervous 
system acts to decreas e filtration during times of 
stress by constricting the afferent arterioles.   
REABSORPTION AND SECRETION   
 After leaving the glomerulus, the efferent 
arterioles soon branch into a large capillary network 
that surrounds the tubules of the nephrons down 
stream from Bowman's capsule.  These capillaries 
are called the peritubular capillaries.  Here, further 
exchange between blood and urine can occur.  
Reabsorption refers to the movement of molecules 
from the urine back into the peritubular capillaries.  
This is important for nutrients that were small 
enough to be filtered into the urine.  Secretion 
refers to the movement of molecules from the 
peritubular capillaries into the nephron tubules.  
This is important for waste molecules that escaped 
filtration. 
 Sodium ions in the cells lining the renal 
tubules are rapidly reabsorbed into the interstitium 
by active transport. Recall that sodium ion 
concentration inside cells is kept low by the 
sodium-potassium exchange pump. This establishes 
a gradient for the facilit ated diffusion of sodium 
ions from the urine into the cells that line the 
nephron tubules. To facilitate this, the edge of the 
tubule cells adjacent to the lumen have multiple 
projections forming a brush border filled with 
carrier proteins. So sodium ions are transported 
from the urine into the cells by facilitated diffusion 
and out of the back of the cell into the interstitium 
by active transport. The carrier proteins for sodium 
ions also have binding sites for other molecules, 
including glucose and amino acids that  are then 
reabsorbed by co -transport with sodium ion. As 
these molecules accumulate within the tubule cells, 
a concentration gradient is established for them to 
leave the back of the cell by facilitated diffusion. 
Thus the only energy required is  the ATP needed 
for the sodium -potassium exchange pump that 
keeps sodium ion concentration low in the 
cytoplasm of the cells lining the tubules.  
 The concentration of sodium ion in the 
urine entering the collecting duct is nearly the same 
as that at the g lomerulus, meaning that if 99% of 
the water is reabsorbed, then 99% of the sodium ion 
must have been reabsorbed.  Most of the sodium

===== PAGE 35 =====
31 
ion is reabsorbed in the proximal tubules but there 
is also some reabsorption in the distal tubule.    
 As mentioned, glucose and amino acids are 
reabsorbed by co -transport with sodium ions.  
Normally all glucose and amino acids are 
reabsorbed by the time the urine leaves the 
proximal tubules.  However, the carrier molecules 
for glucose and the amino acids are saturable. As 
the filtered load of these substances approaches two 
or three times normal, they do begin to appear in 
the urine. In this case, it is said that the tubular 
maximum (Tmax) has been exceeded. 
 Small proteins that filter into the urine are 
reabsorbed by pinocyt osis. Proteins bind to 
receptors on the cell membrane and the membrane 
moves up and over, engulfing them. 
 Potassium and hydrogen ions are secreted- 
transported out of the blood and into the tubules.  
This occurs mainly in the distal tubule.  The energy 
is provided by the reabsorption of sodium ions, so 
this is an example of a counter -transport 
mechanism.  Hydrogen ions can also be secreted by 
active transport independent of sodium ions.  This 
allows the kidneys to play a vital role in the control 
of plasma  hydrogen ion concentration (acid -base 
balance). 
 Most chloride ions are reabsorbed by 
passive transport.  The negatively charged chloride 
ion is attracted to the positive charge of the sodium 
ion and moves with it back into the blood.  Some 
chloride is reabsorbed by active transport. 
 Urea (from breakdown of amino acids) and 
creatinine (from muscle creatine) are waste 
products excreted in high concentration in the urine. 
 About 90% of the creatinine found in the urine is 
filtered and about 10% secreted. Cr eatinine is not 
reabsorbed from the urine.  However, the urea 
molecule is small enough to diffuse back into the 
blood. But because water is reabsorbed more 
efficiently, urea concentration in the urine does still 
become quite high.  
CONCENTRATION 
 To conser ve water when the body is 
dehydrated, the kidney is able to draw water out of 
the urine by osmosis. The osmotic gradient is 
established by the loop of Henle. About 30% of the 
loops of Henle dip deep into the renal medulla. The 
osmotic concentration of urine entering the loop is 
about 300 mOsm. The osmotic concentration at the 
base of the loops can approach 1400 mOsm, while 
the urine leaving the loop and moving into the distal 
tubules can be as low as 50 mOsm. The reason the 
medulla becomes so hyperosmotic i s that the 
ascending limb of the loop of Henle is impermeable 
to water but is permeable to solutes like sodium ion 
and chloride ion.  The influx of these ions into the 
tissues raises the osmotic pressure of tissue fluid 
surrounding the loops of Henle to as  high as 1400 
mOsm at the bottom of the loops.   
 The loop structure of both the loop of 
Henle and the blood vessels (called the vasa recta) 
in the medulla, promote the osmotic gradient using 
the counter-current multiplier principle. The urine 
entering descending loop passes through the tissue 
made hyperosmotic by the ascending portion of the 
loop. Water is pulled out of the urine by osmosis as 
it descends down the loop, making the urine more 
concentrated with solutes that can then be deposited 
in the tissu e of the medulla on the way up the 
ascending loop. With the loss of solute, the urine 
leaving the loop of Henle is very hypoosmotic 
(dilute), creating the osmotic gradient.  
 The water permeability of the late distal 
tubule and collecting duct determine th e water 
concentration of the urine.  Recall, the collecting 
ducts also pass down through the very 
hyperosmotic medulla . This would favor the 
reabsorption of water out of the collecting duct and 
ultimately back into the blood. Normally, the 
collecting duct is not permeable to water.  If water 
is in excess in the body, the collecting duct remains 
impermeable and a dilute urine (as low as 50 
mOsm) is excreted.   
 However, if the body is dehydrated, a 
hormone called antidiuretic hormone  or ADH, 
produced by the hypothalamus and stored in the 
pituitary gland, is released into the blood stream.  
When ADH reaches receptors on the walls of the 
collecting duct, it opens water pores in the 
collecting duct allowing water to be pulled into the 
medulla by the high osmotic  pressure and leaving 
behind a very concentrated urine. In this way , the 
fluid balance function of the kidneys is separated 
from the waste excretion function.

===== PAGE 36 =====
32 
LESSON 11:  FLUID AND ELECTROLYTE BALANCE 
 
  There are two hormones that help regul ate 
fluid balance by targeting the kidney: aldosterone 
and antidiuretic hormone (ADH). 
ALDOSTERONE 
  Aldosterone is a sodium ion retaining 
hormone produced by the adrenal cortex (the outer 
portion of the adrenal glands).  Its target is the distal 
convoluted tubule of the nephron.  There it acts to 
increase the reabsorption of sodium ions by active 
transport. (Recall that most sodium ion is 
reabsorbed in the proximal convoluted tubule.)  It 
also increases the secretion of potassium ions and 
hydrogen ions by counter-transport.  As sodium is 
reabsorbed, water follows by osmosis.  So the 
hormone becomes important when there is a loss of 
both fluid and electrolytes.  Control of aldosterone 
release is quite complex, involving a kidney 
enzyme, a lung enzyme and a plasma protein 
produced by the liver.  The table below shows the 
series of events that occur to correct a drop in blood 
pressure. Unlike control of blood pressure with 
baroreceptors, this mechanism takes many hours to 
produce its effect.  
ANTIDIURETIC HORMONE 
  Antidiuretic hormone (ADH) is produced 
in the hypothalamus of the brain. It passes down the 
stalk of the pituitary gland through nerve fibers and 
is stored in the posterior part of the pituitary gland.  
The target of ADH is the nephron late distal 
convoluted tubule and collecting duct.  It acts to 
make these structures permeable to water.  Water is 
drawn out of the nephron and into the hyperosmotic 
tissues of the renal medulla. Diuresis (increase in 
urine output) is inhibited. ADH is a water retaining 
hormone. 
 ADH release is controlled by the nervous system 
as a reflex.  For reflex control, we need receptors.  
The receptors that stimulate ADH release are the 
osmoreceptors, located in the hypothalamus of the 
brain. These receptors  sense the osmotic 
concentration of body fluids. If fluids become too 
concentrated, ADH release is stimulated and 
enough water is retained to dilute the fluid to 
normal osmotic concentration.  If fluids become too 
dilute, osmoreceptors signal the brain to inhibit 
ADH release and a diuresis occurs until enough 
fluid is lost to return concentration to normal. 
ALDOSTERONE MECHANISM OF ACTION: 
When blood pressure falls or sodium ion levels fall, the cells near the glomerulus of the 
nephrons called juxtaglomerular cells release the enzyme renin into the blood stream. 
  
The blood contains an inactive liver protein called angiotensinogen. Renin causes it to be 
converted to angiotensin I, a partially active form, by cleaving off most of its amino acids. 
  
As angiotensin I is carried through the lungs by the blood, it is converted to angiotensin II by 
the lung enzyme angiotensin converting enzyme (ACE) on cells lining the capillaries. 
  
Angiotensin II is a potent vasoconstrictor that raises the 
blood pressure by increasing vascular resistance. 
                   
Angiotensin II also stimulates the adrenal cortex to release the 
hormone aldosterone into the blood.   
   
Aldosterone stimulates sodium ion reabsorption in the distal 
convoluted tubules of the nephrons.   
    
As sodium ion is actively transported from the tubules into the 
blood, water follows by osmosis, expanding blood volume.
         
                        SYSTEMIC BLOOD PRESSURE INCREASES TOWARD NORMAL

===== PAGE 37 =====
33 
 Diabetes is a general term referring to 
conditions that cause excessive urine output.  
Diabetes mellitus is caused by lack of insulin, the 
hormone that is needed for glu cose uptake by 
certain cells.  With no insulin, glucose is retained in 
the blood. Excessive amounts then enter the 
nephron to be filtered into the urine. Excess glucose 
is retained in the urine because the glucose T max is 
exceeded. The glucose makes the ur ine 
hyperosmotic and water is retained in the urine by 
the high osmotic pressure.  Those with this type of 
diabetes excrete excess amounts of sweet (mellitus) 
urine containing excess amounts of glucose. 
 Diabetes insipidus  is characterized by 
chronic diure sis due to the failure of the 
hypothalamus and pituitary to produce and release 
ADH. With no water retaining hormone, fluids are 
rapidly lost through the kidney.  The watery urine 
in this situation is bitter (insipidus). The 
dehydration produces a tremendo us thirst and the 
victim is continuously going from toilet to drinking 
fountain.  ADH preparations are available to relieve 
this condition. 
FLUID AND ELECTROLYTE BALANCE 
 Aldosterone and ADH correct for two 
different conditions - dehydration and volume 
depletion.  
 Dehydration is characterized by a loss of 
water that is not matched with a loss of electrolytes. 
Either water is lost without electrolyte loss or is lost 
more rapidly than electrolytes. Examples include 
lack of drinking water, diabetes or severe sweating. 
Excessive water loss leaves an excess of 
electrolytes in the body fluids that remain. That is, 
they become hyperosmotic. This is sensed by 
osmoreceptors that trigger the release of ADH to 
increase water reabsorption from the collecting 
ducts. The  water corrects the dehydration and 
dilutes the electrolytes to normal concentration.  
 Infants are more susceptible to dehydration 
because they have a less well -developed ability to 
concentrate urine and have a greater ratio of body 
surface area to mass compared to adults.   
 Volume depletion  is characterized by 
simultaneous loss of both water and electrolytes. 
Examples are hemorrhage, excessive vomiting, 
diarrhea, or skin loss as a result of severe burns. 
The resulting drop is blood pressure triggers the 
release of aldosterone that directly increases 
reabsorptioin of electrolytes from the distal 
convoluted tubule. Water then follows by osmosis.  
FLUID LOADING DEMONSTRATION 
 In the diagrams below, the upper boxes 
represent the immediate effect of the experi mental 
procedure on extracellular fluids and the lower 
boxes show the results of the osmotic shift of water 
due to the extracellular changes.  White boxes 
represent normal conditions and gray boxes 
represent deviations from normal.  Compartment E 
is the extracellular (interstitial fluid) compartment 
and I is the intracellular (cytoplasm) compartment. 
The height of the box represents fluid 
concentration and the width corresponds to fluid 
volume.  Remember, water movement between 
boxes is caused by osmotic pressure gradients.  
 Hypertonic Loading.   Subjects in this 
group drink about 75 ml of 3% salt solution 
(remember 0.9% is isotonic).  The salt is absorbed 
from the digestive tract into the extracellular fluid 
making it more concentrated ( top figure).  The 
hyperosmotic interstitial fluid causes water to be 
drawn out of the cells by osmosis.  As a result of 
water loss, the volume of the cytoplasm is reduced 
and it becomes more concentrated bottom figure). 
 Osmoreceptors sense the increase in 
concentration of t he cytoplasm.  They signal the 
release of ADH, the water retaining hormone and 
enough water is reabsorbed from the urine to dilute 
the salt and return osmotic balance.  Over the next 
few hours these subjects will notice a decrease in 
urine output. The urin e produced, if any, will 
appear very concentrated with a high specific 
gravity compared to water.

===== PAGE 38 =====
34 
Hypotonic Loading.   Subjects in this 
group rapidly consume about a liter of water.  The 
water is absorbed into the extracellular fluid 
diluting its concentration and expanding its volume 
top figure).  The cytoplasm is now more 
concentrated (hyperosmotic) than the interstitial 
fluid so water is drawn in by osmosis.  The water 
expands the volume of the cell s and dilutes the 
concentration of the cytoplasm bottom figure). 
 Osmoreceptors sense the decreased 
concentration and volume receptors the increased 
volume of the cell s.  ADH is inhibited.  The 
collecting duct remains impermeable to water and 
the water is retained in the nephron and excreted in 
the urine.  Over  the next few hours these subjects 
will notice an increase in urine output and their 
urine will appear very dilute with a specific gravity 
very close to that of water. 
 Beer Loading . This group will consume 
two cans of beer.  Beer is hyperosmotic and that 
would tend to increase ADH release, but two cans 
represent significant volume loading which would 
tend to decrease ADH release. But beer contains 
alcohol and alcohol directly inhibits ADH, which 
in turn causes significant diuresis.  This group will 
produce the largest amounts of very dilute urine. 
ASSESSING KIDNEY FUNCTION 
 It is possible to measure blood and urine 
concentrations of many substances excreted by the 
kidney. Urea and creatinine are two nitrogenous 
molecules excreted in high concentration.  Hig h 
BUN (blood urea nitrogen) levels could indicate 
kidney damage, although urea levels also increase 
in other metabolic abnormalities that cause 
excessive breakdown of proteins. 
Creatinine concentration rarely rises due to 
metabolic diseases other than kidney disease so it’s 
a more reliable marker.  Blood levels of both 
metabolites could increase ten-fold in renal failure, 
the amount of increase being proportional to the 
extent of damage. This condition is called uremia 
and is accompanied with edema and acidosis.  The 
acidosis can lead to coma and death. Thus, 
excreting excess hydrogen ions is an essential 
function of the kidneys. 
 Inulin and PAH (para-amino hippuric 
acid) are molecules that can be administered to a 
patient and later the clearance of these mo lecules 
from the plasma can be measured using blood and 
urine samples to more accurately assess kidney 
function.  Once filtered, inulin is neither reabsorbed 
nor secreted, so it is used to calculate glomerular 
filtration rate.   PAH is also filtered at the 
glomerulus but, unlike inulin, the PAH that is not 
filtered is secreted into the tubules, so that more 
than 90% of that given is excreted.  Its clearance 
can be used to measure total blood flow  to the 
kidney.  
Clearance can be used to tell if a filtered 
molecule is subsequently secreted or reabsorbed. If 
the plasma clearance of a substance is less than the 
glomerular filtration rate (about 125 ml/min in a 
normal adult), then some must have been 
reabsorbed. If the plasma clearance of a substance 
is greater t hat the glomerular filtration rate, some 
additional amount must have been secreted into the 
urine.  
 In pyelography, radio -opaque substances 
are given and allowed to filter into the urine.  X-ray 
images then show the structure of the renal tubular 
system (calyces, pelvis, ureters, urinary bladder). 
 Measuring urine concentration (specific 
gravity) in response to varying water loads is a way 
of assessing the ability of the nephrons to 
concentrate urine, as shown in the demonstration 
described above.

===== PAGE 39 =====
35 
LESSON 12: BLOOD  
  
Blood is a connective tissue composed of 
cells suspended within a liquid intercellular matrix 
called plasma.   
FUNCTIONS 
 Blood provides a means of transport; 
transport for substances required to maintain 
homeostasis, and for transport of waste products. 
 Oxygen is carried to tissues from the lungs and 
carbon dioxide is carried to the lungs from the 
tissues. 
 Nutrients (glucose, amino acids, fats, vitamins, 
minerals, water) are transported to tissue cells. 
 Temperature is regulated by dissipating heat to 
dilated peripheral vessels. Constricting these 
vessels conserves heat. 
 Metabolic wastes are carried to the kidneys 
and passed into the urine. 
 Internal secretions including hormones are 
carried to target tissue. 
Blood helps to maintain homeostasis in other ways 
as well. 
 Normal hydrogen ion concentration (pH) is 
controlled, as blood is one of the body's acid -
base buffering systems. 
 Blood plays an active role in the body's 
phagocytic and immunologic defense 
mechanisms. 
CHARACTERISTICS 
Color: Oxygenated blood is bright red.  Blood low 
in oxygen is a darker bluish red. 
Quantity:  A person of average adult weight has 
about 5 liters of blood. 
Viscosity:  Cells and plasma proteins present in 
blood make it 3 to 4 times thicker than water.  If 
too many cells are present, blood becomes so 
viscous that circulation is impaired. 
Composition: Whole blood is normally composed 
of 52% to 58% plasma and 42% to 48% formed 
elements, the term used for blood cells.  These 
percentages can be determined by calculating the 
hematocrit, a measurement of the percent 
formed elements in blood.  A sample of whole 
blood in a glass capillary tube is centrifuged 
forcing the heavier formed elements to the 
bottom. The ratio of formed elements to total 
volume can then be mea sured.  The example in 
the figure shows a hematocrit of about 42.  That 
is, 42% of the blood 
is formed elements. 
Formed elements 
contain three cell 
types:  
erythrocytes also 
called red blood 
cells, RBCs, or red 
corpuscles, 
leukocytes also 
called white bloo d 
cells or WBCs, and 
thrombocytes also called platelets. 
pH:  Blood is just slightly alkaline; the pH is about 
7.4 with a normal range of from 7.35 to 7.45.  
Maintaining a normal pH is important because 
the biochemical reactions in the body tend to be 
pH dependent. 
ERYTHROCYTES 
 Red blood cells are bi -concave discs, 
thinner at the center than at the edges.  Mature red 
blood cells do not have nuclei. They are only 
slightly smaller tha n the lumen of capillaries and 
must travel through single file. Erythrocytes contain 
the vital molecule hemoglobin, consisting of a 
globular protein portion (globin) and four heme 
groups each able to combine with a molecule of 
oxygen. Oxygenated hemoglobin is referreeed   to  
as oxyhemoglobin. Deoxygenated hemoglobin is 
called reduced hemoglobin.  Cyanosis is the 
slightly bluish color of the skin due to the presence 
of abnormal amounts of reduced hemoglobin. 
Carbaminohemoglobin is hemoglobin with carbon 
dioxide bound to it.  Hemoglobin can also bind 
hydrogen ions, helping to contr ol blood pH.  
Carbon monoxide binds more tightly to hemoglobin 
than oxygen does and so causes oxygen starvation.  
The concentration of hemoglobin is about 14 grams 
per 100 ml of blood in females and 16 grams per 
100 ml in males. 
 Erythrocytes are formed in the hemopoietic 
tissue of the red bone marrow found primarily in 
the spongy bone of ribs, sternum, clavicles, 
vertebrae, skull and coxal (hip) bones.  Diagnostic 
samples of red marrow are normally taken by 
needle puncture from the sternum or coxal bones.

===== PAGE 40 =====
36 
 The precursor cell for all blood cells is the 
hemocytoblast.  In the case of red blood cells, the 
hemocytoblast develops into a rapidly dividing cell 
called the erythroblast.  As the erythroblast 
matures, the nucleus is lost and the cell is no longer 
able to divide.  Red cells released from the bone 
marrow are called reticulocytes. Within a day, they 
become erythrocytes. The presence of reticulocytes 
in the blood indicates active bone marrow.   
 Two B vitamins, vitamin B 12 and folic 
acid, are required for  DNA synthesis in dividing 
cells.  Since red cells divide more rapidly than any 
other cell, the first sign of B vitamin deficiency is a 
fall in red cell count.  The stomach lining contains 
intrinsic factor that promotes the absorption of 
vitamin B12.  The vitamin is stored in the liver and 
carried to the red marrow as needed.  About 2 
million erythrocytes are formed every second.  The 
liver and kidneys produce a hormone called 
erythropoietin that stimulates erythrocyte 
production. On sojourn to high altitud e, for 
example, erythropoietin release stimulates 
production of added red cells to compensate for 
reduced oxygen pressure in the atmosphere. 
 The average lifespan of erythrocytes is 120 
days.  They are destroyed in the hemopoietic tissue, 
liver and spleen.  The iron of hemoglobin is stored 
in the liver and the heme pigments passed into the 
bile as bilirubin and biliverdin for excretion with 
the feces. 
 There are about 4.5 million to 5.5 million 
erythrocytes per cubic millimeter of blood.  Of the 
estimated 75 trillion cells in the adult human body, 
a full 25 trillion are erythrocytes. 
 Anemia is a blood  abnormality associated 
with a decrease in red cell count Pernicious 
anemia is caused by poor B vitamin absorption due 
to lack of intrinsic factor in the stoma ch.  
Inadequate dietary iron causes iron deficiency 
anemia.  Aplastic anemia is caused by red marrow 
failure as can occur from radiation exposure.  
Sickle cell anemia is caused by substitution of 
improper amino acids in the hemoglobin molecules. 
Of the 574  amino acids found in a hemoglobin 
molecule, two are substituted.  The sickle shaped 
cells that have lost their strong biconcave shape, 
hemolyze very easily. Thus, sickle cell anemia is a 
type of hemolytic anemia.   
 Polycythemia is an increase in the 
concentration of red cells above normal.  A 
moderate polycythemia occurs on sojourn to high 
altitude. If polycythemia becomes too severe, 
circulation becomes impaired because the blood 
becomes too viscous.   
LEUKOCYTES 
 Leukocytes are nucleated blood cells.  
They act to protect the body against pathogens, 
foreign substances and tissue damage. Unlike 
erythrocytes, leukocytes can leave blood vessels 
and act directly in damaged or infected tissue.  
White cells are characterized by the unique shapes 
associated with their nucleus and the appearance of 
the cytoplasm which is classified as either granular 
or agranular. 
Granular leukocytes are of three types: 
 Neutrophils make up about 60% of the total 
number of white cells. Their granules stain pink 
in either acidic or alkaline stains.  Neutrophils 
are microphagocytes, engulfing small debris 
and bacterial cells.  Their number increases in 
bacterial infections . D ifferential white cell 
counts are often helpful in verifying the 
presence of an infection. 
 Eosinophils compose less than 3% of the total 
number of white cells.  Their granules stain red 
with acidic stains.  They are involved in 
detoxification of foreign proteins entering 
through the lungs and digestive system a nd 
their number rises in response to allergic 
reactions and parasitic infections.  
 Basophils make up less than 1% of while cells. 
Their granules stain blue with alkaline stains.  
Basophils act by releasing heparin and 
histamine at the site of tissue damage.  
Histamine is a vasoactive agent that increases 
blood flow to the affected area and allows the 
interstitial spaces to fill with fluid.  As the 
fluids accumulate, swelling occurs, helping to 
localize the injury by inhibiting the spread of 
toxins.  Heparin prevents the formation of 
blood clots helping to maint ain a continuous 
blood flow to damaged tissue.  
Agranular leukocytes lack cytoplasmic granules.  
There are two types: 
 Lymphocytes compose about 30% of the total 
number of white blood cells.  Lymphocytes are 
the key players in the more specific 
immunological defenses (the immune system).

===== PAGE 41 =====
37 
Some lymphocytes transform into antibod y 
cells (T Cells) that attack other cells marked 
with foreign antigens. Other lymphocytes (B 
cells) release humoral immunoglobulin proteins 
that act as antibodies against foreign antigens.   
 Monocytes are the largest of the white cells.  
They comprise about 5% of the white cell 
count. They act as macrophagocytes, 
engulfing large amounts of debris, digesting it 
with enzymes  from lysosomes . A thick 
yellowish fluid called pus is an accumulation of 
phagocytic end products digested by 
lysosomes.   
 Leukocytes are formed in red bone marrow 
from the same precursor cell s as the erythrocyte. 
Once released from the bone marrow, lymphocytes 
can also be  produced in organs of the lymphatic 
system. The re are normally 5,000 to 10,000 
leukocytes per cubic millimeter  of blood.  The 
normal life span for granular leukocytes is on the 
order of 10 to 12 hours.  Agranular leukocytes last 
much longer.  Monocytes function for weeks or 
months while lymphocytes can  remain active for 
years. 
 Neutorphils, basophils, eosinophils and 
monocytes are all important mediators of the 
inflammatory response that protects the body, in a 
general way, from a variety of invading pathogens. 
The response is characterized by heat and redness 
due to increased blood flow, edema (swelling) 
caused by increased capillary permeability and pain 
caused by chemicals released from damaged cells. 
Chemicals released by the damaged tissue actually 
attract leukocytes to the area, a process called 
chemotaxis.  Lymphocytes are mediators for the 
immune system to be discussed later. 
 A decrease in the number of circulating 
white cells is called leukopoenia.  It often occurs in 
typhoid fever, measles, mumps, influenza and 
exposure to certain types of radi ation.  
Leukocytosis is an increase in white cell count that 
normally occurs during infections, such as an 
appendicitis or mononucleosis, and represents a 
normal defensive response by the body. 
 Leukemia is cancer of the leukocytes.  In 
myeloid leukemia, the cells are derived from the 
bone marrow. In lymphoid leukemia, they’re 
associated with the lymph nodes. Despite increased 
numbers of cells associated with leukemia, the 
victim's resistance to infection is decreased 
because the cells are both anatomically  and 
functionally abnormal. If the cancerous cells 
proliferate in the bone marrow, production of 
erythrocytes and thrombocytes may be adversely 
affected leading to anemia and bleeding disorders.  
THROMBOCYTES 
 Hemostasis refers to the prevention of 
blood loss.  It is achieved by (1) vascular spasm, (2) 
formation of a platelet plug, (3) blood coagulation, 
and (4) growth of fibrous tissue across the vessel 
rupture.  Thrombocytes play a vital role in 
hemostasis. 
 Thrombocytes, also called platelets, are 
important for repairing the hundreds of small 
ruptures that occur throughout the vasculature each 
day.  They are fragments of large cells called 
megakaryocytes that are, like the other blood cells, 
derived from the hemocytoblasts in red bone 
marrow.  Being cell fragments, they have no nuclei 
and cannot divide.  The life span of a thrombocyte 
is about 10 days.  There are about 150,000 to 
350,000 platelets per cubic millimeter of blood.    
 At the site of a rupture, they physically act 
as plugs and in addition, rel ease serotonin, a 
vasoconstrictor that reduces blood flow in the 
damaged vessel by vascular spasm.  If a vascular 
rupture is too large, the thrombocytes alone cannot 
prevent blood loss.  However, the liver produces a 
group of clotting factors that assist t he platelets in 
the repair process. One of these factors is 
prothrombin.  The synthesis of prothrombin in the 
liver requires vitamin K.  Another factor produced 
by the liver is fibrinogen.  These factors circulate 
in inactive form in the plasma.  Damaged vessels 
release an activator substance  that causes 
prothrombin to be converted to thrombin, an active 
form.  Calcium ions are necessary for this 
activation.  Thus, they, along with vitamin K, are 
necessary for normal blood clotting.  The activation 
of prothrombin ultimately leads to the conversion of 
fibrinogen to fibrin.  Fibrin proteins form long 
strands that intertwine making a meshwork over the 
rupture, trapping platelets and forming a plug. The

===== PAGE 42 =====
38 
clotting mechanism is actually more complex than 
described here and requires many other protein 
intermediates. 
 Clotting abnormalities include hemophilia, 
a genetic disease characterized by lack of one of the 
clotting factors. As a result, vascular injuries 
usually lead to dangerous blood losses.  A 
stationary clot that actually plugs the vessel itself is 
called a thrombus.  An embolus is a wandering 
clot- one that has become dislodged from the vessel 
wall.  If an embolus becomes lodged in another 
vessel it can compromise flow to the affected organ. 
 Just as the b ody provides mechanisms to 
form clots that plug ruptured vessels, it also 
provides mechanisms to prevent unwanted clots 
from interfering with normal blood flow.  Rough 
surfaces stimulate clot formation. The smooth 
endothelial lining of all vessels helps prevent clots. 
 Heparin, an anticoagulant produced by basophils, 
controls clotting in areas of injury.  Another 
mechanism involves an inactive plasma protein 
called plasminogen that, when activated to 
plasmin, helps dissolve clots.  Tissue plasminogen 
activator (TPA) is used clinically to dissolve clots 
in the coronary and cerebral circulations. It is 
administered during an actual heart attack or stroke, 
often providing immediate restoration of blood flow 
to ischemic tissue. 
PLASMA 
 Plasma is the liquid inter cellular matrix of 
blood.  It is composed of water (about 92%), along 
with inorganic salts, hormones, respiratory gases, 
nutrients from digested foods, waste products from 
cellular metabolism and liver proteins.  The most 
abundant protein added by the live r is albumin. 
Albumin aids in maintaining colloidal osmotic 
pressure, the osmotic pressure necessary to prevent 
the water in plasma from escaping into the 
interstitial spaces, leading to edema. Protein carriers 
are needed to transport hydrophobic (lipid-soluble) 
molecules through plasma. Fats absorbed from the 
diet and lipid-soluble hormones are examples. The 
liver also deposits the clotting proteins into 
plasma, including prothrombin and fibrinogen . 
Plasma contains antibody proteins (gamma 
globulins) produced by B lymphocytes when they 
are exposed to certain foreign antigens . Through 
various mechanisms, antibody proteins neutralize 
foreign antigens.   
BLOOD GROUPS 
 Blood types are named for the antigens on 
the surface of the erythrocytes.  Antibodies against 
antigens not found on a person's own red cells 
would be found in that person's plasma.  Exposure 
of blood cell antigens to the antibodies that act 
against them would result in agglutination and 
hemolysis and potentially fatal vascular collapse. 
The most familiar blood group is the ABO group.  
A person’s blood cells can be marked with either A 
or B antigens, both A and B antigens, or neither 
antigen. Normally, types are matched in 
transfusions. In certain instances, a recipient may 
tolerate a transfusion from a donor with a different 
blood type.  In theory, type O is a universal donor 
and type AB is a universal recipient.  While type O 
plasma has antibodies against both A and B 
antigens, when the blood is transfused into a type 
AB person, the donated plasma becomes diluted by 
the recipient’s plasma and damage to the recipient's 
blood may be minimal.  However, upon transfusion 
of AB blood into a type O individual, the antibodies 
in the type O plasma would attack the transfused 
AB blood as it entered because AB blood cells have 
both A and B antigens on them. To ensure 
compatibility, blood samples are always cross -
matched before any is transfused. 
 Blood types are not evenly distributed in 
the population. Most (~47%) are type O, type A is 
~ 41%, type B ~ 9% and type AB is ~3%. 
 Another of the blood groups is the Rh 
group, first discovered in rhesus monkeys.  About 
85% of the human population is Rh positive (Rh+). 
They have no Rh antibodies and can receive either 
Rh+ or RH negative ( Rh−) blood.  For the 15% 
Rh−, if they are exposed to Rh+ blood they will 
develop antibodies against it.  If, for example, an 
Rh− female and Rh+ male conceive an Rh+ child, 
and during the course of pregnancy some of the 
fetal blood mixes with the maternal blood, the Rh− 
mother will develop antibodies against Rh+ blood.  
If in a subsequent pregnancy some of the maternal 
blood containing Rh+ antibodies mixes with the 
Rh+ fetal blood, the baby will have a blood reaction 
called erythroblastosis fetalis, a potentia lly fatal 
blood reaction.  The baby must be transfused with 
Rh− blood to sustain vascular function until the 
anti-Rh+ antibodies become inactive and the 
infant’s own Rh+ blood can function normally.

===== PAGE 43 =====
39 
LESSON 13:  THE IMMUNE SYSTEM 
  
The body has three defense mechanisms to 
protect against harmful pathogens.  The first are 
external barriers and would include the skin and 
mucous membranes that line all body cavities open 
to the exterior. The second line of defense includes 
those internal physiological me chanisms that 
protect us against pathogens, also in a general way. 
It includes phagocytosis, inflammation and fever. 
The above mechanisms are generally effective 
against a wide variety of pathogens. Skin keeps out 
all bacteria and most bacteria are unable to survive 
the higher temperatures associated with fever.  
The third line of defense  is the immune 
system, a line of defense that targets only one 
specific pathogen after a previous exposure to it. 
The system is characterized by the three ‘R’s: 
recognize, react, and remember . The immune 
response is initiated by exposure to antigens that 
are found on the surface of every living cell. 
Antigens are complex molecules unique to each 
person. They can be proteins, polysaccharides, 
glycoproteins or lipoproteins. As  the immune 
system develops, the body learns to recognize its 
own antigens, responding only to foreign antigens.  
Lymphocytes are the key players in the 
immune response. While all lymphocytes originate 
from red bone marrow and migrate to lymphatic 
tissue, they take two different pathways. The T 
lymphocytes pass from the bone marrow to the 
thymus gland, a gland found above the heart in 
infants and children. In the thymus gland, the T 
cells are “tested” and only those T lymphocytes 
that do not react with nati ve cells are released into 
the circulation. As a result of the selection 
process, thousands of T lymphocytes, each pre -
programmed to recognize a different group of 
antigens, are released into the circulation.  B cells 
undergo a similar selection process in the red bone 
marrow and then are passed directly to lymphatic 
tissue- the lymph nodes, spleen, tonsils and other 
lymphatic organs to await future activation.  
CELLULAR IMMUNITY 
 T lymphocytes provide protection by 
attacking cells marked with foreign antig ens 
directly. They have cell adhesion factors that 
allow them to bind directly to the cells to be 
destroyed. T lymphocytes  include cytotoxic T 
cells to carry out the attack, helper T cells to assist 
all other lymphocytes in carrying out destructive 
activities and memory T cells to initiate a quick 
response on future exposure to the specific foreign 
antigen.  
 For T cells to respond, they must first be 
presented with the antigen and be “programmed” 
to recognize it. Key to this process are major 
histocompatability (MHC) proteins  found on 
the surface of cells. There are two types.  
 MHC-I proteins are found on the surface 
of nearly all of our nucleated body cells. They 
display native peptides that identify our cells as 
uniquely belonging to us. T cells would no rmally 
not respond to native peptides displayed by MHC -
I proteins. However, if the proteins are modified 
by exposure to a virus or the result of cancer, a T 
cell response could be initiated.  
 MHC-II proteins are found only on cells 
called antigen presenti ng cells  (APCs), and 
display only foreign antigens. APCs include 
macrophages (phagocytic cells other than 
phagocytic blood cells), B lymphocytes and a few 
other cell types. The mechanism used by APCs to 
obtain and display antigens is as follows: 
1. The foreig n cell is ingested into a n APC by 
phagocytosis.  
2. Lysosomes fuse with the vesicle containing 
the foreign cell.  
3. Lysosomal hydrolytic enzymes then degrade 
the foreign cell. 
4. The antigen is also degraded. 
5. That piece of the antigen the serves as the 
recognition factor (called the epitope of the 
antigen) is carried to the MHC -II protein and 
displayed on the APC surface. 
6. The rest of the degraded cell leaves the APC 
by exocytosis.  
T lymphocytes binding to the MHC -II protein 
of the APC use the displayed epitope to program 
themselves to identify all foreign cells displaying 
the particular antigen on their surfaces. The 
programmed lymphocyte divides producing 
clones, each cloned cell ready to defend against 
cells with that particular foreign antigen.

===== PAGE 44 =====
40 
Cytotoxic T cell s bind to the marked foreign 
cells and deliver a lethal infusion of toxic 
chemicals to destroy it. Helper T cells, once 
programmed by the APC, assist by promoting T 
cell and B cell maturation and participating in B 
cell activation. Memory T cells persist i n the 
immune system and mount an immediate attack 
upon second exposure, usually so quickly that no 
noticeable illness occurs.  
HUMORAL IMMUNITY 
B lymphocytes provide protection 
indirectly by releasing protein antibodies into the 
bloodstream. The process occurs as follows: 
1. Foreign antigens bind to complimentary 
receptors on the surface of B cells. 
2. The antigen is taken into the B cell and 
degraded.  
3. The epitope of the antigen is displayed on the 
surface of the B cells by MHC-II proteins. 
4. A T helper cell binds  to the MCH -protein 
complex and releases interleukin.  
5. Interleukin stimulates the programmed B cell 
to divide repeatedly forming clones. 
6. Some clones become memory B-cells, held in 
reserve for future exposures. 
7. Most clones develop into large plasma cells. 
8. Plasma cells produce and release large 
quantities of antibody protein that render the 
antigen harmless in different ways. 
Plasma cells develop mostly within lymph 
nodes although only 10% remain there. The cells 
remain viable for 4 or 5 days and produce 
antibody proteins at a rate of about 2,000 per 
second throughout their lifetime. That explains the 
cells’ very highly developed rough endoplasmic 
reticulum densely covered with ribosomes. 
ANTIBODY PROTEINS 
The antibody proteins produced by plasma 
cells are ref erred to as immunoglobulins (Ig). The 
most common types are IgE responsible for the 
histamine release that initiates asthma attacks, 
IgM that initiates a relatively weak and delayed 
response on first exposure to a foreign antigen, 
and IgG (80% of immunoglo bulin proteins) 
responsible for the immediate and strong response 
on subsequent exposures. 
Antibodies attack antigens and antigenic 
cells through different mechanisms . They can 
neutralize the antigen by masking its active site. 
They can bind to foreign cel ls, marking them for 
phagocytosis (most common). The y can bind 
foreign cells together, a process called 
agglutination. They can cause free antigen 
molecules to precipitate out and be removed by 
phagocytes.  
IMMUNE SYSTEM PROBLEMS 
 Sometimes, the immune res ponse is 
stronger than expected. This is a hypersensitivity 
reaction. Asthma would be an example. Another 
would be anaphylactic shock  in which allergens 
entering the blood cause constriction of small 
airways, massive vasodilation and severe drop in  
blood pressure. 
 Autoimmune disease refers to the 
production of antibodies against the body’s own 
tissues. This could result from structural changes 
in self -antigens or from cross -reactivity. Damage 
to heart tissue often occurs in rheumatic fever 
when the antibod ies that form to attack the 
bacteria, cross-react with heart tissue. 
 HIV virus attacks T helper cells needed 
for the activation of both T and B lymphocytes. 
ACQUIRING IMMUNITY 
 There are four ways of acquiring 
immunity from foreign pathogens. Two are acti ve 
mechanisms, requiring exposure to a foreign 
antigen. Upon exposure, the body develops an 
antibody against it. Two are passive, in which an 
antibody specific to a particular antigen is 
introduced directly into the body.  
 Natural active immunity occurs w hen the 
body is exposed to a foreign antigen during 
normal activities. Exposure to a virus as a result of 
poor hygiene would be an example. Artificial 
active immunity can be induced by purposely 
exposing body tissue to a non-virulent strain of the 
antigen- getting a flu shot, for example.  
 Natural passive immunity occurs when 
antibodies against foreign antigens are passed 
directly from one person to another. During 
breast-feeding, mother passes protective 
antibodies contained in breast milk directly to her  
infant. In artificial passive immunity, antibodies, 
not antigens, are injected into the body. A tetanus 
shot would be an example.

===== PAGE 45 =====
41 
LESSON 14:  PULMONARY VENTILATION AND PERFUSION 
   
The process of producing ATP within the 
mitochondria by “burning” glucose is referred to as 
internal respiration. Oxygen (O2) is required and 
carbon dioxide (CO2) is produced as a waste 
product. The process of external respiration  
associated with the lungs, provides a means of 
supplying the oxygen and disposing of the carbon 
dioxide.  To accomplish this, three processes come 
together in the lungs:  ventilation (getting gas in 
and out), perfusion (bringing blood through the 
lung), and diffusion (exchanging gases between air 
and blood). 
THE RESPIRATORY TRACT 
In discussing the respiratory tract, there are 
three types of tissue that are of particular 
importance: cartilage for support, muscle to 
control airway diameter, and mucous membranes 
to provide mucus to condition the air and serve as a 
first line of defense.  In the larynx, these tissues are 
also important in production of sound. 
As air enters the nasal passage, mixing 
begins the humidification and warming process. 
The air passes over the warm moist layer of mucus 
produced by the cells lining the respiratory tract. 
The air passes through the pharynx (throat) and 
enters the larynx, the opening to the respiratory 
tract.    
 The larynx is supported by cartilages 
joined with ligaments. The larynx contains the 
vocal cords  formed by tight folds of mucous 
membrane. The slit  between the vocal cords is 
called the glottis.  Sounds are produced by forcing 
air through the glottis causing the vocal cords to 
vibrate. The pitch of the sound is controlled by 
changing the tension on the cords; accomplished by 
moving the cartilages wit h the voluntary skeletal 
muscles of the larynx. The trachea extends down 
from the larynx anterior to the esophagus. It is 
supported by cartilaginous rings that are incomplete 
on the posterior side. They prevent the trachea from 
fully collapsing , yet allow enough flexibility to 
accommodate expansion of the esophagus during 
swallowing.   
 Inferiorly, the tracheal division of airways 
into smaller and smaller branches begins. In the 
first division, two primary bronchi form to ventilate 
the right and left lungs.  In all, there are about 20 
generations of airways finally leading to alveolar 
ducts that open to the terminal air sacs called the 
alveoli.  Cartilage in various configurations is 
found in all but the smallest airways to minimize 
collapse. The cells lining the airways secrete mucus 
that acts not only to warm and moisten air but also 
to trap inhaled particulates. The cells have little 
bristles called cilia on their luminal surface that 
beat in unison, sending the particulate-laden mucus 
up the airways and out of the lung.  
 Smooth muscle in the airways controls the 
diameter and thereby regulates the amount of air 
delivered and the distribution of air. Areas of the 
lung that are poorly perfused will contain reduced 
amounts of CO 2 in their alveoli and airways. 
Reduced levels of CO 2 cause airways to constrict. 
In this way, more of the air is distributed to regions 
of the lung that are better perfused. 
 Alveoli have no cartilage, muscle or mucus 
membranes. They are composed of a single layer of 
flattened cells des igned to facilitate gas exchange 
by diffusion. 
VENTILATION 
 The lungs are located in the pleural cavities 
of the thorax.  These are closed cavities, and in a 
closed system, the product of pressure times 
volume is constant.  Air is brought into the lungs by 
decreasing the pressure below atmospheric.  In a 
closed cavity, increasing the volume has the effect 
of decreasing the pressure.  The primary sets of 
respiratory muscles that contract to increase the 
volume of the thorax are the diaphragm and the 
external intercostal muscles- the muscles of 
inspiration.  The diaphragm is a broad, flat muscle 
that separates the thoracic from the abdominal 
cavity and the intercostal muscles are found 
between the ribs. The negative pressure created by 
contraction of these mus cles draws air into the 
lungs.  Relaxation of these muscles expels gas from 
the lungs.  Forceful expiration can be produced by 
contraction of the muscles of expiration including 
the internal intercostal  muscles and abdominal 
muscles. 
 The expansion of the lungs is facilitated by 
the cohesion of the lungs to the walls of the thorax.

===== PAGE 46 =====
42 
 The lungs are not physically attached to the walls 
of the thorax, but are floating in a thin layer of 
liquid called serous fluid.  The liquid acts as a 
lubricant.  It reduces friction when the outer surface 
of the lung is sliding back and forth against the 
cavity wall. But when the rib cage is moving away 
from the surface of the lungs, serous fluid causes 
the lungs to adhere to the cavity wall, expanding as 
the chest wall expands.  
Respiratory pressures can be  expressed 
relative to atmospheric pressure; negative pressure 
means less than atmospheric and positive means 
greater than atmospheric. As illustrated in the figure 
below, the pressure in the pleural cavity (pleural 
pressure) is negative during quiet breathing. On 
contraction of the inspiratory muscles, it drops from 
about -5 cm H2O to -7.5 cm H2O.  Between breaths, 
when there is no airflow, alveolar pressure is zero.  
Expansion of the chest reduces pressure on 
the outside o f the lung. That, along with 
lung cohesion to the cavity walls, causes the 
lungs to expand. Upon expansion, the 
alveolar pressure becomes about 1 cm H2O 
negative and air rushes in.  Relaxation of 
the inspiratory muscles compresses the 
lungs bringing alveolar pressure about 1 cm 
H2O above atmospheric, forcing air out. 
The elastic nature of lung tissue also aids in 
emptying the lungs.  When the muscles 
relax, elastic recoil of the lung tissue forces air out. 
 The volume of gas exchanged in a quiet breath, 
referred to as the “tidal volume” is about 500 ml. 
 The difference between the alveolar 
pressure and pleural pressure is called the 
transpulmonary pressure.  Before inspiration it's 
about 5 cm H2O and at the end of inspiration it's 7.5 
cm H2O.  The amount of air that can be moved into 
or out of the lungs with a given transpulmonary 
pressure provides a measure of the compliance of 
the lung.  Loss of compliance is associated with a 
stiff lung that requires more work to inflate. In the 
example above, 500 ml was moved in and out of the 
lungs with 2.5 cm H2O (7.5 − 5) giving the normal 
average adult compliance of 200 ml/cm H 2O 
(500/2.5).   
 There are two principle forces that have to 
be overcome to inflate the lungs - elastic tissue 
forces and surface tension forces.  Because the lung 
is air -filled, there is a liquid air interface that 
always implies surface tension. Actually, the 
surface tension accounts for about two-thirds of the 
forces that resist inflation . At the interface, water 
molecules on the surface are more strongly attracted 
to water molecules below than to the air molecules 
above.  As a result, the surface tends to contract, to 
minimize the interface with air, making inflation 
more difficult.  Certain alveolar cells secrete a 
surfactant (surface active a gent) that caots the 
inner surface of alveoli and reduces surface tension. 
The surfactant dipalmitoyl lecithin  not only 
reduces surface tension but also helps stabilize the 
population of different sized alveoli as follows:  
The relationship between pressur e, tension and 
radius is expressed by the Law of Laplace.

43 
All alveoli are not the same size, yet all are exposed 
to the same pressure.  According to the equation 
above, if tension were constant (that is, the same in 
the walls of all alveoli), small alveol i would have 
greater pressure and therefore collapse into larger 
ones.   To prevent this, as radius increases, tension 
would have to increase as well, allowing pressure to 
be the same.  This, in fact, occurs.  As the alveoli 
expand, the layer of surfactant  thins out and the 
surface tension increases.  Another factor that keeps 
alveoli from collapsing is the interdependence of 
lung tissue.  All alveoli are interconnected and help 
support each other. 
 A final note- the chest wall also has tissue 
that needs to be expanded to inflate the lung.  The 
chest wall compliance is about the same magnitude 
as lung compliance: 200 ml/cm H 2O.  Considering 
both compliances, a cm H2O would only move 100 
ml of air into the lung. 
LUNG VOLUMES AND CAPACITIES 
 Lung volumes are e asily measured using 
spirometry.  A spirometer is essentially a canister 
open at the bottom, inserted into a water -filled 
canister open at the top. By inserting a breathing 
tube into the airspace created, inspiration and 
expiration causes the upper canister to move up and 
down. The excursions can be calibrated to provide a 
measure of volume. 
 Total lung capacity  (TLC) is about 5.8 
liters in a normal young adult.  Tidal volume (TV) 
is the amount of air inhaled and exhaled in quiet 
breathing.  It's about 0.5 liters.  The maximum 
amount of air that can be inhaled after a TV 
inspiration is the inspiratory reserve volume  
(IRV).  It's about 3 liters.  The total volume of air 
that can be forcefully exhaled after a TV expiration 
is expiratory reserve volume  (ERV).  It's about 
1.1 liters.   The reserve volumes are available 
during times of stress , including exercise . A 
residual volume (RV), about 1.2 liters, remains in 
the lungs even after a maximal expiration.  
 Lung capacities are combinations of lung 
volumes.  For example, vital capacity  (VC) is 
IRV+TV+ERV and equals about 4.6 liters.  It 
represents the maximum amount of air available for 
ventilation per single breath.  Total lung capacity 
is VC+RV and equals 5.8 liters. 
 The volume of gas in the lungs at the end 
of a quiet expiration when all respiratory muscles 
are relaxed is functional residual capacity (FRC). 
 (FRC=RV+ERV) It's measured by helium dilution; 
breathing a known volume (V) of gas with a known 
concentration (C) of helium added. To calculate, 
initial (i) and final (f) concentrations are measured:                             
                FRC = [(CiHe/CfHe)-1] x Vi 
 
 So, if a 5 liter spirometer begins with 10% 
helium and the concentration drops to 7% after 
equilibration with FRC, FRC would be 2.15 lite rs. 
By subtracting ERV  (determined by spirometry ) 
from FRC, the value of RV can be calculated. 
 While normal TV is about 500 ml, not all 
of that volume reaches the alveoli.  Some fills the 
respiratory dead space.  Dead space volume  
includes the volume of t he conducting airways 
where gas exchange doesn't occur, as well as any 
ventilated alveoli that aren't being perfused.  Dead 
space volume is about 150 ml.  The rest, about 350 
ml, is the alveolar volume- the portion of tidal 
volume that actually participates in gas exchange. 
 Lung ventilation is based both on volumes 
and respiratory rate.  If we assume a respiratory rate 
of 12 breaths per minute, then the total ventilation 
during quiet breathing (called minute ventilation)

===== PAGE 48 =====
44 
would be 0.5 liters/breath times 12  breaths/minute, 
or 6 liters/minute.  But only 350 ml actually 
participates in gas exchange.  Alveolar ventilation 
is 0.35 times 12, or 4.2 liters/minute and dead space 
ventilation is 0.15 times 12, or 1.8 liters/minute.  A 
normal breathing pattern is refe rred to as eupnea.  
Apnea is temporary cessation of breathing.  
Dyspnea is labored breathing. 
 Ventilatory problems are classified as 
obstructive or restrictive. Obstructive lung  
diseases are characterized by difficulty in moving 
air into or out of the lungs.  In asthma, exposure to 
certain allergens can cause sudden and 
disproportionate constriction of airway smooth 
muscle. In bronchitis excessive production of 
mucus can form plugs in airways.  Another example 
is pulmonary emphysema, a condition caused by 
smoking.  Emphysema is associated with a loss of 
elastic tissue and therefore a loss of elastic recoil.  
As a result, increased amounts of air become 
trapped in the lung.  Residual volume increases and 
alveolar ventilation decreases. The increased 
pleural pressure required during expiration to 
compensate for lost elastic recoil squeezes airways 
closed resulting in the obstructive nature of the 
disease. 
 Restrictive lung diseases result in a 
decrease in lung compliance.  The lungs become 
stiff.  Premature in fants born with insufficient 
surfactant suffer from infant respiratory distress 
syndrome (IRDS).  Surface tension causes alveoli 
to collapse after each breath.  Breathing requires a 
great deal of effort because, like a balloon, it is 
difficult to get a col lapsed alveolus to begin to 
inflate.  Loss of compliance due to elastic tissue 
forces would include any of the fibrotic lung 
diseases (pulmonary fibrosis). 
 Persons with obstructive lung disease have 
difficulty moving air through airways. They 
minimize the  respiratory effort by taking deep 
breaths at low frequency. They do this because 
increasing tidal volume increases alveolar volume 
but dead space volume does not change 
significantly. Taking 8 deep breaths rather than 12 
would reduce dead space ventilatio n from 1.8 
L/min to 1.2 L/min (0.15 liters x 8), or 0.6 liters less 
air that has to go through narrowed airways.  
 Those with a restrictive disease have 
difficulty expanding their lungs.  They minimize 
effort by taking shallow breaths at higher 
frequency.  Taking 16 shallow breaths rather than 
12 normal breaths increases dead space ventilation 
from 1.8 L/min to 2.4 L/min.  That's more work 
moving unused dead space air, but at least they 
don't have to expand their stiff lungs as much. 
PERFUSION 
 Half the blood pumped by the heart passes 
to the lungs through the pulmonary artery.  That is, 
the cardiac output of the right and left ventricles is 
the same. The flow to the lungs at rest is therefore 
about 5 liters/min in a normal adult.  The pressures 
and their ch anges during the cardiac cycle on the 
pulmonary side of the circulation are basically the 
same as in the systemic circulation except that the 
magnitude of the pressures is about one -sixth that 
on the systemic side.     
 About 9% of the blood volume is foun d in 
the lungs; that's about 450 ml.  Of that, about 70 ml 
is in the capillaries where exchange of gases occurs. 
 Considering the lung capillaries are approximately 
1500 miles long, one ml of blood is stretched out 
over a distance of about 10 miles, illust rating why 
the lung is such an efficient gas exchanger.  Total 
surface area for gas exchange is about 160 m 2, or 
about the size of a tennis court.   
 Pulmonary circulation is controlled 
neurally and humorally and by autoregulation. 
However, autoregulation is different in the lungs.  
In systemic circulation, hypoxia causes arterioles to 
dilate, allowing more blood to perfuse the oxygen-
poor region. In the lungs, hypoxia causes vessels to 
constrict, decreasing blood flow to that area. For 
the lung, hypoxic areas are the areas that are poorly 
ventilated. Constriction diverts blood away from 
poorly ventilated areas and toward better ventilated 
areas. 
 Finally, gas exchange is not the only 
function of the lungs. Since the pulmonary 
circulation receives such a hig h percentage of the 
blood flow (50%), it is the logical site for locating 
metabolic enzymes.  For example, the enzymes that 
convert angiotensin I to angiotensin II are located 
on the cells lining the capillaries of the lungs.  
Other vasoactive agents found  in the blood are 
metabolized on passage through the lungs.  In 
addition, antioxidant enzymes in the lungs help 
protect the body tissues from the harmful effects of 
excess oxidation.

===== PAGE 49 =====
45 
LESSON 15:  PULMONARY GAS EXCHANGE 
 
DIFFUSION  
 Gas exchange occurs between the air in 
the alveoli and the blood in the pulmonary 
capillaries by simple diffusion.  The process of 
diffusion was discussed in an earlier le sson.   
Recall that the parameters that influence diffusion 
can be summarized by  
 While the solubility coefficient (S), 
temperature (T), and molecular weight of 
respiratory gasses (MW) wouldn't be expected to 
change or change enough to cause diffusion 
problems, the pressure gradient (P 1-P2), 
the surface area (A) of the respiratory 
membrane or its thickness (d), could all 
change sufficiently to impair diffusion in 
lung disease. 
 Note that the gradient for 
diffusion is the difference in the pressure 
of a given gas across the membrane.  The 
pressure exerted by any one gas in a 
mixture of gases is called the partial 
pressure of the gas.  In air, it is simply the 
percent concentration times the total gas 
pressure.  For example, if air is 21% 
oxygen and the total atmospheric pressure 
is 760 mmHg, then the PO 2 (partial pressure of 
oxygen) is 760 mmHg × 0.21 or 160 m mHg.  The 
PCO2 of air is negligible. In alveolar gas, the PO 2 
is reduced to about 104 mmHg and the PCO 2 is 
increased to 40 mmHg.  This is because the CO 2 
in the blood perfusing the alveoli is continuously 
diffusing into the alveoli and O 2 is continuously 
being carried away.  Also, the water vapor 
pressure increases from about 3 mmHg in air to 47 
mmHg in alveoli.  This is because inspired gas is 
warmed to 37oC and saturated with water vapor as 
it passes over the moist membranes of the airways. 
   Expired gas PO2 is about 120 mmHg and 
PCO2 is 27 mmHg because expired gas is a 
mixture of alveolar gas and air from the anatomic 
dead space.  Expired gas is 100% saturated with 
water vapor. 
 Blood leaving the lungs through the 
pulmonary capillaries is completely equi librated 
with alveolar gas.  That is, the PO 2 is 104 mmHg 
and the PCO 2 is 40 mmHg.  By the time the blood 
passes through the pulmonary veins to the left 
heart, the oxygen PO 2 has already fallen a bit, 
partly because some low O 2 blood that does not 
come in contact with pulmonary capillaries is 
added.   Systemic arterial PO 2 is normally about 
95 mmHg.  As it passes from systemic arteries 
through the systemic capillaries, oxygen is 
removed and carbon dioxide added. This is 
reflected in the mixed venous blood v alues, blood 
that ultimately will be returned to the lungs  
through the pulmonary artery.  The mixed venous 
(and pulmonary arterial) PO2 is reduced to 40 
mmHg and the PCO2 is increased to 45 mmHg. 
THE RESPIRATORY MEMBRANE 
 The barrier for diffusion consists  of the 
tissues of the capillary and alveolar walls.  
Together, these tissues are called the respiratory 
membrane.  Specifically, moving from air to 
blood, the gases must cross a layer of surfactant, the 
alveolar epithelium consisting of a single layer of 
flattened epithelial cells, an epithelial basement 
membrane, a layer of interstitial fluid, the capillary 
basement membrane and the capillary endothelium 
also composed of a single layer of flattened cells.  
The total thickness of the respiratory membrane 
averages about 0.6 microns and is as thin as 0.2 
microns in places.  The capillary diameters average 
about 8 micro ns so red cells must actually move 
through single file, providing easy access for gases. 
 In the normal adult lung there are about 
300 million alveoli, each covered with a network of 
capillaries.  The total surface area of all respiratory 
D  (P1 – P2)  S  T A
MW  d

===== PAGE 50 =====
46 
membranes combined is about 160 meters 2, about 
the size of a tennis court.  
FACTORS AFFECTING DIFFUSION 
 The parameters that affect diffusion are 
found in the equation discussed above.  Several can 
change in lung disease and result in diffusion 
impairment.  One factor that doesn't change but is 
important is the solubility coefficient.   Carbon 
dioxide is about 20 times more soluble in plasma 
than is oxygen.  The refore, for all practical 
purposes, the factors described below will only 
produce a noticeable effect on oxygen diffusion. 
 The greater the pressure gradient for 
oxygen, the greater the rate of diffusion.  
Hemoglobin helps to maintain the pressure 
gradient by acting as a "sink" for oxygen in the 
alveolus. That is, as soon as oxygen enters the 
plasma and diminishes the pressure gradient, 
hemoglobin removes it from the plasma, restoring 
the gradient.  This continues until hemoglobin is 
saturated. If less hemoglobin is available (anemia, 
for example) the hemoglobin saturates sooner and 
less oxygen can enter the plasma before the 
gradient disappears.  
 The shorter the distance, the greater the 
rate of diffusion.  Pulmonary edema, the 
accumulation of fluid between the cell layers of the 
respiratory membrane, increases the thickness and 
decreases the rate of diffusion.  Fibrotic lung 
diseases result in deposits of fibrous tissue on the 
membrane, increasing thickness and decreasing 
diffusion rate.   
 The rate of dif fusion is directly 
proportional to the total cross sectional area.  Lung 
diseases that would require surgical removal of lung 
tissue would decrease the rate of diffusion by 
reducing surface area.  Certain lung diseases like 
emphysema cause deterioration of  alveolar 
membranes and reduction of surface area.  But it is 
the mismatch of ventilation and perfusion that is the 
usual cause of lost functional surface area.  That is, 
portions of the membrane that are ventilated but not 
perfused or perfused but not ven tilated, cannot 
exchange gas.   
 While only small adverse changes in the 
respiratory membrane are required to impair 
performance during exercise, as much as 25 to 35% 
of the membrane could be lost before a deficit 
would be noticed at rest.  Similarly, the membrane 
can undergo considerable thickening before the 
ability to transfer oxygen at rest would be impaired. 
DIFFUSING CAPACITY 
 Perfusion and ventilation are regulated so 
that the body receives exactly as much oxygen as it 
requires.  At rest, the requir ement is about 230 
ml/min.  The ability of the lungs to exchange 
oxygen can be quantified using the diffusing 
capacity measurement.  Diffusing capacity (DL) is 
the amount of gas exchanged per minute (D) per 
mmHg of pressure gradient PA – Pc bar.   
 For oxygen, the amount transferred at rest 
is about 230 ml/min, and the PO2 difference is 
about 11 mmHg.  That is, on average, the PO2 in 
the alveoli is only 11 mmHg higher than that in the 
capillary blood (refer to the graph below). Dividing 
230 ml/min by 11 mmHg gives the normal resting 
diffusing capacity for oxygen of 21 
ml/min/mmHg.   
 The resting diffusing capacity for oxygen 
does not represent the maximum rate at which the 
lung can exchange oxygen.  During severe exercise, 
the lungs can increase oxygen de livery by more 
than ten-fold.  As we shall see about 97% of the 
oxygen carried out of the lungs is transported by 
hemoglobin, and when hemoglobin leaves the lungs 
it is 100% saturated with oxygen.  Therefore, to 
increase oxygen delivery by ten times requires that 
more hemoglobin be transported to the lungs and/or 
that more oxygen be bound to the hemoglobin 
 - 
 
DL =

===== PAGE 51 =====
47 
molecule.  During exercise, blood flow to the lungs 
(cardiac output) can increase by at least four times 
to 20 L/min. (Resting cardiac output is 5 L/min. )  
To accommodate the increased flow, additional 
capillaries in the lung open up and ventilation 
increases to bring air to the newly opened 
capillaries.  Additionally, increased oxygen demand 
by the tissues means that the hemoglobin returning 
to the lung is less saturated with oxygen than at rest, 
so more oxygen can be added to each available 
hemoglobin molecule.  The effect is that the 
diffusing capacity for oxygen increases to about 65 
ml/min/mmHg in strenuous exercise.  
  Under resting conditions, the PO2 of blood 
entering the lung is about 40 mmHg at rest and the 
PO2 of blood leaving the lung is equal to that of 
alveolar gas; about 100 mmHg. This is shown in the 
figure below. We can’t measure PO2 at 
intermediate points, but two possible alternatives 
are presented in curves A and B. If PO2 increased 
linearly over the entire range, as in curve B, the 
average capillary PO2 would be 70 mmHg.
