[(40+100)/2]  But, if DL is 21 ml/min/mmHg, and D 
is 230 ml/min and P A is 100 mmHg,  then the 
average PO2 must be (100 -11) or 89 mmHg. The 
average PO2 for curve A is 
 
 [(40+100+100+100)/4] = 85 
This is much closer to the expected value of 89 
mmHg. Therefore, the average must be weighted 
toward higher values; meaning blood PO2 must 
approach 100 mmHg very rapidly. 
 According to the figure, by the time blood 
has traveled about a third of the distance through 
the pulmonary capillaries, it is already fully 
equilibrated with oxygen at rest.  This means that 
during exercise when blood flow increases and 
blood spends less time in contact with the 
respiratory membrane, there is still ample time to 
fully equilibrate the blood with alveolar gas.   
 The figure also says something about 
diffusion impairment caused by damage to the 
respiratory membrane. As the damage progresses, 
curve A begins to shift gradually toward curve B. If 
it reaches curve B, then diffusion capacity would be 
about 8 ml/min/mmHg, about 1/3 the normal value. 
And yet, according to the figure, the blood at the 
outlet would still be full equilibrated with oxygen. 
That is, at rest, the victims would not exhibit any 
shortness of breath. But they would have noticed a 
gradual decline in the ability to exert themselves 
until, when curve B is reached, any exertion at all 
will lead to shortness of breath. Lung disease 
caused by smoking, for example, goes completely 
undetected until its later stages in persons who are 
not very active.  
 Hemoglobin saturates with oxygen, so any 
additional oxygen that may be available in the 
alveoli can only be transferred to plasma. And 
oxygen is  very insoluble in plasma. Whereas 
oxygen carrying capacity of the blood is limited by 
the amount of hemoglobin available, carbon dioxide 
is transported in such a way that the more CO 2 
available, the more the blood can carry.  So besides 
being 20 times mor e soluble than oxygen, CO 2 
transport is not carrier limited.  This is another 
reason why diffusion impairment does not affect 
the exchange of CO 2.  Oxygen transport, on the 
other hand, is blood flow dependent. To increase 
oxygen delivery, blood flow must b e increased to 
deliver more and more  unsaturated hemoglobin 
molecules.

===== PAGE 52 =====
48 
LESSON 16: TRANSPORT OF OXYGEN AND CARBON DIOXIDE 
 
OXYGEN TRANSPORT 
 Oxygen is transported in the bloodstream in 
two ways.  More than 97% of the oxygen is carried 
as ox yhemoglobin. Oxygen binds with the iron 
molecule found on the heme groups of the 
hemoglobin molecules within the red blood cells.  
Each molecule of hemoglobin has four iron -
containing heme groups; each iron is able to bind a 
single oxygen molecule. A molecule of hemoglobin 
can therefore carry four oxygen molecules when 
fully saturated.  Less than 3% of oxygen is 
transported in blood as oxygen dissolved in the 
plasma. 
 Oxygen binds somewhat loosely to the 
hemoglobin.  At a P O2 of about 100 mmHg, the 
hemoglobin is fully saturated with oxygen.  As the 
PO2 falls, so does the percent saturation of 
hemoglobin.  That is, in the lungs where the PO2 is 
high (the result of ventilation), oxygen binds to 
hemoglobin and in the body tissues where the P O2 
is low (the resul t of consumption of oxygen by 
mitochondria), hemoglobin releases oxygen.   
 The relationship between PO2 and 
saturation of hemoglobin with oxygen is depicted in 
the curve below - the oxygen -hemoglobin 
dissociation curve.  Note the hemoglobin saturation 
at two important P O2 values. At 95 mmHg, the 
normal P O2 of systemic arterial blood, the 
hemoglobin is nearly 100 % saturated.  At 40 
mmHg, the normal P O2 of mixed venous blood at 
rest, the saturation is still 75%.  This is another 
oxygen reserve the body can rely upon during times 
of increased activity. Note that the PO2 has to fall 
below 60-70 mmHg before significant unloading of 
oxygen from the blood occurs. 
 On average, 100 ml of blood contains 15 
grams of hemoglobin (in males, closer to 16 
gm/100 ml, in fem ales closer to 14 gms/100 ml), 
and a gram of hemoglobin holds 1.34 ml of oxygen 
when fully saturated. Arterial blood hemoglobin is 
about 97% saturated with oxygen so 
    Bound = 15 gm/100ml × 1.34 ml/gm × 0.97 
                = 19.5 ml/100 ml of blood 
That is, every 100 ml of systemic arterial blood 
contains about 19.5 ml of oxygen.    
 Oxygen is not very soluble in plasma.  The 
solubility coefficient is 0.003 ml of oxygen/100 ml 
of blood/mmHg.  The PO2 of arterial blood is about 
95 mmHg so 
Dissolved = 95 mmHg x 0.003 ml/100ml/mmHg 
                 = 0.28 ml/100 ml of blood 
The total amount of oxygen carried in arterial blood 
is 19.8 ml/100 ml blood.  How much oxygen is 
carried in venous blood if the PO2 is 40 mmHg and 
the hemoglobin saturation is 75%? Subst ituting 
0.75 for 0.97 in the “bound” equation above gives 
15.07 ml/100 ml. Substituting 40 mmHg for 95 
mmHg in the “dissolved” equation gives 0.12 
ml/100 ml. The total carried in mixed venous blood 
is 15.2 ml/100 ml. Therefore, on average, the 
tissues consumed 4.6 ml of oxygen from every 100 
ml of blood. If 100 ml is only 1/50th the total blood 
flowing through the capillaries per minute (cardiac 
output is 5000 ml/min), then the total amount of 
oxygen consumed per minute is 4.6 x 50 or about 
230 ml/min.  Thi s was the value we used in 
calculating diffusing capacity earlier.  
 During exercise, the P O2 can easily fall to 
15 mmHg in mixed venous blood.  Using the above 
calculations, you would find that about three times 
as much oxygen is extracted from each 100 m l of 
blood during exercise.  When you consider that 
cardiac output can also increase by 3 or 4 times, 
oxygen delivery can easily accommodate a ten-fold 
increase in oxygen consumption during exercise, as 
we assumed in calculating the diffusing capacity 
during exercise. Yet, during exercise, delivery of 
oxygen by the blood is normally the limiting factor.

===== PAGE 53 =====
49 
CARBON DIOXIDE TRANSPORT 
 Carbon dioxide is 20 times more soluble in 
plasma than is oxygen . C arbon dioxide is 
transported in different ways than oxygen and 
within the ranges of CO2 found in the body. Blood 
can accept all the CO 2 released from the 
mitochondria.  For oxygen, once hemoglobin is 
saturated, very little additional oxygen can be 
transported.  For these reasons, CO 2 is held in the 
plasma at lower pressures and diffusion impairment 
is not a problem. 
 Carbon dioxide is added to the blood in the 
systemic tissue capillaries.  The main source of CO2 
in tissues is the mitochondria where CO 2 is 
produced as a waste in the oxidation of glucose.  
Because of con tinuing mitochondrial production, 
the PCO2 within the tissue cells is about 45 mmHg 
while the PCO2 in arterial blood entering the tissue 
capillaries is 40 mmHg.  As the blood passes from 
artery to vein, it equilibrates with the tissue value 
and increases t o 45 mmHg.  The amount of CO 2 
added under resting conditions is about 4 ml/100ml 
of blood.  Recall that the amount of O 2 consumed 
was nearly 5 ml/100ml of blood.  The ratio of CO 2 
produced to O 2 consumed is 1.0 only if 
carbohydrates are used as the "fuel" source.  Since 
we normally also use fats and proteins in addition 
to carbohydrates, the ratio becomes less than 1.0.    
 About 7% of the CO2 added to the blood is 
dissolved in plasma.  Another 16% is carried bound 
to hemoglobin.  It does not bind to the heme groups 
but rather to the globular protein "globin" groups.  
The combination is referred to as 
carbaminohemoglobin.  Even though oxygen and 
carbon dioxide are carried on different parts of the 
hemoglobin molecule, the binding of one displaces 
some of the other.  So in the tissues, the binding of 
carbon dioxide produced by the mitochondria helps 
to free the oxygen needed by the mitochondria.  In 
the lungs, binding of inspired oxygen helps free 
carbon dioxide to be exhaled as waste. 
 Most (about 77%) of the CO2 produced by 
the cells is transported back to the lungs in the form 
of bicarbonate ion due to the reaction of CO 2 with 
water.  The reaction is 
        CO2 + H2O  H2CO3   HCO3- + H+ 
The enzyme carbonic anhydrase facilitates this 
reaction. The enzyme is found in the erythrocytes.  
So, from the body tissues, the CO2 diffuses into the 
plasma and then into the erythrocytes.  Within the 
erythrocytes, the CO2 is first converted to carbonic 
acid (H2CO3) and then to bicarbonate ion (HCO 3-) 
and hydrogen ion (H +). The hydrogen ion then 
binds to hemoglobin to keep the cells from 
becoming acidic and the bicarbonate ion diffuses 
back into the plasma.  This keeps the concentration 
of hydrogen ions and bicarbonate ions very low 
within the cytoplasm of the erythrocyte.  According 
to the law of mass action, the low concentration of 
product favors formation of more product. The 
above reaction is forced to the right.  To maintain 
the charge distribution, negatively charged chloride 
ions diffuse from the plasma into the ery throcytes 
to replace the negatively charged bicarbonate ions.  
 
 
 
When the blood reaches the lungs, these reactions 
quickly reverse, forming CO 2 again which is then 
passed to the alveoli.   
 The reaction demonstrates the correlation 
between CO 2 concentration and hydrogen ion 
concentration. The respiratory system can be used 
to help maintain normal hydrogen ion 
concentration. If hydrogen ion concentration 
becomes too high (a condition called acidosis), 
increasing ventilation will blow off CO 2 and less 
will be available to react with water to form 
hydrogen ions. If hydrogen ion concentration 
becomes too low (alkalosis), decreasing ventilation 
will allow CO2 to build up in the blood and it will 
react with water to form hydrogen ions. It also 
explains how respi ratory diseases that affect 
ventilation can lead to imbalances in hydrogen ions. 
Hypoventilation, for example, leads to acidosis.

===== PAGE 54 =====
50 
LESSON 17:  CONTROL OF VENTILATION  
 
Respiratory control involves adjusting 
ventilation so that the rate of gas exchange  in the 
lungs matches the rate of gas exchange in the 
tissues, maintaining normal steady -state arterial 
PO2 and PCO 2 values.  In addition, ventilation is 
adjusted to achieve adequate gas exchange using 
the minimum amount of work.   
WORK OF BREATHING 
 At re st, less than 5% of the oxygen 
consumed is used to meet the energy requirements 
of the respiratory system itself. During exercise, 
the oxygen requirement of the respiratory system 
can increase more than 50-fold.  In physical terms  
Work = Force × Distance 
or 
Work = Pressure × Volume 
 In the respiratory system, work (W) can be 
described graphically by plotting pleural pressure 
(P) with volume (V) as shown in the figure below.  
Pleural pressure can be measured by measuring 
pressure in a balloon passed into th e esophagus. 
The pressure in the esophagus is equal to the 
pressure in the pleural (chest) cavity. The slope of 
the curve is lung compliance (C), equal to about 
200 ml/cm H2O in an average adult.   
 
 
The area shaded in gray represents the 
work required to overcome the elastic and surface 
tension forces of the lung tissue.  This is called the 
compliance work or elastic work, W el.  The area 
below the compliance curve  (white) is the work 
required to overcome airway resistance (R). 
Resistance work is designated W R.   To calculate 
the total cost of breathing, work is multiplied by 
breathing frequency (f) to give W dotel and W dotR 
the rate of work.   From the graph you can see that  
From the above equations, note that the rate of 
elastic work changes exponentia lly with volume 
(V2 above) and linearly with frequency (f above). 
The work of breathing for persons with restrictive 
lung disease can therefore be minimized by 
choosing a breathing pattern with a low volume 
and high frequency. 
Compare with the rate for re sistance 
work:  
Resistance work changes exponentially in 
response to changes in rate of air flow  (V dot 2), 
and the greater the breathing frequency, the 
greater the flow rate that’s required.  That is due 
in part to the volume of dead space that remains 
relatively constant irrespective of tidal volume 
changes.  So doubling frequency would double 
flow to the dead space but increase dead space 
resistance work four-fold.   The work of breathing 
for persons with obstructive lung disease can 
therefore be minimize d by choosing a breathing 
pattern with a low frequency and high volume.

===== PAGE 55 =====
51 
In the figure above, note that both the 
resistance work and compliance work change 
exponentially in response to changes in breathing 
frequency and tidal volume.  
We breathe with a freq uency and a 
volume that minimizes the total work rate of 
breathing yet still provides the required alveolar 
ventilation.  The nervous system controls alveolar 
ventilation almost exactly to match the demands of 
the body.  The rate of oxygen diffusion across  the 
respiratory membrane is equal to the body's demand 
for oxygen (about 230 ml/min in the average adult, 
at rest).   
 Neural control of respiration is centered in 
the lower brain and brain stem.  This is the location 
of control mechanisms for all basic d rives that are 
vital to life including respiration, cardiac activity, 
thermoregulation, appetite and thirst and so on.  
Specifically, the "respiratory center" is found in the 
lowest part of the brain stem, the medulla 
oblongata.  Here an inspiratory center  generates 
"ramp" signals, gradually increasing inspiratory 
drive and gradually filling the lungs.  At rest, when 
the ramp signal terminates, the lungs empty 
passively.  During forceful breathing, a separate 
medullary expiratory center stimulate s active 
exhalation of gas from the lungs.  In the area above 
the medulla oblongata called the pons, there is a 
pneumotaxic center that controls the cut -off point 
for inspiration. Strong signals from the 
pneumotaxic center inhibit the inspiratory ramp and 
result in shallower but more rapid breathing. 
 Three stimuli that act to stimulate 
respiration are:  
1. Hypoxia, a fall in PO2 
2. Hypercapnia, an increase in PCO2 
3. Acidosis, a decrease in pH; increase in [H+] 
Below is the reflex arc for correcting hypoxia, 
hypercapnia or acidosis. 
CENTRAL CHEMORECEPTORS 
 The levels of hydrogen ion and P CO2 are 
sensed by central chemoreceptors located in close 
proximity to the respiratory centers themselves.  
These receptors are in contact with cerebral spinal 
fluid (CSF), not blood.  As CO 2 passes easily into 
the CSF, it is converted to HCO 3- and H + in a 
reaction described earlier.  The H + levels are then 
sensed by the receptors. The central 
chemoreceptors respond to very small changes in 
[H+] and P CO2, adjusting ventilation accordi ngly. 
Because equilibration between blood and CSF is 
required for central chemoreceptor stimulation, they 
do take longer to respond. 
 PERIPHERAL CHEMORECEPTORS 
 Oxygen levels are controlled by peripheral 
chemoreceptors.  The peripheral chemoreceptors 
are f ound in the carotid and aortic bodies.  The 
carotid bodies are small clusters of receptor cells 
located near the right and left common carotid 
arteries in the upper part of the neck.  The aortic 
bodies are similar cell clusters near the arch of the 
aorta just above the heart.  Sensory impulses from 
the carotid bodies are transmitted to the respiratory 
centers by fibers of the ninth pair of cranial nerves, 
the glossopharyngeal nerves.  The aortic bodies are 
innervated by the tenth pair of cranial nerves, the  
vagus nerves.  
 The peripheral chemoreceptors sense 
oxygen pressures in arterial blood directly.  P O2 
isn't controlled within as narrow limits as P CO2 is.  
That is, a small decrease in P O2 doesn’t result in a 
compensatory change in breathing pattern. The 
reason is related to differences in the way oxygen 
and carbon dioxide are carried in the blood.  The 
oxygen-hemoglobin dissociation curve is very flat 
when P O2 is above 70  to 80 mm Hg, as seen 
previously.  That is, changes in PO2 at these higher 
oxygen pressures in systemic arterial blood do not 
translate to significant changes in oxygen content 
of the blood. This is not true for CO 2.  Even small 
changes in P CO2 cause significant changes in the 
quantity of CO 2 that can be carried in the blood.  
The peripheral chemoreceptors don't exert much of 
an effect on breathing until arterial PO2 falls below 
70 mmHg. 
 While the peripheral chemoreceptors also 
respond to P CO2 and pH changes, the central 
chemoreceptors don’t allow levels of these

===== PAGE 56 =====
52 
molecules to change enough to reach the threshold 
of the peripheral receptors.  
Hyperpnea is defined as increased 
breathing to meet an increase in metabolic demand. 
By contrast , hyperventilation  is increased 
ventilation without a need.  In the latter, systemic 
arterial PCO2 levels fall significantly.  The most 
immediate effect is to constrict cerebral blood 
vessels. The victim feels lightheaded and may pass 
out. The fall in P CO2 can be countered by 
rebreathing CO2 (rebreathing from a paper bag, for 
example). Hypoventilation refers to an inadequate 
breathing pattern. As a result, PCO2 increases above 
normal ( hypercapnia) and PO2 may fall below 
normal (hypoxia). Obstructive and restrictive lung 
diseases are not the only causes of hypoventilation. 
Paralysis of respiratory muscles, chest  wall 
problems related to trauma or obesity, neurological 
damage, and anesthesia are other examples of 
possible causes.  
 It is important to note that it is the oxygen 
partial pressure (PO2) that is sensed and controlled, 
not oxygen content.  And P O2 values reflect only 
the dissolved oxygen.  Recall that even under 
normal conditions breathing air, less than 3% of the 
oxygen content is dissolved in plasma.  Cutting off 
ones air supply results in violent attempts to get air 
as PO 2 drops significantly . On the other hand, 
carbon monoxide poisoning, which affects only the 
ability to bind oxygen to hemoglobin but doesn't 
affect the oxygen dissolved in plasma (or P O2), is 
so dangerous because oxygen content falls to 
deadly levels without any stimulation of the 
nervous system to increase ventilation.  Arterial 
PO2 remains normal.  
Is the design of the control system flawed? 
 Without hemoglobin available, oxygen can only be 
carried dissolved in plasma. One would have to 
breathe pure oxygen at 3 atmospheres of pressure to 
dissolve sufficient oxygen in the plasma to meet 
resting metabolic needs. Increasing vent ilation 
would serve no purpose since the PO2 of pure air is 
only about 150 mmHg.   
OTHER RECEPTORS 
 Breathing is not controlled only by 
chemoreceptors.   Hold your breath as long as you 
can.  Then exhale into a paper sack and breathe in 
the exhaled gas. Clearly, the stimulus to the 
chemoreceptors that caused you to take a breath has 
not changed.  You inhaled the same air you 
exhaled. Yet you can hold your breath fo r a time 
before you have to take a second breath. Some 
other type of receptor must have been satisfied by 
taking the first breath.  
 Examples of mechanical receptors that may 
have been satisfied are the stretch receptors in the 
chest wall and lung.  Hering-Breuer receptors, as 
they’re called, are stretched as the lung inflates.  At 
a certain tension, they send impulses to the 
respiratory center to terminate the inflation, 
preventing the lungs from over-inflating. As stretch 
decreases to a certain point duri ng deflation, the 
receptors stimulate initiation of the next breath. It is 
activity in these  receptors that signal the 
respiratory centers that a breath has been taken 
and allows a pause to occur before the next breath.

===== PAGE 57 =====
53 
LESSON 18: ACID-BASE BALANCE  
 
The mechanisms that regulate acid -base 
concentrations represent another example of 
physiologic control to maintain homeostasis.  Acids 
are substances that ionize in water to produce 
hydrogen ions.  Bases act to remove them thus 
lowering hydrogen ion concentration.  
The normal concentration of hydrogen ions 
in blood is about 0.00000004 Eq/L.  Although 
small, the enzymatic reactions of physiological 
systems require a hydrogen ion concentration close 
to that value.  It is more convenient to express 
hydrogen ion concentration in pH units.  The pH is 
the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion 
concentration.  For example, a hydrogen ion 
concentration of 0.00000001 Eq/L could be 
expressed as 1.0 × 10-8 Eq/L, meaning that 
0.00000001 would become 1.0 if the decimal point 
was moved to the right by 8 places.  The logarithm 
of 10-8 is -8 and the negative logarithm is 8.  If pH 
is 7, that means the solution has 0.0000001 Eq/L of 
hydrogen ions or ten times more.  Each change of 1 
in pH represents a tenfold change in hydrog en ion 
concentration.   
The possible range for pH is 0 to 14. A pH 
of 7 represents the pH of pure water at room 
temperature and is considered neutral. Solutions 
with higher hydrogen ion concentration than water 
have a pH less than 7 and are acidic. Solutions with 
a lower concentration of hydrogen ions than water 
have a pH higher than 7 and are alkaline or basic. 
The normal hydrogen ion concentration of 
blood expressed in pH units is 7.4 with an 
acceptable range between 7.35 and 7.45. If pH 
falls outside thi s range, the biochemistry of the 
body is adversely affected. The most vulnerable is 
the central nervous system. The condition of too 
many hydrogen ions (lower than normal pH) is 
called acidosis and suppresses the nervous system 
leading ultimately to coma. The condition of too 
few hydrogen ions (higher than normal pH) is 
alkalosis that over -stimulates the nervous system 
leading to convulsions. 
A base is a compound that can neutralize 
an acid by binding up free hydrogen ions. An acid-
base buffer is a solution  of two or more 
compounds that prevents marked changes in 
hydrogen ion concentration when either an acid or 
base is added. The buffer system produces 
hydrogen ions when needed and removes them if 
they become excessive. The body has three 
important buffer s ystems to control pH: protein 
buffers in blood, the phosphate buffer system and 
the bicarbonate buffer system. 
The first line of defense is the protein 
system. Recall how erythrocytes, for example, are 
able to bind hydrogen ions on hemoglobin 
molecules. Many other negatively charged plasma 
proteins can also bind or release positively charged 
hydrogen ions to control free hydrogen ion 
concentration. 
The phosphate buffer system consists of 
phosphate ions HPO 4= and H 2PO4-.  If hydrogen 
ions are in excess, they  are combined with the 
divalent negatively charged ions forming the 
monovalent ion and removing a hydrogen ion (H+). 
If hydrogen ions become depleted, the reaction 
reverses.  
       HPO4= + H+    H2PO4-           (1) 
The phosphate buffer system is most 
important for acid -base balance in kidney tubules 
and inside body cells (cytoplasm pH). 
Outside of cells, the bicarbonate buffer 
system is more important. In the bicarbonate 
system, bicarbonate ion and carbonic acid act as the 
buffer pair. The advantage of t he bicarbonate 
system is that the concentration of the buffer pair 
can be controlled by both the lungs and kidneys. 
Acidosis means there are too many 
hydrogen ions. The kidneys respond by increasing 
the secretion of hydrogen ions and ammonia into 
the urine , and by increasing the reabsorption of 
sodium bicarbonate into the blood as summarized in 
the following chemical reactions. 
                   H+ + NH3     NH4+                   (2) 
where NH3 is ammonia and NH4+ is ammonium ion. 
Here, both hydrogen ion and ammonia are 
secreted into the urine.  By combining to form 
ammonium ions, they become trapped in the urine 
because ammonium ion cannot be reabsorbed back 
into the blood.

===== PAGE 58 =====
54 
Also in acidosis,  
                    H+ + HCO3-    H2CO3              (3) 
where H 2CO3 is carbonic acid and HCO 3- is 
bicarbonate ion. In this reaction the hydrogen ion is 
no longer free.  It has combined with bicarbonate 
ion and no longer contributes to the acidosis.  
Carbonic acid can be broken down to carbon 
dioxide and water.  The lungs help drive the 
reaction by increasing ventilation to get rid of the 
CO2 (product). 
                 H2CO3    CO2 + H2O                (4) 
 To correct alkalosis, secretion of hydrogen 
ion by the kidney is inhibited and less bicarbonate 
is reabsorbed.  In the lungs, ventilation decreases 
and carbon dioxide accumulates, reacts with water 
and produces hydrogen ions. 
     CO2 + H2O    H2CO3    HCO3- + H+   (5) 
 The law of mass action between reactant 
and product is at work determining which 
direction a reaction proceeds. Reactions move in a 
direction away from reactants in high 
concentration and toward reactants in low 
concentration. By controlling the levels of 
appropriate reactants, the body determines in 
which direction reactions will occur. 
ACID-BASE ABNORMALITIES 
 Deviations from normal pH are categorized 
as acidosis or alkalosis. Each can be of respiratory 
or metabolic origin. 
Hypoventilation due to pathologic 
conditions interfering with the ability to breathe 
(such as emphysema, asthma, nerve damage) leads 
to an accumulation of carbon dioxide in the blood 
that reacts with water as in reaction (5) above to 
produce hydrogen ions. This is a respiratory 
acidosis. In respiratory acidosis, the systemic 
arterial blood PCO2 would be above the normal 40 
mm Hg. To deal with the excess in hydrogen ions, 
the kidney would raise blood levels of bicarbonate 
above the normal level of 24 mEq/L. 
During hyperventilation that might occur 
at high altitude, where oxygen in the atmosphere 
is "thinner", the situation is  reversed.  To get an 
equivalent amount of oxygen, ventilation is 
increased.  But because carbon dioxide production 
is the same as at low altitude, increased breathing 
lowers blood carbon dioxide below normal levels 
and reduces hydrogen ion concentration as in 
reactions (3) and (4) above. The result is a 
respiratory alkalosis .  In respiratory alkalosis, 
the systemic arterial blood PCO 2 would be below 
the normal 40 mm Hg. To deal with the loss of 
hydrogen ions, the kidney would lower blood 
levels of bicarbonate below the normal level of 24 
mEq/L. Lower bicarbonate means more hydrogen 
ions. 
 Compensation for imbalances of 
respiratory origin is accomplished by the blood and 
renal buffer mechanisms described above.   
Imbalances of non -respiratory origin are 
termed metabolic.  Here, the respiratory system can 
also participate in compensating for metabolic 
imbalances. 
 Acidosis of non-respiratory origin is termed 
metabolic acidosis.  An example is the acidosis of 
diabetes mellitus.  Keto-acids accumulate as a result 
of excessive fat metabolism needed to produce ATP 
by cells that can't absorb glucose because of a lack 
of insulin. In metabolic acidosis, the systemic 
arterial bicarbonate levels would be below the 
normal 24 mEq/L, being consumed by the excess 
numbers of hydrogen ions. To deal with the excess 
in hydrogen ions, the lungs would increase 
ventilation, blowing off more CO 2, lowering the 
blood PCO2 below the normal level of 40 mm Hg. 
This favors reactions (3) and (4) above. 
 Metabolic alkalosis  is alkalosis of  non-
respiratory origin.  An example would be alkalosis 
caused by loss of stomach acid that occurs during 
excessive vomiting. In metabolic alkalosis, the 
systemic arterial bicarbonate levels would be 
above the normal 24 mEq/L, there being fewer 
hydrogen io ns available to react with it. To deal 
with the reduction in hydrogen ions, the lungs 
would decrease ventilation, building up more CO 2 
in the blood, increasing the blood PCO 2 above the 
normal level of 40 mm Hg. This favors reaction 
(5) above. 
 Compensation of abnormalities by blood 
buffers is rapid, occurring in seconds.  
Compensation by the lungs occurs within minutes, 
providing the abnormality is not of respiratory 
origin.  Renal compensatory mechanisms take hours 
to develop.

===== PAGE 59 =====
55 
 
LESSON 19:  ORGANIZATION OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 
 
Communication and integration of 
activities within the body are controlled by two 
systems; the nervous system and endocrine 
system.  The nervous system transmits information 
electrically, rapidly and along specific pathways.  
The end ocrine system uses chemicals (hormones) 
that are distributed ubiquitously and more slowly 
(limited by the speed of the flowing blood).  
DESIGN OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM 
 Nerve cells that are able to conduct nerve 
impulses are called neurons.  There are about 1 00 
billion in the adult nervous system.  They generally 
have multiple dendrites that receive information 
from sensory receptors or other neurons, and 
transmit the information to effectors or other 
neurons through a single axon that may have 
multiple terminal branches enabling a neuron to 
stimulate many target cells at once.  A single 
neuron may receive input from several hundred to 
many thousands of other neurons.  Neurons can be 
divided into three groups - sensory neurons, motor 
neurons and interneurons.  
 Sensory neurons conduct impulses from 
receptors to the central nervous system (CNS). 
Sensory neurons are also called afferent (Latin: 
carry to) neurons. Receptors are of five basic types.  
Electromagnetic (photo) receptors sense light. 
Chemoreceptors respond to chemical stimulation. 
Thermoreceptors respond to temperature changes. 
Mechanoreceptors respond to mechanical events. 
Pain receptors respond only to painful stimuli. 
 Motor neurons transmit impulses from the 
CNS to effectors.   Since the impulses are being 
carried away from  the CNS, motor neurons are 
also called efferent (to carry away) neurons.  The 
effectors that carry out the "will" of the CNS are 
muscles and glands.  Skeletal muscles are 
controlled by somatic neurons, neurons of the 
voluntary nervous system.  Cardiac muscle, visceral 
muscle and certain glands are under the control of 
autonomic neurons, neurons of the involuntary 
nervous system.   
 Interneurons are neurons that are found 
"between" sensory and motor neurons or other 
interneurons in th e CNS.  Sensory information is 
handled in different ways by the interneurons. 
Probably about 99% of the sensory impulses are 
filtered out (ignored) as they make their way 
through interneuron pathways. That includes 
information from external receptors - the five 
senses.  If the nervous system had to respond to all 
stimuli, it would be completely overwhelmed. So 
most of what you are able to sense never reaches 
your normal level of consciousness.   
 Some sensory information is directly 
transmitted to motor neurons to elicit an immediate 
response.  Pulling away a finger on touching a hot 
stove or increasing heart rate in response to a fall in 
blood pressure are examples.  When a stimulus 
elicits an immediate response, the behavior is called 
a reflex response.   
 Some sensory information is stored in 
memory to be acted upon at some later time (or 
perhaps never to be used). The hallmark of the 
human brain is its ability to not only store 
information, but to combine bits of information 
gathered at different times and  from various 
receptors to produce a "reasoned" output at the 
appropriate time. 
THE CENTRAL NERVOUS SYSTEM 
 The most "primitive" level of nervous 
system control is at the level of the spinal cord.  
Many reflexes involving skeletal muscles will occur 
even if the cord is severed from the brain.  Tapping 
the knee will elicit the reflex kicking of the lower 
leg; the knee-jerk reflex.  The reflex may disappear 
following spinal transection, but within a few 
weeks, it returns.  Reflex tests like the knee jerk are 
routinely used to assess the integrity of the spinal 
cord.  Some autonomic reflexes are rooted in the 
spinal cord. 
 The next level of control is exerted by the 
brain stem and cerebellum.  Autonomic activities 
controlled subconsciously are centered here.  T he 
brain stem controls activities that are basic to life.  
Cardiovascular and respiratory reflexes already 
discussed, are centered here.  Thirst, appetite, 
control of body temperature, sexual cycles, centers 
invoking aggression and submission are all found in 
the brain stem.  The cerebellum coordinates skeletal 
muscle activity, specifying which muscles must 
contract and which relax and by how much, to 
coordinate complex movements.

===== PAGE 60 =====
56 
 
 The cerebral cortex of the cerebrum is 
the highest level and is best developed in the human 
brain. It is the cortex that stores information 
(memory) and acts on it when and if it needs to, 
giving rise to thought processes that allow us to 
reason and solve problems.  The cortex and lower 
brain centers work together. The brain stem 
activates the cortex or can deactivate it (as in a 
coma).  The cortex, in turn, makes the crude control 
mechanisms of the brain stem work with much 
greater precision. 
SYNAPSES 
 One of the key features of the nervous 
system is its ability to evaluate sensory information 
and determine how it should respond, if at all.  
Neurons fire in all or none fashion.  There are no 
weak or strong action potentials.  The ability to 
"grade" information is unique to the synapse.  It is 
there that all -or-none information from  as few as 
several hundred or as many as 100,000 separate 
presynaptic neurons is combined in an attempt to 
make a single postsynaptic neuron fire.    
 We will consider the most common type of 
nervous system synapse; the chemical synapse that 
uses small mo lecule, rapid acting chemical 
transmitter substances.  (There are electrical 
synapses such as those between adjacent cardiac 
muscle cells (intercalated disks) and smooth muscle 
cells. There are also large molecule, slow acting 
transmitters in the CNS but t hey behave more like 
hormones and will be discussed later.   Some of the 
two dozen or so small molecule transmitters act to 
cause action potentials in the postsynaptic cell. 
Others act to inhibit action potential generation.   
 A good example of a small mo lecule rapid 
acting transmitter is acetylcholine.  It is synthesized 
in the synaptic vesicles within the axon terminus.   
Depolarization of the axon causes calcium ions to 
pass into the axon tip from the interstitial fluid.  
Through some poorly understood process, the 
calcium ions enable vesicles to fuse with the cell 
membrane of the presynaptic axon at the synapse 
and release the acetylcholine by exocytosis.  The 
transmitter binds to appropriate receptors on the 
postsynaptic membrane, but is rapidly metabolized 
by the enzyme acetylcholinesterase that is 
produced by the postsynaptic cell.  The choline can 
be transported back into the axon vesicles and 
reconverted to acetylcholine.  The vesicles can 
detach from the cell membrane and be used again.  
The whole process described above occurs in a 
millisecond time frame, allowing neurons that use 
this type of transmitter to fire at very high 
frequencies. 
 To understand how transmitter substances 
are able to stimulate postsynaptic cells, we need to 
recall the normal resting electrochemical gradients 
in excitable tissues.  Sodium ions are distributed 
mainly in the interstitial fluid outside the cell while 
potassium ion is in highest concentration inside the 
cell.  These two ions are the ones most involved in 
the decision making process at the synapse.  Due to 
the abundance of negatively charged proteins 
trapped inside the cell, the inside of the cell is 
electrically more negative in charge than the outside 
so an electrochemical gradient exists.  
 To generate an action  potential, the 
membrane must allow positive charges to enter, 
causing it to depolarize (reverse its polarity).  
When the membrane is suddenly made permeable 
to sodium ions, they flood into the cell, drawn in 
down both the electrical and chemical gradients .  
The inside becomes more positive and that has an 
excitatory effect. Excitatory chemical transmitters 
generate action potentials by binding to receptors 
on ligand gated sodium channels, allowing sodium 
ion to diffuse freely into the cell.  Acetylcholine 
and norepinephrine are examples of excitatory 
transmitters. 
 If the membrane became more permeable 
to potassium ion or chloride ion, which direction 
would the ions move?  The chemical gradient 
favors movement of potassium ion to the outside 
and chloride io n to the inside.  The electrical 
gradient favors movement in the opposite direction. 
   Applying the Nernst equation (studied 
earlier) to these two ions, one can determine that 
the ions would in fact move down their chemical

===== PAGE 61 =====
57 
 
gradients.  Potassium ion would  carry its positive 
charge out of the cell and chloride ion would carry 
its negative charge into the cell.  Both processes 
would make the inside of the cell more negative.  
This would act to inhibit the generation of an action 
potential.  When the inside of a cell becomes more 
negative than normal, the cell is said to be 
hyperpolarized.  Inhibitory transmitter substances 
bind to potassium ion or chloride ion ligand gated 
channels, making the membrane more permeable to 
the respective ion, and therefore more difficult to 
stimulate.  Glycine and gamma-amino butyric acid 
(GABA) are examples of inhibitory transmitters. 
 So how is the synaptic "decision" made?  
Recall that from hundreds to tens of thousands of 
presynaptic neurons are synapsing with the single 
postsynaptic cell.  About 80% synapse with the 
postsynaptic cell ’s dendrites and 20% with the 
cell’s soma (body). A single presynaptic excitatory 
neuron couldn't generate an action potential.  A 
minimum of 100 or so different excitatory neurons 
have to fire simultaneously to excite the cell.  This 
is called spatial summation.  Some excitatory cells 
may fire at high frequency, making them more 
effective in exciting a cell.  This is called temporal 
summation.  Meanwhile, inhibitory presynaptic 
cells may be stimu lated and, exhibiting spatial or 
temporal summation, oppose the effect of the 
excitatory cells.  So if the action potential is to be 
generated, more excitatory cells will be needed. 
 The average CNS neuron has a resting 
potential of about -65 millivolts. T his is less 
negative than the -90 millivolt potential of muscle 
cells and peripheral neurons discussed earlier.  The 
reason for the difference is to provide the CNS with 
the option of inhibiting nerve activity using 
hyperpolarization; that is, bringing the  membrane 
potential below the normal resting potential, 
therefore making it more difficult to reach 
threshold.  Recall, the lower limit for membrane 
potentials based on the Nernst equation for 
potassium ion is about -94 millivolts. So 
hyperpolarization wouldn’t be possible if that was 
the normal resting membrane potential.   
 When a single presynaptic axon changes 
the membrane potential at the synapse, that change 
is conducted throughout the cell. This is why spatial 
summation is possible. The excitatory pr esynaptic 
cells together must increase the potential above a 
certain threshold to initiate an action potential.  The 
threshold for most cells is about -45 millivolts.  
When the threshold is reached, the ligand gated 
sodium channels will suddenly open and t he cell 
will depolarize.  Most of these sodium channels are 
located at the axon hillock, so most action 
potentials will begin at the hillock and not at a 
particular synapse.  Again, this is because the 
change in potential at a given synapse is conducted 
throughout the neuron cytoplasm. 
 Potential changes that make the inside less 
negative are called excitatory postsynaptic 
potentials (EPSP's).  Those that make the potential 
more negative (for example a drop from -65 
millivolts to -70 millivolts) are called inhibitory 
postsynaptic potentials (IPSP’s). 
 Inhibition using IPSP's is called 
postsynaptic inhibition.  It occurs rapidly and for 
short duration.  There is another type of inhibition 
called presynaptic inhibition .  In this case, 
inhibitory neurons act by decreasing the amount of 
excitatory transmitter released by the presynaptic 
neuron.  They do so by making the presynaptic 
neuron less permeable to calcium ions.  Remember 
calcium ions are necessary for fusion of vesicles 
with the synaptic membrane.  Presynaptic inhibition 
occurs more slowly but can be sustained for long 
periods of time. 
 Many factors affect the excitability of 
neurons.  Alkalosis increases excitability while 
acidosis decreases excitability.  Drugs such as 
caffeine increase excitability whi le anesthetics 
decrease excitability.  Lack of oxygen due to loss of 
cerebral blood flow can lead to unconsciousness in 
just seconds.

===== PAGE 62 =====
58 
 
LESSON 20:  SPECIAL SENSES: VISION    
 
The receptors of the eye are photo 
receptors that respond to stimulation by l ight. 
However, most eye structures are not involved with 
responding to light  stimuli but rather in 
conditioning the light stimuli before they are 
sensed. 
The outer portion of the eye is composed of 
three tissue layers, the outer sclera, the middle 
choroid and the inner retina.  The outer protective 
layer is the white fibrous sclera.  Anteriorly, the 
sclera becomes the translucent cornea.   
 The middle layer is the choroid, black in 
color because it absorbs light of all visible 
wavelengths. Light that has a lready stimulated the 
receptor cells is absorbed allowing the image on the 
retina to continuously “refresh”.   Anteriorly, the 
choroid forms the iris and ciliary body, structures 
that surround the lens of the eye.  The iris and 
ciliary body are intrinsic (internal) eye muscles.  
The ciliary body is attached to the edges of the lens 
by suspensory ligaments.  The iris extends down 
in front of the lens. The choroid is not a complete 
layer.  A hole in the center of the iris, called the 
pupil, allows light to enter the eye.  The choroid is 
a very vascular layer providing blood to the inner 
layer of receptor cells.  The inner tissue layer is 
called the retina.  This layer contains the actual 
photoreceptor cells of two types, rods and cones, 
covered by layers of con ducting nerve cells 
described below.  The retina is an incomplete layer, 
covering only the back two-thirds of the eyeball.   
ACCOMMODATION 
 As parallel rays of light from distant 
sources pass through the biconvex lens of the eye, 
they are refracted or bent  such that the rays will 
converge with their focal point falling on the fovea 
centralis of the 
retina.  A convex 
lens that is able to 
focus parallel light 
one meter beyond 
it, has a refractive 
power of 1 diopter. 
 The retina of the 
eye is only about 
17 millimeters from 
the lens.  The refractive power of the lens is 
therefore 59 diopters for parallel rays of light from 
distant sources.  But light rays originating close to 
the eyes are diverging as they reach the lens.  If the 
lens were unable to increase its  refractive power, 
the focal point would fall behind the retina.  The 
image hitting the retina would be blurred.  The 
ability to correct this problem is referred to as  
accommodation. Increasing the refractive power 
requires a convex lens with greater curva ture.  To 
focus distant objects, the muscles of the ciliary 
body are relaxed and the diameter of the ciliary 
body is maximal.  This puts tension on the 
suspensory ligaments, causing the lens to flatten. 
For near vision, the ciliary muscles contract, 
decreasing the diameter of the ciliary body and 
relaxing the suspensory ligaments.  This allows the 
lens to bulge, increasing the refractive power 
appropriately.   
 Emmetropia refers to the ability to 
accommodate correctly.  In hyperopia or far -

===== PAGE 63 =====
59 
 
sightedness, the  eyeball is too short and the 
refractive power has to be increased by the ciliary 
muscles, even for distant objects.   Even maximal 
contraction fails to converge light from near objects 
sufficiently. Glasses with biconvex lenses are 
required to increases the convergence of light rays 
before they enter the lens.   
 In myopia or near-sightedness, the eyeball 
is too elongated and the focal point for distant 
objects falls in front of the lens.  Since the lens 
lacks the ability to cause light to diverge, distant  
objects appear blurred.  Glasses with biconcave 
lenses are required to diverge the light rays before 
they enter the eye.  As one ages, the ability to 
accommodate is lost.  This is called presbyopia.  
Bifocal lenses are then required to correct for both 
near and far vision. 
 Cataract is a condition of the lens that 
often occurs as part of the aging process. The lens 
becomes cloudy, opaque and distorted causing 
blurred vision and possible blindness. It can be 
corrected by removing the lens and replacing it with 
an artificial implant.  
 The other intrinsic eye muscle associated 
with the choroid layer is the iris.  The radial and 
circular muscles of the iris regulate the size of the 
center opening called the pupil.  Contraction of 
radial fibers increases the size of the pupil allowing 
more light to pass onto the retina.  Contraction of 
the circular fibers decreases the size of the pupil 
reducing the amount of light entering the eye.  
GLAUCOMA 
 The anterior cavity of the eye (in front of 
the lens) is filled with aqueous humor, a watery 
nutrient solution that nourishes the outer lens and 
inner corneal surfaces.  Aqueous humor is produced 
continuously by the ciliary body, enters the anterior 
cavity between the lens and iris, flows centrally 
from behind the iris, then through the pupil, finally 
moving peripherally in front to the iris toward the 
scleral venous sinus (canal of Schlemm) near the 
edges of the cornea where it is returned to the 
venous circulation.  The fluid pressure of aqueous 
humor is about 15 mmHg.  If  aqueous humor is 
produced faster than it is reabsorbed, it accumulates 
in the anterior cavity, increasing the intraocular 
pressure.  This condition is called glaucoma.  The 
increase in pressure is reflected into the posterior 
cavity (behind the lens), fil led with a more 
gelatinous substance called vitreous humor .  
Unlike aqueous  humor, it is not produced 
continuously. The fairly non-compressible vitreous 
humor is pushed against the posterior of the eye, 
squeezing blood vessels and compromising blood 
flow to the receptor cells.  Receptor cells starved of 
nutrient may die, causing permanent blindness. 
THE RETINA 
 The light-sensitive photo-receptors are the 
rods and cones.  They are in the inner-most layer of 
cells, the retina, lying just in front of the choro id 
layer.  The elongated cells consist of an outer 
segment containing a pigment that reacts 
chemically when stimulated by light rays.  The 
pigment molecules impregnate stacks of 
membranes that form the outer segment.  In cones, 
the membranes taper toward t he tip of the cell, in 
rods they don't.  About 40% of the outer segment is 
visual pigment.  In rods, the visual pigment is 
retinal combined with a protein called scotopsin.  
The combination is called rhodopsin.  When the 
rhodopsin molecules are stimulated by light, they 
undergo a step-wise degradation back to retinal and 
scotopsin.  This chemical decomposition occurring 
in the cell membrane stimulates the receptor cell. 
 Unlike other neurons, nerve cells of the 
retina conduct impulses by hyperpolarization 
rather than depolarization.   The outer segment of 
the receptor cells is more permeable to sodium ion 
than the typical neuron. Therefore, the resting 
membrane potential is a less negative than the 
typical neuron.  The breakdown of rhodopsin makes 
the membra ne less permeable to sodium ion, 
making the inside of the cell more negative rather 
than less negative.  This is hyperpolarization.  The 
advantage is that this hyperpolarization mechanism 
can be graded corresponding to stimulus intensity 
whereas the action  potential resulting from 
depolarization is all or none.  Rods are adapted for 
seeing in dim light, but can't see in color. In 
addition, rods do not provide good visual acuity. 
 Cones also contain the visual pigment 
retinal, but the protein scotopsin is re placed by 
proteins that react with specific wavelengths of 
light.  Some cones respond to shorter wavelength 
blue light, some to intermediate wavelength green 
and some to longer wavelength red.  Intermediate 
colors result from partial stimulation of differe nt 
cone populations that the brain can interpret as

===== PAGE 64 =====
60 
 
colors resulting from a mixture of red, blue or 
green. The concept is illustrated in the figure below. 
Partial or differential stimulation is possible 
because of hyperpolarization. Looking at the 500 
nanometer point on the graph below, if the blue and 
red cones are hyperpolarized at 25% maximum and 
green cones at 75% maximum, the brain perceives a 
color having a 500 nanometer wavelength. In 
addition to seeing in color, cones provide greater 
visual acuity. To accomplish this, however, they 
require a significant amount of light. Cones do not 
do well in dim light.    
 Rods and cones are able to adapt to 
different light intensities by converting between the 
active pigment retinal and the inactive form called 
retinol, also called vitamin A .  In bright light, 
over-stimulation of rods would produce a 
continuous bright flash against the retina, 
overwhelming the receptors.  To prevent this, 
significant amounts of retinal are inactivated by 
conversion to retinol.  The eye has undergone light 
adaptation.  Now suppose you enter a dark movie 
theater from bright sunlight.  At first, the room is 
totally dark and you are unable to determine where 
the empty seats are located.  Within a very short 
time, the eyes undergo dark adaptation.   Stored 
retinol is converted back to retinal and rhodopsin is 
formed.  Soon you can see exactly where everyone 
is seated.   
 As you leave the theater and enter bright 
sunlight, your eyes are over-stimulated to the point 
that even with constric ted pupils it is necessary to 
squint or close your eyes because of the brightness. 
 Soon sufficient retinal is deactivated by conversion 
to retinol, and your eyes are light adapted again.  
Adaptation makes it possible to see from extremes 
of dim starlight to bright sunlight, a range in 
intensity of about 10 billion-fold. 
 Overlying the rods and cones are layers of 
other neuronal cells including bipolar and ganglion 
cells.  The ganglion cells are the ones that 
ultimately transmit action potentials to the bra in.  
The intermediate cells make it possible to 
precondition the stimuli before they are passed to 
the brain.  Interconnections through stimulatory and 
inhibitory synapses make it possible to amplify the 
stimulus.  On average, a single ganglion cell 
receives stimuli from 60 rods or 2 cones.  This 
pattern of innervation explains why rods are better 
suited for dim light but have poor er visual acuity 
compared to cones . In dim light, an individual 
ganglion cell has as many as 60 rods “searching” 
for a light impulse. However, when stimulated, the 
ganglion cell can’t determine which rod was 
responsible. It’s like painting with a broad brush. 
The picture lacks detail.  
 If a ganglion cell is stimulated by a cone, it 
can pinpoint the source because there are only on e 
or two cones that could be responsible. A detailed 
picture emerges. However, in dim light there is little 
chance that a sufficient number of individual cones 
will be stimulated  in order to project a detailed 
image. 
 Intermediate cells also enable the eye  to 
detect changes in the visual field (detect 
movement), enhance edges for greater visual acuity 
and perceive color.  Light must pass through these 
inner layers of cells to reach the rods and cones 
except in the fovea centralis.  This area, less than a 
square millimeter, contains only cones with no 
overlaying cells and is the area of greatest visual 
acuity. The focal point, referred to in the discussion 
of accommodation, is the fovea centralis. It is 
marked with a yellow spot called the macula lutea.  
 Macular degeneration , an age -related 
condition, is an impairment of this most sensitive 
part of the retina. It is associated with blurred 
central vision and gradual loss of vision beginning 
centrally.  A detached retina , due to injury or 
irregularity of protein fibers attaching the retina to 
the pigment layer below, can survive for up to 
several days. However, if not repaired surgically, it 
will become permanently damaged.

===== PAGE 65 =====
61 
 
CENTRAL PROCESSING OF VISION 
 An optic nerve is attached to the back of 
each eye. The n erve is sensory only, carrying 
afferent impulses for the receptor cells of the eye. 
At the point where the optic nerve is attached, there 
are no overlying rods or cones. This location on the 
retina is called the optic disk or blind spot.  
 At the base of the brain, optic nerve fibers 
innervating the medial half of each retina crossover. 
This is called the optic chiasma.  From the optic 
chiasma, optic tracts lead to the visual cortex of 
the brain, located in the posterior part of the 
cerebral cortex.  Damag e to an optic nerve affects 
vision to the eye that the nerve innervates.  Damage 
to an optic tract affects vision in the lateral half of 
one eye and the medial half of the other eye.   
 The partial crossover of the optic nerves 
makes sense when one conside rs how images are 
projected onto the retina. From the above figure, 
one can imagine an object positioned to the left of 
the eyes, for example, and note that the image 
would fall on the right side in both retinas and be 
sensed by the right cerebral cortex.  Some believe 
that this may explain why it would be most 
practical for the right side of the cerebral cortex to 
control the muscles on the left side of the body. 
 Visual impulses are carried to the primitive 
visual system in the brain stem.  While the brai n 
stem structures are not able to provide conscious 
vision, they do control some of the subconscious 
reflexes associated with vision such as reflex eye 
movements associated with head movement,  
avoidance reflexes and other tracking reflexes.  For 
example, the eyes can remain focused on an object 
even if the head is moving.  As the head moves to 
the left, the eyes move to the right.           From the 
brain stem structures, impulses are conducted to the 
primary visual areas in the occipital (posterior) 
lobes of the cerebral cortex of the cerebrum. It is 
there that the impulses are translated to conscious 
visual images and then passed to surrounding 
association areas of the cortex for interpretation.  It 
is in the association areas that a face is associated 
with a person, that scenery is associated with a 
particular location. 
EXTERNAL EYE MUSCLES  
 Eye movements are provided by extrinsic 
voluntary skeletal muscles, six for each eye, that 
move the eyeball within its socket.  These muscles 
make it possible to cha nge the visual field by 
changing eyeball position without having to move 
the head. The muscles move the eyes so that the 
light rays brought to a focal point by the lens, fall 
directly on the fovea centralis, the area of greatest 
visual acuity. 
DEPTH (DISTANCE) PERCEPTION 
 We can judge depth or distance in three 
ways.  First, if we are familiar with the size of an 
object, we can judge its distance based on the size 
of the image on the retina.  Secondly, we judge 
distance by the phenomenon of moving parallax.  
Even if a close object moves only slightly, it will 
still sweep across a large portion of the retina.  If a 
distant object moves the same amount, its image on 
the retina will hardly move at all.  By evaluating the 
perceived movement, we can judge distanc e.  The 
third mechanism is by stereopsis- binocular vision. 
Because the eyes are over two inches apart, they 
don’t “see” the same images.  The closer the object, 
the more different the image.  Interpretation of the 
magnitude of the difference enables us to  interpret 
distance.  Stereopsis works best for near objects.

===== PAGE 66 =====
62 
 
LESSON 21:  HEARING, TASTE AND SMELL
 
THE EXTERNAL AND MIDDLE EAR 
 Sound begins as vibrations of air collected 
by the external ear flap (auricle), channeled down 
the ear canal (external audi tory meatus) to the 
tympanic membrane, commonly called the eardrum. 
The external ear conducts sound vibrations using 
air as the medium.   
 The chamber on the inner side of the 
tympanic membrane is the middle ear.  Three small 
bones in each ear, referred to as ossicles, conduct 
sound waves through the middle ear.  Thus, in the 
middle ear, sound is conducted as vibration of solid 
bone.  The handle of the first ossicle (called the 
malleus) is seated against t he inner side of the 
tympanic membrane.  The opposit e end of the 
malleus is connected to the second ossicle called 
the incus, which in turn connects to the last ossicle, 
the stapes.  The lever arrangement and structure of 
the ossicles acts to intensify the force of the 
vibrations.  This is necessary because  the receptor 
cells in the inner ear are in a liquid environment (a 
requirement for living cells).  Since liquid has more 
inertia than air, conduction from air to liquid 
requires more force.  The force of the stapes against 
the oval window (the opening to the inner ear) is 
more than 20 times greater than the force generated 
at the tympanic membrane.   
 Muscles attached to the ossicles are able to 
reduce the force of sound vibrations.  These 
muscles contract reflex ively in response to loud 
noises.  This hel ps to protect the delicate inner ear 
structure from excessively forceful vibrations. 
 For the tympanic membrane to vibrate 
optimally, the pressure on both sides must be equal. 
 A narrow tube called the auditory (Eustachian) 
tube runs from the middle ear to  the pharynx 
(throat) at the back of the nasal passages.  This tube 
allows the middle ear to equilibrate with 
atmospheric pressure.  
THE INNER EAR 
 The inner ear contains receptors for both 
hearing and balance.  The receptors for balance will 
be covered later.  The inner ear structure containing 
the receptors for hearing is called the cochlea.  The 
cochlea and the other inner ear structures are 
embedded in the temporal bones of the skull.  The 
temporal bones form the central portion of the sides 
and floor o f the cranial cavity.  When the stapes 
pushes inward against the oval window, the fluids 
in the cochlea are not compressed, however.  This 
is because there is another opening between the 
cochlea and middle ear called the round window.  
When the oval window  is pushed inward, the 
membrane over the round window is pushed 
outward (and visa versa). 
 The cochlea is essentially three parallel 
tubular chambers coiled up like a snail.  The oval 
window opens into the upper chamber called the 
scala vestibuli. A thin membrane called Reissner’s 
 membrane separates the scala vestibuli from the 
middle chamber, the scala media (also called the 
cochlear duct).  A much thicker membrane called 
the basilar membrane separates the scala media 
from the lower channel called the scala tympani.  
The scala tympani and scala vestibuli are filled with 
a fluid called perilymph, a fluid derived from, and 
very similar to, cerebral spinal fluid, the fluid found 
in and around the brain and spinal cord. The scala 
media contains endolymph produced in the scala 
media itself. Endolymph is unique in that it contains 
high concentrations of potassium ions.  It is thought 
that the high potassium environment helps to 
increase the sensitivity of the receptors for hearing. 
 Reissner’s membrane serves onl y to 
separate the perilymph from the endolymph and 
offers no barrier for sound wave conduction.  The 
basilar membrane is directly involved in sound 
wave conduction. Fibrous rods, attached only at one 
end, extend through the tissue of the basilar 
membrane from one side to the other.  At the base 
of the cochlea, near the oval window, the fibers are

===== PAGE 67 =====
63 
 
short and thick.  Moving toward the tip of the 
cochlea, they gradually become longer and thinner. 
The resonant frequency of the membrane thus 
becomes lower as one moves from base to tip.  
While the sound vibrations at a given pitch are 
conducted through the endolymph all along the 
scala media, only the segment of basilar membrane 
with the same frequency will begin to vibrate. Each 
frequency therefore stimulates a group of receptors 
at a different place (place principle) on the basilar 
membrane, enabling the brain to sense different 
pitches of sound. 
 The receptors for hearing are found on the 
basilar membrane in the scala media.  The receptor 
is called the organ of Corti.  The receptor is a 
mechanoreceptor consisting of an array of hair 
cells below a membrane called the tectorial 
membrane.  Small bristles called cilia project 
upward from the hair cells into a gel that coats the 
inner surface of the tectorial membrane .  As the 
basilar membrane vibrates up and down, the 
movement causes the hair cells to rub back and 
forth against the tectorial membrane. The 
mechanical movement stimulates the cells 
producing an action potential.   
 Loudness is translated as sound wave 
amplitude.  Higher amplitudes cause receptor cells 
to fire with greater frequency (temporal 
summation), cause surrounding receptor cells to fire 
(spatial summation), and cause more receptors to 
reach threshold.  The brain interprets the more 
intense stimulation as louder sound. 
CENTRAL PATHWAYS 
 Nerve impulses from the cochlea pass to 
the brain through the eighth pair of cranial nerves.  
They project upward through the brain stem to the 
superior part of the temporal lobe  of the cerebral 
cortex found under the  temporal bone. There is 
partial crossover of pathways so each side of the 
brain receives impulses from both ears.  Auditory 
impulses also pass to the reticular formation of the 
brain stem.  This formation regulates the body's 
"state of wakefulness". Sudden loud noises send a 
"shockwave" of stimulation through the entire brain 
by way of the reticular formation, bringing the brain 
to full alert. 
 Surrounding the primary auditory cortex is 
an association area. (This is similar to the 
arrangement in the visual  cortex of the occipital 
lobe.)  The association area is necessary for sound 
interpretation.  Without it, for example, you could 
still hear words and even repeat them, but you 
wouldn't be able to give them any meaning. 
DEAFNESS 
 Deafness is of two types - nerve deafness 
and conduction deafness.  In nerve deafness , 
neither vibrations transmitted through the external 
and middle ear, nor vibrations conducted directly 
through the bones of the skull are perceived.  
Amplification devices are ineffective.   
 In conduction deafness , transmission of 
sound vibrations from the external ear to the inner 
ear is impeded. The problem could be caused by a 
damaged tympanic membrane, fusion of the 
ossicles, accumulation of earwax, plugg ed 
Eustachian tubes or other blockages. In conduction 
deafness, sound vibrations transmitted through the 
external and middle ear are diminished, but sound 
produced by vibrating a tuning fork against the 
skull is normal.  Amplification devices can usually 
correct conduction deafness, at least partially. 
CHEMICAL SENSES OF TASTE AND SMELL 
 Taste receptors called the taste buds  
consist of groups of about 40 cells each embedded 
in the epithelial tissue of the tongue.  A small 
cluster of hair -like structures called microvilli 
extend from each taste bud  onto the surface of the 
tongue.  Molecules of substances entering the 
mouth bind to proteins in the membranes of the 
microvilli, causing them to depolarize.  The taste 
buds are therefore examples of chemoreceptors.   
 Although taste buds generally respond  to 
many different chemicals, most have a preference 
for a particular taste.  Receptors have been 
categorized into five main types: salty, sweet, sour, 
bitter and umami. Unami (“pleasant”) is most 
apparent in foods containing glutamate.  We can, 
however, distinguish between hundreds of tastes, so

===== PAGE 68 =====
64 
 
each taste must result from simultaneous 
differential stimulation of all types of taste buds by 
a given chemical.  This is analogous to our ability 
to distinguish hundreds of shades of colors using 
just three types of cones.   
 Taste not only serves to attract us to food 
necessary for survival, but probably to specific 
foods required for proper nutrition.  For example, 
salt depleted subjects choose salty food, 
hypoglycemic subjects preferentially select sweet 
food.  Taste is also protective.  The most sensitive 
receptors are the bitter taste buds.  Plant poisons are 
composed of bitter alkaloid molecules and 
perception of the unpleasant bitter taste helps us to 
avoid eating them. 
 Odor is detected by single olfactory 
receptor cells embedded in the mucosa in the upper 
part of the nasal passages.  Tufts of cilia protrude 
into the mucous layer covering the nasal passages 
and odorant molecules bind to proteins in these 
tufts, causing the cells to depolarize.  Thus, only 
volatile chemicals that can be sniffed into the nose 
and only chemicals that can dissolve in mucus can 
be smelled.  The olfactory receptors are another 
example of chemoreceptors. 
MECHANICAL TACTILE SENSATION 
 Touch receptors are mechanoreceptors of 
the skin.  There are basically three types.  The first 
are receptors near the surface of the skin that 
respond to light touch. They are particularly 
abundant on the fingertips and lips that are able to 
discern spatial characteristics of objects.  Then 
there are deep er receptors in the skin that sense 
deep, continuous pressure.  Finally there are rapidly 
adapting receptors specially designed to sense 
vibration.  
THERMORECEPTORS 
 Temperature is sensed by separate 
populations of warmth and cold receptors found 
directly below the skin. Cold receptors outnumber 
warmth receptors by as much as ten to one. 
Extremes of temperature also activate pain 
receptors. It is thought that cold and warmth 
receptors are stimulated by changes in their 
metabolic rates. These changes result from the fact 
that temperature changes the rate of chemical 
reactions occurring inside cells. Rate 
approximately double s for every 10°C change in 
temperature. Thermoreceptors respond quickly  
and sense steady -state temperatures without 
adapting. They respon d very intensely to changes 
in temperature, rapidly adapting to the change - as 
in the initial thermal shock when jumping into a 
cool lake  that quickly adjusts to a perception of 
the actual temperature of the water.  
PAIN RECEPTORS 
 The pain receptors in th e skin and other 
tissues are all bare nerve endings. Three types of 
stimuli excite them - mechanical, thermal and 
chemical. Pain receptors sensitive to chemicals are 
stimulated by chemicals released from damaged 
tissue and are responsible for the slow, suff ering 
type of pain following tissue injury. 
 Common causes of pain include tissue 
ischemia and muscle spasm. Loss of blood flow to 
a tissue causes oxygen starvation (ischemia). The 
pain is thought to be due to accumulation of lactic 
acid in the tissues, a condition that results from 
excessive anaerobic glucose metabolism.  The 
onset of pain is directly related to the oxygen 
demand. Blocking flow to exercising muscle 
causes pain within half a minute. In resting 
muscle, loss of oxygen may not result in pain f or 
several minutes. Muscle spasms cause pain by 
direct compression of mechanical pain receptors, 
but the ischemia that results from the spasm also 
contributes to the pain. Oxygen demand actually 
increases during spasm. 
 Headache is the result of pain refer red to 
the surface of the head from deep structures. It 
may be of intracranial or extracranial origin. 
Examples of intracranial headaches include 
headaches caused by inflammation (e.g. 
meningitis), low cerebral spinal fluid pressure, 
alcohol and vascular c hanges. Migraine headache 
is a type of vascular headache. It is thought the 
migraine is initiated by vasospasm of cerebral 
vessels that produces a visual aura and other 
symptoms associated with the onset. For some 
reason, the vascular smooth muscle eventua lly 
loses tone and the vessels dilate. That begins the 
pain. It may be that the pain is caused by 
stretching of pain receptors in the wall of the 
dilated vessels.  
 Common causes of extracranial headache 
include spasm of skeletal muscles of the head, 
sinus headaches and headaches caused by eye 
disorders.

===== PAGE 69 =====
65 
 
LESSON 22:  SENSORY PATHWAYS 
 
The sensory pathways we will consider 
involve both the brain and spinal cord.    
THE SPINAL CORD 
 The spinal cord runs through the vertebral 
foramen (= hole) of  the vertebral column.  The 
organization of the spinal cord is illustrated in the 
figure below.  The cord itself is separated into right 
and left halves by an anterior fissure  and 
posterior sulcus.  A narrow band of gray matter 
called the gray commissure  forms a 
communication link between the two halves.  A 
central canal  continuous with the ventricles 
(cavities) of the brain is found in the gray 
commissure. Cerebral spinal fluid flows through it. 
The central portion of the  spinal cord is heavily 
concentrated with motor neuron and interneuron 
cell bodies and therefore appears as gray matter.  
(Sensory neuron cell bodies are in the dorsal root 
ganglion.)  The gray matter portions of the cord are 
called the horns and are named by location 
(anterior, posterior a nd lateral horns).  The outer 
portion of the spinal cord contains bundles of nerve 
fiber axons and dendrites carrying impulses upward 
to the brain (sensory information) and bundles 
carrying impulses downward from the brain (motor 
information).   The nerve fibers of the bundles are 
myelinated for rapid conduction and therefore 
appear as white matter .  The fiber bundles are 
referred to as nerve columns and are named for 
their location (anterior, posterior or lateral) in the 
cord as shown to the left.  Within the columns are 
bundles of nerve fibers called tracts. They are 
described according to the direction of nerve traffic. 
Ascending tracts carry sensory impulses to the brain 
and descending tracts carry motor impulses from 
the brain.  Specific tracts are norm ally named by 
giving the origin followed by the destination of 
impulses in the tract. For example, the 
spinothalamic tract would be an ascending tract 
(sensory) while the corticospinal tract would be a 
descending tract (motor).   
 Communication between the  peripheral 
nerves and the spinal cord occurs at 31 levels of the 
cord.  At each level, a pair of spinal nerves emerges 
from the cord and passes out from the vertebral 
column through an intervertebral foramen.  Each of 
the 62 spinal nerves is attached to t he cord at two 
points: a ventral root carrying motor information 
and a dorsal root that is sensory. 
SENSORY IMPULSES 
 Peripheral nerves transmitting somatic 
signals from surface receptors on the body serve as 
good examples of sensory pathways.  Sensory 
impulses travel from surface receptors along 
dendrites of the sensory nerves toward the spinal 
cord.  The dendrites enter the dorsal root.  Their 
nerve cell bodies are located in the dorsal root 
ganglion.  An axon extends from the cell body into 
the posterior horn of the spinal cord gray matter.  
From the posterior horn, interneurons carry the 
impulses to ascending tracts in the white matter of 
the cord to be carried to the brain.  Two of the  
ascending tracts carrying surface receptor impulses 
are the dorsal column-medial lemniscal pathway 
and the anterolateral pathway.   
SPINAL CORD IN CROSS-SECTION 
a.  CENTRAL CANAL 
b.  ANTERIOR FISSURE 
c.  POSTERIOR SULCUS 
WHITE MATTER: 
      1. POSTERIOR (DORSAL) COLUMN 
      2. LATERAL COLUMN 
      3.  ANTERIOR (VENTRAL) COLUMN 
GRAY MATTER: 
      4. POSTERIOR (DORSAL) HORN with 
              INTERNEURON cell bodies 
       5. LATERAL HORN with 
               Autonomic MOTOR cell bodies 
       6. ANTERIOR (VENTRAL) HORN with 
                Somatic MOTOR cell bodies 
SPINAL NERVE: 
        7. DORSAL ROOT GANGLION with 
                 SENSORY neuron cell bodies 
        8. DORSAL (SENSORY) ROOT 
        9. VENTRAL (MOTOR) ROOT

===== PAGE 70 =====
66 
 
DORSAL COLUMN PATHWAY 
 Nerve fibers ascending through the dorsal 
(posterior) columns are myelinated and of large 
diameter.  Therefore, they transmit impulses 
rapidly; some over 100 m/sec.  T hey are suited for 
sensations that change rapidly.  In addition, these 
nerves have a high degree of spatial organization.  
The brain (cerebral cortex) is able to detect their 
point of origin with a high degree of localization.  
Impulses are transmitted with very fine gradations 
of intensity.   Receptors using this pathway are, 
however, limited to mechanoreceptors sensitive to 
touch, stretch and pressure. The pathway involves 
three sensory nerve fibers in series. 
 The first neuron carries the impulse from 
the receptor, through the dorsal columns, to the 
medulla oblongata at the base of the brain stem. 
There it synapses with the second sensory fiber that 
passes upward, through the medial lemniscus, to the 
thalamus.  Before ascending, the second fibers 
decussate (cross over).  Sensations arising from one 
side of the body will be perceived by the opposite 
side of the brain.  From the thalamus, a third 
sensory fiber projects upward into the cerebrum. 
(The thalamus is located at the core of the 
cerebrum.)  The inne r mass of the cerebrum 
consists mainly of white matter (myelinated) tracts 
including those transmitting sensory information 
from the thalamus.  Covering the cerebrum is a 
layer of gray matter called the cerebral cortex.   
 The cerebrum is divided into right and left 
hemispheres by a deep fissure running along the 
midline.  In turn, the hemispheres are separated into 
lobes by less prominent grooves, each referred to as 
a sulcus.  One is the central sulcus, running from 
medial to lateral across each hemispher e.  Each 
convolution (raised area) of the cortex is called a 
gyrus and the gyrus behind the central sulcus is 
called the post-central gyrus.  It is to this gyrus that 
most innervation from touch receptors from the 
surface of the skin traveling through the dorsal 
column-medial lemniscal pathway, project.   
 The high degree of localization 
characteristic of this system is reflected in the 
organization of the post-central gyrus.  The gyrus is 
mapped out, moving across from medial to lateral, 
beginning with sen sory innervation from the foot 
and ending on the lateral side with innervation from 
the head.  The concept that nerve pathways to and 
from specific parts of the body are permanently 
associated with specific areas of the central nervous 
system is referred t o as the labeled line principle.   
The post-central gyrus is referred to as the somatic 
sensory area of the cortex.  If the somatic sensory 
area is stimulated electrically, the subject would not 
report feeling any stimulation of his/her brain, but 
would report feeling the stimulation on the part of 
the body associated with the cortex area being 
stimulated.  "I feel something on my tongue."  "I 
feel something on my right shoulder." 
 The size of the cortex area devoted to a 
given amount of surface area on the body is directly 
related to the density of touch receptors on the body 
surface.  For example, fingertips have a very high 
density of touch receptors and are represented by a 
large area of cortex.  If two pins 2 millimeters apart 
touched the fingertips sim ultaneously, the cortex 
could perceive that there were indeed two pins.  On 
the back, where receptors are less dense, two pins 
touched any closer together than 50 millimeters 
apart might be perceived as only one pin. 
 Behind the somatic sensory area are located 
somatic sensory association areas.  Here we are 
able to interpret touch sensations.  For example, if 
the area associated with a "peach" were stimulated 
electrically, the subject would actually experience 
the feel of a peach.  It would feel spherical and 
appropriately soft and fuzzy. 
 One sense that requires very rapid 
transmission of afferent information is 
proprioception, the ability to sense the tension and 
position of the muscles.  For the brain to control 
skeletal muscles, it must know their current position 
and how they are moving at any given instant.  
Proprioception impulses are transmitted by the 
rapid dorsal column-medial lemniscal pathway. 
THE ANTEROLATERAL SYSTEM 
 Nerve fibers ascending through the 
anterolateral system are generally of small er 
diameter and many are not myelinated.  Therefore, 
they transmit impulses more slowly than the dorsal

===== PAGE 71 =====
67 
 
columns- 40 m/sec or less.  They are suited for 
sensations that change more slowly, and over 
broader areas.  They are less organized spatially and 
cannot localize the source of input with the fidelity 
of the dorsal column -medial lemniscal system.  
Receptors using this pathway include pain, thermal, 
crude touch, tickle and itch, and sexual stimuli. 
 The pathway involves two or three sensory 
nerve fibers in  series. Sensory nerves from the 
dorsal root of the spinal nerves synapse with the 
second sensory neuron in the posterior horn of the 
spinal cord.  The second neuron immediately 
crosses the cord through the gray commissure and 
ascends to the brain through the anterior 
spinothalamic tract or the lateral spinothalamic 
tract.  Impulses from the left side are projected to 
the right brain and spinal cord and visa versa.  From 
the thalamus, a third fiber may project upward to 
the sensory cortex.  Many fibers, esp ecially those 
for pain, terminate in brain stem nuclei.   
 
PAIN SENSATIONS 
 Pain is a protective mechanism that alerts 
the body to conditions that threaten to damage 
tissues.  Pain is of two types; fast (sharp) pain that 
is felt in a fraction of a second, and slow (aching) 
pain requiring seconds or minutes to occur.  Both 
types of pain receptors use the anterolateral 
pathways, however fast pain fibers are myelinated 
and larger than slow pain fibers.  They conduct at 
velocities of about 30 m/sec.  Slow fibers are small 
and unmyelinated conducting at about 1 m/sec.  
Fast pain fibers pass from the thalamus to the 
sensory cortex making fast pain easier to "localize" 
than slow pain.  On the other hand, the slow ache of 
chronic pain is centered in the brain stem a nd 
persists even if cortical centers are blocked.  Some 
brain stem pathways connect to the reticular 
activating system, bringing the body to full alert in 
an attempt to relieve the pain.   
 An example of the interplay between pain 
receptors would be an app endicitis attack.  The 
slow fibers innervating the appendix would produce 
an aching pain that would be "referred" to the 
general location in the abdomen from which the 
appendix migrated during fetal development, the 
general area around the umbilicus.  However, if the 
infection spreads to the abdominal wall covering 
the appendix, fast pain receptors in the wall will
