sense the exact location of the pain.  
SENSORY ADAPTATION 
 Different sensory systems of the body have 
different adaptive abilities. Receptors s ensing 
threatening conditions adapt very slowly, if at all.  
Examples include pain receptors, chemoreceptors 
controlling blood gasses and baroreceptors 
controlling blood pressure.   Other receptor systems 
adapt almost completely to a stimulus. 
 Mechanoreceptors vary in their ability to 
adapt.  Pacinian corpuscles of the skin adapt in a 
fraction of a second, necessary for their role in 
sensing vibration.  The corpuscle contains a viscous 
fluid that exerts pressure on the receptor only for 
the fraction of a s econd it takes the fluid to 
redistribute itself in response to the deformation of 
the corpuscle.  Baroreceptors that sense blood 
pressure will adapt in a day or two if blood pressure 
remains constant. The firing rate of the baroreceptor 
is proportional to the stimulus intensity.  But after a 
day or so, the firing rate begins to decrease even 
though the stimulus intensity remains the same.   
 Photoreceptors adapt by redistributing the 
photoreceptive pigments as described earlier.  
Chemoreceptors adapt slowly if at all.   Application 
of cologne is perceived by the wearer for only 
minutes.  Yet the odor is there; it is detected by 
persons coming in contact with the wearer for 
hours.  In this case, it is not the chemoreceptor that 
adapts.  The adaptation takes place centrally where 
afferent sensory impulses are inhibited in the 
olfactory bulb of the brain itself.  Thermoreceptors 
respond most strongly to changes in temperature, 
rapidly adapting to the new steady -state 
temperature, sensing it without further adaptation. 
 Because of the protective role for pain 
receptors, they do not adapt to pain but continue to 
respond until the threat is removed.  However, there 
are mechanisms to attenuate pain.  These involve 
neural pathways using the endorphin and 
enkephalin transmitters of the brain’s opiate system. 
 These are inhibitory transmitters that block 
transmission of pain impulses.  The pain control 
centers are located in the brain stem and posterior 
horns of the spinal cord.

===== PAGE 72 =====
68 
 
LESSON 23:  MOTOR PATHWAYS 
   
Basic motor control begins 
with neural circuits operating totally 
within the spinal cord. Indeed, 
experimental animals with cord 
transections at or below the level of 
the brain stem still exhibit the 
complex motor reflexes governing 
righting, walking, running, scratching 
and the like. Each spinal nerve has 
two roots. One is the posterior = dorsal = sensory = 
afferent root. The other is the anterior = ventral = 
motor = efferent root. 
SPINAL CORD REFLEXES 
 Stretching a muscle causes it to increase in 
length.  The increase is sensed by spindle fibers in 
the belly of the muscle and stretch receptors in the 
muscle tendons.  These receptors initiate a reflex 
contraction of the muscle.  This reflex, called the 
stretch reflex is exemplified in the knee jerk reflex. 
  Tapping the knee causes stretch in the 
tendon receptors and a nerve impulse projects 
from the receptor, through the dorsal root of the 
spinal nerve, to the anterior horn of the spinal 
cord. There is synapses with the motor nerve to 
the quadriceps m uscle of the thigh. A motor 
impulse causes the muscle to contract and the 
lower leg jerk forward. 
The purpose of the reflex is to prevent 
oscillations in the contraction by damping or 
smoothing the movement.  The reflex is normally 
facilitated by higher br ain centers, and a stronger 
than normal response indicate damage to higher 
motor pathways.  But the reflex is spinal in nature. 
If the cord were transected, the reflex would 
initially disappear because of loss of brain 
facilitation.  However, once the cord  had adapted 
to loss of  higher control, the reflex would 
reappear even without nerve impulses from the 
brain. 
 Most spinal reflexes are a bit more 
complicated, involving at least one interneuron.  In 
the flexor reflex, stimulation by a painful stimulus 
can cause reflex muscle flexion.  Stepping on a tack 
causes flexion of the muscles of the affected leg, 
pulling the foot on that leg off of the stimulus 
(tack).    
This is an ipsilateral (same side) reflex.  At 
the same time, an extensor reflex in the opposit e 
limb occurs, causing the opposite leg to straighten 
(extend).  This allows that leg to bear the entire 
weight of the body, enabling the person to remain 
standing. Here, interneurons are involved. 
Interneurons carry the stimulus from the posterior 
horn of the spinal cord to the anterior hours of the 
spinal cord. For the flexor reflex, the impulse is 
project to the anterior horn on the same side of the 
spinal cord. This is an ipsilateral reflex. For the 
extensor reflex, the interneuron projects across the 
gray commissure to the anterior horn on the 
opposite side. This is a contralateral reflex. 
 Examples of inhibitory pathways can also 
be found within the spinal cord.  When a group of 
muscles contract to produce a movement, the 
antagonist muscles must simult aneously remain 
relaxed.  In the reflex pathway just described, 
reciprocal innervation of muscles blocks impulses 
to extensors to keep them relaxed when flexors 
contract and visa versa.  This is called reciprocal 
inhibition. 
 Many of the most common motor 
behaviors are coordinated by neural circuits 
originating within the spinal cord itself.  In an 
animal with spinal transection from higher brain 
centers, pressure on the soles of the feet will cause 
it to stand or even make walking movements, 
tickling its pelt will cause it to scratch, laying it on

===== PAGE 73 =====
69 
 
its back will cause it to attempt to right itself.  
Movements involving multiple muscle groups 
acting simultaneously are made possible by spinal 
reflex pathways and reciprocal innervation. 
CEREBRAL CORTEX MOTOR PATHWAYS 
 The outer layer of gray matter covering the 
cerebrum is called the cerebral cortex. The 
primary motor cortex lies along the pre-central 
gyrus of the cerebral cortex, just in front of the 
central sulcus.  Like the sensory area on the post -
central gyrus, the pre -central gyrus is mapped out, 
moving from medial to lateral, beginning with 
muscles of the foot and ending on the lateral side 
with muscles of facial expression.  More than half 
of the gyrus controls muscles of the hands, fingers 
and face.  Sti mulation of these areas might cause 
only a small number of muscle fibers to contract, 
necessary for producing fine movements.  This is 
tied to the concept of motor units discussed earlier. 
Stimulation of cortex areas less well represented 
would cause a lar ger group of muscles to contract. 
In the large leg muscles, contraction of just a few 
muscle fibers would have no effect. Anterior to the 
primary motor cortex is a pre-motor cortex.  
Neurons here stimulate muscle groups involved in 
particular tasks such as  word formation or hand 
skills.   
 Most fibers from the cortex pass down the 
brain stem and spinal cord through the lateral 
corticospinal tract.  Fibers cross the brain stem in 
the medulla oblongata, such that the motor cortex 
on a given side of the brain controls skeletal 
muscles on the opposite side of the body.  Fibers 
passing from cortex areas for muscles involved with 
detailed movement, particularly hand and finger 
muscles move directly down the cord to the 
appropriate level of the spinal cord.  Fibers  
controlling other muscles synapse in brain stem 
nuclei.  While damage to the motor cortex seriously 
impairs delicate movements, gross motor activity 
can still be controlled.  The cortex is involved more 
with "commanding" muscles to contract.  Control 
occurs mainly in the brain stem and spinal cord  
itself.   
ANTIGRAVITY MUSCLES 
 Extensor muscles that straighten the 
vertebral column and extend the arms and legs are 
important in maintaining an erect posture against 
the forces of gravity.  These muscles are controlled 
from brain stem nuclei.  One of these centers that 
receives its primary sensory afferent information 
from the vestibular apparatus of the inner ear, 
consists of a group of vestibular nuclei  in the 
medulla oblongata.  Motor nerves are carried to the 
antigravity muscles through the lateral and medial 
vestibulospinal tracts.   
THE VESTIBULAR APPARATUS 
 Besides sensing hearing, the inner ear is 
also important in equilibrium and balance.  A 
portion of the inner ear called the vestibule extends 
from the  cochlea.  The vestibule contains two 
membranous chambers called the saccule and 
utricle.  Within these chambers are hair cells 
supported in a gel matrix.  Calcium carbonate 
particles called otoliths are entangled in them.  The 
forces of gravity acting on the otoliths cause them 
to be pushed or pulled in different directions against 
the receptor surface of the hair cells.  By sensing 
the direction of gravitational force, the hair cells 
can deduce the position of the head. Action 
potentials from the hair cells are transmitted to the 
brain through the eighth cranial nerves, the same 
nerves carrying impulses from the cochlea.  The 
vestibular apparatus is primarily involved with the 
ability to perceive the position of the head or static 
equilibrium.  It is also able to sense linear 
acceleration, but angular motion requires the 
semicircular canals. 
 Extending from the vestibular apparatus are 
three semicircular canals , positioned at right 
angles to each other.  Each canal has an enlarged 
portion at one end called the ampulla.  Within the 
ampulla are hair cells also supported in a gel 
matrix.  Motion of the head causes the endolymph 
of the semicircular canals to swirl through the 
ampullae and push against the hair cells.  The force 
of the swirling endolymph is diffe rent within the 
different ampullae depending on the direction of 
head motion.  Resulting nerve impulses are 
interpreted as movement of the head.  The

===== PAGE 74 =====
70 
 
semicircular canals sense acceleration, deceleration, 
and head rotation.  They are, therefore, more 
involved with dynamic equilibrium.   
 One example of the usefulness of vestibular 
sensations is the ability to continue looking at a 
particular object even when the head is moving.  As 
the head moves to the right, the extrinsic eye 
muscles move the eyes to the l eft at exactly the 
same rate to compensate.   However, if the swirling 
has gained sufficient momentum and the head stops 
too rapidly, the momentum of the fluid causes it to 
continue its movement.  The eyes, believing the 
head is still moving, continue to s can in the 
opposite direction and we feel as if the world is 
spinning.  
THE CEREBELLUM 
 The cerebellum is a large mass of neural 
tissue located below the cerebrum and behind the 
brain stem.  It functions to monitor and make 
adjustments to the motor activities initiated by other 
areas of the central nervous system. It receives 
information regarding the desired sequence of 
muscle contraction from higher brain centers 
including the cerebral cortex. It also receives 
information concerning the present position a nd 
motion of muscles from peripheral receptors 
including the proprioceptors. If the desired 
movement doesn't compare favorably to the 
movement actually occurring, the cerebellum sends 
corrective signals outward to adjust the muscle 
activity appropriately.  As a result, movements are 
smooth and fluid.  This level of control is especially 
important in rapid movements that require a high 
level of coordination; movements associated with 
running or dancing or playing a musical instrument. 
Without cerebellar inpu t, rapid movements still 
occur, but become very abnormal. Slower 
movements are less affected. 
 Another control function of the cerebellum 
involves the sequencing of movements.  The 
cerebellum predicts and plans for the next 
movement even while the present movement is still 
underway.   Some movements occur so rapidly that 
the entire sequence of muscle actions must be 
planned prior to execution.  These movements are 
called ballistic movements and require cerebellar 
control to occur in a normal way.  Punching fingers 
down on a keyboard is an example of a ballistic 
movement. 
 Cerebellar control is learned. The 
cerebellum learns from its mistakes and gradually 
improves on its control.  A young child learning to 
eat needs a bib. But gradually the feeding 
movements become smooth and coordinated.  A 
child learning to walk moves with a staggered gate. 
Walking movements become more fluid as 
cerebellar control improves.  
THE BASAL GANGLIA 
 The basal ganglia are gray matter areas 
found deep within the white matter tracts  of the 
cerebrum.  They receive input from the cerebral 
cortex and send output back to the cerebral cortex.  
The basal ganglia are involved in the cognitive 
control of patterns of behavior.  These include 
"skilled" types of movements.  Examples include 
athletic skills, penmanship skills, and job skills such 
as those involved in crafts such as woodworking.  
Output from the basal ganglia would go to pre -
motor areas of the cortex involved with controlling 
combinations of muscles rather than primary motor 
cortex areas controlling individual muscles. 
 Basal ganglia control of motor function 
makes use of cognitive processes.  That is, thinking 
processes combining current sensory input with 
memory input from past experiences stored mainly 
in the cerebral cortex.  This gives one the ability to 
plan and execute rapid motor responses based on 
current information and past experience. 
 Finally, the basal ganglia provide an overall 
inhibitory or dampening effect on muscle 
contractions, helping to stabilize or "steady" 
contracting muscles. Most neurotransmitter 
substances used by basal ganglia neurons are 
inhibitory transmitters. Disease processes involving 
basal ganglia centers result is various kinds of 
uncontrolled spontaneous movements ranging from 
tremors to flailing m ovements of the appendages.  
Parkinson's disease and Huntington's chorea are 
examples.  Parkinson's disease  is caused by 
relatively low concentrations of the 
neurotransmitter dopamine.  It is characterized by 
rigidity of voluntary muscles and tremors of th e 
fingers and extremities.  Huntington's chorea is a 
genetic disease characterized by flicking 
movements in the joints and distorted , exagerated 
movements of the entire body.  It's caused by loss of 
cells that secrete the inhibitory neurotransmitter 
GABA.

===== PAGE 75 =====
71 
 
LESSON 24:  THE BRAIN  
 
 For purposes of discussion, we shall divide 
the brain into six parts: the cerebrum, the 
cerebellum, the diencephalon, the mesencephalon, 
the pons and the medulla oblongata. The last three 
will be considered collectively as the brain stem. 
CEREBRUM 
 The cerebrum makes up about two -thirds 
of the brain.  It is divided into left and right cerebral 
hemispheres separated by the longitudinal fissure. 
 Each hemisphere is further divided into lobes 
named for the cranial bone that covers and protects 
them: the frontal lobe , parietal lobe , occipital 
lobe, and temporal lobe .  The lobes are also 
separated from each other by sulcus that is less well 
defined as the longitudinal fissure. The central 
sulcus separates the frontal lobe from the parietal 
lobes.  The lateral sulcus separates the frontal and 
parietal lobes from the temporal lobes.  There is no 
well-defined sulcus between the occipital and 
parietal lobes. 
 
 The outer five millimeters or so thickness 
of the cerebrum is compos ed of dense layers of 
nerve cell bodies and appears as gray matter.  This 
is called the cerebral cortex .  Although only 
several millimeters in thickness, the cerebral cortex 
contains about 100 billion neurons .  In order to 
accommodate the large number of n eurons, the 
surface is thrown into folds.  Each surface 
convolution thus formed is called a gyrus. The 
depression or fold between gyri is called a sulcus.  
 Functionally, the cortex contains sensory, 
motor, and association areas. Many of these areas 
have b een mapped out using various techniques 
including electrical stimulation of the exposed 
cortex.  For example, the precentral gyrus of each 
hemisphere (the cortex region just anterior to the 
central sulcus) is a somatic motor area .  
Stimulation of this part  of the cortex causes 
contractions of skeletal muscles.  The precentral 
gyrus is organized relative to the parts of the body 
the muscles control.  Moving up, across and then 
down the gyrus from medial to lateral, muscles 
responding move from toes to feet to leg to trunk to 
arm to hand to fingers to head. 
 The post central gyrus of each hemisphere 
(the cortex area just posterior to the central sulcus) 
is a somatic sensory area .  Direct stimulation of 
this part of the cortex would be perceived by the 
subject as something touching a certain part of their 
body.  It too is localized in similar fashion as the 
motor area- face, hand, arm, torso, legs, feet. 
 Examples of other localized functional 
areas of the cortex include an auditory area in each 
temporal lobe as sociated with hearing, and the 
occipital lobe cortex on either side associated with 
vision.  
 While the sensory and motor functions 
described above have control areas in both 
hemispheres, the hemispheres are different. For 
most of us, the left hemisphere is the categorical or 
“dominant” hemisphere. Language and all 
language-based abilities including science, math 
and logic, are seated in the categorical hemisphere. 
For example, the motor speech area (Broca’s area) 
found in the frontal lobe of the categorica l 
hemisphere enables us to communicate in words.  
 The opposite hemisphere (right for most of 
us) is referred to as the representational 
hemisphere. It views the word in a more holistic 
way. It is the seat of imagination, music, art, 
perception of patterns , spatial relationships, and 
comparisons of smells, tastes, sights, and sounds. 
 Besides sensory and motor areas, all lobes 
of the cortex also contain association areas.  For 
example, behind the somatic sensory area (touch) is 
an association area for touch . Stimulation of the 
sensory area would be perceived as something 
touching my body. Stimulation of the touch 
association area might be interpreted as something 
“fuzzy” touching my body. Stimulate the auditory 
area and you perceive it as a sound. Stimulate the 
association area surrounding the auditory area and 
you might associate the sound - a bell, a robin, 
someone’s voice. The somatic motor cortex causes

===== PAGE 76 =====
72 
 
individual muscles to contract. The association area 
in front of the motor cortex produces complex 
movements, the result of many muscles contracting 
in proper sequence. These areas reach their highest 
development in the human species.  Not only can 
we associate sensory information and motor 
responses, but we can also store information using 
cortex neurons (m emory) and process this 
information to solve problems (reason).  
 The somatic, visual and auditory 
association areas all come together at the top and 
back of the temporal lobe. This confluence of 
different sensory interpretive areas is most highly 
developed in the categorical (usually left) 
hemisphere and is considered to be the seat of 
intelligence. It is called the general interpretive 
area (Wernicke’s area). 
 Parietal lobe association areas involve 
understanding speech and using words to express 
ourselves on the categorical hemisphere and 
interpretation of nonverbal experiences (art and 
music) on the representational hemisphere. 
 The neurons of the cortex and lower brain 
centers are interconnected by myelinated white 
matter tracts coursing through the cer ebrum below 
the cortex.  The cortex of the right hemisphere 
communicates with the cortex of the left 
hemisphere via myelinated fibers of the corpus 
callosum, located at the bottom of the longitudinal 
fissure.  Since each hemisphere carries out different 
functions ( categorical vs. representational), the 
corpus callosum is functionally quite important. 
 There are regions of gray matter deep 
within the cerebrum called the basal ganglia.  As 
discussed earlier, the basal ganglia act together with 
the cerebral co rtex to control "patterns" of the 
behavior.  In addition, inhibitory neurons act to 
"steady" our muscular movements. 
 A region in the temporal lobes referred to 
as the amygdala serves as the center of the limbic 
system, brain areas extending to the diencephalon 
and base of the cerebrum that control emotions and 
motivational behavior.  The amygdala interprets the 
information- a disgusting sight, an enjoyable 
experience. Output goes in two directions. Output 
to the hypothalamus and other lower brain centers 
causes a visceral response- heart races, hair stands 
on end. Output to the pre -frontal cortex is 
responsible for our behavioral response to the 
emotional experience- fighting, fleeing, embracing. 
Damage or stimulation of areas associated with our 
emotions c an lead to blunted or exaggerated 
responses to emotional experiences. Damage to the 
amygdala can completely abolish any sense of fear. 
Controlling our emotions serves a survival function 
motivating us to fight that which threatens us or to 
flee from threats that we cannot overcome. 
THE CEREBELLUM 
 Located below the occipital lobe and 
behind the brain stem is the second largest portion 
of the brain, the cerebellum.  As discussed earlier, 
the cerebellum coordinates muscle movements, 
making them occur in a smooth and fluid way.   
THE DIENCEPHALON 
 The diencephalon is located at the top of 
the brain stem and is surrounded by the cerebrum.  
Its two main parts are the thalamus and the 
hypothalamus.  The thalamus serves as a relay 
center for higher areas of the bra in.  It is a crude 
sensory area, not well mapped out like the 
postcentral gyrus.  Certain sensations of a more 
generalized nature, like slow pain, are perceived 
mainly in the thalamus.   
 The hypothalamus is a link between the 
nervous system that communica tes with electrical 
impulses and the endocrine system that 
communicates with hormones.  It produces 
antidiuretic hormone  (ADH) that acts on the 
kidney to retain water.  It produces oxytocin that 
stimulates uterine contractions.  It also produces 
hormones that control the pituitary gland, a small 
gland connected to the base of the hypothalamus.   
 Neural functions of the hypothalamus 
include centers that provide for the body's basic 
needs (survival): a thirst center, a center 
controlling appetite, one regula ting body 
temperature, and an area controlling sexual 
cycles, to name a few.   
 Within the hypothalamus, specific 
anatomical regions associated with reward and 
punishment are found. When stimulated in 
experimental animals, these areas have profound 
effects on behavior.  A rat taught to push a lever 
that triggers electrical stimulation of a reward 
center will push the lever to the exclusion of all 
other behaviors including eating and drinking.

===== PAGE 77 =====
73 
 
Studies of this kind provide insights to addictive, 
self-destructive behaviors we are all subject to. 
THE BRAIN STEM 
 Collectively, the brain stem consists of the 
mesencephalon (midbrain) extending down from 
the diencephalon, the pons and the medulla 
oblongata, just above and connected to the spinal 
cord.  In one sense , these structures might be 
thought of as extensions of the spinal cord, but 
because they are located within the skull, they are 
considered part of the brain.  They do contain 
tracts, such as the medial lemniscus, spinaothalamic 
or corticospinal tracts dis cussed earlier; tracts that 
communicate with higher brain centers.  But the 
brain stem is more than a spinal cord extension.  
Like the hypothalamus, it contains nuclei that 
control vital life functions.  Most reflexes of the 
autonomic nervous system that control involuntary 
muscle are centered in the brain stem.  Recall that 
the pons and medulla oblongata act as centers for 
control of breathing and blood pressure.  Reflexes 
that control the antigravity muscles are centered in 
nuclei of the brain stem, inclu ding the vestibular 
nuclei discussed previously.   
THE RETICULAR FORMATION 
  The reticular formation, also called the 
reticular activating system, acts to control the flow 
of information passing through the brain stem.  
When the reticular formation is acti ve, we’re 
awake.  When the system is suppressed, we sleep. 
In addition, the reticular formation helps to filter 
out unimportant sensory information.  Without 
some sort of filtering, the cortex would be totally 
overwhelmed with input.  When activated by a loud 
noise or pain, for example, our senses are 
heightened and more afferent information gets 
through.  Neurons of the reticular formation extend 
throughout the entire length of the brain stem.   
Injury to these neurons can place the victim in a 
coma with n o arousal from even the strongest 
stimulation   
 Sleep is an unconscious state from which 
one can be aroused by sensory or other stimuli. 
That one can be aroused distinguishes sleep from 
coma. Sleep is known to be an active process 
initiated within the ret icular formation. There are 
two different types of sleep- slow-wave and REM 
(rapid eye movement) sleep. 
  Slow-wave sleep is a deep sleep that 
occurs during the first hour of the sleep cycle.  It is 
extremely restful sleep associated with a drop in 
peripheral vascular tone and many other vegetative 
functions. There is a 10 -30% decrease in blood 
pressure, respiratory rate and metabolic rate. 
Dreams occurring in slow -wave sleep are not 
recorded into memory.  
 As one becomes more rested, intermittent 
episodes of REM sleep begin to occur every 90 
minutes or so. Bouts of REM sleep generally last 
from 5 to 30 minutes each. REM sleep is associated 
with active dreaming greatly depressed muscle 
movement. Persons are actually more difficult to 
arouse during REM sleep. The brain is very active 
during REM sleep; brain metabolism may increase 
as much as 20%.  
 The brain itself benefits the most from 
sleep. Prolonged periods of wakefulness are 
associated with progressive malfunction of thought 
processes and sometimes even abnormal behavioral 
activities. Persons can become irritable and even 
psychotic after periods of forced wakefulness.   
 INFORMATION MANAGEMENT 
 One of the higher brain functions most 
highly developed in the human brain is the ability to 
process information. This involves acquiring new 
information ( learning), storing and retrieving 
information ( memory) and eliminating trivial or 
unused information ( forgetting). Forgetting is 
actually quite important because if all information 
were retained indefinitely, the ability to process 
information would be completely overwhelmed.  
 Brain injured persons are often unable to 
store new information ( anterograde amnesia) or 
retrieve existing information ( retrograde 
amnesia).  It is a defect in declarative memory- 
for example, the ability to recall past events. It does 
not affect skill (procedural) memory- the ability 
to tie your shoes, for example. 
 The hippocampus in the temporal lobes is 
an important learning center. It learns from sensory 
input while the event is happenin g. Then it 
repeatedly “plays” this memory into other areas 
within the cerebral cortex for long-term storage.  
 Synaptic pathways through the brain are 
not permanent and fixed. Learning and memory 
involve creating information pathways through the 
brain by m odifying existing synapses or creating 
new synaptic pathways.  Three types of memory

===== PAGE 78 =====
74 
 
that are generally recognized are short -term, 
intermediate long-term and long-term. 
 Short-term memory lasts for seconds and 
enables us to remember what just happened 
allowing a sequence of events to flow together. It 
enables us to remember a phone number long 
enough for us to dial it, as long as we keep thinking 
about it. It probably occurs as a nerve impulse 
reverberates or “echoes” for a few seconds. 
 Intermediate long-term memory can last 
for minutes (looking for lost car keys) or hours 
(“cramming” for an exam).  It probably involves 
mechanisms that enable specific neurons to fire 
more easily or release more neurotransmitter when 
stimulated. For example, pre -synaptic fac ilitation 
can increase the influx of calcium ions in pre -
synaptic neurons that is necessary for vesicles to 
release neurotransmitter. It’s the opposite of pre -
synaptic inhibition discussed earlier. The amount of 
information that can be stored is limited. 
 Long-term memory  enables large 
amounts of information to be stored up to a 
lifetime. It involves a more deliberate learning 
process characterized by repetition and 
reinforcement. Long -term memory involves 
physical remodeling of synapses with more 
transmitter vesicles in pre-synaptic neurons or more 
receptor sites on post -synaptic neurons. It can also 
involve formation of new synapses through growth 
and branching of new nerve processes, or 
reconfiguring existing cells to enable them to 
discharge more easily.   
CEREBRAL SPINAL FLUID 
 Cavities within the brain contain capillary 
tufts that produce a filtrate of blood closely 
resembling plasma called cerebral spinal fluid  
(CSF).  CSF is produced continuously and flows 
through the cavities of the brain (the four 
ventricles) and the central canal of the spinal cord.   
 Surrounding the brain and spinal cord are 
three layers of connective tissue membranes called 
the meninges.  The outer two layers ( dura mater 
and arachnoid mater) form a sort of loose sack 
around the b rain and spinal cord.    A third layer, 
the pia mater, fits tightly over the surface of the 
brain and spinal cord.  The space thus formed 
between the arachnoid mater and pia mater is the 
subarachnoid space .  CSF flows out from the 
ventricles of the brain a nd around the brain and 
spinal cord through the subarachnoid space. 
 Suspending the brain in CSF prevents the 
compressional damage to brain tissue that would 
occur if it were resting on the floor of the cranium. 
It also functions to sbsorb shocks from any impacts 
to the head.  The total volume of CSF is about 150 
ml.  CSF is produced continuously and is totally 
replaced about three times a day.  This means it has 
to be reabsorbed into the blood at the same rate it's 
produced.  Since the skull cannot expand,  any 
accumulation of CSF would increase intracranial 
pressure (that is normally about 10 mm Hg) and 
squeeze brain capillaries leading to permanent brain 
damage.  The condition is called hydrocephalus. 
 The endothelial cells of most brain 
capillaries form tight junctions that act as selective 
barriers for transport between blood and the fluid of 
the brain, both interstitial fluid and CSF.  This is 
often referred to as the "blood -brain barrier".  It is 
quite permeable to water, respiratory gases and 
lipid sol uble molecules (like alcohol). But 
electrolytes and large organic molecules in the 
blood that might interfere with nerve activity in the 
brain are excluded.    
CRANIAL NERVES 
 The brain and spinal cord comprise the 
central nervous system. Thirty -one pairs of spinal 
nerves branch off from the spinal cord, as discussed 
previously. These spinal nerves, along with their 
branches, form the spinal portion of the peripheral 
nervous system. In addition, twelve pairs of cranial 
nerves emerge from the diencepahlon an d brain 
stem. They are involved with motor and sensory 
function associated with the head and neck. Motor 
nerves would include nerves to control eye 
movements, swallowing and facial expression. 
Sensory nerves would include nerves for vision, 
taste, smell, hearing and facial touch. 
 One pair of cranial nerves actually passes 
down the neck and into the thoracic and abdominal 
cavities.  The nerves, called the vagus nerves, help 
to control involuntary function in all internal organs 
of the thoracic and abdominal cavities.  Damage 
to the spinal cord would therefore not affect 
function of the thoracic and upper abdominal 
organs.

===== PAGE 79 =====
75 
 
LESSON 25:  THE AUTONOMIC NERVOUS SYSTEM   
 
Cardiac muscle, visceral muscle and 
glandular secretory activity are controlled by the 
autonomic nervous system .  A nerve, such as a 
cranial nerve or spinal nerve or the sciatic nerve in 
the leg, is basically a bundle of individual nerve 
fibers.  Nerve fiber refers to the axons or dendrites 
of individual neurons (nerve cells).  Each individual 
nerve fiber is specialized, carrying only one 
particular kind of impulse.  Some, for example, are 
sensory in nature while others carry only motor 
impulses.  An individual nerve fiber cannot be both 
sensory and motor.  Individual nerve fibers within a 
nerve can also be classified as either somatic or 
autonomic, but not both.  Somatic nerve fibers 
control voluntary muscle and autonomic nerve 
fibers control involuntary muscle and some glands. 
A nerve can contain a mixture of sensory and 
motor nerve fibers.  It  can contain a mixture of 
autonomic and somatic nerve fibers. 
 In some parts of the nervous system, 
sensory and motor nerve fibers do not travel in the 
same nerve.  Recall that the dorsal root of a spinal 
nerve has only sensory nerve fibers and the ventral 
root only motor nerve fibers.  Autonomic fibers also 
travel as separate bundles of fibers as they branch 
off from cranial nerves or spinal nerves .  For 
example, shortly after passing from the vertebral 
column, the dorsal and ventral roots of spinal 
nerves join forming a single nerve of mixed sensory 
and motor, somatic and autonomic nerve fibers.  
But almost immediately, a bundle of autonomic 
nerve fibers called the white ramus branches off 
the main spinal nerve.  All of the autonomic fibers 
leave the spinal nerve at this point.  Another bundle 
of nerve fibers called the gray ramus reconnects 
with the spinal nerve next to the white ramus 
allowing some of the autonomic fibers to return to 
the spinal nerve. 
 Autonomic sensory afferent fibers  
conduct sensory i mpulses toward the central 
nervous system.  Visceral receptors sense changes 
in blood pressure, gastrointestinal activity, blood 
oxygen levels, body temperature, and the like.  In 
the case of autonomic sensory afferent fibers 
associated with the spinal cor d, the nerve fibers 
enter the cord by way of the dorsal root along with 
somatic sensory afferent fibers.  Their cell bodies 
are located in the dorsal root ganglion. 
 Autonomic motor efferent fibers  are 
organized differently than the somatic motor fibers. 
Somatic motor neurons associated with the spinal 
cord have their cell bodies in the anterior horn of 
the spinal cord.  But autonomic motor neuron cell 
bodies are found in the lateral horn of the spinal 
cord.  For somatic motor neuron pathways, a single 
motor neuron bridges the distance between the 
brain or spinal cord and skeletal muscle fiber - a 
distance that can be several feet in length.  For the 
autonomic motor pathway, two motor neurons in 
series bridge the gap between the central nervous 
system (CNS) and the involuntary muscle or gland. 
The two fibers synapse with each other at an 
autonomic ganglion (cluster of neuron cell bodies) 
somewhere between the CNS and the effector.  The 
nerve fiber originating from the CNS is called the 
preganglionic fiber. The  fiber that ultimately 
synapses with the effector is called the 
postganglionic fiber.  For spinal autonomics, the 
preganglionic motor neuron cell body is in the 
lateral horn of the spinal cord and the 
postganglionic motor neuron cell body is in the 
autonomic ganglion itself. The postganglionic 
fibers go on to innervate visceral smooth muscle, 
cardiac muscle or glands. 
 Peripheral autonomic nerve fibers emerge 
from the central nervous system at three levels: 
 The cranial autonomics are associated with 
four pa irs of cranial nerves; specifically the 
oculomotor nerves (III), the facial nerves (VII), 
the glossopharyngeal nerves (IX) and the vagus 
nerves (X).  The other eight pairs of cranial 
nerves have no autonomic fibers. 
 The thoracolumbar autonomics are found a s 
part of spinal nerve pairs C -8 (cervical), T -1

===== PAGE 80 =====
76 
 
through T -12 (thoracic) and L -1 & L -2 
(lumbar). 
 The sacral autonomics are associated with 
sacral nerve pairs S-2, S-3 and S-4 (sacral). 
 Postganglionic motor neurons have their 
cell bodies in the autonomic ganglia.  These ganglia 
are categorized according to location.  There are 
three types: 
 The paravertebral ganglia are arranged along 
either side of the vertebral column.  On each 
side, individual ganglia are interconnected to 
the ones above and below, and fo rming a 
“chain” of ganglia. 
 The prevertebral ganglia are located just in 
front of the vertebral column.  These ganglia 
are also interconnected by networks of nerves 
called plexuses. The solar plexus  is one 
example.  
 The two types of ganglia just described  
receive preganglionic axons from the 
thoracolumbar autonomics.  Since the ganglia 
described above are relatively close to the spinal 
cord, the preganglionic fibers are short and the 
postganglionic fibers are long. 
 A third type of ganglia, t he terminal ganglia, 
are located near the end of the autonomic 
efferent pathway.  They are next to, or actually 
incorporated in, the effector organ tissue.  The 
preganglionic fiber is long and the 
postganglionic fiber short.  Because of their 
anatomic location, terminal ganglia are not able 
to interconnect.  Terminal ganglia receive 
preganglionic impulses from the cranial and 
sacral autonomic efferents. 
 Based on both anatomic and functional 
characteristics, the autonomic nervous system is 
divided into two divisions: 
 The sympathetic division includes all 
autonomic nerve pathways whose preganglionic 
fibers stem from the thoracolumbar region of 
the spinal cord.  The "sympathetic" division 
comes to the aid of the body during times of 
stress. 
 The parasympathetic division inclu des all 
autonomic nerve pathways whose fibers arise 
from the craniosacral branches of the CNS.  
Because the craniosacral nerves emerge above 
and below the sympathetic, they are called the 
"parasympathetics" (para = around). 
 Recall that an electrical impul se cannot 
cross a synapse.  Neurotransmitter chemicals cross 
the synapse.  The neurotransmitter used at the 
synapses between pre- and post-ganglionic neurons 
in paravertebral, collateral and terminal ganglia is 
acetylcholine.  Postganglionic axons go on to  
synapse with visceral effectors (involuntary muscle 
and glands). Most organs are under dual 
innervation.  For example, both branches of the 
autonomic nervous system innervate the heart.  
They act antagonistically.  The sympathetic fibers 
speed up the hear t while parasympathetic fibers 
slow it down. If both sympathetic and 
parasympathetic nerves stimulated the effector 
using the same neurotransmitter, how would the 
effector know which way to respond?  At the 
synapse between the postganglionic nerve and the 
muscle or gland, acetylcholine is the transmitter 
only for the parasympathetic neurons.   
Norepinephrine is the neurotransmitter associated 
with sympathetic fibers.  In this way the effector 
can differentiate between sympathetic and 
parasympathetic stimuli. There are a few exceptions 
discussed below.    
 At the neuromuscular junctions, fibers with 
acetylcholine as the transmitter are called 
cholinergic and fibers that use norepinephrine are 
called adrenergic.  (Norepinephrine is also called 
noradrenalin.) Synapses at the autonomic 
neuromuscular junctions are adrenergic for 
sympathetic nerves and cholinergic for 
parasympathetic nerves.   
 The parasympathetic nervous system 
controls routine bodily functions under normal, 
unstressed conditions.  Its effects ar e localized to 
specific organ systems as needed without affecting

===== PAGE 81 =====
77 
 
other systems.  Sympathetic nerves are active 
during times of stress.  The sympathetic system is 
called the "fight or flight" system.  The networks of 
nerves interconnecting its ganglia make  
sympathetic responses more general in nature, 
involving many organ systems simultaneously. 
Each system is necessary. The 
parasympathetic helps conserve bodily resources.  
The sympathetic enables us to respond to stress.  In 
the context of the sympathetic nervous system, 
stress includes not only unusual situations involving 
danger.  Stress also includes routine activities -  
running to catch a bus. There are exceptions to dual 
innervation.  The sweat glands, adrenal glands and 
arrector pili muscles of the skin are innervated only 
by the sympathetic nervous system.  Since there are 
no parasympathetic competitors, two different 
transmitter chemicals are not required.  
Acetylcholine is used at all of the ne rve and 
neuromuscular synapses. 
 
SYMPATHETIC 
1. Nerve impulses originate from the thoracolumbar 
preganglionic autonomic fibers. 
2. Synapses between the pre - and post -ganglionic 
neurons are located in the prevertebral and 
paravertebral ganglia.  The ganglia are 
interconnected making the effects generalized. 
3. The preg anglionic fibers are short and the 
postganglionic fibers are long. 
4. Norepinephrine is the neurotransmitter at the 
synapse of the postganglionic motor nerve with 
the effector. 
5. The sympathetic is the division of the nervous 
system that comes to the aid of the body in times 
of stress. 
6. Metabolic energy is rapidly consumed. 
7. Effects as specific organs respond to the stress: 
a. Pupils of the eyes dilate to allow more light access to 
rods and cones. 
b. Salivary gland secretions are reduced.  Mouth 
becomes dry. 
c. Heart rate and strength of beat increase providing 
added blood flow for oxygen delivery. 
d. Airways dilate and lung ventilation increases bringing 
more oxygen to the lungs. 
e. Liver releases stored glucose into the blood for use 
as fuel for energy production. 
f. Adrenal glands release the hormones norepinephrine 
and epinephrine that reinforce the sympathetic 
response. 
g. Kidneys produce less urine as blood flow to them is 
reduced. 
h. Urinary bladder walls relax and sphincter tightens 
retaining urine. 
i. Digestive system activity is inhibited.  Secretion and 
motility decrease. 
j. Blood vessels divert flow away from abdominal 
organs to skeletal muscle. 
k. Arrector pili muscles of skin constrict causing goose 
bumps. 
l. Sweat glands, particularly those of armpit and groin, 
increase secretory activity. 
PARASYMPATHETIC 
1. Nerve impulses originate from the craniosacral 
preganglionic autonomic fibers. 
2. Synapses between the pre - and post -ganglionic 
neurons are located in the terminal ganglia.  The 
ganglia are not interconne cted so effects are 
localized to specific organ systems. 
3. The preganglionic fibers are long and the 
postganglionic fibers are short. 
4. Acetylcholine is the neurotransmitter at the 
synapse of the postganglionic motor nerve with 
the effector. 
5. The parasympathetic is the division of the nervous 
system that takes care of us in quiet times 
between stresses. 
6. Metabolic energy (ATP) is conserved. 
7. Effects on specific organs are conservative: 
a. Pupils constrict reducing the amount of light entering 
eye to conserve rods and cones. 
b. Salivary gland secretions increase as food is 
ingested. 
c. Heart rate and strength of beat are reduced 
conserving the heart at rest. 
d. Airways constrict and lung ventilation is reduced as 
energy is conserved. 
e. Liver stores glucose in preparation for increased 
need at a later time. 
f. Adrenal glands have no innervation from the 
parasympathetic nervous system. The glands are 
exceptions to the “dual innervation” concept. 
g. Kidneys produce urine at an appropriate rate to clear 
metabolites and balance body water. 
h. Urinary bladder responds normally to the micturition 
reflex and voluntary control. 
i. Digestive system secretion and motility increase as 
needed for proper digestion at mealtime. 
j. Blood vessels provide appropriate flow to organs of 
the digestive and urinary systems. 
k. Arrector pili muscles are not innervated.  
 
l. Sweat glands are not innervated.

===== PAGE 82 =====
78 
 
LESSON 26:  TRANSPORT AND DIGESTION OF NUTRIENTS  
 
The digestive system is centered around a 
tube called the gastrointestinal (GI) tr act.  The 
segments of the tract passing through the body are, 
in order beginning at the mouth, the oral cavity, 
pharynx (throat), esophagus, stomach, small 
intestine, large intestine (colon), rectum and 
anus.  Accessory organs associated with the GI 
tract include the salivary glands, teeth, tongue, 
liver, gallbladder and pancreas. 
 Foods taken into the body must be broken 
down: proteins into amino acids, fats into fatty 
acids and glycerol, and complex carbohydrates into 
simple sugars.  This is digestion.  The breakdown 
products must then be transferred into the blood.  
This is absorption.  That which is not absorbed is 
processed for defecation. 
INTESTINAL BLOOD FLOW 
 In the abdominal region, a long sheet of 
membranous tissue spreads out from its attachment 
at the posterior abdominal wall.  It then fans out and 
the other end is attached to one edge of the entire 
length of the intestines.  This membranous web, 
called the mesentery, supports all of the blood 
vessels, lymphatic vessels and nerves that extend to 
the intestines.  Blood low in nutrients is carried to 
the intestines through the mesenteric arteries that 
branch directly off the abdominal aorta.  Intestinal 
capillary beds, described later, receive nutrients 
absorbed from the gut and transport them throug h 
mesenteric veins to the hepatic portal vein that 
carries blood into the liver.  In the liver, another 
capillary network provides for the extraction and 
processing of nutrients and removal of any bacteria 
or contaminants that might have been absorbed. 
Blood from the liver containing the processed 
nutrients is transported into the inferior vena cava 
by the hepatic vein .  Thus, the arterial blood 
carried to the gut is passed through two capillary 
beds before returning to the heart. A system a veins 
that connect two capillary beds is referred to as a 
portal system. 
THE ORAL CAVITY 
 Digestion begins as food enters the oral 
cavity (mouth). Teeth begin the mechanical 
digestion of food. Tooth development occurs in two 
stages.  First, 20 deciduous or baby teeth eru pt.  
They are later replaced by the 32 permanent teeth-
eight in each quadrant.  In each quadrant, beginning 
at the front and moving back, there are 2  incisors, 
the cutting teeth; 1 cuspid or canine, the tearing 
teeth; 2 premolars or bicuspids, the grinding teeth; 
and 3 molars or tricuspids, also for grinding.  The 
last molar in each quadrant is the wisdom tooth.  
There are no deciduous molars; molars erupt only 
once as permanent teeth. 
 Chemical digestion begins as saliva is 
mixed with the food.  Saliva is  secreted by three 
pairs of exocrine salivary glands: the parotid 
glands, in each cheek below and anterior to the 
ears; the submandibular glands in the posterior 
lower jaw; and the sublingual glands  in the 
anterior part of the lower jaw  under the tongue .  
Saliva enters the oral cavity through salivary ducts. 
It contains water, salts and significant amounts of 
salivary amylase, an enzyme  that breakdowns the 
complex carbohydrate  called starch i nto the 
disaccharides called maltose.  Saliva secretion is 
under n eural control. The parasympathetic 
nervous system increases flow, the sympathetic 
decreases flow. 
 The tongue helps to position food in the 
oral cavity for chewing and swallowing. Taste 
buds on the surface of the tongue function as 
chemoreceptors, helping us to identify the nature of 
substances we are taking into the mouth. 
THE ESOPHAGUS 
 From the oral cavity, food moves into the 
pharynx or throat, where voluntary muscles pass it 
into the esophagus by means of the swallowing 
reflex.  The esophagus connects the pharynx to the 
stomach.  It is about ten inches in length  and runs 
posterior to the heart and trachea along the dorsal 
wall of the thoracic cavity. From the esophagus 
down, the digestive tract has a similar pattern of 
layering in all segments.  The inn er lining of the 
tract is mucous membrane (mucosa).  Next is a 
layer of submucosal tissue containing blood and 
lymph vessels and nerves.  Then there are the 
visceral muscle layers.  The inner layer is a circular 
layer and the outer layer runs longitudinall y.  
Finally, the outer tissue layer is the fibroserous 
layer or serosa. The only secretion of the 
esophagus is mucus.  It functions in protecting and

===== PAGE 83 =====
79 
 
lubricating the inner mucosal tissues of the 
esophagus.   
PERISTALSIS 
 The movement of food downward towar d 
the stomach and then on through the rest of the 
digestive tract is caused by waves of muscle 
contractions referred to as peristaltic contractions.  
A ring of smooth muscle circling the tube constricts 
and the constriction is moved forward, squeezing 
the contents of the tube ahead of it. In the gut, the 
waves of peristalsis move from the oral end toward 
the anal end.  Peristaltic waves of contraction are 
used to move material through many other smooth 
muscle tubes of the body as well.  Peristalsis is 
stimulated by parasympathetic nerve impulses 
initiated by distension of the gut with food. But 
visceral muscle can contract without nerve 
stimulation. As the tension in the wall of the 
intestine increases to a certain limit, the muscle 
spontaneously contracts, displacing the food 
downstream and thereby relieving the tension. 
 Multiple rings of muscle sometimes 
contract simultaneously, helping mix the contents 
with digestive juices. These back-and-forth 
contractions are called segmentation contractions.   
THE STOMACH 
 The stomach occupies the upper left 
portion of the abdominal cavity.  The four regions 
of the stomach, shown in the figure, are the cardia, 
the fundus, the body and the antrum. The stomach 
wall has the same basic layers described above 
(mucosa, submucosa, muscular layers, serosa) but 
there are differences in the structure of the layers.  
For example, there is a third inner oblique muscle 
layer below the circular and longitudinal.  The 
mucosa has three types of glandular cells.  Besides 
the goblet cells that secrete mucus, there are also 
parietal cells that secrete hydrochloric acid (HCl) 
and peptic cells that secrete a digestive precursor 
enzyme called pepsinogen. The role of these 
secretions is discussed below. 
 As food is deposited in the stomach, th e 
stomach is able to distend.  This is possible because 
the walls of the empty stomach form thick folds 
called rugae.  Also, the stomach muscles reflexly 
relax to allow distension as food enters.  Under 
neural and endocrine control, the stomach can act 
as both a digestive organ and a food storage organ.   
The functions of the stomach are: 
 storage of partially digested food (now called 
chyme) 
 secretion of gastric juices by the glandular cells 
 mixing of food by the three muscular layers 
 secretion of intrinsic factor required for vitamin 
B-12 absorption 
 some absorption of water, alcohol, fat soluble 
drugs and vitamins 
 secretion of the hormone gastrin 
 partial digestion of proteins.                  
 The processes of gastric (stomach) 
digestion has been divided into three phases:  
   The Cephalic Phase.  This phase is reflexly 
initiated by the sight and smell of good food.  
Parasympathetic (vagus nerves) impulses sent to 
the stomach prepare it for the food's arrival by 
stimulating secretory activity and muscle 
contractions even before the food has entered.  
Thus it is important to have food that looks, 
tastes and smells good. 
 The Gastric Phase.  The presence of food in the 
stomach initiates this phase.  The lower region 
of the stomach secretes the hormone gastrin into 
the blood.  When the blood containing gastrin 
reaches the fundus and body of the stomach, it 
causes secretion of gastric juices.  Parietal cells 
secrete hydrochloric acid (HCl). HCl secretion is 
mediated by histamine, released from the 
stomach lining. Peptic cells secrete pepsinogen.  
In the acidic environment provided by HCl, 
pepsinogen is converted to the enzyme pepsin 
that acts by breaking large proteins into small 
protein fragments.  Digestion of the stomach 
mucosa itself is prevented by the mucus 
secretions of goblet cells.  Digestive activity of 
the stomach is facilitated by churning and 
mixing caused by stomach muscle contractions.

===== PAGE 84 =====
80 
 
 The Intestinal Phase.  Chyme entering the small 
intestine stimulates it to release the hormone 
cholecystokinin. Besides exerting control on the 
pancreas and gallbladder, cholecystokinin 
inhibits gastric digestion in the stomach. In 
effect, the intestines are signaling the stomach to 
stop digesting and begin storing chyme until the 
intestines are finished processing th e newly 
arrived material. Also, distension of the small 
intestine produces an enterogastric nerve reflex 
that reduces gastric activity by inhibition of the 
parasympathetic nerves to the stomach.  
 Emptying of the stomach is controlled by 
the pyloric sphincter muscle located between the 
stomach and small intestine. 
THE SMALL INTESTINE 
 The small intestine is found folded in the 
abdominal cavity below the liver and stomach.  In 
life (with muscle tone) it is probably 10 to 12 feet 
long. The first portion of th e small intestine is the 
"C" shaped duodenum.  It's less than a foot in 
length.  Next is the jejunum making up about 2/5 
of the remaining length of the small intestine.  Last 
is the ileum.  There is no apparent anatomical 
division between the jejunum and i leum, but on 
close examination, the jejunum has a thicker and 
more vascular wall.   
 The inner layer of the small intestine 
(mucosa) is very velvety in appearance because of 
the millions of finger -like projections called villi 
that line the inner wall.  These projections increase 
the surface area of the small intestine for more 
efficient absorption of nutrients.  Below the mucosa 
is a submucosal layer with blood vessels and lymph 
vessels to transport absorbed nutrients through the 
circulation.  Next are the smooth muscle layers, an 
inner circular and outer longitudinal layer.  These 
muscles move the chyme through the intestinal tract 
by peristaltic contractions  and mix with 
segmentation contractions.  The outer supporting 
layer of the small intestine is the serosa, part of the 
serous membrane system of the abdominal cavity.  
The functions of the small intestine include: 
 completing the process of digestion 
 absorbing products of digestion into the blood 
 secreting necessary hormones and enzymes. 
 In the small intestine, pancreatic juices, bile 
and intestinal secretions all help to bring digestion 
to completion. Some of the digestive juices are 
produced by the exocrine portion of the pancreas.  
The pancreas is a flat, glandular organ located 
below the stomach.  Pa ncreatic juices are of two 
types. The first is a watery type rich in 
bicarbonate ions that neutralizes the acidic chyme 
entering the duodenum.  When acidic chyme enters 
the duodenum, the duodenum releases the hormone 
secretin into the blood.  When the horm one 
secretin reaches the pancreas, the release of the 
watery juice is stimulated.  The juice is carried to 
the duodenum through the pancreatic duct, where 
it neutralizes the stomach acid. 
  The second type of pancreatic juice is an 
enzyme rich mixture.  Ch yme entering the small 
intestine stimulates the duodenum to release the 
hormone cholecystokinin.  In turn, this hormone 
stimulates the pancreas to release enzymes into the 
duodenum through the pancreatic duct.  Digestive 
enzymes are also secreted by the in testinal mucosa 
itself. The enzymes include amylase for digestion 
of starch to the maltose and the enzymes sucrase, 
maltase and lactase that convert the se 
disaccharides to the monosaccharides of glucose, 
fructose, and galactose.   
 Also secreted are proteases including 
trypsin, chymotrypsin and carboxypeptidase, to 
complete the digestion of peptides entering from the 
stomach and lipase that digests emulsified fat to 
fatty acid and glycerol.   
 Fat digestion is facilitated by 
emulsification with bile. Bile is produced by the 
liver and stored in the gallbladder.  The liver 
occupies the upper right side of the abdominal 
cavity and the gallbladder is found on the liver's 
inferior surface. Bile salts and lecithin in bile act to 
emulsify fats, that is, break them down into tiny fat 
droplets.  In this way, the surface area of the fat 
globules is greatly increased making the fat more 
accessible to lipase. This increases the efficiency of 
fat digestion.  The hormones of the duodenum 
described above are also important in controlling 
bile.  Secretin stimulates the liver to produce bile 
from the steroid molecule cholesterol.  Some to the 
bile produced enters the gallbladder where it is 
concentrated and stored. The hormone 
cholecystokinin causes the gallbladder to contract, 
releasing the stored bile into the duodenum.

===== PAGE 85 =====
81 
 
LESSON 27:  ABSORPTION OF NUTRIENTS  
 
 The inner surface of the small intestine has 
millions of small finger-like projections called villi. 
 The villi increase the surface area for absorption by 
about ten times.  In addition, the outer surface of 
each villus is covered with single layer of columnar 
epithelial cells, and the cell membrane at the upper 
surface of the cell has projections called microvilli. 
The villi and microvilli together increase the 
surface area for absorption by at least 500 times.  A 
section through a villus with the associated blood 
and lymph vessels is shown below.   
 The six basic nutrients absorbed from the 
diet are carbohydrates, proteins, fats, vitamins, 
minerals, and water.    
 Minerals include positively charges 
electrolytes like sodium, iron, potassium, calcium 
and magnesium that are all actively transported into 
the capillaries of the villi.  Sodium ion, for 
example, diffuses across the microvilli of the 
epithelial cells lining the small intestine and is then 
actively transported out of the opposite side of the 
cell into the interstitial fluid and blood of the 
capillaries. The hormone aldosterone that 
increased sodium reabsorption in the kidney tubules 
also increases sod ium absorption from the gut.  
Negatively charged ions like chloride ion, are 
absorbed along with the positive ions by attraction 
to the opposite charge.  Ions with a single positive 
charge like sodium ions are more easily absorbed 
than ions with a double positive charge like calcium 
ions or magnesium ions.  Calcium absorption does 
occur more efficiently when vitamin D is available, 
however.  Even so, about 50 times more sodium ion 
is absorbed than calcium ion.  Fortunately, the 
nutritional requirement for divalent ions is not very 
great.  These are just some of the many vital 
minerals absorbed from the intestinal tract each 
day. 
 Carbohydrates are broken down into 
monosaccharides (simple sugars) prior to 
absorption.  The monosaccharides glucose 
(dextrose) and galactose are absorbed into the villi 
by secondary active transport, also called co-
transport.  A sugar and sodium molecule bind 
simultaneously to the carrier protein through which 
sodium diffuses into the cell and the sugar follows 
by co-transport.  Exp enditure of ATP moves the 
sodium ion from the epithelial cell to the interstitial 
space as described above, reestablishing the sodium 
gradient and allowing another sodium and sugar to 
enter. Thus, s ugar can be transported against the 
concentration gradient using sodium transport as 
the source of energy.  The sugar moves out of the 
epithelial cell by facilitated diffusion and enters the 
interstitial fluid and plasma.  Fructose is absorbed 
by facilitated diffusion.  That is, while a carrier is 
required, ATP and sodium transport are not.  
Fructose cannot be carried against a concentration 
gradient.  Of the three major monosaccharides 
transported, galactose is most easily absorbed, then 
glucose and then fructose.  The simple sugars enter 
the capillaries of the villi and are then transported 
by the portal vein to the liver.  In the liver, fructose 
and galactose are converted to glucose and stored. 
 Proteins are digested to tri -peptides, di -
peptides or single amino acids that are then 
absorbed by active transport into the villi.  Most 
peptides are absorbed using the sodium ion co -
transport mechanism described above.  Some are 
absorbed by facilitated diffusion.  The uptake of 
amino acids is very efficient.  Most amino acids are 
already absorbed in the first half of t he small 
intestine.  Because of different binding 
characteristics of the amino acids, at least five

===== PAGE 86 =====
82 
 
different binding proteins have been identified in 
the cell membrane of intestinal endothelial cells.  
The amino acids enter the villi capillaries and move 
to the liver through the portal vein. 
 Fats are absorbed as fatty acids  and 
glycerol.  Bile salts that emulsified the fat, 
facilitating digestion, combine with the fatty acids 
and glycerol forming micelles.  This makes fatty 
acids and glycerol soluble in c hyme.  The micelles 
move toward the epithelial cells where the fatty 
acids and glycerol move by diffusion (not active 
transport) through the lipid membranes into the 
epithelial cells of the villi.  The bile remains behind 
to form more micelles.  Within the  epithelial cells, 
fatty acids and glycerol recombine into fat 
molecules and form small fat globules.  Then a 
protein covering forms around the globule.  This 
protein coated fat globule is called a chylomicron.  
The chylomicrons that then leave the endothe lial 
cells are too large to be absorbed into the capillaries 
and so they diffuse into the lymph by way of the 
more permeable central lacteals.  From there they 
move through the lymphatic system entering the 
blood through the thoracic duct in the neck region.   
 Water is absorbed by osmosis.  As other 
nutrients are absorbed, the interstitial fluids and 
capillary plasma become hyperosmotic and the high 
osmotic pressure (pulling force) draws water out of 
the intestines and into the blood. 
 Vitamins are absorbed by diffusion.  Fat 
soluble vitamins (including vitamins A,D,E, and K) 
are absorbed along with fats.  In fact, after a 
cholecystectomy (gallbladder removal), the less 
efficient absorption of fats often leads to 
deficiencies in the fat-soluble vitamins. 
THE LARGE INTESTINE 
 Chyme passes from the ileum into the first 
segment of the large intestine, called the cecum, 
through the ileocecal valve.  The large intestine, or 
colon, is about 5 feet long.  The segments of the 
colon are illustrated below.  Note the narrow 
extension of the cecum called the vermiform 
(worm-like) appendix, a vestigial organ associated 
with the lymphatic system. From the cecum, 
material moves upward on the right side of the 
abdominal cavity through the ascending colon, 
across the upper part of the abdominal cavity from 
right to left through the transverse colon, down the 
left side of the abdominal cavity through the 
descending colon and back to the midline through 
the sigmoid colon.  The last 8 or so inches of the 
gut is called the rectum and, below it, the anal 
canal opens to the outside.    The muscle layers of 
the colon include inner circular and outer 
longitudinal muscles.  The longitudinal muscles are 
not complete, but form three thick bands called the 
taeniae coli .  Tonic contraction  to these bands 
produce characteristic bulges called the haustra in 
the wall of the colon. 
 The only secretion of the epithelial cells 
lining the colon is mucus.  The mucus lubricates 
the walls of the colon, protects the walls from 
harmful bacterial metabolic by-products and helps 
hold the feces together.  
 Within the colon, bacterial action converts 
the remaining chyme to fecal material.  Absorption 
from the colon is limited mainly to water, some 
electrolytes and vitamins.  Significant amounts of 
sodium an d chloride ions are absorbed from the 
colon.  Water is the absorbed along with the 
electrolytes by osmosis.  The water reabsorption is 
important in determining the final consistency to 
the fecal material.  Too much reabsorption and the 
stool is too firm, leading to constipation. Too little 
water reabsorption and the stool is too watery, 
causing diarrhea.  Although retention of feces in 
constipation is uncomfortable, the potential for 
dehydration makes diarrhea far more dangerous.  
The total daily dietary water intake from solid food 
and liquid is normally about 1.5 liters.  Another 7 
liters is secreted into the digestive tract as mucus, 
saliva and other digestive juices.  Most of this fluid 
volume is reabsorbed in the small intestine.  Only 
about 200 ml enters the colon.  About half of that is 
reabsorbed and the rest excreted with the feces. 
 In the process of metabolizing the colonic 
contents, colonic bacteria produce several important 
vitamins including several B vitamins and vitamin 
K.   The vitamins are then absorbed into the blood.  
Vitamin deficiencies often result when colonic 
bacterial production is inhibited.   Newborns, who

===== PAGE 87 =====
83 
 
have not yet developed a bacterial flora, are at 
higher risk for vitamin deficiency. 
THE LIVER 
 Besides its role in the process of digestion 
and absorption of nutrients associated with the 
digestive system, already discussed, the liver is 
involved with vascular and metabolic functions that 
impact on every other organ system. 
 Vascular Functions . The liver is an 
expansible organ abl e to serve as a blood storage 
area.  Normally, about 10% of the blood volume is 
found in the liver, but the capacity can increase to 
accommodate nearly 30% of the blood volume 
when necessary.  In addition, because the veins in 
the hepatic sinuses are so permeable, nearly half the 
circulating lymph is produced in the liver.  In fact, 
an increase in hepatic venous (outflow) pressure of 
as little as 3 mmHg above normal can cause the 
liver to literally sweat fluid that fills the abdominal 
cavity- a condition called ascites.   
      In the process of absorption from the small 
intestine, it is normal for significant numbers of 
bacteria to be absorbed into the intestinal 
capillaries.  Recall that intestinal blood is 
transported to the liver capillaries through the portal 
vein before being returned to the heart.  In the liver 
sinuses, phagocytes engulf nearly all bacterial cells, 
cleansing the blood before passing it to the hepatic 
vein. 
 Metabolic Functions. The liver buffers the 
blood concentration of glucose.  At mealtime, the 
liver converts excess glucose to glycogen, a 
complex carbohydrate for storage.  (Muscle cells 
are also able to produce glycogen for storage).  This 
process is called glycogenesis and is stimulated by 
the hormone insulin. In addition, excess 
carbohydrate can be converted to fat in the liver and 
then transported to adipose cells for storage.  
Between meals, glycogen is converted back to 
glucose as needed in the process of glycogenolysis. 
The hormone glucagon stimulates this process. In 
addition, if stores of glucose are insufficient to meet 
between meal demands, liver enzymes are actually 
able to convert amino acids or fats to glucose, a 
process called gluconeogenesis.  The hormone 
cortisol initiates this reaction.  The ammonia 
residue left over from amino acid deamination is 
detoxified in the liver by conversion to urea that is 
passed to the kidney for excretion.  This is just one 
example of the liver's ability to break down and 
detoxify many blood borne wastes, hormones and 
drugs. In the liver, deteriorating red blood cells are 
broken down.  The iron from hemoglobin is stored 
while the toxic hemoglobin pigment, bilirubin, is 
passed into the bile for excretion. 
 Besides glycogen and iron, the liver stores 
many other substrates required to maintain  
homeostasis. Vitamins included are A, B-12, and D. 
  Nearly 90% of the plasma proteins are 
produced by the liver.  These include the clotting 
proteins prothrombin and fibrinogen, the vasoactive 
precursor angiotensinogen and the most abundant 
plasma protein, albumin. 
 The liver is also an endocrine gland, 
secreting the hormone somatomedin that mediates 
many of the effects of growth hormone and 
erythropoietin, needed to promote erythrocyte 
production. 
VITAMINS AND MINERALS 
  Below is a table containing a par tial list of 
vitamins and minerals and some of their important 
functions- functions described previously in 
discussions of other organ systems.

produce hormones that are released into the blood, carried to the target organ (organ with receptors for that particular hormone), and 
produce the desired effect.  The important GI hormones involved in the processes of digestion are summarized below.  Note that some
