LESSON 28:  DIGESTIVE WASTES
 
COLONIC MOTILITY 
 Movement of material through the colon is 
somewhat different than in the small intestine.   
Modified weak but continuous contractions of the 
taeniae coli muscles in the ascending colon 
gradually move the forming fecal material toward 
the transverse colon for storage.  The slow process 
can take 8 hours or more.  From the transverse 
colon onward, the muscles are inactive most of the 
time.  But two or three times a day, especially after 
a meal, mass contractions of long segments of the 
smooth muscle occur, first at the transverse colon, 
then farther on down.  These mass contractions 
force the fecal material into the rectum for 
defecation.  Distension of the rectum by fecal 
material initiates the autonomic defecation reflex.  
Mass contractions along with contraction of the 
abdominal muscles and stiffening of the diaphragm 
by breath holding all contribute to the mass 
movement of fecal material.  The outflow from the 
colon is controlled by an intern al and external 
sphincter muscle . The timing of d efecation can 
normally be controlled because the external 
sphincter muscle is voluntary  skeletal muscle.  If 
postponed, material moves back into the sigmoid 
colon and is retained until the next stimulus. 
FECES 
 The average American adult passes about 
130 grams of digestive waste (called feces) each 
day.  The amount is dependent on the type of diet.  
Persons on a vegetarian diet produce significantly 
more.  Plant materials contain large amounts of 
roughage, ma inly in the form of cellulose that 
cannot be digested or absorbed by humans. 
Approximately 75% of the stool is water (about 100 
ml).  Of the dry weight, about 30% is dead bacteria, 
30% roughage indigestible by the human digestive 
system, some fat and a lit tle protein produced by 
the colonic bacteria, and some minerals and 
electrolytes.  Pigments from blood cells broken 
down in the liver and deposited into the small 
intestine along with the bile gives the fecal material 
a golden brown color.  Obstruction of the bile ducts 
would give the feces a slate gray color.   
Fecal consistency of feces depends on 
water content.  Normally, the feces should have the 
consistency of a ripe banana.  If too much water 
remains, diarrhea (literally "flow-through") results. 
Too little water in the feces leads to constipation. 
FLATUS 
 On the average, there is about 150 ml of 
gas, called flatus, in the gastrointestinal tract.  
About a third of that amount is in the stomach; 
mostly swallowed air.  Stomach flatus is primarily 
nitrogen and oxygen.  It is released by eructation 
(belching), absorbed into the blood, or passed on to 
the small intestine.  The back -and-forth motion of 
gas in the pylorus of the stomach and its passage 
through the pyloric sphincter causes gushing sounds 
called borborygmi. 
 Very little gas is normally found in the 
small intestine.  The little that is present produces 
the soft rumbles heard through the abdominal wall 
and are an sign of an active healthy bowel.  Because 
of the large surface area available in the sm all 
intestine, most gas is rapidly absorbed into the 
blood.  Any that might remain would be passed on 
to the colon. 
 About two-thirds of the flatus is found in 
the colon; most of it produced by the colonic 
bacteria in the process of metabolizing digestive 
wastes.  Gases produced include oxygen, nitrogen, 
carbon dioxide, methane, hydrogen and hydrogen 
sulfide.  The combination of these last three gases 
means that colonic gas is not only potentially 
flammable, but explosive as well.  Rare cases of 
colonic gas explosions resulting in serious injury 
have been reported.   
The amount and composition of colonic gas 
depends on the substrates provided to the bacterial 
flora.  Beans and other legumes, for example, 
provide large quantities of oligosaccharides that are 
rapidly metabolized by bacteria yielding significant 
amounts of carbon dioxide gas. 
 The odor of fecal material and colonic 
flatus is due to pungent indole, skatole and thiole 
compounds produced by bacterial action.  The 
thioles, for example, are compounds containing the 
sulfhydryl group (SH-).  Several common examples 
of thiole compounds are: 
 Hydrogen sulfide (H2S), released from rotting 
eggs.

===== PAGE 90 =====
86 
 Methyl mercaptan (CH3-HS), added to odorless 
natural gas as a warning. 
 Butyl mercaptan (CH3-CH2-CH2-CH2-HS), 
component of skunk odorant. 
 Much of the colonic flatus is absorbed.  
Fortunately, the liver metabolizes the foul smelling 
compounds so the odors don't volatilize into expired 
gas.  That which is not absorbed is transmitted to 
the rectum and through the anus.  For this, intra -
abdominal pressure is increased to raise the 
pressure in the rectum.  Gas is expelled through the 
voluntarily contracted external anal sphincter 
providing a slit too narrow for solid feces to pass.  
As gas is expelled, the folds of anal mus cle may 
vibrate and, based on the same principle used by the 
double-reed bassoon, sound a low-pitched note.  
 The receptors that respond to pressure 
against the anal sphincter are quite sensitive to the 
consistency of material exerting the pressure (as 
you may know from experience).  One is usually 
able to judge whether the material is gaseous (and 
risk passing it directly), liquid (usually requiring 
immediate use of a rest room), or solid (able to be 
expelled with less urgency).  
 Finally, the volume of ga s in the colon is 
dependent on the barometric pressure.  During 
sudden decompression at 40,000 feet, a volume of 
100 ml of colonic gas would suddenly expand to 
between 700 and 800 ml - beyond the capacity of 
the colon.  So next time you fly, "in the event o f 
sudden decompression", donning the oxygen mask 
may not be your only problem. 
CONSTIPATION 
 It's difficult to specify when normal 
variation in frequency, size, and quantity of bowel 
movements becomes abnormal.  While most people 
empty their bowel once a d ay, many perfectly 
healthy people defecate after every meal and others 
only once a week.  Too many people believe that 
one must produce results once a day or be 
overwhelmed by the disaster of irregularity. 
 The symptoms of constipation can include 
mental depression, dull headache, restlessness, loss 
of appetite with or without nausea, abdominal 
distention with heaviness and swelling and foul 
breath.  Treatments include bowel irritants or 
stimulants (castor oil), stool softeners (mineral oil) 
and bulk laxatives (prunes, magnesium salts).  The 
bulk laxatives like milk of magnesia and prunes     
contain magnesium salts. As is the case with 
calcium ion (Ca++), magnesium ion (Mg++), having 
a double positive charge (divalent), is not easily 
absorbed into the blood .  It makes the intestinal 
contents hyperosmotic, tending to pull water into 
the stool by osmosis.  This softens the stool and 
adds to the bulk. 
 Voluntary suppression of the defecation 
reflex may lead to constipation.  The longer the 
feces remain in the c olon, the more time available 
for water reabsorption out of the intestinal tract to 
occur.  If a person routinely fails to defecate when 
the reflex occurs, the reflex becomes progressively 
weaker and the person becomes prone to 
constipation. 
 The symptoms of constipation are not due 
to fecal toxins entering the blood.  If the liver is 
healthy, any toxins will be metabolized.  Many 
animals in fact, ingest their feces.  For them, some 
nutrients that could not be digested on first pass can 
be digested on secon d pass after the colonic 
bacteria have worked on them.  The symptoms of 
constipation are related to the presence of bulk in 
the colon.  They can be duplicated by distending the 
colon with an inflated balloon or cotton.  There are 
numerous accounts of peopl e who have retained 
enormous masses of feces for months without 
suffering any serious effects other than the burden 
of carrying a colon ic content of 60-100 pounds of 
fecaliths.  The condition is called megacolon. 
 Once constipation has been relieved by a 
bowel movement, the symptoms of constipation 
disappear almost immediately.  This also argues 
against toxins as the cause of the symptoms.  If 
toxins were involved, it would take much more time 
for them to be cleared from the system. 
 A much more serious pro blem is diarrhea 
that can potentially lead to severe dehydration and 
loss of blood pressure.

===== PAGE 91 =====
87 
LESSON 29: METABOLIC PATHWAYS 
 
The two basic components of metabolism 
are shown in the figure below.  The first is 
anabolism, the process of forming large molecules 
from smaller ones.  The example shown is the 
synthesis of large protein molecules from individual 
amino acids.  Protein synthesis is regulated by DNA 
segments called genes that code of a strand of 
messenger RNA. The RNA is used to ensure the 
proper alignment of amino acids i n a given strand 
of protein. The process requires energy in the form 
of ATP.  
The second process is catabolism, 
breaking down large molecules into smaller ones. 
An example is the digestion of large complex 
carbohydrates, for exampl e breaking down large 
starch molecules into glucose. Ca rbohydrate 
catabolism doesn't end with glucose. In this lesson 
we'll quickly review how glucose enters cells and, 
in combination with O 2, is further catabolized to 
CO2 and H 2O for the purpose of provid ing 
sufficient energy to synthesize ATP from ADP plus 
inorganic phosphate (P i).  The series of chemical 
reactions in the catabolism process are all carefully 
controlled in step-wise fashion involving series of 
specific enzymes.  
A byproduct of the chemical  pathways 
described above is heat production. Most of the 
energy derived from the catabolism of glucose is in 
the form of hear. So we must also consider how the 
body regulates temperature, faced with both 
internal and external thermal stresses.  
 The rela tionship between anabolism and 
catabolism for the two examples given is shown in 
the figure. Protein synthesis requires ATP drrived 
from the catabolism of glucose.  But the catabolism 
of glucose cannot occur without protein enzymes.  
It's a chicken and egg problem! 
 The catabolism of glucose links the 
digestive system that provides the fuel (g lucose) 
and the respiratory system that provides the oxygen 
needed for the complete oxidation of glucose and 
eliminates the carbon dioxide waste produced.   
Conversion of glucose and oxygen to 
carbon dioxide and water involves three processes: 
glycolysis, the citric acid cycle , and the electron 
transport system. 
GLYCOLYSIS: 
1 GLUCOSE  2 PYRUVIC ACID + 4 H+ + 4 e- 
Energy: 2 ADP + 2 Pi  2 ATP 
Glycolysis occurs in the cytoplasm of the 
cell and does not require oxygen.  The stepwise 
conversion of a 6 -carbon glucose molecule to two 
3-carbon pyruvic acid molecules involves step-wise 
production of intermediates. Production of each is 
controlled by a unique enzyme.  As the rea ctions 
proceed, there is a net production of two ATP 
molecules per glucose molecule. The overall 
reaction is summarized above.  The electrons and 
hydrogen ions produced will be important in the 
last phase of glucose catabolism, the electron 
transport system. 
 The process of 
glycolysis occurs in several 
stages: 
 Phosphorylation. A 
phosphate molecule is 
added to glucose to 
maintain the 
concentration gradient 
for glucose uptake and 
trap glucose within the 
cell. 
 Priming. A second 
phosphate is added to 
create an i somer- two 
halves of the molecule

===== PAGE 92 =====
88 
are mirror images of eachother. 
 Cleavage. The 6 -carbon molecule is split 
creating two identical 3-carbon molecules. 
 Oxidation. The 3 -carbon molecules are 
oxidized with electrons transferred to the 
oxidizing agent, NAD+ (nicotinomide adenine 
dinucleotide). 
 Dephosphorylation. The transfer of phosphate 
from the glucose metabolites to ADP, forming 
ATP and the final product, two pyruvic acids. 
If sufficient oxygen is available, the pyruvic acid 
produced enters the mitochondria and the next stage 
of metabolism begins.  If there is not sufficient 
oxygen, pyruvic acid is converted to lactic acid that 
accumulates in the tissues causing a local acidosis.  
The low tissue pH contributes to the pain associated 
with muscle fatigue.  With oxygen, the pyruvic acid 
continues on to enter the mitochondria where 
oxygen-requiring ( aerobic) processes are 
responsible for most of the ATP produced from the 
metabolism of glucose. Indeed, without the ATP 
produced from the aerobic processes within the 
mitochondria, life processes would cease. 
THE CITRIC ACID CYCLE: 
2 PYRUVIC ACID  6 CO2 + 20 H+ + 20 e- 
Energy: 2 ADP + 2 Pi  2 ATP 
 The citric acid cycle occurs in the 
mitochondria.   Oxygen is not used in the cycle, 
but if the last phase (the electron tr ansport system) 
is stopped for lack of oxygen, the products of the 
citric acid cycle accumulate and the cycle stops. 
The citric acid cycle begins with the 
combining of a molecule of coenzyme -A (CoA) 
with an acetyl group formed from pyruvic acid. In 
the conversion, a carbon is lost from pyruvic acid 
and forms CO2. The resulting 2-carbon acetyl CoA 
molecules enter the cycle. In the first step, the 2 -
carbon acetyl CoA combines with the 4 -carbon 
end product of the cycle to form the 6 -carbon 
citric acid molecule for which the cycle is named.  
Key events occurring as part of the citric 
acid cycle are: 
 Cleavage of the 3 carbons from the initial 
pyruvic acid molecule. Each carbon is used 
to synthesize a molecule of CO2.    
 Phosphorilation of ADP. For each acetyl -
CoA entering the cycle, a molecule of ATP is 
produced. 
 Oxidation of citric acid cycle substrates. In 
each oxidation step, the electrons removed 
are carried off by the oxidizing agents NAD + 
or FAD (flavin adenine dinucleotide). In all, 
ten electrons are obtain ed from each acetyl 
CoA molecule passed through the cycle. 
Again, each reaction in the cycle requires 
its own enzyme, and the enzymes are found in the 
mitochondria.  
The citric acid cycle produces 1 molecule 
of ATP and 3 carbon dioxide molecules per 
molecule of pyruvic acid. A molecule of glucose 
provides two pyruvic acids. Thus, 2 ATP and 6 CO2 
molecules are produced per molecule of glucose. In 
addition, note that more hydrogen ions and 
electrons are generated - a total of 20 each per 
molecule of glucose.  
THE ELECTRON TRANSPORT SYSTEM: 
24 H+ + 24 e- + 6 O2  12 H2O 
Energy: 34 ADP + 34 Pi  34 ATP 
 The electron transport system consists of a 
series of electron carrier enzymes located on the 
inner mitochondrial membranes.  The electrons, 24 
per molecule of glucose, are provided by glycolysis 
and the citric acid cycle and carried to the electron 
transport system by the oxidizing agents mentioned 
above.  Each carrier enzyme accepts an electron 
pair and passes it on to the next carrier.  It can then 
accept another electron pair.  If the next enzyme in 
the series cannot pass its electrons, the cycle stops.  
So it is crucial that the last enzyme carrier in the 
series be able to pass on its electrons.  This is where 
oxygen is used.  The last enzyme in the series 
donates the electrons needed for hydrogen ions and 
oxygen to combine to form water.   As long as 
oxygen is available to act as the final electron 
acceptor, the electrons continue to flow down the 
chain. So besides producing CO 2, mitochondria 
require O2 used to form H2O. 
 In addition to their outer lipid membrane, 
mitochondria have an inner folded membrane 
within which the enzymes needed for the transfer of 
electrons are embedded. As electrons are passed 
from one enzyme to the next, hydrogen ions are 
transported from the central region of the

===== PAGE 93 =====
89 
mitochondria to the region between the inner and 
outer membranes. As the H+ ions accumulate, a 
concentration gradient is created for the diffusion of 
H+ ions back to the central region. To return, the 
H+ ions pass through a p rotein pore with an 
associated ATP-synthase enzyme. Passage of H+ 
ions activates the ATP -synthase that results in the 
production of ATP. In all, up to 34 molecules of 
ADP are converted to ATP for each molecule of the 
original glucose oxidized. The electron  carrier 
system is responsible for most of the ATP produced 
from glucose catabolism, but it can't operate 
without hydrogen ions and electrons supplied by 
way of glycolysis and the citric acid cycle.  And it 
can't function without oxygen from the lungs. 
GLUCOSE METABOLISM SUMMARY: 
1 GLUCOSE + 6 O2  12 H2O + 6 CO2 
Energy: 38 ADP + 38 Pi  38 ATP 
CARBOHYDRATES 
 While carbohydrate has other uses, most 
carbohydrates are converted to glucose and 
oxidized to produce ATP energy as described 
above. Excess glucose can be stored as glycogen in 
muscle and the liver, or it can be converted to fat 
and stored in the liver or in adipose tissue.  If 
insufficient glucose is available, fatty acids , 
glycerol, and deaminated (nitrogen removed) 
amino acids  can enter the pathways  described 
above to produce ATP.  
LIPIDS  
 Lipid molecules include the triglycerides, 
phospholipids and cholesterol.  The most common 
dietary lipid is triglyceride.  It consists of the three-
carbon molecule glycerol with three fatty acid 
chains, one chain bonded to each glycerol carbon.  
The fatty acids are each 12 to 20 carbons in length. 
Triglyceride is used as insulation (subcutaneous fat) 
and for support and protection (fat pads 
surrounding the kidneys). But m ost triglyceride is 
used as fuel for energy.  While most tissues can use 
triglyceride to provide energy, there are some 
exceptions. The brain is an important one. It can 
only use glucose.  
The first step in fat catabolism is hydrolysis 
of triglyceride into glycerol and free fatty acids. 
The hydrolysis products are then carried through 
the blood and taken up as needed by active cells. 
Within the cells, glycerol is converted to glycerol-3-
phosphate and enters the glycolysis pathway at the 
cleavage stage.  
Fatty acids must be transported into the 
mitochondria. There the carbons are broken off in 
pairs to form acetyl coenzyme A (acetyl CoA), the 
molecule that enters the citric acid cycle. The 
process is referred to a beta oxidation because the 
series of reactions leading to formation of acetyl 
CoA require  oxidation of the second or “beta” 
carbon. Beta oxidation provides an additional 
source of hydrogen ions and electrons for the 
electron transport system. The 18-carbon fatty acid 
stearate, for example, provides 146 molecules of 
ATP from the electrons and h ydrogen ions 
generated through beta oxidation and the citric acid 
cycle. 
The liver produces significant quantities of 
acetyl CoA through beta oxidation, especially 
where there is an additional need for energy derived 
from fat. The amount of acetyl CoA is u sually in 
excess of that required by liver cells themselves. 
The excess is transported through the blood to other 
cells. But acetyl CoA is first converted to ketoacids 
for transport. In certain conditions, diabetes 
mellitus for example, the level of ketoacids in blood 
can become high enough to cause ketoacidosis 
leading to suppression of the nervous system and 
even coma.   
 Phospholipids are not used as energy 
sources.  They are formed when one of the three 
fatty acids of the triglyceride is replaced with a  
phosphate group. Phospholipids are important 
structurally. They are abundant in bimolecular 
lipid layer of cell membranes and in nerve tissue 
(the myelin sheath). Cholesterol is not 
metabolized for energy. More than 75% of the 
cholesterol in the body is c onverted to bile in the 
liver.  Significant amounts are found in the outer 
layers of the skin, helping to provide a barrier 
against excessive water loss by evaporation.  
Some cholesterol is also used in the synthesis of 
steroid hormones. 
 Since fat and water don’t mix, lipids would 
condense into large plugs of fat if carried freely 
though the watery body fluids. Therefore, fats are 
transported combined with proteins to form 
lipoproteins. The more protein there is, the higher 
the density of the lipoprotein. Very low density 
lipoproteins (VLDL) have the most fat and

===== PAGE 94 =====
90 
cholesterol. As fats are deposited in adipose tissue, 
the lipoprotein becomes less dense - low-density 
lipoprotein (LDL). High density lipoproteins 
(HDL), with proportionately more protein, 
scavenge cholesterol and carry it to the liver to be 
metabolized. HDLs are therefore, beneficial 
because they lower blood cholesterol. 
PROTEINS 
Proteins are digested to amino acids in the 
gut. Amino acids are absorbed into the blood, 
transported to cells and used to form different 
proteins with amino acid sequences determined by 
messenger RNA derived from DNA genes. Some 
proteins are used structurally, as in collagen of bone 
or actin and myosin of muscle. Some function as 
enzymes, facilitating chemical reactions in the body 
to maintain life functions.  Some are used as  
chemical mediators- certain protein hormones, for 
example. 
Each of the 20 amino acids that form 
proteins can, with the proper enzymes, be converted 
to a substrate in the citric acid cycle.  The first step 
in amino acid conversion is removal of the nitrogen 
containing amine group.  The nitrogen is converted 
to urea and excreted in the urine.  Amino acids can 
be obtained from the diet or taken from tissue 
proteins or enzymes.  If dietary protein is 
insufficient, tissue protein catabolism can exceed 
tissue protein anabolism and the victim enters a 
state of negative nitrogen balance associated with 
starvation.  A positive nitrogen balance means 
anabolism exceeds catabolism, associated with 
youthful growth.  To synthesize proteins, all 
necessary amino acids must be available. The body 
can produce most, but not all, of them. Therefore, 
diet is an essential source of amino acids. Meat 
contains all 20  amino acids . Vegetables do not. 
Therefore, a vegetarian diet sh ould consist of a 
mixture of vegetables to ensure all amino acids are 
included.  
REGULATION OF FOOD INTAKE 
 Consistent with the principle that control of 
functions vital to life originate from lower brain 
centers, control of feeding is centered in the 
hypothalamus. Lateral hypothalamic centers 
stimulate feeding (hunger centers) while more 
medial centers are inhibitory (satiety centers).  
Structures even lower on the brain stem control the 
mechanics of eating; salivation, chewing, 
swallowing.  Higher center s in the cerebral cortex 
can impact appetite as well, since the sight, smell or 
taste of certain consumables can “put you off your 
food”.   
 Regulation of feeding involves both long -
term and short -term mechanisms.  Levels of fat 
storage within the body pro vide long -term 
regulation. When supplies of triglyceride are high, 
feeding centers are suppressed.  A chemical 
mediator called leptin, released from fat cells, is 
involved. Leptin produced in proportion to fat 
reserves acts to suppress appetite. With fat reserves 
elevated, caloric intake can be reduced. 
As we continue eating, our appetite 
disappears more rapidly than the time it takes for 
ingested food to be converted to fat.  So there must 
also be short -term mechanisms to control feeding.  
For this, gastrointestinal distention and secretion of 
certain hormones, including cholecystokinin, have 
both been shown to strongly inhibit feeding centers. 
In addition, studies on subjects with esophageal 
fistulas (opening allowing food in the esophagus to 
pass directly  out of the body) shows that just 
passing food through the mouth eventually 
suppresses appetite.  
The only chemical mediator shown to 
stimulate appetite  is the hormone ghrelin. It is 
produced by the stomach lining and the rate of 
production increases as the stomach empties.

===== PAGE 95 =====
91 
LESSON 30: ENERGETICS AND THERMOREGULATION 
 
ENERGY UNITS 
Energy consumption in the body is 
measured in Calorie units.  A Calorie is equal to 
the energy required to increase the temperature of 
1000 grams of water by 1 o C. (Kilocalorie is more 
correct although Calorie with a capital ‘C’ is used.) 
A gram of carbohydrate or protein provides about 4 
Calories of energy.  A gram of fat provides about 9 
Calories.  Water provides no metabolic energy but 
is usually a significant part of fo ods containing 
carbohydrates and proteins. On the other hand, fat 
and water don’t mix.  So, the pat of butter on a 
baked potato can provide more energy than the 
whole potato itself.  
To meet the minimum energy needs of the 
body, the adult requirement is ap proximately 2400 
Calories per day. Increasing level of activity 
increases the Caloric requirement. Jogging 
consumes about 500 Calories per hour; walking, 
about 200 Calories per hour; watching TV (couch 
potato), about 100 Calories per hour or 2400 
Calories per day. 
If Caloric input exceeds output, we gain 
weight. If output exceeds input, we lose weight. 
THERMOREGULATION 
Body core temperature is normally 
maintained at about 37 o C (98.6 o F).  The core 
temperature refers to the temperature in the interior 
as op posed to the surface skin temperature that 
fluctuates with the environment.   Heat is a by -
product of metabolism.  As with a car engine, when 
the body burns fuel, it provides work energy plus 
heat.  All living cells are constantly consuming a 
basal level of energy that is continuously being 
replaced.  Therefore, a basal level of heat is 
continuously produced by the body.  During 
exercise or under the influence of certain hormones, 
such as those from the adrenal medulla or thyroid 
gland, energy and heat pro duction increase.  The 
body must eliminate any heat in excess of that 
required to keep body temperature normal.   In 
cold conditions, the body can produce needed 
heat by increasing metabolic activity.  
      The body exchanges heat with the 
environment.  U nder normal conditions at room 
temperature, about 60% of excess body heat is 
exchanged by radiation- infrared heat rays 
transmitted by everything in the universe.  A second 
route for heat exchange is conduction- physical 
contact with an object of different  temperature.  
Heat flows from the warmer to the cooler object.  
About 3% of excess heat is exchanged in this way.  
Convection is a special case of conduction where 
heat is conducted to air.  Cool air comes in contact 
with the warm body, picks up heat and rises away 
to be replaced by more cool air.  About 15% excess 
heat is lost in this way.  In hot environments we 
may facilitate this process with a fan.  Finally, 
about 22% of excess heat is lost by evaporation.  
About 600 ml of water evaporates off the ski n and 
out of the lungs each day.  To convert a gram of 
water to water vapor requires 0.58 Calories of heat 
energy, so the body loses about 350 Calories of heat 
per day by evaporation.   
Mechanism Exchange* 
Radiation (infrared waves) 60% 
Conduction (direct contact) 3% 
Convection (air contact) 15% 
Evaporation (of sweat) 22% 
* normal room temperature, at rest 
 The percent heat loss through the different 
mechanisms can change. During exercise, for 
example, the percent heat loss by evaporation of 
sweat increases significantly. Convection becomes 
a serious source of heat loss for exposed flesh on a 
cold and windy day (wind chill factor). 
ADAPTING TO COLD TEMPERATURES 
 In cold weather we use several mechanisms 
to reduce heat loss: 
 Vasoconstriction of peripheral blood vessels in 
the skin keeps more blood (and the heat that it 
contains) in the core of the body.  
 Cold receptors make us uncomfortable so we 
add clothes or crawl under a blanket to better 
insulate the surface.   
 Increasing metabolic heat production p rovides 
additional heat by: 
− increasing muscle activity. Muscle 
contractions, including those that cause 
shivering, produce heat. 
− increasing brown fat  metabolism.  With 
brown fat, production of ATP and heat by 
mitochondria is uncoupled.  Sympathetic 
stimulation causes these mitochondria to

===== PAGE 96 =====
92 
produce heat without ATP.  This is best 
developed in infants. 
− increasing thyroid hormone production.  
Thyroid hormones increase metabolic 
rate.  This adaptation probably requires 
months of continuous cold exposure to 
become effective.     
 Contracting arrector pili muscles of the skin 
provides more insulation for fur bearing animals, 
but in man just causes “goose bumps". 
 The above mechanisms would maintain the 
normal body temperature of someone naked at 
temperatures as low as 55oF.     
ADAPTING TO HOT TEMPERATURES  
 In warm weather we need to eliminate 
excess metabolic heat.  If environmental 
temperature is below body temperature, the 
following mechanisms are effective: 
 Vasodilating peripheral vessels sends heat to the 
surface where it can be transferred to the 
environment. 
 Heat receptors make us uncomfortable so we 
eliminate clothing for better heat exchange with 
the environment.  
 Heat convection can be increased by air 
movement, so we use a fan or find a cool breeze. 
 If the environmental temperature is actually 
higher than core temperature, the above 
mechanisms become ineffective.  The body is now 
gaining more heat by radiation, convection and 
conduction from objects in the environment than it 
is losing by these mechanisms.  There are two 
additional mechanisms that are effective at any 
temperature. 
 Decreasing activity reduces metabolic heat 
production.      
 Producing sweat using sweat glands and then 
evaporating sweat off the surface of the skin 
consumes body heat to provide  the energy 
necessary to convert water from the liquid to gas 
(vapor).  This works best if the air is not already 
saturated with water vapor (with low humidity). 
 Normal core temperature can be maintained in 
temperatures as high as 140oF of dry heat. 
TEMPERATURE CONTROL CENTER 
 The "set -point" for temperature is 
controlled in the hypothalamus of the brain.  
Surface receptors associated with the skin and core 
receptors associated with the spinal cord and 
abdominal viscera sense cold and heat. The core 
temperature will change by 1 degree for every 30 
degrees change in environmental temperature.  The 
gain (correction divided by error) is therefore about 
30, indicating a very efficient hypothalamic control. 
There are separate receptors that respond to heat 
and cold. They are not thought to respond to 
temperature directly, but the changes in metabolic 
rate within the cell that results from the change in 
temperature. That is, heat increases the rate of 
chemical reactions and metabolic rate.  
FEVER 
  Fever induced by bacterial infection is a 
defense mechanism of the body.  This is an 
example of how it's supposed to work:  The core 
temperature is "set" by the hypothalamus.  Certain 
invading bacteria release pyrogens. A pyrogen is 
any molecule that is able to increase the "set point". 
The body raises the temperature to the new "set 
point" by cold adaptation mechanisms described 
above including muscle contractions, the shivers 
that signal fever onset, and “chills” that prompt us 
to bundle up.  The higher temperatures de stroy the 
bacteria and no more pyrogens are released.  
Without pyrogens, the "set point" returns to normal. 
 The body brings temperature back to normal by 
stimulating heat adaptation mechanisms including 
sweat production; a sign that the fever is 
"breaking".   
 While some pyrogens act directly on the 
hypothalamus, others stimulate leukocytes exposed 
to pyrogenic bacteria to release interleukin -1.  
Prostaglandins, local hormone -like mediators, are 
required for this. Interleukin -1 then causes the 
hypothalamus to raise body temperature.  Aspirin 
inhibits prostaglandin and is thus effective in 
lowering body temperature.  Since fever is a 
defense mechanism, preventing it by taking aspirin 
isn't necessarily a good idea  under normal 
circumstances.

===== PAGE 97 =====
93 
LESSON 31:  HORMONE CHARACTERISTICS 
 
The two master control systems of the body 
are the nervous system and endocrine system. In 
the nervous system, discussed earlier, control is 
exerted by rapidly transmitted electrical impulses 
conducted to specific locations by nerve fibers.  In 
the endocrine system, certain glands release 
chemicals called hormones that are carried to 
target organs by the bloodstream and there bind to 
specific receptor sites. Unlike nerve impulses, 
hormones travel more slowly, being limited by the 
flow rate of blood. Hormones produce a more 
generalized effect because, unlike nerve impulses 
that can be targeted to specific synapses, hormones 
stimulate all of its  receptors in contact with the 
blood flow. 
 Endocrine glands are ductless.  Their 
secretions (hormones) are carried by the circulatory 
system.  Exocrine glands, like salivary glands or 
sweat glands, have ducts to carry their secretions to 
the site of action.   
 Hormones are specific substances that are 
secreted by endocrine glands and put into t he 
circulatory system, whereby they go to their site of 
action and produce an effect.  There are two basic 
types of hormones based on chemical structure - 
steroid and non-steroid hormones.  
 Steroid hormones produced in the  reproductive 
organs and adrenal co rtex are synthesized from 
cholesterol.  
 Non-steroid hormones are synthesized from 
amino acids. There are three basic varieties: 
− Amines are modified amino acids. 
Hormones of the thyroid gland and adrenal 
medulla are modifications of the amino 
acid tyrosine. 
− Polypeptides are short chains of amino 
acids, less than ten in length. They include   
hormones synthesized in the hypothalamus. 
− Proteins are longer chains of amino acids. 
Hormones of the pituitary gland and 
pancreas are examples.  
 Steroid hormones act by  entering the cell 
and its nucleus and activating certain genes that in 
turn stimulate production of certain proteins.  These 
proteins then produce the effect. Because of the 
time required for this process to occur, steroid 
hormones are slow acting, requiring 1 or 2 days. 
  Most non-steroid hormones act at the cell 
membrane by activating a second messenger that in 
turn initiates the cascade of reactions leading to the 
desired effect.  In one commonly used pathway, the 
hormone activates an enzyme called adenylate 
cyclase in the cell membrane.  The enzyme 
converts ATP molecules to cyclic adenosine 
monophosphate (cAMP), the second messenger.  
The cAMP triggers a sequence of events leading to 
activation of proteins that lead to the desired effect. 
 Because one m olecule of hormone can activate 
many molecules of the second messenger, with 
further amplification occurring down the cascade, 
the second messenger system amplifies the effect of 
the hormone.  Since non-steroid hormones act at the 
cell membrane, their acti on occurs more quickly 
compared to steroid hormones.  They can produce 
their effect within minutes or hours. 
PROSTAGLANDINS 
     Prostaglandins are chemical mediators 
derived from fatty acids.  They are, in effect, locally 
acting hormones.  Unlike true hor mones that are 
released and carried in the blood to a distant site of 
action, prostaglandins act locally in the same organ 
in which they are produced.  They are produced as 
needed and are rapidly destroyed after use.  
Prostaglandins are found in most organ  systems.  
They may act to cause visceral muscle contractions. 
In blood vessels they may cause vasoconstriction. 
In the uterus, they can cause contractions associated 
with menstrual cramps. Some relax visceral muscle 
and dilate blood vessels. Some stimulate secretion. 
They can cause secretion of some hormones and 
secretion of stomach acid, for example.  Some play 
a role in causing inflammation. Arthritis is an 
example. They are involved in the process of fever. 
The therapeutic effects of aspirin are all rel ated to 
its ability to inhibit the actions of prostaglandins - 
reducing inflammation, fever and pain. 
CONTROL OF OUTPUT 
 For some hormones, control of output is 
straightforward. The endocrine glands that regulate 
blood glucose and calcium levels respond dir ectly 
to concentrations of these molecules in blood. 
 Release of some hormones is stimulated by 
the nervous system. Receptors sensing the need for 
the hormone send sensory impulses to the nervous

===== PAGE 98 =====
94 
system. Form the brain, motor impulses are sent to 
the appropriate gland. (Recall the effectors of the 
nervous system are both muscles and glands). 
 Hormones of the hypothalamus and 
pituitary gland are involved in the control of 
endocrine output as well. The hypothalamus is 
anterior to the upper part of the brain s tem.  The 
pituitary gland is just below it connected by a 
stalk.  The pituitary gland is also referred to as the 
hypophysis.  The posterior portion derived from 
brain tissue is called the neurohypophysis and the 
anterior portion derived from facial tissue is called 
the adenohypophysis.   
The hypothalamus produces  releasing 
hormones and inhibiting hormones that regulate 
the endocrine activity of the anterior pituitary 
gland.  The anterior pituitary hormones, in turn, 
control the endocrine activity of other target 
endocrine glands. For example, thyrotropin 
releasing hormone ( TRH) produced by the 
hypothalamus causes the anterior pituitary to 
release thyroid stimulating hormone ( TSH).  The 
hormone TSH then stimulates the thyroid gland to 
release thyroxine and triiodothyronine.  
Endocrine activity of the adrenal cortex, ovaries 
and testes are similarly controlled, as summarized 
in the table below.  
       Hormone 
release by the target 
gland is controlled by 
a negative feedback 
loop process 
whereby high blood 
levels of the target 
gland hormone act to 
inhibit the production 
of rel easing and 
stimulating hormones 
and levels of the 
target gland hormone are reduced. Low blood 
levels of the target gland hormone act to stimulate 
the production of releasing and stimulating  
hormones. “Negative” simply refers to the change 
in direction. Negative feedback is  an important 
controlling mechanism in the nervous system as 
well. Receptors sense blood pressure is going too 
high and nerves send impulses to make it go back 
down. We'll consider negative feedback control of 
hormones in more detail as we discuss individual 
target glands. Two anterior pituitary hormones, 
growth hormone and prolactin, do not target 
specific endocrine glands.   
ENDOCRINE GLANDS 
 The major hormone producing gl ands and 
organs, and [hormones] produced, are: 
 HYPOTHALAMUS [antidiuretic hormone 
(ADH), oxytocin, releasing/inhibiting hormones] 
 PITUITARY GLAND  
− Anterior [stimulating hormones] 
− Posterior [stores ADH and oxytocin] 
 THYROID GLAND [thyroxine, 
triiodothyronine, calcitonin] 
 PARATHYROID GLANDS [parathyroid 
hormone] 
 ADRENAL GLANDS  
− Cortex [mineralocorticoids, glucocorticoids] 
− Medulla [epinephrine, norepinephrine] 
 PANCREAS [insulin, glucagon] 
Other organs and the [hormones] they 
produce, to be described elsewhere along with the 
organ systems of which they are a part, include:  
 OVARIES [estrogens and progesterone] 
 TESTES [testosterone] 
 STOMACH [gastrin, ghrelin] 
 DUODENUM [cholecystokinin, secretin] 
 KIDNEY and LIVER [erythropoietin and 
somatomedin] 
 PLACENTA [estrogens and progesterone] 
 TROPHOBLAST [human chorionic 
gonadotropin]

LESSON 32:  HYPOTHALAMUS, PITUITARY GLAND AND THYROID GLAND 
 
HYPOTHALAMUS 
 Besides the releasing and inhibiting 
hormones, the hypothalamus also produces two 
hormones, ADH and oxytocin, that ar e carried 
through nerve fibers to the posterior pituitary gland 
for storage.   
Antidiuretic hormone (ADH) and its 
effects on nephrons was discussed previously. It’s 
an example of a hormone controlled by the nervous 
system. If osmoreceptors sense dehydratio n, 
neurally induced ADH release follows. The 
hormone targets the distal convoluted tubules of 
nephrons, opening  the pores and allowing water 
reabsorption from the urine to correct dehydration. 
The other hormone of the hypothalamus 
that is stored in the pit uitary gland is oxytocin.  
Oxytocin has two main functions in females.  It 
stimulates the uterine contractions that occur during 
labor and delivery, and it contracts the ducts in 
mammary tissue during lactation causing milk 
ejection from breast tissue. Lik e ADH, oxytocin 
levels are also controlled by the nervous system. 
Receptors in the uterine cervix are stimulated by 
pressure from the dropping fetus. Sensitive 
receptors at the nipple are stimulated by suckling. 
In both cases , release  of oxytocin follows.  In 
males, oxytocin may promote contractions in the 
reproductive ducts to facilitate ejaculation of 
semen. 
PITUITARY GLAND 
 Besides the stimulating hormones, the 
anterior pituitary gland produces growth hormone 
and prolactin.       
Growth hormone  (GH), a p rotein 
hormone produced by the anterior pituitary, 
stimulates body cells to increase in size and divide 
more rapidly.  The hypothalamus produces growth 
hormone releasing hormone (GHRH) to promote 
GH production by the pituitary.  GH increases the 
rate of am ino acid uptake by cells, increasing the 
rate of protein synthesis.   
Long bones of the body, including those 
of the arms and legs, originally develop as 
cartilage in the fetus. At birth, the cartilage has 
been replaced by bone except at thin disks near 
the top and bottom of each bone. The discs are 
called the epiphyseal discs . These discs persist 
until the late teens or early twenties. As long as 
the discs of cartilage remain, bones can continue 
to elongate and we grow taller. GH increases the 
growth of cartilage in the epiphyseal discs of long 
bones, increasing their rate of elongation.   
A good way to demonstrate the importance 
of a hormone is to observe what happens if levels of 
the hormone become abnormal. Insufficient 
amounts of GH during childhood (pr ior to closure 
of the epiphyseal discs) can result in hypopituitary 
dwarfism.  This is characterized by short stature 
but a body that is still normally proportioned.  The 
effect of too much GH (from an anterior pituitary 
tumor, for example) depends on the state of the 
epiphyseal discs.  Before disk closure, too much 
GH causes gigantism- the individual grows much 
larger than normal.  Height may exceed eight feet. 
But again, the body has normal proportions. After 
closure of the discs , bones can no longer grow  in 
length. Now  excess growth hormone causes 
acromegaly.  Bones grow thicker rather than 
longer. Soft tissue accumulates producing enlarged 
fingers, toes and facial features. The brow and jaw 
protrude and the tongue enlarges.  
Growth hormone causes the liver to release 
a factor called somatomedin that is also necessary 
to promote growth. Even if growth hormone levels 
are normal, lack of somatomedin also results in a 
variety of dwarfism. 
The anterior pituitary also produces 
prolactin, a hormone that stimulat es mammary 
glands to produce milk.  In this case, the dominant 
effect of the hypothalamus seems to be inhibition of 
prolactin secretion.  The hypothalamus releases 
prolactin inhibiting hormone (PIH) to reduce 
prolactin release by the pituitary.  PIH levels fall 
during pregnancy and s hortly after the birth of a 
child, she begins producing breast milk. Prolactin is 
produced in males, though its role is unclear. High 
levels have been shown to cause erectile 
dysfunction and infertility in males. 
 THE THYROID GLAND  
 The thyroid gland is found in the neck on 
the anterior surface of the trachea just below the 
larynx.  It consists of right and left lobes connected

===== PAGE 100 =====
96 
inferiorly by a portion of the gland called the 
isthmus.  Like all endocrine glands, the thyroid has 
a rich blood supply to deliver raw materials for 
hormone production and to transport the hormone 
to the target organ. On the posterior surface of the 
thyroid gland are four small glands called the 
parathyroid glands . Hormones of the thyroid 
gland regulate metabolic rate and hormones of both 
the thyroid and parathyroid glands regulate blood 
calcium levels. 
CONTROL OF METABOLIC RATE 
 The thyroid gland produces the hormones 
thyroxine (T4) and triiodothyronine (T3) that act 
to increase metabolic rate.  Hormones controlling 
metabolic rate are produced in thyroid follicles. 
Follicles are composed of spheres of simple 
cuboidal epithelium filled with a glycoprotein 
substance called the colloid.  The follicular cells 
collect the raw materials required to synthesize 
thyroxine and T3.  These hormones are produced in 
the colloid and stored on large molecules called 
thyroglobulin, to be released from the colloid as 
needed.  TSH (thyroid stimulating hormone) 
stimulates the synthesis of thyroxine and T 3 and 
their release into the blood from the thyroglobulin 
molecules.  The hormone calcitonin, to be 
discussed later, is produced by cells between the 
follicles. 
 The raw materials required for hormone 
production in the follicles are iodine and the amino 
acid tyrosine.  The concen tration of iodine in the 
thyroid gland is as much as 50 times the 
concentration circulating in blood.  Two tyrosine 
molecules combine with three iodine molecules to 
produce the hormone triiodothyronine (T3) and two 
tyrosine molecules combine with four iodi ne 
molecules to produce thyroxine (T4).   
 Once released from the thyroid gland, T 3 
and thyroxine bind to specific plasma proteins for 
transport to the target cells.  At the site of action 
they are released to interact with the cells as free 
hormone. T3 binds less tightly to the protein carrier 
and is thus faster acting than thyroxine.  It is also 
about five times more potent than thyroxine.  On 
the other hand, the effects of thyroxine are longer 
lasting.  About 95% of the circulating hormone 
released from the f ollicles is thyroxine, although 
significant amounts of thyroxine are converted to T3 
in the plasma.  
 Unlike other non-steroid hormones that act 
at the cell membrane, thyroid hormone penetrates 
the cell and cell nucleus much the way steroid 
hormones do. It exerts its effect by activating genes 
associated with the synthesis of enzymes 
responsible for increasing metabolic activity within 
that cell.   
 Blood levels of thyroid hormone are 
controlled by negative feedback. As blood levels 
of the target gland hormone (thyroxine or T3, in this 
case) rise, the hypothalamus and anterior pituitary 
sense that hormone concentration is adequate and 
reduce their output of 
TRH and TSH, slowing 
hormone production in 
the follicles.  Levels of 
thyroid gland hormones 
changing in o ne 
direction feedback to 
cause levels of 
controlling hormones in 
the hypothalamus and 
pituitary to change in the 
opposite direction 
(negative feedback).  If levels of thyroxine and T3 
become too low, the hypothalamus and pituitary 
gland are stimulated to increase output of TRH and 
TSH.  Thus, the hypothalamus and pituitary gland 
stimulate the thyroid gland just enough to produce 
the required amounts of thyroxine and T 3.  A 
similar feedback loop from the adrenal cortex, 
testes and ovaries regulates the levels of the 
releasing and stimulating hormones they require to 
maintain proper levels of their respective hormone 
production. 
 Persons with normal circulating levels of 
thyroxine and T 3 are termed euthyroid.  If levels 
are higher than normal, the condition is te rmed 
hyperthyroidism and if levels are too low, the 
condition is hypothyroidism. Thyroid status can be 
assessed by measuring blood levels of thyroid

===== PAGE 101 =====
97 
hormone or by measuring thyroid uptake of a small 
amount of ingested radioactive iodine. The iodine 
accumulates in the gland allowing the size of the 
gland to be determined by scanning the neck with 
an instrument that detects radioactivity.  If the 
equipment to perform these tests is not available, 
thyroid function can be determined more simply by 
measuring basal metabolic rate ( BMR).  This is a 
measure of the minimum amount of oxygen 
consumption necessary to maintain the body in a 
totally rested condition.  The normal adult value is 
about 250 ml of oxygen per minute .  The fasted 
and resting individual breathes quietly from a 
mixture with a known quantity of oxygen and the 
rate at which the oxygen removed is calculated.  
Hyperthyroid individuals would consume more 
oxygen than normal and hypothyroid individuals 
less than normal.  Some of the features of thyroid 
imbalance are presented in the table below. 
THYROID IMBALANCES 
 Thyroid disease may be caused by a defect 
in the thyroid gland itself (a primary defect), or by 
defects in glands that control thyroid secretion (a 
secondary defect).  For example, hyperthyroidi sm 
may be caused by tumorous growth of the thyroid 
gland, a primary defect, but is more often caused by 
an immune system defect that results in the 
production of a substance that acts like TSH with 
the secondary effect of producing an overactive 
thyroid. An enlarged thyroid gland in the 
hyperthyroid patient is called a toxic goiter .  
Treatment may involve surgical removal of thyroid 
tissue or destruction of thyroid tissue accomplished 
by having the patient ingest highly concentrated  
radioactive iodine.  
   Hyperthyroidism is often accompanied by 
edema formation in the back of the orbits of the 
eyes.  The result is a characteristic bulging of the 
eyeballs referred to as exophthalmus.   The most 
common type of hyperthyroidism, called Graves’s 
disease, is caused by defect in the immune system 
and is accompanied by exophthalmus. 
 Hypothyroidism results from reduction in 
hormone output from the thyroid gland.  It is 
important to realize that hypothyroidism can also 
produce enlargement of the gland.  This is calle d a 
simple goiter.  Consider the patient suffering from 
a dietary deficiency of iodine.  Without iodine, even 
a healthy gland cannot produce thyroid hormone. 
As levels of the hormone decrease, we expect levels 
of TSH to increase by negative feedback.  The 
increased stimulation of the thyroid gland by TSH 
causes it to enlarge producing a simple goiter.  
However, no matter how large the thyroid becomes, 
without iodine the patient remains hypothyroid.  
Hypothyroidism is relieved by oral consumption of 
synthetic thyroid hormone. 
 Thyroid hormone is important for normal 
protein synthesis and nerve activity.  Thyroid 
deficiency in newborn infants causes retarded 
growth and mental retardation.  This condition is 
called cretinism.  In cretinism, skeletal growth is 
retarded more than soft tissue growth, so unlike the 
pituitary dwarf, the infant is neither of normal size 
nor normally proportioned.  The effects of cretinism 
often fail to appear until after birth because the 
developing fetus is exposed to maternal thyroi d 
hormone. 
   Hyperthyroid Hypothyroid 
Personality Restless, irritable, “hyper” Listless, lethargic, mentally dull 
Cardiovascular system Elevated heart rate, blood pressure Heart rate, blood pressure reduced 
Gastrointestinal system 
Increased motility, secretion. Prone 
to diarrhea. Weight loss despite 
good appetite. 
Decreased motility, secretion. Prone to 
constipation. Weight gain despite poor 
appetite. 
Temperature control 
Increased metabolic rate translates 
to increase heat production. 
Intolerant of hot weather. 
Decreased metabolic rate translates to 
difficulty in producing heat. Intolerant of 
cold weather. 
Cell function 
Cellular metabolism and 
mitochondrial activity are increased. 
Increased glucose and oxygen 
uptake. 
Cellular metabolism and mitochondrial 
activity are decreased. Decreased 
glucose and oxygen uptake.

LESSON 33:  ADRENAL GLANDS AND PANCREAS  
 
THE ADRENAL GLANDS  
  The adrenal glands are located one above 
each kidney (suprarenal).  As is true for all glands, 
they have an extremely rich blood supply.  
Structurally, the adrenal g land is composed of an 
outer cortex and an inner medulla.  Covering the 
cortex is a connective tissue capsule.  The cortex is 
composed of three concentric zones of glandular 
epithelial cells with sinusoid spaces between.  The 
cortical hormones are made and  stored within the 
cells and secreted into the blood flowing through 
the sinusoid spaces.    
  The cortex surrounds the inner medulla.  
This region is composed of clumps of glandular 
epithelial cells with large sinus spaces between.  
Similar kinds of cells are also found near adrenergic 
synapses in the sympathetic nervous system.  While 
cortex cells can regenerate if partially destroyed, 
medullary cells cannot.  However, cells of the 
cortex are more vital than those of the medulla. 
 As described below, t he secretory activity 
of the adrenal cortex is controlled through the 
renin-angiotensin system and by negative 
feedback involving the pituitary gland and 
hypothalamus.  Hormone release from the medulla 
is initiated by nerve impulses from the sympathetic 
nervous system  as part of the body's normal 
response to stress.  
MEDULLARY HORMONES 
About 75% of the hormonal output of the 
medulla is epinephrine (adrenalin) and 25% is 
norepinephrine.  They are amine -based hormones 
and, like the thyroid amines, are derived fr om the 
amino acid tyrosine.  The medullary hormones act 
to prolong and enhance the effects of the 
sympathetic nervous system. Recall that 
norepinephrine is also a transmitter substance at 
neuromuscular junctions in the sympathetic nervous 
system. The effec ts of medullary hormones on 
target organs mimic sympathetic nervous system 
effects.  The endocrine effects begin more slowly 
but generally last longer than the neural effects.  
The most striking effect of medullary hormones is 
their ability to increase blood pressure .  Blood 
pressure can be increased by increasing the output 
of the heart or by narrowing the blood vessels. 
Epinephrine increases blood pressure 
primarily by increasing heart rate and stroke 
volume, thereby increasing cardiac output.   
 
While e pinephrine does vasoconstrict vessels in 
certain beds (the digestive organs and kidneys, for 
example), it vasodilates vessels in other organs 
(skeletal muscle that needs more blood during 
stress, for example). The effects cancel; total 
peripheral resistance does not change much. 
  Norepinephrine increases cardiac output 
only slightly compared to epinephrine.  However, 
norepinephrine vasoconstricts blood vessels in all 
target organs, causing a significant increase in 
total peripheral resistance and blood pressure.   
 The sympathetic effects of the medullary 
hormones are not limited to the cardiovascular 
system.  Epinephrine is a potent bronchodilator and 
is frequently used to dilate bronchioles constricted 
during by asthma attacks.  Epinephrine also helps to 
elevate blood glucose by stimulating the conversion 
of glycogen to glucose in the liver, a process called 
glycogenolysis (literally, the lysis of glycogen). 
 Loss of the gland would decrease the 
body's efficiency in responding to stress but is 
generally not life threatening.  Excessive secretion 
of medullary hormone causes severe sustained or 
intermittent hypertension.   
HORMONES OF THE CORTEX 
 The hormones of the cortex  are of three 
varieties: glucocorticoids, mineralocorticoids, and 
some androgenic (male)  steroids.  The steroid 
hormones of the adrenal cortex are derived from the 
steroid molecule cholesterol.

===== PAGE 103 =====
99 
Mineralocorticoids help 
maintain electrolyte 
balance by increasing the 
reabsorption of sodium 
ions from the distal 
convoluted tubules in 
renal nephr ons. As 
sodium ions reenter the 
blood, the plasma 
becomes hyperosmotic 
and, as a result, water is 
reabsorbed as well.  
Reabsorption of sodium also causes secretion of 
potassium and hydrogen ions into the urine.  The 
main human mineralocorticoid is aldosterone.  Its 
control by the renin -angiotensin system was 
discussed along with the urinary system.   
 The glucocorticoids help to insure the 
body has enough available fuel to meet its energy 
needs.  The main fuel of the body is glucose and the 
glucocorticoids help provide glucose by mobilizing 
fatty acids and amino acids and stimulating the liver 
enzymes that then convert them into glucose by the 
process of gluconeogenesis (literally, the birth of 
new glucose).  The primary glucocorticoid in 
humans is cortisol.  Release of cortisol is 
stimulated by the hypothalamic hormone CRH 
(corticotrophin releasing hormone) and the anterior 
pituitary hormone ACTH (adrenocorticotropic 
hormone).  High levels of cortisol then reduce the 
production of CRH and ACTH by negative 
feedback control.   
 The adrenal 
glands are important 
in helping us deal with 
stress.  The hormones 
of the medulla 
enhance the effects of 
the sympathetic 
nervous system 
providing adequate 
oxygen and blood 
flow.  In addition, 
stress stimulates the 
hypothalamus to 
release CRH triggering the chain of events leading 
to the release of cortisol and elevation to blood 
glucose- additional fuel for increased energy 
demands in stress.  
 Pharmacologic (not physiologic) doses of   
glucocorticoids act to reduce inflammat ion in 
injured tissues.  The inflammatory response is one 
of the body's defense mechanisms that promotes 
healing, so use of anti -inflammatory drugs is not 
always of benefit. 
 The sex steroids of the adrenal cortex are 
androgenic (male) sex hormones.  The h ormones 
are present even in females where they are 
responsible for pubic and axillary hair growth.   
ADRENAL CORTEX ABNORMALITIES 
 Hypofunction of the adrenal cortex occurs 
in Addison's disease, characterized by muscular 
weakness and fatigue caused by hypo glycemia 
related to insufficient cortisol.  A more serious 
problem is the reduced sodium ion and water 
uptake due to lack of aldosterone.  This can lead to 
loss of blood pressure and fatal shock if not treated. 
 Because reabsorption of sodium ions is linke d to 
secretion of hydrogen ions (counter -transport) loss 
of aldosterone is associate with hydrogen ion 
retention leading to acidosis and coma.  Low levels 
of cortisol stimulate release of excessive amounts 
of ACTH. The hormone ACTH is chemically 
similar to a hormone that enables certain animals to 
darken their skin color to blend into their 
surroundings.  It is the excess ACTH that causes the 
darkening of the skin associated with Addison's 
disease.  
 Hyperfunction of the adrenal cortex is 
called Cushing's syndrome.  Excess amounts of 
cortisol cause significant loss of protein to 
gluconeogenesis.  Muscle wasting, bruising, poor 
healing, thinning of the skin and osteoporosis all 
result. The hyperglycemia caused by excess 
gluconeogenesis stimulates the pancreas to release 
the hormone insulin in an attempt to lower glucose 
levels. If the hyperglycemia is severe, the over -
stimulated pancreas fails, resulting in diabetes 
mellitus. Excess aldosterone causes salt and water 
retention leading to hypertension. If levels  of 
androgenic hormones rise, sexual precocity in 
males and masculinization in females may also 
occur. 
HORMONES OF THE PANCREAS 
 The pancreas, an important accessory 
organ of the digestive system, is also the source of 
the endocrine hormones insulin and gl ucagon.  
Together, these two hormones maintain the normal

===== PAGE 104 =====
100 
blood glucose concentration at between 80 and 120 
mg per 100 ml of blood. 
 Scattered throughout the pancreas are 
clusters of endocrine cells called the islets of 
Langerhans.  Each islet is supplied by a network of 
capillaries for hormone transport. The two main cell 
types in the islets are the alpha cells that produce 
the hormone glucagon, and the beta cells that 
produce insulin.  Both of these hormones are 
proteins. 
 Insulin has the effect of lowering blood 
glucose by increasing the uptake of glucose into 
liver, muscle and adipose tissue cells.  In liver and 
muscle, the glucose taken up is converted to a 
storage molecule called glycogen.  The conversion 
of glucose to glycogen is called glycogenesis 
(literally, the birth of glycogen).  In adipose cells, 
glucose is converted to fat.  The stimulus for insulin 
release from beta cells is hyperglycemia- high 
blood glucose. Blood sugar normally begins to 
increase during a meal as glucose is absorbed into 
the blood. Insulin causes most of the absorbed 
glucose to be temporarily stored for release as 
needed between meals. 
 Glucagon acts to increase blood glucose by 
releasing it from the liver.  The stimulus for 
glucagon production is hypoglycemia (low blood 
sugar).  Between meals, as cells consume glucose 
for energy production, blood glucose levels fall.  
Glucagon then acts to increase blood sugar 
primarily by converting glycogen back to glucose - 
the process of glycogenolysis.  Recall that 
epinephrine also promotes glycogenolysis, although 
not as efficiently as glucagon.  So while there is 
only one hormone that lowers blood glucose 
(insulin), there are three that increase blood glucose 
levels (epinephrine, glucagon and cortisol). 
 DIABETES MELLITUS 
  There are no other hormones that have the 
same effect as insulin on reducing blood sugar.  In 
diabetes mellitus the body is either unable to 
produce insulin or respond to insulin. T he result is 
high blood sugar (hyperglycemia).  The signs of 
diabetes mellitus related to  the defect in glucose 
metabolism are:  
 Hyperglycemia due to impaired glucose 
uptake by target cells, 
 Glycosuria (glucose in the urine) as glucose 
filters into the urine faster than it can be 
reabsorbed back into the blood,  
 Polyuria (increased urine outpu t) as glucose 
draws water into the urine by osmosis, 
 Polydypsia (increased thirst) to replace water 
lost in urine,  
 Ketosis (fruity smelling ketone molecules in 
the blood) caused by over -using fat oxidation 
as an energy source, 
 Acidosis (high concentration of hydrogen ions 
[low pH] in the blood) caused by the acidic 
ketone residues,  
 Coma resulting from acidotic suppression of 
nervous system.        
  Even diabetics treated with insulin often 
suffer complications related to structural changes in 
their blood vessels.  These include poor circulation, 
visual problems, renal disease, infection, 
ulcerations, peripheral vascular disease and heart 
disease.   
 Taking too much insulin causes the diabetic 
to go into insulin shock .  Recall that insulin 
increases the uptake of glucose in liver, muscle and 
fat.  It is not required for glucose uptake by nerve 
tissue.  In addition, the only substrate the nervous 
system can metabolize for energy is glucose.  For 
this reason, an overdose of insulin can be fatal.  The 
excessive insulin causes too much glucose to enter 
liver, muscle and fat, leaving the nervous system 
starved for glucose.  As a result, the diabetic lapses 
into the convulsions characteristic of insulin shock. 
The hypoglycemia of insulin shock is reversed by 
giving sugar. 
 There are two types of diabetes mellitus - 
juvenile onset and adult onset.  Juvenile onset is the 
more serious form.  The body attacks its islet cells 
as if they were foreign, leaving them totally 
dysfunctional.  The juvenile onset diabetic must be 
treated with insulin injection or inhalation.  Insulin 
is a protein and would be digested if taken orally. 
  Adult onset diabetes is less severe. Here, 
insulin receptors become less responsive.  The 
defect can often be controlled by diet and exercise 
alone.  If the hyperglycemia persists, the adult onset 
diabetic can often be treated with drugs that 
stimulate insulin receptors. Obesity has been linked 
to the development of adult onset diabetes.

===== PAGE 105 =====
101 
LESSON 34:  THE PARATHYROID GLANDS AND BONE PHYSIOLOGY 
 
SKELETAL SYSTEM 
 The predominant tissue of the skeleton is 
bone.  Bone is a solid connective tissue composed 
of living osteocytes (bone cells) surrounded by an 
intercellular matrix composed mainly of inorganic 
calcium salts and collagen  protein.  Bone surfaces 
are covered with a fibrous connective tissue called 
the periosteum.  This covering is continuous with 
the coverings of tendons that connect bones to 
muscles and ligaments that connect bones to each 
other.  Cavities within bones are lined with a 
fibrous connective tissue endosteum and filled 
with red or yellow bone marrow.  Additionally, an 
extensive network of blood vessels within bone 
matrix supplies the cells with required nutrients. 
 Osteocytes are arranged in concentric rings 
around a central osteonic canal  that contains the 
blood vessels and nerves.  The concentric rings of 
bone matrix that form around each canal are called 
lamellae.  The cavity within which the bone cell is 
found is called the lacuna. Canaliculi (little canals) 
run between the lacun ae providing a path for 
nutrients from the osteonic canal.  Bone arranged in 
dense parallel networks of osteons is called 
compact bone .  In other places in a bone, the 
osteocytes may be arranged in more of a loose 
network of branching plates called trabeculae.  
This bone is called spongy (cancellous) bone.  The 
canaliculi extend from the lacunae directly to the 
outer surface of the trabeculae.  The spaces between 
trabeculae are filled with marrow.       
 In the flat bones of the skull, spongy bone 
is sandwi ched between inner and outer layers of 
compact bone.  In long bones, the shaft of the bone 
is compact and the ends are spongy bone covered 
with a thin layer of compact bone. 
BONE COMPOSITION 
 Bone is living tissue composed of 
osteocytes located within a no n-living intracellular 
matrix.  About two-thirds of the matrix is inorganic; 
mainly calcium phosphate and calcium carbonate 
salts.  The calcium salts provide strength but also 
make the bones brittle.  About one -third of the 
matrix is organic, the main cons tituent being 
collagen protein.  This provides a certain degree of 
flexibility.  With age, the bone matrix gradually 
becomes less organic and more inorganic and bone 
becomes more brittle. Also associated with aging is 
the tendency to develop osteoporosis, a disease in 
which bone matrix is replaced by softer fibrous and 
fatty tissues.  The weakened bones are prone to 
spontaneous fracture.  Many factors contribute 
including a history of inadequate dietary calcium 
and loss of estrogen after menopause in women.   
 Vitamins D and C are important for bone 
development.  Vitamin D is required for absorption 
of dietary calcium. A deficiency results in 
osteomalacia (rickets).  Bones lose rigidity and 
weight-bearing bones may begin to bow.  Vitamin 
C is necessary for c ollagen formation and a 
deficiency can result is brittle bones.  
 Several hormones affect bone growth and 
development; the rationale for covering the skeletal 
system in this lesson .  Growth  hormone is the 
obvious example but other hormones are involved 
as well.  The balance between calcium ion 
concentration in the blood and calcium ion 
concentration in bone is controlled by the two 
hormones discussed below. 
BONE MARROW 
 Marrow is found filling cavities within the 
bone.  The two types of marrow are yellow marrow 
and red marrow. 
 Initially, bone marrow is red.  It contains 
the blood forming cells for all types of blood cells, 
including the erythrocytes.  The red color comes 
from the oxygen transporting molecule hemoglobin. 
 Initially, the spleen and liver also  produce blood 
cells but beyond infancy, all blood cells except for 
certain white cells are produced exclusively in red 
marrow.  Red marrow is replaced by yellow 
marrow in many bone cavities but persists in the 
spongy bone spaces of the skull, sternum, rib s, 
vertebrae and pelvis.   
 Yellow marrow that comes to occupy other 
bone cavities is composed primarily of adipose 
tissue stores.  
SKELETON DEVELOPMENT 
 The two mechanisms for bone formation 
are intramembranous and endochondral 
(cartilaginous).

===== PAGE 106 =====
102 
 The flat bones that form the skull are 
examples of bone that develops by an 
intramembranous process.  Initially, a fibrous 
connective tissue membrane forms from primitive 
connective tissue cells that then differentiate into 
bone forming cells called osteoblasts.  A s the 
osteoblasts begin to deposit bone matrix, the 
membrane is gradually replaced by bone.  At birth, 
these membranes have not completely ossified.  
Soft spots in the skull, called fontanals, persist for 
up to eighteen months or so after birth.   
 Long bo nes of the body increase in 
diameter by an intramembranous process. 
Osteoblasts in the periosteum, a thin layer of 
fibrous tissue covering the bones, begin to deposit 
bone around the outer edges.  The center of a long 
bone has a hollow space called the medulary 
cavity that is lined with a layer of fibrous tissue 
called the endostium. As new bone is layered on the 
outside, bone -digesting cells called osteoclasts 
reabsorb bone from the inner surface beneath the 
endosteum, enlarging the diameter of the medulary 
cavity. The overall thickness of the ring of bone, in 
this way, remains the same. The tubular nature of 
long bones uses the same principle as tubular steel 
for scaffolding, providing maximum strength with 
minimum weight.  
 Other bones in the body develop  by 
endochondral bone formation.  Long bones of the 
arms and legs are examples.  Initially, the bone 
appears as cartilage. Cartilage cells called 
chondrocytes occupy the lacunae. Then, gradually, 
the chondrocytes enlarge and then deteriorate to be 
replaced by osteoblasts that begin to deposit bone 
matrix.  In long bones, ossification begins in the 
shaft of the bone called the diaphysis.  This is the 
primary ossification center that begins to develop in 
early prenatal development, beginning at about the 
third month.  During infancy and early childhood, 
secondary ossification centers form on either end of 
the bone.  The end of a long bone is called the 
epiphysis.   
 The cartilage plates that persist between the 
diaphysis and the distal and proximal epiphyses are 
called the epiphyseal plates.  As long as these 
cartilage disks persist, bone can continue to grow in 
length along plate surface.  By late teens to early 
twenties, all cartilage plates have been completely 
ossified. The bone can no longer grow in length. On 
the ends of bones that form freely movable joints, a 
layer of hyaline cartilage persists, providing a 
smooth, low friction surface for bones moving 
against each other in the joint.   
JOINTS 
 Joints or articulations are places where two 
or more bones c ome together. Classification of 
joints is made both according to the amount of 
movement possible and the type of tissue forming 
the structure of the joint. By movement, joints are 
classified as immovable, slightly movable, and 
freely movable. By structure, joints are classified as 
fibrous, cartilaginous or synovial.  
Fibrous joints occur when two bones join 
together with just a layer of fibrous connective 
tissue between them . Most are synarthroses, the 
term for immovable joints. Examples are the 
sutures joining the bones of the skull.  The jagged 
edges of bone form a tight, interlocking joint. 
Cartilagenous joints form with a layer of 
cartilage separating the bones. Most cartilaginous 
joints are amphiarthroses, the term for slightly 
movable joints.  The symphysis pubis connecting 
the two pelvic bones and the joints between the 
vertebrae are examples.  In the vertebral column, a 
disk of cartilage separates the bodies of adjoining 
vertebrae. Only a slight amount of movement is 
possible between any two of the vertebrae. 
 Synovial joints, are joints that allow free 
movement.  Synovial fluid, secreted by synovial 
membranes lining the joint cavity, lubricate the 
joint.  The smooth cartilage covering the ends of the 
bones and the lubricating fluid help to reduce 
friction between the moving bones. Synovial joints 
are also referred to as diarthroses, the term for 
freely movable joints. Diarthrotic joints are 
subdivided into several kinds based on the range of 
free motion possible. These include: 
 Ball and socket  joints, such as the hip and 
shoulder.  Here movement is possible in many 
planes, allowing flexion and extension as well as 
adduction and abduction and all combinations in 
between. 
 Hinge joints such as the elbow and knee allow 
movement in only one plane (flexion an d 
extension). 
 Pivot joints involve rotation such as occurs 
turning the head side to side or rotating the

===== PAGE 107 =====
103 
radius on the humerus, turning the palms up or 
down. 
 Gliding joints such as those of the wrist and 
ankle, involve bones sliding over one another. 
 
CALCIUM REGULATION 
 Calcium is an important structural component 
in bones and teeth. It is also required to initiate 
muscle contraction and is necessary to activate 
certain enzymatically controlled reactions such as 
blood clotting.  Calcium  ion reduces the inf lux of 
sodium ions into nerve cells and thus inhibits action 
potentials, suppressing the nervous system.  Plasma 
calcium ion levels are controlled by two hormones- 
parathyroid hormone (PTH) from the parathyroid 
glands, and calcitonin from the thyroid gland. 
PARATHYROID HORMONE 
  The parathyroid glands produce 
parathyroid hormone (PTH), a protein hormone that 
has the effect of increasing blood calcium ion 
levels when they begin to fall below normal.  PTH 
provides calcium  ion by acting on three major 
systems. PTH removes calcium  ions from bone, 
increases the reabsorption of calcium ions from the 
nephrons of the kidneys, and increases the intestinal 
absorption of calcium ion.   The effect of PTH on 
the absorption of calcium from the gut is indirect.  
PTH acts i n the kidneys to cause vitamin D to be 
converted from an inactive to an active form.  
Vitamin D then acts on the small intestines to 
increase the absorption of dietary calcium ion. 
  Hyperparathyroidism results in elevated 
blood calcium ion levels.  As men tioned above, 
elevated blood calcium ion levels  suppresses the 
nervous system, leading to fatigue, muscle 
weakness and altered mental functions.  Excess 
calcium ion filtration in the kidney often results in 
kidney stone formation.  Excess reabsorption of 
calcium ion from the skeleton results in softening of 
the bones and can lead to spontaneous fractures. 
Excess calcium ion can also lead to dangerous 
cardiac arrhythmias. The cause of 
hyperparathyroidism is usually a tumor in a 
parathyroid gland that may be surgically removed. 
 Hypoparathyroidism results in lower than 
normal blood calcium  ion levels that reduce the 
efficiency of contracting muscle and over-stimulate 
the nervous system.  As a result the patient often 
suffers from muscle spasms, tetany and 
convulsions.  Severe spasm of laryngeal muscles 
has been known to cause suffocation.   Because 
administration of PTH has undesirable side effects, 
treatment usually involves giving injections of 
calcium salts or massive doses of vitamin D.  
CALCITONIN 
  Besides the hormones produced in the thyroid 
follicles, the thyroid gland also produces the 
hormone calcitonin that lowers blood calcium ion 
levels when they become higher than normal.  The 
hormone is produced by "C" cells located between 
the thyroid follicles.   Unlike PTH, calcitonin has 
one major site of action - the skeleton.  Calcitonin 
lowers blood calcium ion concentration by 
increasing its uptake in bone.  Calcitonin released at 
mealtime causes the calcium absorbed from the gut 
to be taken out of the blood and stored in bone.    
      Control of levels of the calcium  ion regulating 
hormones is simple and direct.  Low blood calcium 
ion stimulates the parathyroid glands to release 
PTH and high blood calcium ion levels stimulate 
the thyroid gland to produce calcitonin.
