LESSON 35:  THE MALE REPRODUCTIVE SYSTEM  
 
Primary Reproductive Organs:                     
 The right and left Testes                  
− Seminiferous tubules produce sperm cells. 
− Interstitial Cells (of Leydig) produce male sex 
hormones (androgens, mainly testosterone.) 
 Accessory Sex Organs: 
− Seminal Vesicles (2) 
− Prostate Gland (1) 
− Bulbourethral Glands (2) 
 Duct system: 
− Epididymis (2) 
− Vas Deferens (2)  
− Ejaculatory Duct (2) 
− Urethra (Penis) (1) 
TESTES 
 The 
right and left 
testes are 
located in a 
pouch-like 
sac called the 
scrotum.  
Each testis is 
divided into 
about 250 
lobules in 
which are 
found coils of 
seminiferous tubules. Uncoiled, the total length of 
the tubule  system would be about half a mile.  
Within these tubules, the sperm begin the develop. 
Between the tubules, the interstitial cells produce 
androgen hormones. 
SPERMATOGENESIS 
 Germinal epithelial cells, called 
spermatogonia are located near the outer wall of 
the seminiferous tubules. Throughout the male’s 
lifetime, they co ntinue to divide by mitosis, 
ensuring a continuous supply. Starting at puberty, 
groups of them begin to transform into primary 
spermatocytes. Each spermatogonium, containing 
46 chromosomes in humans, will develop into four 
sperm with 23 chromosomes each.  This 
transformation occurs in a cell division process 
called meiosis shown below.  Spermatogonia can 
therefore divide by mitosis into new spermatogonia 
or develop into primary spermatocytes.  Unlike 
mitosis, in which the number of chromosomes is 
preserved a s cells divide, meiosis is a reduction 
division in which the number of chromosomes is 
reduced by half, that is then followed by a second 
division. In meiosis, the primary spermatocytes 
with 46 chromosomes first divide into two 
secondary spermatocytes with 23 chromosomes 
each.  Each of the two secondary spermatocytes 
divides into two spermatids, still with 23 
chromosomes each.  The four spermatids then 
transform into sperm cells.  As the cells progress 
through the stages of meiosis, they move toward the 
center of the tubules where the sperm are released. 
The sperm are passively moved through the tubules 
and out of the testis.  Movement is generated by 
peristaltic contraction of the walls of the duct 
system. 
 Also associated with the walls of the 
seminiferous tubules are large sustenticular cells 
(Sertoli cells ) that play a role in nourishing 
germinal cells, controlling their transformation and 
protecting them during the vulnerable periods of 
cell division.  
 Once formed, e ach normal sperm has a 
body containing the genetic information 
(chromosomes), and a tail called a flagellum for 
locomotion. A structure called the acrosome sits 
atop the body of each sperm cells. The acrosome 
contains enzymes necessary to digest away the cells 
and matrix that surrounds the ovum.  
 Each milliliter of semen contains about 100 
million sperm.  Large numbers of sperm are 
required for two reasons.  First, most sperm do not 
find their way to the site of fertilization, the

===== PAGE 110 =====
106 
female uterine tube.  Secondly, sperm release an 
enzyme to help  breakdown the ring of ovarian 
cells that surround the ovum.  Too few sperm 
results in too little of this enzyme. 
 Only one sperm cell will enter an ovum in 
the process of fertilization.  When it enters an 
ovum, the ovum is changed so that all other sperm 
are repelled. The change involves a reversal of 
polarity of the ovum cell membrane and the release 
of chemical inhibitors from the penetrated ovum. 
  Human cells have 44 chromosomes called 
autosomes and 2 sex chromosomes for a total of 46. 
 Males have an X and a Y sex chromosome.  In fact, 
males are males because they have an X and a Y 
chromosome in each cell. Since the chromosomes 
are divided in meiosis, half the sperm contain an X 
chromosome and half a Y chromosome.  In females, 
both sex chromosomes are X (females being 
females because they have 2 X chromosomes) and 
all ova contain one X chromosome.  Therefore, if 
an X sperm fertilizes an ovum, a female offspring 
results (XX) and if a Y sperm fertilizes and ovum, a 
male results (XY). 
THE PATHWAY FOR SPERM 
 Adjacent to each testis is a coiled tubule 
about 20 feet long called the epididymis.  Sperm 
enter the epididymis from the testis.  Here they are 
kept in an acidic environment to keep them 
immobile.  Glycogen is secreted for energy.  Sperm 
maturation is c ompleted in the epididymis.  The 
sperm are moved by peristaltic contractions of the 
walls of the epididymis. A duct called the vas 
deferens extends from the lower part of each 
epididymis.  The vas deferens carries sperm, by 
peristaltic contractions, into t he pelvic cavity and 
toward the prostate gland .  Sperm can spend 
several weeks in the vas deferens. Near the prostate, 
the vas deferens is enlarged forming an ampulla.  At 
this point, secretions from the seminal vesicles are 
added.  Seminal vesicle secretions contain fructose 
for energy and prostaglandins to stimulate uterine 
contractions helping to propel the sperm toward the 
uterine tubes, the site of fertilization.  On each side, 
the duct from the seminal vesicle and the vas 
deferens join to form a single ejaculatory duct. This 
occurs at the outer edge of the prostate gland. As 
these ducts enter the prostate gland, one on each 
side, they are now called the ejaculatory ducts.  
While still within the prostate gland, the ejaculatory 
ducts join the urethra extending from the urinary 
bladder.  In males, the urethra serves both a urinary 
and reproductive function.  The prostate gland adds 
alkaline secretions of a milky consistency to the 
urethra.  The rise in pH enables the sperm to 
become more mobile.  The pros tate also releases 
enzymes to help breakdown semen that is slightly 
coagulated as it enters the uterus.   
 Two other glands called the bulbourethral 
glands at the base of the penis secrete mucus into 
the semen.  The mucus, along with that produced in 
the vagina, provide lubrication helping to establish 
the satisfactory massaging sensation important for 
copulation.  In all, 3 to 5 milliliters of semen is 
released from the penis. 
 Sperm release involves erection, emission 
and ejaculation.  Erection is under c ontrol of the 
parasympathetic nervous system.  Three bands of 
erectile sinusoidal tissue, the two corpora 
cavernosa in the superior portion of the shaft of the 
penis and the single corpus spongiosum below, 
extend the through the length of the penis.  The 
urethra runs through the center of the corpus 
spongiosum.  Nerve impulses from the 
parasympathetic nervous system cause inflow 
vessels to dilate and outflow vessels to constrict and 
the tissue engorges with blood, enlarges and 
stiffens.  As is typical for p arasympathetic control, 
erection is a single -organ response. It may or may 
not occur in association with emission and 
ejaculation. It may occur during sexual stimulation 
or spontaneously.  
 Emission refers to process of moving the 
sperm and the secretions of the reproductive glands 
into the urethra, ready for discharge. It is essentially

===== PAGE 111 =====
107 
a process of “charging and pressurizing the lines” 
using a fire hose analogy. Ejaculation refers to the 
release of sperm, under pressure, from the penis .  
Emission and ejaculation occur together during the 
process of orgasm under control of the sympathetic 
nervous system. Sympathetic responses are 
generally “whole body responses” with multiple 
organ systems responding in coordinated fashion. 
Orgasm is a “whole body response”  accompanied 
with rhythmic skeletal muscle contractions, 
increased heart rate  and respiration, sweating and 
the like, all under sympathetic nervous system 
control. 
ANDROGEN FUNCTION OF THE TESTES  
 The interstitial cells, located between the 
seminiferous tubules, secrete androgenic (male sex) 
steroid hormones. In human males, the primary 
androgen is testosterone. The anterior pituitary 
hormone ICSH (interstitial cell stimulating 
hormone) stimulates androgen production and 
release using the 
familiar negative 
feedback control 
system shown in 
the figure . (In 
females, this 
same pituitary 
hormone is more 
appropriately 
called luteinizing 
hormone or LH.)  
 The anterior pituitary also produces another 
stimulating hormone that targets the primary 
reproductive organs. The hormone is called follicle 
stimulating hormone (FSH) , the same hormone 
produces in women and named for its target in the 
female ovary. Despite the fact that males do not 
have follicles, the name is still used. In males, FSH 
does not control hormone s ecretion as it does in 
females, however it is necessary to begin the 
process of spermatogenesis. FSH stimulates the 
sustanticular cells of the seminiferous tubules to 
initiate spermatogenesis, the process of sperm 
development from spermatagonia described earlier. 
The sustanticular cells, in turn, secrete a hormone 
called inhibin that reduces FSH secretion, keeping 
the process of sperm production under control by 
negative feedback. 
 
TESTOSTERONE 
 Testosterone is produced in the male fetus 
and in the first we eks after birth in the male infant 
to initiate development of male reproductive organs 
and the descent of the testes into the scrotum.  The 
cooler temperature in the scrotum is important for 
sperm survival.  Muscles in the wall of the scrotum 
contract and pull the testes closer to the body wall if 
the temperature becomes too cool. Testosterone 
production then stops until the time of puberty.  
After puberty, testosterone completes the 
development of secondary sex organs and 
reproductive ducts.  Testosterone is also required to 
complete each cycle of spermatogenesis. 
   Certain other characteristics not directly 
related to reproduction are influenced by 
testosterone.  Body hair growth (except the scalp) 
and sebaceous gland secretion are stimulated by 
testosterone.  In fact, female androgens (probably 
from the adrenal cortex) are responsible for axillary 
and pubic hair growth and acne in females as well.  
Testosterone is responsible for the enlarged larynx 
(deeper voice) in males.  Its effects on tissue growth 
result in the rougher skin, more muscular build, and 
taller stature in males.  Fat deposition patterns are 
influenced by sex hormones as well.  Males tend to 
accumulate excess fat in the anterior abdominal 
wall above the waist producing the characteristic 
male potbelly.    
STERILIZATION 
 Sterilization is achieved by interrupting the 
pathway for sperm. In males, this is accomplished 
by cutting and ligating both vas deferens 
(vasectomy) within the scrotum. In females the 
uterine tubes are cut and ligated ( tubal ligation), 
blocking the path to the ovarian end of the uterine 
tube where fertilization takes place. In either 
procedure, the blood vessels are not ligated. The 
blood levels of reproductive hormones are not 
changed. Because hormones are responsible for 
maintaining secondary reproductive organs, these 
structures continue to function normally. The 
prostate gland, seminal vesicles and bulbourethral 
glands continue to produce their secretions. Semen 
still forms, but it is sperm -free. Erection, emission 
and ej aculation occur as before. In females, 
hormones continue to direct the sexual cycles. 
Ovulation and menstruation continue to occur. 
Sperm, however, are unable to reach the ova that 
are produced.

===== PAGE 112 =====
108 
LESSON 36:  THE FEMALE REPRODUCTIVE SYSTEM 
 
Primary Sex Organs 
 The Ovaries (2) 
− Follicular cells produce ova and estrogen 
− Luteal cells produce estrogens and 
progesterone 
 Accessory Organs 
− Uterine tubes (2) 
− Uterus 
− Vagina 
− Vestibular glands (2) 
− Clitoris 
− Labia 
 
OOGENESIS 
 In females, oogonia cells are formed from 
the germinal epithelium and develop into about a 
million primary oocytes early in fetal life.  These 
primary oocytes then remain dormant until puberty. 
By that time, more than half of them have been lost. 
Only about 500 of those remaining will eventually 
become mature ova.  Beginning at menarche (the 
first sexual cycle), about 20 primary oocytes begin 
to mature with at the onset of each cycle.  Of these, 
only one will develop into a mature ovum by 
meiosis, as shown below.  The primary oocyte with 
46 chromosomes divides into a single secondary 
oocyte with 23 chromosomes and a smaller polar 
body that fails to develop. The secondary oocyte 
divides into an ovum and another polar body.  
Unlike the male in which each spermatogonium 
produces four sperm, a single oogo nium produces 
only one ovum. 
 The ova are produced in the primary 
female reproductive organs, the right and left 
ovary.   With e ach sexual cycle, many primary 
follicles begin to develop under the influence of 
FSH. Only one will become a mature follicle, 
however. Cells of the maturing primary follicle 
surround the developing ovum and produce 
estrogen hormones, the principal estrogen in human 
females being estradiol. 
 A cavity called the antrum forms within 
the follicle.  The cavity is filled with a nutritive and 
hormone rich follicular fluid. The ovum, 
surrounded with a thick matrix called the zona 
pellucida and a ring of cells called the corona 
radiata, is found on the edge of the antrum.  The 
primary follicle has now become  a secondary 
follicle.  As the fo llicle continues to mature, it 
moves toward the surface of the ovary, appearing as 
a sort of blister.  Soon the mature follicle ruptures 
and the secondary oocyte and its surrounding cells 
are released from the follicle and the ovary.  This is 
ovulation.  T he free secondary oocyte and 
associated cells are drawn into the uterine tube 
aided by currents produced by the beating of 
millions of cilia that line the tub ule walls.  Within 
the end of the uterine tube nearest the ovary, 
fertilization take place. If fer tilized, t he second 
meiotic division occurs forming a fertilized ovum 
and polar body.  
 The follicular cells left behind in the ovary 
develop into the corpus luteum  that, under the 
influence of luteinizing hormone from the anterior 
pituitary gland , produce s both estrogens and the 
hormone progesterone. If fertilization has not 
occurred, the corpus luteum persists only until the 
end of the sexual cycle, about two weeks after 
ovulation. By then, it has converted to a corpus 
albicans that no longer produces hor mones and is 
ultimately lost.

===== PAGE 113 =====
109 
 ACCESSORY REPRODUCTIVE ORGANS 
 A uterine tube  (also called oviduct or 
fallopian tube) extends from each ovary to the 
uterus.  The wall of the uterine tube is not 
continuous with the wall of the ovary , but  its 
enlarged end ca lled the infundibulum has finger-
like projections called fimbriae that cup the ovary. 
The two uterine tubes are connected to the upper 
portion of the centrally located uterus.  The 
fertilized ovum is transported to the uterine cavity 
and attaches to the in ner endothelial lining of the 
uterus called the endometrium.  Below the 
endometrium is the uterine myometrium composed 
of smooth muscle that contracts during labor and 
delivery under the stimulation of the hormone 
oxytocin. The outer uterus is covered with  a 
connective tissue perimetrium. 
 Inferiorly, the uterus forms the cervix or 
neck that opens into the vagina.  The vaginal walls 
are lined with epithelial cells that secrete mucus for 
lubrication during intercourse.  In the female, 
urinary and reproductive systems are separate.  The 
urethra is anterior to the vagina.  Inner and outer 
folds of skin called the labia minor  and labia 
major, respectively, surround the anterior urethral 
orifice and posterior vaginal orifice.  Beneath the 
labia minor are the two vestibular glands that also 
contribute to mucus production.  Anteriorly, the 
labia minor extend to the clitoris, a small projection 
composed of erectile tissue similar to that in the 
male penis.  Like the penis, this tissue also engorges 
with blood and stiffens during arousal. 
THE SEXUAL CYCLE 
  The sexual cycle includes events occurring 
in the ovary (ovarian cycle) and events that occur in 
the uterus (menstrual cycle). 
 Follicular development in the ovaries 
occurs in a cycle of variable length averaging 2 8 
days.  The timing of the events is under control of 
the hypothalamus.  Hypothalamic hormones control 
the secretion of gonadotropic hormones of the 
anterior pituitary that, in turn, control follicular and 
luteal development in the ovaries and the 
production of female reproductive hormones by 
follicular and luteal cells.  The ovarian hormones, 
in turn, regulate the cyclic events occurring in the 
uterus.  The hierarchy of control and cyclic changes 
in hormone levels are illustrated below.    
 The first day of the cycle is, by convention, 
the first day of menstruation.  This part of the 
cycle lasts for about five days.  During this time, 
levels of FSH (follicle stimulating hormone) from 
the anterior pituitary begin to rise.  This hormone 
stimulates some of the primary follicles in the ovary 
to begin developing.  This is the follicular phase of 
the ovary.  Follicular cells of the ovary produce 
estrogen hormones that, in turn, stimulate the 
uterine lining to proliferate replacing uterine tissue 
lost at menstruati on.  This is the proliferative 
phase of the uterus. 
 On about day fourteen following onset of 
menstruation, the secondary oocyte is released from 
the ovary (ovulation), ending the follicular phase of 
the ovary.  The follicular phase is the most irregular 
phase time-wise, making it difficult to predict the 
day of ovulation based on the beginning of the 
previous menstruation.  Ovulation is triggered by a 
sudden rise in the hormone LH. After ovulation, LH 
levels remain high and cause the transformation of 
the ovarian follicle into a corpus luteum.  This is

===== PAGE 114 =====
110 
the luteal phase of the ovary.  The cells of the 
corpus luteum produce both estrogens and 
progesterone and these ovarian hormones act on the 
uterus to continue the development of the uterine 
lining.  The lini ng becomes more vascular and the 
cells more secretory, ready to provide nutrients for 
a zygote.  This is the secretory phase of the uterus. 
 Near the end of the cycle the level of 
GnRH from the hypothalamus falls.  As a result, 
LH levels from the pituitary  fall.  With no 
luteinizing hormone, the corpus luteum begins to 
deteriorate.  The corpus luteum is the source of 
estrogens and progesterone so with the failure of 
the corpus luteum, levels of these hormones fall.  
Since the ovarian hormones are required t o sustain 
the uterine lining, when their level falls, the uterine 
lining begins to shed.  Because of the vascular 
nature of the lining, its loss is associated with the 
bleeding that marks the beginning of menstruation 
and the first day of the next cycle.  The luteal phase 
lasts about fourteen days.  Its duration is not as 
variable as the duration of the follicular phase. 
 As with other hypothalamic and anterior 
pituitary hormones, gonadotropin levels are 
controlled by negative feedback as shown in the 
figure.  GnRH stimulates the release of FSH and 
LH.  FSH stimulates the release of estrogens and 
LH stimulates the release of both estrogens and 
progesterone from the ovary.  High levels of 
ovarian hormones then suppress the output of 
hormones from the hypotha lamus and anterior 
pituitary (negative feedback).  This can be 
demonstrated in a practical way by the oral 
contraceptive. Oral contraceptives contain synthetic 
estrogens and progesterone that keeps the blood 
levels of these steroid hormones high.  By negative 
feedback, this results in low levels of LH and FSH. 
 But for ovulation to occur, a sudden increase in LH 
is required.  By preventing the rise in LH, the birth 
control pill prevents ovulation.   With no ovum 
released, fertilization cannot occur. 
 
 
 When the supply of primary follicles is 
exhausted in the late forties, early fifties, menstrual 
cycling stops.  This is menopause.  Female steroid 
hormones are no longer produced within the ovaries 
and the cyclic development and shedding of the 
uterine endometrial lining stops.

===== PAGE 115 =====
111 
LESSON 37:  PREGNANCY AND FETAL DEVELOPMENT  
 
FERTILIZATION  
 Since the ovum only survives for about a 
day at most, fertilization occurs shortly after 
ovulation. Fertilization occurs near the 
infundibulum of the uterine tube. The fertilized 
ovum or zygote spends about six days in the uterine 
tube then enters the uterus and implants in the 
endometrium.   
 Toward the end of the sexual cycle, levels 
of GnRH and LH fall as usual.  However, if 
implantation is to be sustained, levels of estrogen 
and progesterone from the corpus luteum must be 
maintained.  To replace the stimulatory function of 
LH in the ovary, certain cells derived from the 
zygote called trophoblast cells produce the 
hormone HCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) 
that sustains the maternal corpus luteum for about 
the first trimester of pregnancy.  That is, the corpus 
luteum continues to produce estrogen and 
progesterone for about three months. Presence of 
HCG in the urine is used to confirm pregnancy.  
When HCG is no  longer produced, the corpus 
luteum no longer generates steroid hormones.  
Levels of estrogens and progesterone remain high, 
however, because by the third month, the placenta 
is developed and is now able to produce them.  The 
placenta remains the source of these hormones until 
the onset of labor. 
PRENATAL DEVELOPMENT 
 Period of the Zygote.  Fertilization occurs 
in the ovarian end of the uterine tube.  Over the 
next five to seven days, the zygote makes its way to 
the uterus to become implanted in the uterine lining. 
This is the time required for the uterine lining to 
reach full thickness under stimulation by the 
ovarian hormones. At implantation, this period 
ends.  
  Within 24 hours of fertilization, the ovum 
begins its cell divisions forming first a solid sphere 
of cells and then a hollow ball of cells called the 
blastocyst.  At this point, the cells all derived from 
the single ovum differentiate into two types:  The 
cells forming the hollow sphere are called 
trophoblast cells.  They help sustain the embryo 
but are not destined to become part of the embryo.  
The rest of the cells form an inner cell mass called 
the embryoblast within the cavity of the blastocyst. 
 These are the cells that will form the embryo, fetus, 
and ultimately, the infant.  About a week af ter 
fertilization, the trophoblast cells penetrate the 
endometrium and the next phase begins. 
 Period of the Embryo .  The second 
through eighth week after fertilization is the period 
of the embryo.  During this time, the embryoblast, 
now called the embryo,  develops the rudiments of 
all internal organs.  By the middle of this period, a 
primitive heart is already beating and pumping 
blood through the developing circulatory system.  
By the eighth week, the embryo is about 3 
centimeters long and weighs about 5 grams.  Its 
head, eyes, ears, nose, mouth  and the limbs with 
fingers and toes, clearly distinguish it as human.  It 
is surrounded by a membrane called the amnion 
filled with amniotic fluid for cushioning and 
support. 
 The trophoblast, now called the chorion, 
also undergoes dramatic changes.  The embryo 
becomes attached to the chorion by a slender stalk 
called the umbilical cord. Chorion cells develop 
projections called the chorionic villi that extend 
over the entire outer surface of the chorion.  
Eventually only those villi adjacent to the 
endometrium of the uterus will persist and form the 
fetal portion of the placenta.   With the placenta 
well established, the period of the embryo comes to 
an end. 
  Period of the Fetus.  The beginning of the 
third month to the time of birth is the period of the 
fetus.  During this time, organs established during 
the embryonic period develop and grow more 
rapidly.  By the end of fetal life, the fetus will have 
grown to about 50 centimeters in length and weigh 
about 3000 gram s (6 -8 pounds).  The first fetal 
movements occur in the fifth month of pregnancy.   
The brain grows most dramatically.  At birth, the 
infant is only about 5% of adult size.  But the brain 
is already 25% of adult size.  This is apparent when 
observing the relatively large size of the cranium of 
the infant relative to the size of the facial bones.    
 During this period, the chorion develops 
into a placenta. The fetus is attached to the 
placenta by the umbilical cord . Besides 
producing estrogen and progestero ne, the placenta 
must act as the lungs, kidneys and digestive

===== PAGE 116 =====
112 
system of the fetus. The need for fetal circulation 
to the placenta is clear.  The fetal lungs are 
collapsed and airless, not exchanging oxygen or 
carbon dioxide. The fetal digestive tract is no t 
absorbing nutrients and the fetal kidneys are not 
clearing wastes from the blood. All these 
functioins must be provided by maternal organs. 
Blood, low in nutrients and high in wastes, is 
carried from the fetus to the placenta through two 
umbilical arteri es. Blood is carried to tufts of 
capillaries called the chorionic villi  of the fetal 
placenta. The villa are bathed with maternal blood 
flowing through sinus cavities of the maternal 
placenta. Oxygen and other nutrients are 
transferred from maternal to fet al blood and 
transported to the fetus through the single 
umbilical vein.  Carbon dioxide and other fetal 
wastes are transferred to the maternal blood. 
THE FETAL HEART 
 Because the fetal lungs are not ventilating, 
it would seem wasteful to send half the ca rdiac 
output through the pulmonary circulation as occurs 
when the lungs are ventilating. Two anatomical 
pathways in the fetal circulation divert blood away 
from the pulmonary artery and shunt it directly into 
the systemic circulation. Blood entering the ri ght 
atrium can pass 
directly into the 
left atrium 
through an 
opening in the 
septum called 
the foramen 
ovale.  From 
there it enters 
the left ventricle 
and is pumped into the aorta.  Blood that does pass 
to the right ventricle and then to the pulmonary 
artery can still avoid circulating through the lungs. 
A vessel called the ductus arteriosus  transfers 
pulmonary arterial blood directly into the aorta. At 
birth, the inflation of the lungs with air causes the 
vessels of the lungs to expand as well. Pulmonary 
vascular resistance falls and blood flow through 
the foramen ovale and ductus arteriosus reverses. 
A one -way valve in the foramen ovale closes to 
block flow. Muscles in the ductus arteriosus begin 
to constrict and within a week, it is totally 
occluded. 
 
MATERNAL ADJUSTMENTS 
 Adjustment to the pregnant mother’s 
physiology are required to meet the demands of  
pregnancy.  
For the digestive system, the increase in 
metabolic rate causes an increase in appetite, 
ensuring availability of adequate nutrients for 
growth of the embryo and fetus. A normal weight 
gain of about 24 pounds is expected. During the 
third trimester, nutrients are required faster than 
they could be provided from the diet. To prevent a 
deficiency, reserves of nutrients begin to be stored 
in the pla centa during the second trimester. A 
negative effect on the digestive is maternal 
heartburn and indigestion caused compression of 
the intestines in the congested abdominal cavity. 
 By full term, the placenta is going to 
require a maternal blood flow of about 600-650 ml 
per minute. During pregnancy, maternal blood 
volume increase s by about 30% due to fluid 
retention and hemopoiesis (blood cell production). 
Cardiac output gradually increases by about 30% 
over the course of the pregnancy. A negative 
circulatory effect is increased risk of hemorrhoids 
and varicose veins due of compression of 
abdominal blood vessels  that impedes venous 
return through the inferior vena cava. 
 The increase in metabolic rate requires 
about a 50% increase in ventilation as pregnancy 
proceeds. The increase is achieved primarily by 
increasing respiratory rate. Again, congestion in 
the abdominal cavity makes it difficult to breathe 
more deeply. 
 In the kidneys, maternal glomerular 
filtration rate increases by about 50% and urine 
output i ncreases slightly, enabling the mother to 
dispose of additional wastes of the developing 
fetus. Aldosterone and the reproductive hormones 
act on the kidneys to promote water retention and 
sustain blood pressure. Compression of the 
urinary bladder makes it necessary to void more 
frequently. 
 Growth of skin in the abdominal area may 
produce stretch marks associated with dermal 
expansion. There may be a temporary darkening 
of skin on the nose a cheeks, called chloasma or 
“the mask of pregnancy”.

===== PAGE 117 =====
113 
CHILDBIRTH 
 About 280 days (nine months) after 
fertilization, levels of estrogen and progesterone 
fall and oxytocin levels rise inducing contractions 
of the uterine myometrium as the infant is born.  
Parturition is the process of giving birth by means 
of contractions o f the mother’s uterine and 
abdominal muscles. Contractions occurring at full 
term are initiated hormonally. Progesterone 
inhibits contractions and estrogen and oxytocin 
stimulate contractions. During late pregnancy, the 
pituitary stores more oxytocin and t he uterus 
develops more oxytocin receptors. The uterine 
myometrial mass increases throughout pregnancy 
as uterine smooth muscle cells undergo 
hyperplasia (divide). Oxytocin stimulates 
contraction of the uterine myometrium directly. 
Weak contractions, calle d Braxton -Hicks 
contractions, can occur during pregnancy as “false 
labor”.   
 Contractions begins about 30 minutes 
apart, gradually increasing in frequency to 1 -3 
minutes apart. The intermittent contractions allow 
time in between to ensure adequate blood f low is 
maintained to the uterus and fetus. Contractions 
are strongest in the fundus and body of the uterus, 
propelling the fetus toward the cervix. 
 Childbirth is an example of a positive 
feedback loop. Pressure of the fetal head against 
the cervix causes sensory nerves to signal the 
release of oxytocin. The contractions caused by 
oxytocin cause the fetal head to be forced against 
the cervix with more pressure. This initiates the 
release of more oxytocin that causes more 
pressure and so on.  As uterine contr actions 
progress, a spinal reflex causes the contraction of 
abdominal muscles to provide added help in the 
delivery process.  
PUERPERIUM 
 Mother’s recovery from pregnancy, 
occurring over a period of about six weeks, is 
referred to as the puerperium. During  this period, 
the uterus shrinks to its pregravid weight in about 
four weeks. The process, referred to as involution, 
is accomplished by autolysis (self-digestion) using 
lysosomal enzymes. Vaginal discharge resulting 
from involution is called lochia. 
 Involution is aided by breastfeeding that 
suppresses estrogen production. The release of 
oxytocin needed for milk ejection, also facilitates 
the process by causing uterine contractions that 
reduce blood flow to the shrinking uterus.  
LACTATION 
  During pregnan cy, more extensive 
development of alveolar ducts and glands within the 
breasts occurs under the influence of estrogen and 
progesterone.  Levels of the anterior pituitary 
hormone prolactin also increase. While the 
function of prolactin is to stimulate milk production 
in breast tissue, the high levels of estrogen and 
progesterone during pregnancy override the effect.  
With the falling levels of the steroid hormones at 
birth, prolactin begins to stimulate production of 
milk rich in galactose, calcium and fats.  Following 
birth, levels of prolactin would return to pre -
pregnancy levels within a week or so.  But each 
time the mother nurses, tactile stimulation of the 
nipple causes a surge in prolactin release to sustain 
milk production.  With continued stimulation , the 
ability to lactate could last for years.  Tactile 
stimulation of the breast also causes a neural reflex 
that results in the release of the hypothalamic 
hormone oxytocin, causing contraction of the 
alveolar ducts and milk ejection.

LESSON 38:  EXERCISE PHYSIOLOGY  
  
As a way of demonstrating how the organ 
systems we've examined work together for the 
benefit of the organism, let's consider how the 
organism responds to the stress of exercise. 
ENERGY PRODUCTION 
 The energy required by the body is in the 
form of ATP, adenosine triphosphate.  Strenuous 
exercise increases ATP consumption by as much as 
ten times.  The stored ATP in muscle cells is used 
up within a few seconds.  The ADP remaining after 
energy expenditure can be recharged to ATP by 
creatine phosphate.  The phosphate is donated to 
ADP converting it to ATP.  But this source is 
probably depleted after about 10 seconds of 
strenuous exercise.  Stored glycogen in the muscle 
can be anaerobically converted to pyruvic acid 
(glycolysis) and then lactic acid, providing ATP 
lasting only for about a minute or so of strenuous 
exercise.  Sustaining exercise beyond this time 
requires a continuous supply of nutrients from 
outside the cells and a continuous supply of oxygen 
from the lungs.  ATP is then produced in the 
mitochondria by burning carbohydrates, fats and 
proteins through glycolysis, the citric acid cycle and 
the electron transport chain.  The nutrients for this 
source of ATP are provided by the digestive system 
and stored in the liver and muscle (as glycogen) or 
adipose tissue (as fat).  The mitochondrial energy 
lasts as long as the nutrients and oxygen can be 
provided to the cells.    
 
Energy System Activity Time 
Creatine Phosphate  Weight lifting 10 sec 
CP and GLA Home run  
GlycogenLactic Acid Tennis ~1 min 
GLA and Aerobic Boxing  
Aerobic Jogging No limit 
 
MUSCLES IN EXERCISE 
 Exercise can be categorized into two 
extremes; exercise requiring primarily strength and 
exercise involving primarily endurance.  There are 
actually two types of muscle cells, one type adapted 
for strength and one type for endurance.  Strength 
exercises promote the formation of fast twitch 
(white) muscle cells with more enzymes for 
metabolism of cr eatine phosphate and muscle 
glycogen.  These cells can respond quickly 
providing considerable strength, but the energy 
sources they are designed to use are rapidly 
expended, as discussed above.   Endurance 
exercises promote the development of slow twitch 
(red) muscle.  These cells are designed to make use 
of external nutrients and oxygen.  The cells have 
more mitochondria, a better blood supply and 
contain myoglobin, a protein similar to hemoglobin 
that enables them to store their own oxygen.  The 
mitochondrial energy source lasts as long as 
nutrients are provided. All muscles in the body 
actually contain both types of cells.  The ratio of 
white to red cells varies between different muscle 
groups and between individuals.  The differences 
between individuals are genetically determined.  So 
some athletes are born to be sprinters (muscle 
strength) while others are born to be marathon 
runners (muscle endurance). 
 Exercise promotes muscle growth by 
causing muscle cells to hypertrophy (enlarge) and 
increase their c ontent of contractile protein (actin 
and myosin).  Overall muscle mass is the result of 
not only of training but also the reproductive 
hormone environment. While muscle cells in males 
and females provide the same strength, the anabolic 
steroid hormone test osterone enables males to 
achieve a total muscle mass as much a 40% greater 
than females.   
  Weight training, in which muscles are 
provided with the greatest resistance possible, 
builds muscle mass most rapidly. Muscle cells can 
increase size by up to 30%  after 6 to 8 week of 
resistance training. No load training provides very 
little or no increase in muscle mass.   
 
ENDURANCE EXERCISE 
 Energy production to sustain endurance 
exercise requires a continuous supply of nutrients 
and oxygen for the exercising c ells.  The most 
important nutrient for muscle endurance is the 
glycogen stored in the muscle itself.  Muscle has a 
maximum capacity of about 40 grams of glycogen 
per kilogram of muscle.  To maximize the amount 
of glycogen in muscle, a high carbohydrate die t is 
required.  “Carbo loading” can double the amount 
of glycogen in the muscles and thus the time to 
exhaustion compared to a mixed diet.  A high fat 
diet actually decreases the time to exhaustion.   
Carbohydrates are also provided from 
glycogen stored in  the liver and from glucose 
solutions consumed during the exercise period.  
However, as time passes, carbohydrate becomes 
less important and fat becomes more important as 
the source of nutrient for energy production.  After

===== PAGE 119 =====
115 
3 or 4 hours of exercise, fat be come the major 
nutrient source.   
 Delivery of adequate raw materials (and 
elimination of metabolic wastes) requires 
participation of several organ systems: 
THE NERVOUS AND ENDOCRINE SYSTEMS 
 The whole body response to exercise is 
coordinated by the sympat hetic nervous system.  
Receptors in exercising muscle along with 
chemoreceptors and baroreceptors stimulate the 
lungs, heart and blood vessels to respond 
appropriately, increasing delivery of nutrients to 
skeletal muscles.  The endocrine system provides 
many important hormones to help meet the 
demands of exercise.  Epinephrine and 
norepinephrine, from the adrenal medulla, are the 
most important. The hormones extend and prolong 
the sympathetic effects.  They act as a backup for 
the nervous system. For example, if the heart were 
denervated (following a heart transplant), 
epinephrine would still increase cardiac output 
during exercise. Hormones like cortisol and 
glucagon help to provide increases in blood glucose 
to fuel ATP production. Glucagon, the protein 
hormone, would act most quickly, causing liver 
glycogen to be converted to glucose and raising 
blood glucose levels. Cortisol, being a steroid 
hormone, would not be immediately effective 
during the bout of exercise.  Recall that steroid 
hormones alter protein  production by activating 
genes within the nucleus of target cells and require 
hours or even days to become fully effective. 
THE CARDIOVASCULAR SYSTEM 
 Blood vessels in muscle dilate during 
exercise.  Part of the vasodilation is due to intrinsic 
factors.  Metabolites from exercising muscles 
themselves act to vasodilate blood vessels.  Part of 
the dilation is due to extrinsic factors.  For example, 
epinephrine vasodilates vessels in skeletal muscle.  
Recall from the Poiseuille relationship that there is 
a direct relationship between flow and radius to the 
fourth power.   The vasodilation in muscle vessels 
and the rise in blood pressure can increase local 
blood flow to muscle by about 25 times in 
strenuous exercise.  Yet, the ability to increase 
blood flow is the limiting factor in exercise. 
 Intrinsic mechanisms (Starling's Law of the 
Heart) can increase stroke volume and, therefore, 
cardiac output by 100 - 150%.  In this mechanism, 
increased venous return causes increased strength 
of contraction in heart muscle and increased cardiac 
output.  Extrinsic (humoral and neural) mechanisms 
act to further increase cardiac output by increasing 
both stroke volume and heart rate.  Together, 
intrinsic and extrinsic mechanisms can increase 
cardiac output to nearly five tim es resting level.   
The relative importance of stroke volume and heart 
rate in providing increased cardiac output is shown 
in the figure.  Notice that most of the increase in 
output is due to heart rate increase.  Note that stroke 
volume reaches its maximum when cardiac output 
is only half maximum, so the further increase in 
cardiac output is only due to increased heart rate.  
 In non -endurance trained individuals, the 
increase in output relies more on the heart rate 
increase.  Regular endurance exercise s trengthens 
the heart by enlarging the cardiac muscle cells 
(hypertrophy), enabling it to pump a larger stroke 
volume and making the heart less reliant on a 
higher heart rate during exercise. This significantly 
increases cardiac efficiency.  In fact, while the 
untrained individual can increase resting cardiac 
output by 300 to 400 percent during maximal 
exercise, a marathon runner can increase cardiac 
output by 500 to 600 percent and when maximum 
cardiac output has been reached, the runner’s heart 
rate will still be less than that of the non-athlete. To 
begin to produce this change, the endurance 
exercise needs to be fairly intense (at least 60% 
maximum), for at least twenty minutes, performed 
at least three times a week. 
THE RESPIRATORY SYSTEM 
 Oxygen consumption is about 250 ml/min 
at rest in an adult male.  During strenuous exercise, 
this can increase about 10 to 20 times, depending 
on level of training. The increase in oxygen 
consumption is accompanied by a linear increase in 
ventilation, accomplished by in creasing both 
breathing frequency and depth.  (A portion of the

===== PAGE 120 =====
116 
inspiratory and expiratory reserve volumes is added 
to the normal tidal volume.)  Even during maximal 
exercise, ventilation is only at about 65% 
maximum.  Therefore, ventilation is normally not a 
limiting factor in endurance exercise. (But smokers 
beware!) 
  The increase in ventilation is 
accompanied by an increase in lung perfusion as 
cardiac output is increased.  The combined effect is 
to open additional pulmonary alveoli and 
capillaries, the reby increasing the surface area 
available for diffusion of respiratory gases.  
Specifically, the oxygen diffusing capacity of the 
lung increases from about 23 ml/min/mmHg at rest 
to about 60 ml/min/mmHg in strenuous exercise.   
The increase in diffusing c apacity is 
sufficient to maintain normal arterial blood gas 
levels- a PO2 of 100 mm Hg and a P CO2 of 40 mm 
Hg. However, while oxygenation of blood takes 
only about a third of the time available at rest, all 
the time available may be required during exercise. 
So smokers who have damaged their lungs to the 
extent that it takes all the time available to 
oxygenate their blood at rest, are going to become 
immediately short of breath during exercise and 
their arterial P O2 will be significantly below 
normal.  
More oxygen is extracted from hemoglobin 
during exercise, so mixed venous PO 2, normally 
about 40 mm Hg, may fall to around 10 or 15 
mmHg. The mixed venous hemoglobin that is 
normally still 75% saturated with oxygen, may now 
be less than 20% saturated.  
BODY HEAT AND FLUID BALANCE 
 Increasing ATP production is accompanied 
by increased heat production.  To dissipate this 
heat, blood flow to the skin is increased.  At the 
body surface heat is lost by the conventional means- 
infrared radiation, convection, conducti on and 
evaporation. In e vaporation of sweat containing 
water and salts, as much as 5 to 10 pounds of water 
(along with salt) per hour can be lost during 
strenuous exercise on a hot day.  After the exercise, 
salt and water are replenished through the urinar y 
system under endocrine control. The hormone ADH 
increases water reabsorption, correcting for 
dehydration (water loss). The hormone aldosterone 
increases reabsorption of salt and water, correcting 
for volume depletion (salt and water loss). Besides 
reabsorbing salt from urine, aldosterone also 
reduces the s alt content of sweat. If bouts of 
exercise are repeated daily, within a week or so, 
levels of salt in sweat fall significantly. 
RECOVERING FROM EXERCISE 
 Exercise depletes the stores of oxygen, 
glycogen and phosphocreatine in the body and 
causes lactic acid to accumulate.  Oxygen is stored 
in the air in the lungs and in body fluids as well as 
in chemical association with the hemoglobin and 
myoglobin molecules.  
Following exercise, hemoglobin and 
myoglobin oxygen saturation is significant ly 
reduced, as mentioned above. To recover the loss, 
ventilation (oxygen consumption) remains high  
even after the exercise has been completed.   The 
oxygen thus provided restores phosphocreatine and 
oxygen levels within minu tes.  In addition, the 
oxidative metabolism consumes some of the lactic 
acid that accumulated.  The rest of the lactic acid is 
metabolized back to glucose in the liver and the 
glucose used to replenish glycogen.  Metabolic 
removal of lactic acid is a slower process that lasts 
for about an hour after the exercise period.   
 Glycogen replacement requires much more 
time.  While a high carbohydrate diet is best, it still 
takes about two days to bring glycogen stores back 
to pre-exercise levels.  On a high fat diet, glycogen 
stores may not be back to normal even after five 
days.  So for maximum efficiency, one should allow 
at least two days of rest between endurance events 
of several hours or more duration.    
 The ability of the organism to function 
under normal conditions and under conditions of 
stress, as in exercise, involves the coordinated 
efforts of all organ systems working together.  
BENEFITS OF EXERCISE 
 Many studies have shown that people who 
maintain appropriate body fitness, including 
exercise and weight control, live longer. Some of 
the benefits of exercise include: 
 Reduced blood pressure, cholesterol and low 
density lipoproteins lower the risk of heart 
disease. 
 Increased physiological reserves available in 
times of sickness. 
 Reduced risk of chronic d iseases related to 
obesity including type II diabetes. 
 Reduced risk of several type s of cancers 
through mechanisms still not fully understood.
